# The Odyssey — Modern English Prose

Translated directly from the Greek text in `seeds/greek.json`
(Perseus/Loeb 1919, ed. A. T. Murray), with no reference to any other
translation. A book marked *[not yet translated]* falls back to a
reader placeholder at build time.

## Book I

Tell me, Muse, of the resourceful man who wandered so long and far after he sacked the sacred city of Troy. He saw the cities of many peoples and came to know their minds, and at sea he suffered many pains in his heart, struggling to save his own life and bring his companions home. But he could not save them, hard as he tried—they were destroyed by their own recklessness, the fools, for they ate the cattle of the Sun-god, Helios, and the god took away their day of return. Of these things, goddess, daughter of Zeus, tell us too, starting wherever you please.

By now all the others who had escaped sheer destruction were safe at home, having survived the war and the sea. Odysseus alone, longing for his home and his wife, was held back by the queenly nymph Calypso, a goddess among goddesses, in her hollow caves, wanting him for her husband. But when, as the years turned, the time came that the gods had spun out for him to return home to Ithaca, not even there was he free of hardship, even among his own people. All the gods pitied him—except Poseidon, who kept up his relentless anger against godlike Odysseus, until he should reach his own land.

But Poseidon had gone off to visit the distant Ethiopians, who live divided in two, the farthest of men, some where the sun sets and some where it rises, to receive a sacrifice of bulls and rams. There he sat, enjoying the feast, while the rest of the gods were gathered together in the halls of Olympian Zeus.

Among them the father of gods and men was first to speak, for in his heart he remembered noble Aegisthus, whom Orestes, Agamemnon's far-famed son, had killed. Thinking of him, he said to the immortals: “How wrong it is that mortals blame the gods! They say their troubles come from us, when in fact it is their own recklessness that brings them pain beyond their fate. Just so Aegisthus, beyond his fate, took Agamemnon's wedded wife and killed him when he came home, though he knew it meant his own sheer ruin—for we ourselves had warned him, sending Hermes, the sharp-eyed slayer of Argus, to tell him not to kill the man nor court his wife: Vengeance will come from Orestes, Agamemnon's son, once he grows up and longs for his own land. So Hermes told him, but for all his good intentions he could not persuade Aegisthus's heart, and now he has paid for everything at once.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “Father of us all, son of Cronus, highest of rulers—that man deserved exactly the ruin he got, and so may anyone else perish who does such things! But my heart aches for wise Odysseus, poor man, who has long suffered far from his own people, on an island ringed by the sea, the very navel of the ocean. It is a wooded island, and a goddess lives there in her halls, the daughter of grim Atlas, who knows the depths of every sea and himself holds up the tall pillars that keep earth and sky apart. His daughter keeps the poor grieving man there, always trying to charm him with soft, coaxing words into forgetting Ithaca. But Odysseus, longing even just to see the smoke rising from his own land, wants only to die. Does your heart not go out to him at all, Olympian? Did Odysseus not please you with sacrifices beside the Argive ships, on the broad plain of Troy? Why then, Zeus, are you so angry with him?”

Zeus who gathers the clouds answered her: “My child, what a thing to say! How could I ever forget godlike Odysseus, who surpasses other mortals in sense, and who has given the greatest offerings to the immortal gods who hold the wide heavens? No—it is Poseidon, the earth-holder, who nurses an unrelenting grudge, ever since Odysseus blinded his son, the godlike Cyclops Polyphemus, mightiest of all the Cyclopes. The nymph Thoosa bore him, daughter of Phorcys, lord of the barren sea, after lying with Poseidon in the hollow caves. Since then, the earth-shaker does not kill Odysseus outright, but drives him wandering far from his own country. Come, then—let us all here plan his homecoming, how he may return; and Poseidon will let go of his anger, for he cannot, alone, keep fighting the will of all the other immortal gods together.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him again: “Father of us all, son of Cronus, highest of rulers—if this is now truly pleasing to the blessed gods, that resourceful Odysseus should return home, then let us send Hermes, the guide, the slayer of Argus, to the island of Ogygia, so that as quickly as possible he may tell the fair-haired nymph our firm decision, that steadfast Odysseus is to set out for home. And I myself will go to Ithaca, to rouse his son further, and to put courage in his heart to call the long-haired Achaeans to assembly and speak out against all the suitors, who are forever slaughtering his crowding sheep and his shambling, crooked-horned cattle. I will send him to Sparta and to sandy Pylos, to ask after his father's return home, in hopes he may hear something, and so that he might win a good name for himself among men.”

So she spoke, and bound beneath her feet the beautiful sandals, immortal and golden, that carried her over water and over the boundless earth alike, swift as the blowing wind. She took up her powerful spear, tipped with sharp bronze, heavy and huge and strong, with which she brings down the ranks of heroes she is angry with, she, the daughter of a mighty father. She went darting down from the peaks of Olympus, and came to stand in the land of Ithaca, at Odysseus's outer gate, on the threshold of the courtyard, the bronze spear in her hand, disguised as a stranger, Mentes, chieftain of the Taphians.

There she found the arrogant suitors. They were amusing themselves at that moment with a game of draughts in front of the doors, sitting on the hides of cattle they themselves had slaughtered. Heralds and busy attendants were mixing wine and water for them in bowls, while others were wiping down the tables with porous sponges and setting them out, and still others were carving up great quantities of meat. Telemachus, godlike in looks, was the first by far to see her, for he sat among the suitors with his heart heavy with grief, picturing in his mind his noble father, imagining him arriving from somewhere and scattering the suitors through the halls, winning back his honor and ruling his own household once more. As he sat among the suitors thinking these thoughts, he caught sight of Athena.

He went straight to the doorway, indignant in his heart that a guest should be kept standing so long at the gate. Coming close, he took her right hand, relieved her of the bronze spear, and spoke to her, saying: “Welcome, stranger—you will be treated kindly here. Once you have had your fill of dinner, you can tell us what you need.” So saying he led the way, and Pallas Athena followed. When they were inside the lofty hall, he carried the spear and set it against a tall pillar, in a polished spear-rack where many other spears of steadfast Odysseus stood; then he led her to a chair and seated her, spreading a fine cloth beneath, an elegant, well-made chair with a footstool for her feet. He drew up an ornately carved seat for himself nearby, apart from the suitors, so that his guest, troubled by their noise, would not lose his taste for the meal among such overbearing men, and so that he might ask him about his absent father. A servant brought water in a fine golden pitcher and poured it over a silver basin for their hands to be washed, and drew up a polished table beside them. The grave housekeeper brought bread and set it before them, adding many good things, generous with what she had; the carver lifted platters of every kind of meat and set them down, and set golden cups beside them; and a herald kept coming by to pour their wine.

Then the arrogant suitors came in and took their places in order on the couches and chairs; heralds poured water over their hands, maidservants heaped bread in baskets, and young men filled the mixing-bowls to the brim with wine. They reached out their hands to the good things spread before them. When they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, the suitors turned their minds to other things—song and dancing, the crowning pleasures of any feast. A herald placed a beautiful lyre in the hands of Phemius, who was compelled to sing for the suitors; and as he touched the strings he struck up a fine song.

Meanwhile Telemachus spoke to bright-eyed Athena, leaning his head close so the others would not hear: “Dear stranger, will you be angry with me for what I am about to say? Song and the lyre are all that matter to men like these—easy enough, since they are devouring another man's livelihood without paying for it, a man whose white bones, I suppose, are rotting somewhere in the rain on the mainland, or rolling in the waves of the sea. If they ever saw him come back to Ithaca, they would all pray to be quicker on their feet rather than richer in gold and clothing. But instead he has died some wretched death, and there is no comfort left for us, even if someone on this earth says he is coming—the day of his return is gone for good. But come, tell me this, and be honest with me: who are you, and where are you from? Where is your city, who are your parents? What kind of ship did you come on, and how did the sailors bring you to Ithaca? Who did they claim to be? For I do not imagine you came here on foot. And tell me this too, truly, so I may know: is this your first time here, or are you a family friend of my father's? For many men used to visit our house in the old days, since he too was one who traveled much among people.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “Very well, I will tell you all this quite truly. I am Mentes, son of wise Anchialus, and I am lord of the oar-loving Taphians. I have put in here now with my ship and my crew, sailing over the wine-dark sea to men of a foreign tongue, bound for Temese, in search of copper, and I carry gleaming iron to trade. My ship lies at anchor out in the country, away from the city, in the harbor of Rheithron, below wooded Neion. Our fathers were guest-friends of each other from way back—you may go and ask the old hero Laertes, who they say no longer comes to the city, but lives apart in the countryside in hardship, with only an old woman servant to give him food and drink, whenever weariness takes hold of his limbs as he creeps along the slope of his vineyard. I have come now because they told me your father was at home again—but it seems the gods are still hindering his path. For godlike Odysseus is not dead, not yet, not on this earth—he is still alive somewhere, held back on the wide sea, on some island surrounded by water, and hard, savage men keep him there against his will. Now I will make you a prophecy, as the immortals put it in my heart, and as I believe it will come to pass, though I am no seer, and I know nothing certain from the flight of birds: he will not be away from his own dear country much longer, not even if iron chains hold him. He will find a way to come back, for he is a man of many resources. But come, tell me this, and be honest with me: are you truly, in your own size, the son of Odysseus himself? You look remarkably like him about the head and the fine eyes—we used to spend time together often like this, before he went up to Troy, where the best of the other Argives went as well, in their hollow ships. Since then I have not seen Odysseus, nor has he seen me.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Well then, stranger, I will tell you quite honestly. My mother says I am his son; I myself do not know—no one has ever really known their own parentage for certain. I only wish I had been born the son of some fortunate man whom old age overtook among his own possessions. But instead, since you ask me this, they say I am the son of the unluckiest man that ever lived.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, said to him: “The gods have not, in fact, left your family nameless for the future, since Penelope bore a son like you. But come, tell me this, and be honest with me: what is this banquet, what is this crowd here? What need is this of yours? Is it a feast, or a wedding? For it is clearly not a potluck where each man contributes. It seems to me these men are dining in your house with such shameless arrogance that any sensible man who came upon it would be outraged at the sight of so much disgrace.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Stranger, since you ask and want to know all this—this house was once rich and blameless, I suppose, while that man was still at home. But now the gods have willed otherwise, plotting evil, and have made him vanish more completely than any man alive—for I would not grieve so much even for his death, if he had fallen among his companions in the land of Troy, or died in the arms of his friends once the fighting was over. Then the whole body of Achaeans would have built him a grave-mound, and he would have won great glory for his son as well, for all time to come. But now the storm-spirits have snatched him away without any fame at all; he is gone, unseen, unheard of, and has left me only grief and lamentation. And it is not only for him that I grieve and groan—the gods have piled other troubles on me besides. All the chief men who rule the islands, Doulichion, Same, wooded Zacynthus, and all who hold power over rocky Ithaca itself, all of them are courting my mother and wasting away my household. She neither refuses the hateful marriage nor is able to bring it to an end; and they, meanwhile, are eating up my substance and ruining my house—soon they will destroy me too.”

Pallas Athena, deeply stirred, said to him: “This is terrible—you clearly need Odysseus badly, so far away, to lay his hands on these shameless suitors. If only he would come now and stand at the outer gate of the house, wearing his helmet, carrying his shield and two spears, just as he was the first time I saw him in our house, drinking and enjoying himself, on his way back from Ephyra, from Ilus son of Mermerus—for Odysseus had gone there too, in his swift ship, seeking a deadly poison to smear on his bronze-tipped arrows, though Ilus refused to give it to him, out of respect for the everlasting gods, but my own father gave it to him instead, for he loved him greatly—if Odysseus, being the man he was then, could come face to face with these suitors now, they would all find a quick death and a bitter wedding indeed.

But truly, whether he returns and takes revenge, or not, lies in the lap of the gods. As for you, I urge you to think how you might drive the suitors out of your halls. Listen now, and pay attention to what I say: tomorrow, call the Achaean lords to assembly, and speak your mind to all of them, calling the gods to witness. Tell the suitors to scatter, each to his own home; and as for your mother, if her heart is set on marrying again, let her go back to the hall of her powerful father, and her family can arrange her marriage and gather the many gifts that should go with a beloved daughter. And to you yourself I will give this careful advice, if you will take it: fit out the best ship you can find with twenty oarsmen, and go find out about your father, who has been gone so long—perhaps someone will tell you something, or perhaps you will hear some rumor sent by Zeus, which most often brings word to men. Go first to Pylos and question noble Nestor, then on to Sparta, to fair-haired Menelaus, for he was the last of the bronze-armored Achaeans to reach home. If you hear that your father is alive and on his way back, then, hard as it will be, you can endure to wait one more year. But if you hear that he is dead, no longer among the living, then return to your own dear country, build him a grave-mound, and give him all the funeral honors that are fitting, and give your mother to a new husband. Once you have done all this and seen it through, then turn your mind to how you might kill the suitors in your halls, whether by trick or in the open—you are no longer a child, that you should behave like one.

“Have you not heard what fame godlike Orestes won among all men, when he killed the man who murdered his father, cunning Aegisthus, who had slain his glorious father? You too, my friend—for I see you are fine and tall—be brave, so that men yet unborn may speak well of you. But now I must go down to my swift ship and my crew, who I am sure are growing impatient waiting for me. See to these things yourself, and take my words to heart.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Stranger, you have spoken all this to me kindly, as a father might speak to his son, and I will never forget it. But please, stay a while now, eager as you are to be on your way, so that once you have bathed and delighted your heart, you may go to your ship carrying a gift, glad at heart—a fine and precious thing, which will be a keepsake from me, of the kind that dear guest-friends give to one another.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “Do not hold me back any longer, eager as I am to be on my way. Whatever gift your heart moves you to give me, give it to me on my way back, to carry home—choose something very fine, and it will be repaid in kind.”

So bright-eyed Athena spoke, and was gone—she flew up and away like a bird, and put strength and courage into his heart, and made him think of his father even more than before. And as he turned it over in his mind, he was struck with wonder, for he guessed that it had been a god. At once, godlike as he was, he went among the suitors.

The famous singer was singing for them, and they sat in silence, listening—he sang of the bitter homecoming of the Achaeans from Troy, which Pallas Athena had laid upon them. From her room upstairs, Penelope, wise daughter of Icarius, took in his inspired song, and came down the tall staircase of her house, not alone, but with two of her maids attending her. When she, shining among women, reached the suitors, she stood by the pillar of the sturdy-built roof, holding her shining veil before her face; a faithful maid stood on either side of her. Then, in tears, she spoke to the godlike singer: “Phemius, you know many other songs to charm mortal hearts, deeds of men and gods that poets celebrate—sing them one of those, sitting here among them, and let them drink their wine in silence; but stop this song, this painful one, which always breaks my heart in my breast, since it is I above all who am struck by grief beyond forgetting. For it is such a dear head that I long for always, remembering him—a man whose fame spreads wide through Hellas and the heart of Argos.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “My mother, why do you begrudge our good singer the chance to delight us however his mind moves him? It is not singers who are at fault, but Zeus, surely, who gives to each of these men who live by their labor whatever he wishes. There is no blame in this man singing of the Danaans' grim fate; for people always praise more highly whichever song is newest to reach their ears. Let your heart and spirit bear to listen—Odysseus was not the only one to lose his day of return at Troy; many other men perished as well. Go back into the house, and see to your own work, the loom and the distaff, and tell your maids to get on with their tasks; talk is men's business, all men's, but mine most of all, for the authority in this house is mine.”

She was astonished, and went back into the house, taking her son's wise words to heart. She went up to her room with her attendant women and wept there for Odysseus, her beloved husband, until bright-eyed Athena cast sweet sleep upon her eyes.

Meanwhile the suitors made an uproar throughout the shadowy hall, all of them praying to lie beside her in bed. And thoughtful Telemachus was the first among them to speak: “Suitors of my mother, you who wear your insolence so openly—for now, let us dine and enjoy ourselves, and let there be no shouting, since it is a fine thing to listen to a singer such as this man, whose voice is like a god's. But at dawn let us all go and take our seats in the assembly, so that I may tell you plainly, in so many words, to leave this house. Go feast elsewhere, eating up your own goods, taking turns from house to house. But if it seems better and more profitable to you to go on destroying one man's livelihood without paying for it, then go ahead and strip it bare—but I will call upon the everlasting gods, in hope that Zeus may grant that your deeds be repaid in kind: then you would perish in this house, unavenged.”

So he spoke, and all of them bit their lips, amazed that Telemachus should speak with such boldness. Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, answered him: “Telemachus, it must be the gods themselves teaching you to speak so grandly, and with such daring. I hope the son of Cronus never makes you king in sea-girt Ithaca, though it is indeed your birthright.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him in turn: “Antinous, will you be angry with me for what I say? I would be glad to accept that, if Zeus offered it to me. Do you really think that the worst thing that could happen to a man? It is no bad thing to be king—one's house quickly grows rich, and one is held in greater honor oneself. But there are, of course, plenty of other Achaean lords in sea-girt Ithaca, young and old, and one of them may well hold that place, now that godlike Odysseus is dead. As for me, I mean to be master of my own house, and of the servants that godlike Odysseus won for me.”

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered him in turn: “Telemachus, this indeed lies in the lap of the gods—which of the Achaeans will be king in sea-girt Ithaca. But you may surely keep your own possessions and rule your own house; and may no man ever come and strip you of your goods by force, against your will, so long as Ithaca still stands. But I would like to ask you, good sir, about the stranger—where does this man come from, what country does he claim as his own, where is his family, his native land? Does he bring some news of your father's coming, or has he come here on some business of his own? How suddenly he sprang up and was gone, without even waiting to be known! And yet he did not look, to my eyes, like a man of no account.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him in turn: “Eurymachus, my father's homecoming is lost for good. I no longer trust any news, wherever it might come from, nor do I pay heed to any prophecy my mother might seek out, calling some soothsayer into the hall. That stranger was a family friend of my father's, from Taphos—he says he is Mentes, son of wise Anchialus, and that he rules the oar-loving Taphians.” So Telemachus spoke, though in his heart he knew she had been an immortal god.

The suitors then turned back to dancing and delightful song, and made merry, waiting for evening to come; and as they made merry, the dark evening came upon them, and then at last each man went home to bed. Telemachus, meanwhile, went to his own room, high up in the beautiful courtyard, in a place with a wide view all around, and there he went to bed, turning many thoughts over in his mind. Beside him, carrying blazing torches, walked faithful Eurycleia, daughter of Ops, son of Peisenor, whom Laertes long ago had bought with his own wealth, still in the flower of her youth, paying the price of twenty oxen, and honored her in his house as much as his own faithful wife, though he never once slept with her, avoiding his wife's anger. She it was who now carried the blazing torches beside him, and loved him more than any of the other maids, for she had nursed him since he was a small child.

He opened the door of his well-built room, sat down on the bed, and pulled off his soft tunic, and put it into the old woman's careful hands. She folded and smoothed the tunic, and hung it on a peg beside the corded bedstead, then went out of the room, pulling the door shut by its silver handle, and drawing the bolt home by its strap. And there, all through the night, wrapped in a fleece of wool, he lay pondering in his heart the journey that Athena had shown him.

## Book II

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, the dear son of Odysseus rose from his bed and dressed, slung a sharp sword over his shoulder, bound fine sandals beneath his shining feet, and stepped out of his room looking like a god. At once he ordered the clear-voiced heralds to summon the long-haired Achaeans to assembly. The heralds made the call, and the men gathered quickly. When they had assembled and were all together, he made his way to the meeting place, a bronze spear in his hand, not alone — two swift dogs followed at his heels — and Athena poured a marvelous grace over him, so that all the people gazed at him in wonder as he came. He sat down in his father's seat, and the elders made way for him.

The first to speak was the hero Aegyptius, an old man bent with age who knew countless things. His own dear son, the spearman Antiphus, had sailed with godlike Odysseus in the hollow ships to Ilium, land of fine horses — and the savage Cyclops had killed him in his vaulted cave, making of him the last of his suppers. Three other sons the old man had: one, Eurynomus, ran with the suitors, and two kept up their father's farms. But not even so did he forget the lost one, grieving and mourning still. Weeping for him, he addressed the assembly: “Hear me now, men of Ithaca. Never once has our assembly met, never a session been held, since godlike Odysseus left in the hollow ships. Who has called us together now? On whom has such need fallen — one of the young men, or one of the older? Has he heard some news of the army's return that he can tell us plainly, having learned of it first? Or is there some other public matter he means to raise? A good man he seems to me, a blessed one. May Zeus accomplish some good for him, whatever it is his heart desires.”

So he spoke, and the dear son of Odysseus took the omen of his words gladly. He sat no longer; eager to speak, he stood up in the middle of the assembly, and the herald Peisenor, a man of prudent counsel, put the speaker's staff into his hand. Then he addressed himself first to the old man: “Old sir, that man is not far off — you'll see for yourself in a moment — the one who called the people together: it was I. Grief has come to me beyond all others. I have heard no news of the returning army to report to you plainly, nor is there any other public matter I mean to raise. This need is my own — the trouble that has fallen on my house, twofold. First, I have lost my noble father, who once was king among all of you here, and who was gentle as a father. And now something far worse — something that will soon tear my whole house apart and destroy my livelihood altogether: suitors have descended on my mother, against her will, the own dear sons of the leading men here. They shrink from going to the house of her father Icarius, so that he might set the bride-price for his daughter himself and give her to whom he pleases, to whoever finds favor with him. Instead they haunt our house day after day, slaughtering our cattle and sheep and fat goats, feasting and drinking our sparkling wine with no restraint. It is all being consumed. For there is no man here such as Odysseus was, to drive this ruin from the house. I am not the man for it — not now; I would only prove feeble, untrained in strength as I am. Believe me, I would defend myself if I had the power. What is being done here has passed all bearing: my house is being devoured, and not even decently. Feel some shame yourselves at last; feel it before our neighbors round about, and dread the anger of the gods, that in their outrage at these evil deeds they may not turn them back on your own heads. I beg you, by Olympian Zeus, and by Themis who dissolves the assemblies of men and seats them: hold off, my friends. Leave me alone to waste away in my bitter grief — unless my father, good Odysseus, ever wronged the well-greaved Achaeans out of malice, and you are repaying me for it out of malice in return, by urging these men on. Better for me if it were you yourselves eating up my stores and herds. If you were eating them, there might one day be repayment: we would go through the town pressing our claim, demanding our goods back, until everything was returned. As it is, you are heaping onto my heart pains that have no remedy.”

So he spoke in anger, and dashed the staff to the ground, bursting into tears; and pity took hold of all the people. Then all the rest sat silent, and no one dared answer Telemachus with hard words. Only Antinous answered him: “Telemachus, you tall talker, you unbridled temper — what a thing you've said, trying to shame us! You would love to fasten the blame on us. But it is not the Achaean suitors who are at fault here; it is your own dear mother, who knows more tricks than any woman alive. It is three years already — soon it will be four — that she has been cheating the hearts in the Achaeans' breasts. She gives hope to all of us, makes promises to each man, keeps sending us messages — while her mind intends something else entirely. Here is the latest trick she devised in her heart: she set up a great loom in the halls and began weaving a web, fine-threaded and very wide, and told us straightaway: ‘Young men, my suitors — since godlike Odysseus is dead, be patient, however eager for my marriage, until I finish this cloth, so that my spinning does not go to waste: a burial shroud for the hero Laertes, for the day when the ruinous doom of death that lays men low takes him — so that no Achaean woman in the land can hold it against me if a man who won so much should lie without a winding-sheet.’ So she said, and our proud hearts consented. And then, by day she would weave at the great web — and by night she would unravel it, with torches set beside her. For three years the trick escaped us and she kept the Achaeans persuaded; but when the fourth year came, as the seasons rolled around, then one of her women, who knew all about it, told us, and we caught her in the act of unraveling the splendid web. So she finished it — unwillingly, because she had to.

“Now here is the suitors' answer to you, so that you may know it in your own heart, and all the Achaeans may know it too: send your mother away, and tell her to marry whichever man her father bids and pleases her. But if she goes on tormenting the sons of the Achaeans much longer — priding herself on what Athena has given her beyond other women, skill in beautiful work, a good mind, and cleverness such as we never hear of even in the women of old, the fair-tressed Achaean women of former days, Tyro and Alcmene and garlanded Mycene: not one of them had a mind like Penelope's — then this once, that cleverness has miscalculated. For the suitors will go on devouring your livelihood and your goods just as long as she keeps to this purpose the gods are now putting in her breast. She is winning a great name for herself — and for you, only the loss of a great livelihood. As for us, we are not going back to our estates, or anywhere else, until she marries whichever of the Achaeans she chooses.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Antinous, there is no way I can drive out of this house, against her will, the woman who bore me and raised me. My father is somewhere far away, alive or dead. It would cost me dearly to repay Icarius all that dowry, as I must if I send my mother away by my own choice. From her father I would have evil to suffer, and heaven would send more besides, for my mother as she left the house would call down the dread Furies on me; and I would have the censure of men. No — I will never speak that word. If your own hearts feel any shame at all, then leave my halls; feast somewhere else; eat your own stores, taking turns house by house. But if it seems better and more profitable to you that one man's living be devoured without payment — then carve away. I will cry out to the gods who live forever, and hope Zeus may grant that deeds of requital come to pass: then you would die inside this house, and no payment made for you either.”

So spoke Telemachus, and in answer far-seeing Zeus sent two eagles flying down from the mountain peak. For a while they sailed on the wind's breath, side by side, wings outstretched; but when they reached the middle of the many-voiced assembly, they wheeled and beat their thick wings, glaring down on all the heads below, and death was in that look; then they tore with their talons at each other's cheeks and necks, and shot away to the right, over the houses and the town. The men were astonished at the birds, seeing them with their own eyes, and wondered in their hearts what was to come of it.

Then the old hero Halitherses, Mastor's son, spoke among them — he surpassed all men of his generation in reading birds and declaring what fate intended. With good will toward them he spoke: “Hear me now, men of Ithaca. And to the suitors above all I declare this, for a great wave of ruin is rolling toward them. Odysseus will not be long parted from his people now; he is somewhere near already, I think, sowing death and doom for these men, every one — and evil is coming too for many of the rest of us who live on clear-skied Ithaca. Long before that, let us think how to stop them — or rather let them stop themselves; it is better for them, and soon. I am no untried prophet; I know this art. And I say that for that man everything has come to pass as I told him it would, when the Argives embarked for Ilium and resourceful Odysseus went with them. I said that after suffering many evils, and losing all his companions, unknown to everyone, in the twentieth year he would come home. And now all of it is coming true.”

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered him: “Old man, go home and prophesy to your children, or they may come to harm someday. In this matter I am a far better prophet than you. Plenty of birds pass to and fro under the sun's rays, and not all of them mean anything. As for Odysseus — he died far away, and I wish you had perished with him. Then you would not be spouting all this prophecy, or egging on Telemachus in his anger, hoping he'll send a gift home to your family, if he happens to give one. But I will tell you this, and it will come to pass: if you, with all your old and well-worn knowledge, talk a younger man round with your words and stir him to violence, it will go the harder for him, first of all — he'll accomplish nothing by it anyway — and on you, old man, we will lay a fine that will grieve your heart to pay. Your pain will be a heavy one. And to Telemachus, before everyone here, I make my own proposal: let him order his mother back to her father's house. Her family will arrange the marriage and put together a wedding-portion, a generous one, such as should go with a beloved daughter. Not before then, I think, will the sons of the Achaeans give up their hard courting — since we fear nobody in any case, and certainly not Telemachus, for all his speeches; nor do we care a straw for any prophecy that you, old man, may pronounce — it will come to nothing, and only make you more hated. Yes, his goods will be devoured, badly, and no fair balance made, so long as she keeps putting the Achaeans off in this marriage. And we, waiting here day after day, competing for the prize of her excellence, will go courting no other women, such as it would be fitting for each of us to wed.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Eurymachus, and all you other lordly suitors — I am done pleading with you about this, and done speaking of it. The gods know it all now, and so do all the Achaeans. But give me a swift ship and twenty companions to carry me on my way there and back. For I am going to Sparta and to sandy Pylos, to ask after my long-gone father's return — perhaps some mortal will tell me something, or I may hear a rumor from Zeus, which most often carries news to men. If I hear that my father is alive and coming home, then, worn as I am, I can hold out one more year. But if I hear that he is dead and gone, I will come back to my own dear country, heap up a burial mound for him, and pay him the full funeral rites that are fitting — and give my mother to a husband.”

With that he sat down; and there rose up Mentor, who was noble Odysseus's old companion — when Odysseus left in the ships he had put the whole household in his charge, to obey the old man and keep everything safe. With good will toward them he spoke: “Hear me now, men of Ithaca. Let no sceptered king ever again be kind and gentle from his heart, or know justice in his mind; let him rather be harsh always, and work wickedness — since no one remembers godlike Odysseus, not one of the people he ruled, though he was gentle as a father. I do not so much begrudge the proud suitors their deeds of violence — the malice of their minds is at their own risk: they are staking their own heads when they devour the house of Odysseus by force, telling themselves he will never return. No — it is the rest of the people I am angry with, the way you all sit there dumb, and never say a word of rebuke to make the suitors stop, though you are many and they are few.”

Leocritus, son of Euenor, answered him: “Mentor, you mischief-maker, you brainless fool — what a thing to say, urging them to stop us! It would be an ugly business to fight over a dinner with men who outnumber you. Even if Ithacan Odysseus himself came home and found the lordly suitors feasting in his house, and set his heart on driving them from his hall — his wife would have small joy of his coming, however she has longed for him: right there he would meet an unseemly death, if he fought men who outnumbered him. You have not spoken as you should. Come now — people, scatter, each man to his own work. Mentor and Halitherses can speed this boy on his journey, being his father's friends from the beginning. Though I think he'll sit here in Ithaca a long while yet, gathering his news; that voyage he will never make.”

So he spoke, and dissolved the assembly in haste. The people scattered, each to his own house, and the suitors went off to the house of godlike Odysseus.

But Telemachus walked apart, down to the shore of the sea, and washed his hands in the grey salt water, and prayed to Athena: “Hear me, you who came yesterday, a god, to our house, and told me to go by ship over the misty sea to learn of my long-gone father's return. All of it the Achaeans are obstructing — the suitors most of all, in their wicked arrogance.”

So he prayed, and Athena came near him in the shape of Mentor, his very form and voice, and spoke winged words to him: “Telemachus, you will be no coward hereafter, and no fool, if any of your father's fine spirit has been instilled in you — such a man he was for finishing what he undertook, in deed and word. Then this journey of yours will be no idle errand, and no failure. But if you are not the true son of that man and of Penelope, then I have no hope that you will accomplish what you intend. Few sons, indeed, are the equals of their fathers; most are worse, and few better. But since you will be no coward hereafter and no fool, and since Odysseus's cunning has not wholly deserted you, there is every hope you will bring this work to pass. So let the suitors' schemes and plots alone — they are fools, with no sense and no justice, and they know nothing of the death and black doom that is near them, to destroy them all in a single day. As for you, the journey you have set your heart on is not far off now — I am so much your father's friend that I will fit you out a swift ship, and come with you myself. Go back to the house now, and mix among the suitors; and get provisions ready, stow everything in vessels — wine in two-handled jars, and barley-meal, the marrow of men, in strong-sewn skins. I will go through the town and quickly gather a volunteer crew. There are ships aplenty in sea-girt Ithaca, new ones and old; I will look them over and pick out the best for you, and we'll rig her at once and launch her on the broad sea.”

So spoke Athena, daughter of Zeus; and Telemachus lingered no longer once he had heard the voice of the god. He went back toward the house with a heavy heart, and found the proud suitors in his halls, skinning goats and singeing fat hogs in the courtyard. Antinous came straight at him, laughing, and clamped a hand on his and spoke to him by name: “Telemachus, tall talker, unbridled temper — no more of this brooding over hard deeds and hard words! Come, eat and drink with me, just as you used to. The Achaeans will see to everything you asked for, of course — a ship and a picked crew of rowers, so you can get to sacred Pylos all the faster, on the trail of your glorious father.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Antinous, there is no way I can sit at dinner among you in your insolence, and eat in silence, and take my ease. Was it not enough, suitors, that you carved up so much of my good property in the years when I was still a child? Now that I am grown, and hear the talk of other men and understand it, and the spirit is rising in me, I will do my best to send an evil doom upon you, whether I go to Pylos first or work it here in this very land. But go I will — and it will be no idle journey I speak of — go as a passenger, since it seems I am not to be allowed a ship or rowers of my own. No doubt you found that arrangement more profitable.” He spoke, and wrenched his hand out of Antinous's hand, easily.

The suitors went on preparing their feast through the house, and as they did they mocked him and jeered with cutting words. One of the arrogant young men would say: “No question about it — Telemachus is planning our murder! He'll bring back champions from sandy Pylos, or even from Sparta, he's so terribly keen. Or maybe he means to go to Ephyra, that rich farmland, and fetch deadly poisons from there, and drop them in the wine-bowl, and kill us all at once.” And another of the arrogant young men would answer: “Who knows? Maybe if he goes off in a hollow ship, he'll wander and die far from his friends — just like Odysseus. That would only mean more work for us, though: we'd have to divide all his property among ourselves, and hand the house over to his mother — for her and for the man who marries her.”

So they talked. But Telemachus went down to his father's storeroom, a wide, high-roofed chamber where gold and bronze lay piled, and clothing in chests, and olive oil in fragrant plenty; and jars of old, sweet-drinking wine stood there, holding the unmixed divine drink within them, ranged in order along the wall, against the day Odysseus might come home again after all his sufferings. The close-fitted double doors were kept locked, and a stewardess was there night and day, guarding everything with the shrewdness of her mind: Eurycleia, daughter of Ops son of Peisenor. Telemachus called her into the chamber and said to her: “Nurse, come, draw me off wine in two-handled jars — the sweet wine, the choicest after that which you keep in expectation of that ill-fated man, godlike Odysseus, should he come from somewhere after all, having escaped death and the fates. Fill twelve jars, and fit them all with lids. And pour barley-meal into well-sewn skins for me — twenty measures of mill-ground barley. Keep this to yourself. Have it all put together in one place; I will collect it this evening, as soon as my mother has gone up to her room and is thinking of sleep. I am going to Sparta and to sandy Pylos, to ask after my dear father's return, in hope of hearing something.”

So he spoke, and his dear nurse Eurycleia wailed aloud, and through her tears spoke winged words: “Why, dear child, why has this notion come into your head? Where would you go, over all that earth, an only son, and so beloved? He is dead far from home, godlike Odysseus, in some strangers' land. And these men, the moment you are gone, will plot evil for you behind your back — a trick to destroy you — and then divide all this among themselves. Stay here, sitting on what is yours! There is no need for you to suffer hardship on the barren sea, or go wandering.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Take heart, nurse. This plan is not without a god behind it. But swear you will not breathe a word of it to my dear mother until the eleventh or twelfth day comes, or until she misses me herself and hears that I have gone — so that she does not mar her lovely face with weeping.” So he spoke, and the old woman swore a great oath by the gods. And when she had sworn and completed the oath, she drew off wine for him at once in the two-handled jars, and poured barley-meal into the well-sewn skins; and Telemachus went back into the hall and mixed among the suitors.

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, thought of another thing. In the likeness of Telemachus she went everywhere through the city, and standing beside each man she spoke her word, telling them to gather at evening beside the swift ship. And she asked Noemon, the glorious son of Phronius, for a swift ship; and he promised it to her gladly.

The sun went down, and all the streets fell into shadow. Then she hauled the swift ship down to the sea and stowed in her all the tackle that well-benched ships carry, and moored her at the mouth of the harbor. The good crew gathered around her in a body, and the goddess put heart into every man.

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, thought of yet another thing. She went to the house of godlike Odysseus, and there she poured sweet sleep over the suitors and muddled them as they drank, and knocked the cups from their hands. They rose and went off to their beds through the town, and sat up no longer, for sleep was falling on their eyelids. Then bright-eyed Athena called Telemachus out of the well-built halls, in the shape of Mentor, his very form and voice: “Telemachus, your well-greaved companions are already sitting at their oars, waiting for your word to start. Come, let us go; let us not hold up the journey any longer.”

So saying, Pallas Athena led the way swiftly, and he followed in the footsteps of the god. When they came down to the ship and the sea, they found the long-haired crew on the beach, and the strong prince Telemachus spoke among them: “Come, friends, let us fetch the provisions — everything is gathered ready in the hall. My mother knows nothing of this, nor the servant women; one alone has heard my plan.”

So saying he led the way, and they went with him. They carried everything down and stowed it in the well-benched ship, as the dear son of Odysseus directed. Then Telemachus went aboard, with Athena leading; she sat down in the stern, and Telemachus sat beside her. The men cast off the stern-lines, and came aboard themselves and sat down at the rowing-benches. And bright-eyed Athena sent them a following wind, a fresh West Wind, singing over the wine-dark sea. Telemachus called to his companions and ordered them to lay hands on the tackle, and they obeyed him: they raised the pine mast and set it in the hollow mast-box, made it fast with the forestays, and hauled up the white sail with twisted ox-hide ropes. The wind filled the belly of the sail, and around the stem the dark wave sang loud as the ship went on her way; she ran over the swell, making good her course.

When they had made the tackle fast throughout the swift black ship, they set out mixing-bowls brim-full of wine, and poured offerings to the immortal gods who live forever — and beyond all others to the bright-eyed daughter of Zeus. And all night long, and into the dawn, the ship cut on her way.

## Book III

The sun sprang up, leaving the beautiful waters, into the brazen sky, to shine for the immortals and for mortal men upon the grain-giving earth; and they came to Pylos, Neleus's well-built citadel. On the shore of the sea the people were offering sacrifice — all-black bulls for the dark-haired Earthshaker. There were nine companies seated, five hundred men in each, and each company held nine bulls before it. They had just tasted the inner parts and were burning the thigh-pieces for the god when the ship put straight in; the crew brailed up and furled the trim ship's sail, moored her, and stepped ashore. Telemachus stepped from the ship, and Athena led the way.

The goddess, bright-eyed Athena, spoke to him first: “Telemachus, no more bashfulness now, not a scrap of it. This is why you sailed the sea — to find out about your father: where the earth holds him, what fate he has met. Come, go straight up to Nestor, tamer of horses; let us see what counsel he keeps hidden in his breast. Beg him yourself to speak the exact truth. He will not lie; he is far too wise.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Mentor, how am I to go up to him? How do I even greet him? I have no practice yet in close-woven speech — and a young man feels shame in questioning his elder.” The goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered: “Telemachus, some of it your own heart will supply, and some a god will prompt. You were not, I think, born and raised without the good will of the gods.”

So speaking, Pallas Athena led the way quickly, and he followed in the footsteps of the god, and they came to the gathering-place of the men of Pylos, where Nestor sat with his sons, and around them their companions were making the feast ready, roasting meats and fixing others on spits. When they saw the strangers they came crowding over, all of them, clasping their hands in welcome and calling them to sit. Nestor's son Peisistratus came up first, took them both by the hand, and settled them at the feast on soft fleeces spread over the sand of the shore, beside his brother Thrasymedes and his father. He served them portions of the inner meats, poured wine into a golden cup, and, pledging her, said to Pallas Athena, daughter of aegis-bearing Zeus: “Pray now, stranger, to lord Poseidon — his is the feast you have come upon in arriving here. And when you have poured your libation and prayed, as is proper, give your friend the cup of honey-sweet wine to pour in turn; he too, I imagine, prays to the immortals — all men have need of the gods. But he is the younger, my own age, and so I give the golden cup first to you.”

With that he set the cup of sweet wine in her hand; and Athena was pleased with the man's tact and fairness, that he had given the golden cup to her first. At once she prayed at length to lord Poseidon: “Hear us, Poseidon, earth-holder, and do not begrudge us, who pray to you, the accomplishment of these things. To Nestor first and to his sons grant honor; then give gracious recompense to all the rest of the Pylians for this glorious hecatomb; and grant besides that Telemachus and I may return with that done for which we came here in our swift black ship.” So she prayed — and she herself was bringing it all to pass. She gave Telemachus the fine two-handled cup, and the dear son of Odysseus prayed in the same way. Then, when they had roasted the outer meats and drawn them off the spits, they divided the portions and feasted a glorious feast.

When they had put away their desire for drink and food, the Gerenian horseman Nestor began: “Now is the better moment to inquire of our guests and ask who they are, now that they have had their pleasure of food. Strangers, who are you? From where do you sail the watery ways? Is it on some business — or do you rove at random over the sea like pirates, who wander staking their own lives and bringing harm to other men?”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him, taking courage — for Athena herself had put courage in his heart, that he might ask about his absent father, and that good report of him might spread among men: “Nestor, son of Neleus, great glory of the Achaeans, you ask where we are from, and I will tell you. We have come from Ithaca, under Mount Neion; and our business is private, not public, as I shall explain. I have come seeking any wide-spread word of my father — should I hear any — of noble, much-enduring Odysseus, who once, men say, fought at your side and sacked the city of the Trojans. Of all the others who fought the Trojans we have heard where each man died his sorry death; but his death the son of Cronus has left unheard-of. No one can say for certain where he perished — whether he was brought down on the mainland by enemies, or lost at sea among the waves of Amphitrite. So I have come to your knees, to ask if you will tell me of his sorry end — whether you saw it with your own eyes, or heard the tale of him wandering from another; for beyond all men his mother bore him to sorrow. And do not soften it for me out of pity or courtesy, but tell me exactly what sight you came upon. I beg you: if ever my father, good Odysseus, promised you word or deed and made it good, in the land of the Trojans where you Achaeans suffered — remember it for me now, and tell me the exact truth.”

The Gerenian horseman Nestor answered him: “Friend, since you have called back to my mind the misery we endured in that land — we unbroken-spirited sons of the Achaeans — all our roving in ships over the misty sea for plunder wherever Achilles led, and all our fighting around the great city of king Priam: there our best were slain. There warlike Ajax lies, there Achilles, there Patroclus, a counselor equal to the gods; there my own dear son, strong and faultless Antilochus, surpassing swift of foot, and a fighter. And many other evils we suffered besides — what mortal man could tell them all? Not if you stayed five years, six years, questioning me on all that the noble Achaeans suffered there — you would grow weary and go home to your own country first. Nine years we stitched evil around them, plying every kind of stratagem, and the son of Cronus barely brought it to an end. And in cunning no man there was willing to match himself openly against godlike Odysseus, since he far surpassed us all in every kind of stratagem — your father, if you are really his son. Wonder takes me as I look at you: your way of speaking, certainly, is his, and you would not think a younger man could speak so like him. In all that time godlike Odysseus and I never once spoke on opposite sides, in assembly or in council; with one heart, with judgment and shrewd counsel, we planned how things might turn out best for the Argives.

“But when we had sacked Priam's steep city and gone to our ships, and a god scattered the Achaeans, then Zeus devised in his heart a bitter homecoming for the Argives, since not all of them were sensible or just; and so many of them came to a bad end through the deadly anger of the bright-eyed daughter of the mighty father, who set a quarrel between the two sons of Atreus. They called all the Achaeans to assembly — recklessly, in no due order, at the setting of the sun, and the sons of the Achaeans came heavy with wine — and each spoke out the reason he had gathered the host. Menelaus urged all the Achaeans to think of their homecoming over the broad back of the sea; but that did not please Agamemnon at all, for he wanted to hold the host back and offer holy hecatombs, to appease the terrible anger of Athena — fool, who did not know she was not to be persuaded: the mind of the gods who live forever is not quickly turned. So the two of them stood exchanging hard words, and the well-greaved Achaeans sprang up with a tremendous din, and counsel split them in two. That night we rested, nursing hard thoughts against each other — for Zeus was preparing the disaster of that evil — and at dawn half of us hauled our ships down to the bright sea and loaded our goods and our deep-girdled women aboard. Half the host held back and stayed there with Agamemnon, son of Atreus, shepherd of the people; and we, the other half, embarked and rowed off. The ships ran fast, for a god smoothed the yawning sea.

“At Tenedos we made sacrifice to the gods, longing for home; but Zeus did not yet intend our return — cruel one — and he stirred up evil strife a second time. Some turned their curved ships back — Odysseus among them, that shrewd lord of many devices, taking the side of Agamemnon son of Atreus once more; but I fled on with the ships that followed me in a body, for I knew the god was devising evil. The warlike son of Tydeus fled too, and roused his companions. Late upon our track came fair-haired Menelaus, and caught up with us at Lesbos as we debated the long sea-passage — whether to sail above rugged Chios, by the isle of Psyria, keeping Chios on our left, or under Chios, past windy Mimas. We asked the god to show us a sign, and he showed one, and bade us cut across the middle of the open sea to Euboea, to escape disaster the quickest way. A whistling wind sprang up astern, and the ships ran fast down the fish-filled ways, and put in at Geraestus in the night; there we laid many bulls' thighs on the fire for Poseidon, having measured out that great stretch of sea. It was the fourth day when the companions of Diomedes, tamer of horses, moored their trim ships at Argos; but I held on for Pylos, and the wind never dropped from the moment the god first sent it blowing.

“So I came home, dear child, without news; I know nothing of the others, which of the Achaeans were saved and which were lost. But whatever I hear as I sit here in my halls, that you shall learn, as is only right; I will hide nothing. The spear-wielding Myrmidons came home safe, they say, led by the glorious son of great-hearted Achilles; safe too Philoctetes, Poeas's splendid son. Idomeneus brought back to Crete all his companions who survived the war — the sea took none from him. And of the son of Atreus, you yourselves have heard, far away as you live: how he came home, and how Aegisthus devised his miserable destruction. But Aegisthus paid for it grimly enough — so good a thing it is that a son of the dead man be left! For that son took vengeance on his father's killer, on treacherous Aegisthus, who had slain his famous father. You too, my friend — and I see how tall and fine you are — be brave, so that men yet unborn may speak well of you.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Nestor, son of Neleus, great glory of the Achaeans — yes, that son took his vengeance in full, and the Achaeans will carry his fame far and wide, a song for men to come. If only the gods would clothe me in strength like that, to take vengeance on the suitors for their galling trespasses — the men who insult me and contrive their reckless outrages! But the gods have spun no such happiness for me — not for my father, not for me. As things stand, one simply has to endure.”

The Gerenian horseman Nestor answered him: “Friend — since you bring it up and mention it yourself — they say that many suitors for your mother's hand fill your halls against your will and are working evil there. Tell me: do you submit to this willingly? Or do the people through the land hate you, following some voice of a god? Who knows whether one day he may come and repay their violence — alone, perhaps, or with all the Achaeans behind him? If only bright-eyed Athena chose to love you as she once cared for glorious Odysseus in the land of the Trojans, where we Achaeans suffered — for I never saw the gods love a man so openly as Pallas Athena stood openly by him — if she chose to love you like that, and cared for you in her heart, then many a one of those men would have his marriage driven clean out of his mind.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Old sir, I do not think that word will ever be fulfilled. What you say is too large. Awe takes hold of me. I could not hope for it — not even if the gods willed it so.” But the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, said to him: “Telemachus, what a word has escaped the fence of your teeth! Easily, if he wishes, a god can bring a man safe home even from far away. For my part, I would rather suffer a world of pains and then come home and see my day of returning, than come home and be killed at my own hearth, as Agamemnon was killed by the guile of Aegisthus and his own wife. Though death — death is common to all, and that not even the gods can ward off from a man they love, when the ruinous doom of death that lays men low once takes him.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Mentor, let us speak of it no more, grieved though we are. For him there is no true homecoming any longer; the immortals have already devised his death and black doom. But now there is another thing I wish to ask Nestor, since beyond other men he knows judgments and wisdom — three generations of men, they say, he has ruled, and to my eyes he seems like an immortal. Nestor, son of Neleus, tell me the truth of it: how did wide-ruling Agamemnon, son of Atreus, die? Where was Menelaus? What death did treacherous Aegisthus contrive for him, seeing the man he killed was far the better of the two? Was Menelaus not in Achaean Argos, but wandering somewhere among strangers, that Aegisthus took his courage from that, and killed?”

The Gerenian horseman Nestor answered him: “Then I will tell you the whole truth, child. You can guess yourself how it would have fallen out if Atreus's son, fair-haired Menelaus, coming back from Troy, had found Aegisthus alive in the palace: no heaped earth would ever have covered that man's corpse; dogs and birds would have devoured him where he lay on the plain outside the city, and no Achaean woman would have wept for him — so monstrous was the thing he plotted. For while we sat over there accomplishing our many labors, he, at his ease in a corner of horse-pasturing Argos, kept charming Agamemnon's wife with words. At first, indeed, she refused the shameful thing — noble Clytemnestra — for her mind was still sound; and there was a singer at her side whom the son of Atreus, when he left for Troy, had strictly charged to guard his wife. But when the doom of the gods bound her to her undoing, then Aegisthus took that singer to a desert island and left him there, prey and plunder for the birds — and led her, as willing as he was, home to his own house. Many thigh-pieces he burned on the gods' holy altars, many offerings he hung up, woven stuffs and gold, at having accomplished the monstrous thing his heart had never truly hoped for.

“We were sailing together on the way from Troy, the son of Atreus and I, in perfect friendship; but when we reached holy Sunium, the headland of Athens, there Phoebus Apollo came with his gentle arrows and killed Menelaus's helmsman with the steering-oar of the running ship in his hands — Phrontis, Onetor's son, who surpassed all the tribes of men at steering a ship when storm-winds drove. So Menelaus, however he strained to be on his way, was held back there, to bury his companion and pay him his funeral rites. But when he too got off over the wine-dark sea in his hollow ships, and came in his running to the steep height of Malea, then far-seeing Zeus laid out a hateful road for him: he poured out a blast of shrieking winds, and the waves swelled monstrous, big as mountains. There he cut the fleet in two. One part he drove toward Crete, where the Cydonians lived around the streams of Iardanus. There is a smooth cliff, dropping sheer to the sea, at the far edge of Gortyn on the misty deep, where the South Wind pushes a great wave against the western headland toward Phaestus, and a little reef holds off that great water. There one part came, and the men barely escaped destruction; their ships the waves smashed to pieces on the rocks. But the other five dark-prowed ships, wind and water carried and drove to Egypt. So Menelaus, gathering much substance and gold, wandered there with his ships among men of foreign speech — and all that time Aegisthus at home was plotting his wretchedness. Seven years he reigned over Mycenae rich in gold, having killed the son of Atreus, and the people were crushed beneath him; but in the eighth came his evil: godlike Orestes came back from Athens and killed his father's murderer, treacherous Aegisthus, who had slain his famous father. He killed him, then held a funeral feast for the Argives, over his hateful mother and cowardly Aegisthus; and on that same day Menelaus of the great war-cry arrived, bringing wealth in plenty, all the freight his ships could carry.

“So you, friend — do not go wandering long and far from your house, leaving your wealth behind you and men in your halls as arrogant as those, or they will divide it all up and devour it, and your journey will have been for nothing. But to Menelaus I do urge and bid you go, for he has lately come home from abroad, from peoples out of whose lands a man could not honestly hope to return, once storms had driven him astray into a sea that vast, so vast that not even the birds cross it in the round of a year — great as it is, and terrible. Go now, with your ship and your companions; or if you prefer to go by land, chariot and horses are at your service, and my sons are at your service too, to be your escorts to shining Lacedaemon, where fair-haired Menelaus is. Beg him yourself, so that he tells you the exact truth. He will not lie; he is far too wise.”

So he spoke; and the sun set and darkness came on. The goddess, bright-eyed Athena, spoke among them: “Old sir, all this you have told rightly and well. But come — cut the tongues now, and mix the wine, so that when we have poured to Poseidon and the other immortals we may think of sleep; it is the hour. Already the light has gone under the darkness, and it is not proper to sit long at the gods' feast, but to go home.” So spoke the daughter of Zeus, and they listened to her voice. Heralds poured water over their hands, young men filled the mixing-bowls brim-full of drink and served a first portion to everyone's cup; they threw the tongues in the fire, and stood and poured the libation over them. And when they had poured and drunk what their hearts desired, then Athena and godlike Telemachus both made to go back to their hollow ship.

But Nestor held them back, taking hold of them with his words: “Zeus forbid — and the other immortal gods forbid — that you two should go from my house to your swift ship as if from some man utterly threadbare and poor, who has no store of cloaks and blankets in his house for himself or his guests to sleep soft in! I have cloaks in plenty, and fine blankets. Never, surely, shall this man's dear son — Odysseus's son — lie down on a ship's deck-planks while I am alive, or while sons of mine are left in the halls after me to entertain guests, whoever comes to my house.” The goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “Well said, dear old friend; and it is fitting that Telemachus obey you, since it is far better so. He shall go with you now, and sleep in your halls; but I will go to the black ship, to hearten our crew and give them their instructions. For I am the only older man among them, I may say: the rest are younger men who follow out of friendship, all of them of an age with great-hearted Telemachus. There I will lie down by the black hollow ship tonight; and in the morning I go to the great-hearted Cauconians, where a debt is owed me — no new one, and no small one. But this man, since he has come to your house, send on his way with a chariot, and with a son of yours; and give him horses, the lightest-footed you have, and the strongest.”

So speaking, bright-eyed Athena went away in the likeness of a sea-eagle, and amazement took all who saw it. The old man marveled, when his eyes beheld it, and he took Telemachus's hand and spoke to him by name: “Friend, I have no fear you will prove base or cowardly, if at your age the gods already go with you as guides. For this was no other of those who keep the halls of Olympus — this was the daughter of Zeus herself, most glorious Tritogeneia, she who honored your noble father too among the Argives. Be gracious, Queen: grant good fame to me, to my sons, and to my honored wife; and to you in return I will sacrifice a yearling heifer, broad-browed, unbroken, that no man has yet led under the yoke — I will sacrifice her to you with her horns sheathed in gold.” So he prayed, and Pallas Athena heard him.

Then the Gerenian horseman Nestor led them, his sons and his daughters' husbands, to his beautiful house. When they reached the king's glorious house they sat down in order on couches and chairs, and for their coming the old man mixed a bowl of sweet-drinking wine, eleven years old, which the housekeeper now opened, loosening the cap. Of this the old man mixed a bowl, and pouring libation he prayed long to Athena, daughter of aegis-bearing Zeus. And when they had poured and drunk what their hearts desired, the others went home to sleep, each man to his own house, but the Gerenian horseman Nestor had Telemachus, dear son of godlike Odysseus, sleep there, on a corded bedstead under the echoing portico, and beside him Peisistratus of the good ash spear, leader of men, who alone of his sons was still unmarried in the palace. He himself slept in the inner chamber of the high house, and his lady wife made ready his bed and his rest.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, the Gerenian horseman Nestor rose from his bed, went out, and sat down on the polished stones that stood before his high doors, white and gleaming with oil. On these Neleus had used to sit in the old days, a counselor equal to the gods; but he, long since broken by fate, had gone down to Hades, and now Nestor of Gerenia sat there in his turn, warden of the Achaeans, scepter in hand. Around him his sons gathered in a body as they came from their rooms: Echephron and Stratius, Perseus and Aretus and godlike Thrasymedes; and sixth came the hero Peisistratus. They brought godlike Telemachus and set him at their side, and the Gerenian horseman Nestor began: “Quickly, dear children, accomplish my wish, so that first of all the gods I may propitiate Athena, who came to me in visible presence at the god's rich feast. One of you go to the plain for a heifer, so that she comes as quickly as may be, driven by the herdsman; one go to great-hearted Telemachus's black ship and bring all his companions, leaving only two; one bid the goldsmith Laerces come here, to sheathe the heifer's horns in gold. The rest of you stay here together, and tell the servants inside to prepare a feast through our glorious halls — seats, and firewood, and fresh water.”

So he spoke, and they all fell to bustling. The heifer came from the plain; the companions of great-hearted Telemachus came from the swift trim ship; the smith came with his bronze tools in his hands, the instruments of his craft — anvil and hammer and well-made tongs — with which he worked gold; and Athena came to receive the offering. The old horseman Nestor gave gold, and the smith worked it and sheathed the heifer's horns with it, so that the goddess might rejoice to see the lovely thing. Stratius and godlike Echephron led the heifer by the horns; Aretus came from the chamber carrying lustral water in a flowered basin, and in his other hand barley-grains in a basket; Thrasymedes, steadfast in battle, stood by with a sharp axe in his hand, to strike the heifer down; Perseus held the blood-bowl. The old horseman Nestor began the rite with the water and the barley, and praying earnestly to Athena he cast the firstling hairs of the head into the fire.

When they had prayed and thrown down the barley-grains, at once Nestor's son, high-hearted Thrasymedes, stepped close and struck: the axe cut through the neck-tendons and loosed the heifer's strength. The women raised the ritual cry — the daughters and the sons' wives and Nestor's honored wife Eurydice, eldest of the daughters of Clymenus. Then the men lifted the heifer from the wide-wayed earth and held her, and Peisistratus, leader of men, cut her throat. When the black blood had run out and the life had left the bones, they quickly dismembered her, cut out the thigh-pieces in due order, wrapped them in a double fold of fat and laid raw flesh upon them. The old man burned them over split wood, and poured sparkling wine over them, while the young men beside him held five-pronged forks in their hands. When the thigh-pieces were consumed and they had tasted the inner parts, they carved up the rest, and pierced it on spits, and roasted it, holding the sharp spits in their hands.

Meanwhile lovely Polycaste bathed Telemachus — youngest daughter of Nestor son of Neleus. When she had bathed him and rubbed him with olive oil, and put a beautiful mantle and a tunic about him, he stepped from the bath looking like the immortals in build, and went and sat by Nestor, shepherd of the people. When they had roasted the outer meats and drawn them off, they sat down and feasted, and good men waited on them, pouring wine into golden cups. And when they had put away their desire for drink and food, the Gerenian horseman Nestor began: “My sons, bring fine-maned horses and yoke them for Telemachus under the chariot, so that he may be on his way.”

So he spoke, and they heard and obeyed, and quickly yoked swift horses beneath the chariot. The housekeeper put in bread and wine, and dainties such as god-nurtured kings eat. Telemachus mounted the beautiful chariot, and beside him Nestor's son Peisistratus, leader of men, mounted and took the reins in his hands. He touched the horses with the whip, and the pair flew off, nothing loath, into the plain, leaving the steep citadel of Pylos behind. All day long they shook the yoke they bore between them.

The sun set and all the streets fell into shadow as they came to Pherae, to the house of Diocles, son of Ortilochus, whom Alpheus begot. There they slept the night, and he set the gifts of hospitality before them. And when early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, they yoked the horses and mounted the inlaid chariot, and drove out of the gateway and the echoing portico. He touched the horses with the whip, and the pair flew off, nothing loath. They came into the wheat-bearing plain, and there they pressed on to their journey's end — so well did the swift horses carry them. And the sun set, and all the ways grew dark.

## Book IV

They came to the hollow of Lacedaemon, deep in its ravines, and drove to the house of glorious Menelaus. They found him giving a wedding feast for many kinsmen in his house — a double one, for his son and for his blameless daughter. Her he was sending to the son of Achilles, breaker of ranks: in Troy he had first promised and pledged to give her, and now the gods were bringing the marriage to pass, and he was sending her with horses and chariots to the famous city of the Myrmidons, whom her bridegroom ruled. For his son he was bringing home a bride from Sparta, Alector's daughter — strong Megapenthes, his well-loved son, born to him of a slave woman; for to Helen the gods granted no more children after she had first borne her lovely daughter Hermione, who had the beauty of golden Aphrodite.

So they were feasting in the great high-roofed house, the neighbors and kinsmen of glorious Menelaus, taking their pleasure; and among them a divine singer sang to the lyre, and two tumblers whirled through their midst as he led off the song. Meanwhile the two travelers, the hero Telemachus and Nestor's splendid son, halted with their horses at the outer gate. Lord Eteoneus, busy squire of glorious Menelaus, came out and saw them, and went back through the house to bring word to the shepherd of the people; standing close he spoke winged words: “There are two strangers here, Menelaus, fostered of Zeus, two men, and they look like the stock of great Zeus himself. Tell me — shall we unyoke their swift horses, or send them on for someone else to entertain?”

Deeply vexed, fair-haired Menelaus answered him: “You were no fool before, Eteoneus, son of Boethous, but now you are talking nonsense like a child. The two of us ate hospitality enough at other men's hands before we came home — may Zeus only spare us hardship hereafter! Unyoke the strangers' horses, and bring the men in to be feasted.”

So he spoke, and Eteoneus hurried back through the hall, calling the other busy squires to come with him. They loosed the sweating horses from under the yoke and tied them at the mangers, throwing down spelt mixed with white barley; they leaned the chariot against the gleaming entrance wall, and led the men into the divine house. And the two marveled as they looked through the house of the king fostered by Zeus, for a radiance like the sun's or the moon's filled the high-roofed house of glorious Menelaus. When they had had their pleasure of gazing, they stepped into the polished baths and bathed; and when the maidservants had bathed them and rubbed them with oil, and put fleecy cloaks and tunics about them, they took their seats beside Menelaus, son of Atreus. A servant brought water in a fine golden pitcher and poured it over a silver basin for them to wash, and drew up a polished table beside them; the grave housekeeper set out bread and many good things, giving freely of her store; the carver lifted and served platters of every sort of meat, and set golden cups beside them. Fair-haired Menelaus greeted them with a gesture: “Take of the food, and be welcome; and after you have supped we shall ask who among men you are. The breed of your parents is surely not lost in you — you are of the breed of scepter-bearing kings, fostered of Zeus; no base men could father such sons.” So saying, he took in his hands the fat roast chine of an ox that had been set before him as his own portion of honor, and set it before them. And they reached out their hands to the good things lying ready.

When they had put away their desire for drink and food, Telemachus spoke to Nestor's son, leaning his head close so the others would not hear: “Look, son of Nestor, delight of my heart — the flashing of bronze through the echoing halls, and gold and amber and silver and ivory! The court of Olympian Zeus within must be like this — such unspeakable wealth everywhere. Awe takes me as I look.” Fair-haired Menelaus overheard him, and spoke winged words: “Dear children, with Zeus no mortal could compete — his halls and possessions are deathless. Among men, another may rival me in wealth — or may not. Much I suffered and far I wandered before I brought these things home in my ships, in the eighth year: to Cyprus and Phoenicia and the Egyptians I wandered, and reached the Ethiopians and the Sidonians and the Erembi, and Libya, where the lambs are horned from birth, and the ewes bear three times in the round of a year — there no lord nor shepherd goes wanting for cheese or meat or sweet milk, but the flocks yield milk for the milking all year long.

“But while I roamed those lands gathering much wealth, another man killed my brother — by stealth, unlooked-for, through the guile of his accursed wife. So it is with no joy that I rule over all this wealth. You will have heard the tale from your fathers, whoever they are, for I have suffered much, and I lost a house full of good living, holding many fine things. Would that I lived in these halls with only a third part of it, and that the men were alive who died then in wide Troy, far from horse-pasturing Argos! Often, sitting here in our halls, I grieve and mourn them all — sometimes I ease my heart with weeping, and sometimes I stop, for a man is soon sated with chill lament.
