But for all of them, grieved as I am, I do not mourn so much as for one man, who makes sleep and food alike hateful to me when I remember him. For no Achaean labored as Odysseus labored and endured. And for him it was to be nothing but trouble, and for me sorrow never to be forgotten — because he is so long gone, and we do not even know if he is alive or dead. They must be mourning him now, old Laertes, and steady Penelope, and Telemachus, whom he left a newborn in his house.”

So he spoke, and in Telemachus he stirred the desire to weep for his father: tears fell from his eyelids to the ground when he heard his father's name, and he held up his purple cloak before his eyes with both hands. Menelaus noticed him, and pondered in his mind and heart whether to leave it to the young man to name his father himself, or to question him first and test him point by point.

While he was turning this over in his mind and heart, Helen came out of her fragrant high-roofed chamber, looking like Artemis of the golden distaff. Adraste set out a well-made chair for her; Alcippe brought a rug of soft wool; Phylo brought her silver work-basket, the one Alcandre had given her, wife of Polybus, who lived in Egyptian Thebes, where the houses hold the greatest store of treasure. Polybus had given Menelaus two silver bathtubs, a pair of tripods, and ten talents of gold; and his wife besides gave Helen beautiful gifts of her own — a golden distaff, and a basket on wheels, silver, its rims finished in gold. This the handmaid Phylo brought and set beside her, stuffed with fine-spun yarn, and across it lay the distaff wound with violet-dark wool. She sat down on the chair, a footstool under her feet, and at once began questioning her husband on everything: “Do we know yet, Menelaus, fostered of Zeus, who these men claim to be, who have come to our house? Shall I hide my thought or speak the truth? My heart bids me speak. For I say I have never seen such a likeness in anyone, man or woman — awe takes me as I look — as this man bears to the son of great-hearted Odysseus, to Telemachus, whom that man left a newborn in his house when for my sake — shameless creature that I was — you Achaeans came up under Troy, with bold war in your hearts.”

Fair-haired Menelaus answered her: “Now that you say it, wife, I see the likeness too. Odysseus's feet were just like this man's, and his hands, and the glances of his eyes, and his head, and the hair above. And just now, when I was remembering Odysseus and telling of all he endured and labored for my sake, this man let a bitter tear fall from under his brows, holding his purple cloak up before his eyes.”

Then Nestor's son Peisistratus answered him: “Menelaus, son of Atreus, fostered of Zeus, leader of hosts — this is indeed that man's son, as you say. But he is modest, and his heart shrinks from making a show of forward words on a first visit, in your presence, whose voice delights us both like a god's. The Gerenian horseman Nestor sent me with him as his escort, for he longed to see you, in hope you might give him some word or deed of help. Many are the sorrows a son has in his halls when his father is gone and there are no other defenders — as it is now with Telemachus: his father is gone, and there are no others in the land to ward off ruin.”

Fair-haired Menelaus answered him: “Well now! So there has come to my house the son of a man truly dear, who for my sake endured so many trials. I used to tell myself that when he came home I would befriend him beyond all the other Argives — if only Olympian Zeus, far-seeing, had granted the two of us our return over the sea in our swift ships. I would have settled him in Argos, given him a city and built him a house, and brought him from Ithaca with his goods and his son and all his people — emptying out one of the cities that lie around here and are ruled by me. Living close, we would have seen each other constantly, and nothing would have parted us, loving and delighting in one another, until death's black cloud folded us round. But such things, I suppose, the god himself begrudged, who has made that unlucky man, alone of all, a man with no homecoming.”

So he spoke, and stirred in them all the desire to weep. Argive Helen wept, the daughter of Zeus; Telemachus wept, and Menelaus, son of Atreus; nor could Nestor's son keep his eyes dry, for he remembered in his heart blameless Antilochus, whom the shining son of bright Dawn had slain. Remembering him, he spoke winged words: “Son of Atreus, old Nestor always said you were wise beyond mortal men, whenever we spoke of you in his halls and questioned one another. So now, if it may be, listen to me. I take no pleasure in weeping at supper — and early-born Dawn will come soon enough. Not that I grudge weeping for any mortal who dies and meets his fate: this is the only tribute left to miserable mortals, to cut a lock of hair and let a tear fall down the cheek. For my brother too is dead — not the meanest of the Argives; you would have known him. I never met him, never saw him, but they say Antilochus surpassed others, swiftest of foot and a fighter.”

Fair-haired Menelaus answered him: “Friend, you have said all that a wise man might say or do, even one older than you — from such a father, no wonder you speak so wisely. The breed of a man is easily known, when the son of Cronus spins out good fortune for him in his marrying and his begetting — as now he has granted Nestor, all his days, to grow old sleek in his halls, and his sons to be shrewd men and excellent spearmen. So let us leave the weeping that came over us, and think again of our supper. Let them pour water over our hands. In the morning there will be stories enough for Telemachus and me to tell out to one another.” So he spoke, and Asphalion, busy squire of glorious Menelaus, poured water over their hands; and they reached out their hands to the good things lying ready.

Then Helen, daughter of Zeus, thought of another thing. Into the wine they were drinking she cast a drug that dissolves grief and anger and brings forgetfulness of every evil. Whoever swallowed it, once it was mixed in the bowl, would let no tear fall down his cheeks that whole day — not though his mother and father lay dead, not though men cut down his brother or his own dear son with the bronze before his face, and his own eyes saw it. Such cunning drugs the daughter of Zeus possessed, good ones, given her by Polydamna, wife of Thon, a woman of Egypt — where the grain-giving earth bears drugs in greatest plenty, many good in mixture and many baneful, and every man is a physician, wise beyond all peoples; for they are of the line of Paeon. When she had cast it in, and ordered the wine poured, she took up the talk again and said:

“Menelaus, son of Atreus, fostered of Zeus — and you, sons of good men: Zeus gives good and ill, now to one man, now to another, for he can do all things. Sit in the hall now, then, and dine, and take pleasure in stories; and I will tell one that suits the hour. I could not tell nor number all the exploits of steadfast Odysseus, but here is one thing the strong man did and endured in the land of the Trojans, where you Achaeans suffered. He disfigured himself with cruel blows, threw a foul wrap about his shoulders like a servant's, and slipped into the wide-wayed city of the enemy; disguised, he counterfeited another man entirely, a beggar — he who at the Achaean ships was nothing of the kind. In that shape he slipped into the Trojans' city, and they were all taken in; I alone recognized him through his disguise and questioned him — and he in his cunning kept evading me. But when at last I was bathing him and rubbing him with oil, and had put clothes on him, and sworn a mighty oath not to reveal him among the Trojans until he had reached the swift ships and the huts, then finally he told me the whole plan of the Achaeans. And when he had killed many Trojans with the long-edged bronze, he went back to the Argives, bringing much intelligence with him. Then the other Trojan women wailed aloud — but my heart rejoiced, for it had already turned toward going home again, and I mourned too late the blindness Aphrodite gave me when she led me there, away from my own dear country, forsaking my child and my bridal chamber and a husband who lacked nothing, in mind or looks.”

Fair-haired Menelaus answered her: “Yes, wife, all of that you have told rightly. I have come to know the counsel and mind of many heroes in my time, and traveled over much of the earth, but never have my eyes seen a heart like the heart of steadfast Odysseus. Here is another thing that strong man did and endured, inside the carved wooden horse, where all the best of the Argives sat, bringing death and doom to the Trojans. Then you came there — some god must have prompted you, meaning to hand glory to the Trojans — and godlike Deiphobus came with you. Three times you circled the hollow ambush, feeling it with your hands, and you called the best of the Danaans by name, making your voice like the voices of each of the Argives' wives. And I, and the son of Tydeus, and godlike Odysseus, sitting there in the middle, heard you calling. The two of us started up, eager to come out or answer at once from within — but Odysseus held us back and checked us, for all our longing. Then all the other sons of the Achaeans kept silent; only Anticlus was still bent on answering you aloud. But Odysseus clamped his great hands over the man's mouth and held them there without mercy, and saved all the Achaeans, and held on until Pallas Athena led you away.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Menelaus, son of Atreus, fostered of Zeus, leader of hosts — it is the more bitter: none of that saved him from grim destruction, not even a heart of iron within him. But come, send us off to bed, so that we may lie down now and take our pleasure of sweet sleep.” So he spoke, and Argive Helen ordered the servants to set out bedsteads under the portico and pile them with fine purple blankets, spread rugs over them, and lay fleecy cloaks on top for covering. The maids went out of the hall with torches in their hands and made up the beds, and a herald led out the guests. So they slept there in the forecourt of the house, the hero Telemachus and the splendid son of Nestor; and the son of Atreus slept in the inner chamber of the high house, and beside him lay long-robed Helen, shining among women.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, Menelaus of the great war-cry rose from his bed and dressed, slung a sharp sword over his shoulder, bound fine sandals beneath his shining feet, and stepped out of his chamber looking like a god. He sat down beside Telemachus and spoke to him by name: “What need has brought you here, hero Telemachus, to shining Lacedaemon, over the broad back of the sea? Public business or private? Tell me the truth of it.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Menelaus, son of Atreus, fostered of Zeus, leader of hosts — I came in hope you might tell me some word of my father. My house is being eaten up, my rich farms are ruined; the house is full of enemies who slaughter my crowding sheep and my shambling, crooked-horned cattle without end — my mother's suitors, in their overweening insolence. So I have come to your knees, to ask if you will tell me of his sorry end — whether you saw it with your own eyes, or heard the tale of him wandering from another; for beyond all men his mother bore him to sorrow. Do not soften it for me out of pity or courtesy, but tell me exactly what sight you came upon. I beg you: if ever my father, good Odysseus, promised you word or deed and made it good, in the land of the Trojans where you Achaeans suffered — remember it for me now, and tell me the exact truth.”

Deeply vexed, fair-haired Menelaus cried out: “Shame on them! Into the bed of a lion-hearted man they want to lie down, the cowards themselves! As when a deer beds her newborn suckling fawns in a mighty lion's thicket-lair, and goes out grazing the mountain slopes and grassy hollows, and then the lion comes back to his own bed and lets loose an unseemly doom on both alike — so Odysseus will let loose an unseemly doom on those men. O father Zeus, and Athena, and Apollo — if only, being such a man as he was once in well-built Lesbos, when he rose to wrestle Philomeleides on a challenge and threw him mightily, and all the Achaeans rejoiced — if such as that Odysseus might come among the suitors! They would all find swift death and a bitter marriage. As for what you ask and beg of me: I will not swerve aside and speak of other things, and I will not deceive you. What the unerring old man of the sea told me, not a word of it will I hide or keep from you.

“In Egypt the gods still held me back, though I strained to come home, because I had not offered them full hecatombs — and the gods always insist their commands be remembered. There is an island in the surging sea in front of Egypt — Pharos, men call it — as far offshore as a hollow ship makes in a whole day with a stiff breeze blowing behind her; and in it a harbor with good anchorage, from which men put their trim ships out to sea once they have drawn dark water. There the gods kept me twenty days, and never a sign of the sea-winds that carry ships over the broad back of the sea. And now all our provisions would have been spent, and the strength of my men, if a goddess had not pitied me and saved me: Eidothea, daughter of mighty Proteus, the old man of the sea — for I stirred her heart above all. She met me as I wandered alone, apart from my companions — they were forever roaming the island fishing with bent hooks, hunger gnawing at their bellies. She came and stood by me and said: ‘Are you a fool, stranger, and slack-witted with it? Or do you give in gladly, and enjoy your sufferings? All this time you are penned on the island and can find no way out, and the heart of your companions grows faint.’ So she spoke, and I answered her: ‘Whichever of the goddesses you are, let me say this: I am not penned here willingly. I must have offended the immortals who hold wide heaven. Tell me — for the gods know everything — which of the immortals fetters me here and has bound me from my road; and tell me of my homecoming, how I am to go over the fish-filled sea.’

“So I spoke, and the shining goddess answered at once: ‘Then I will tell you, stranger, quite exactly. There haunts this place a certain unerring old man of the sea, immortal Proteus of Egypt, who knows the depths of every sea, a servant of Poseidon; they say he is my father, who begot me. If you could somehow lie in ambush and lay hold of him, he would tell you your road and the stages of your journey, and your homecoming, how you are to go over the fish-filled sea. And he would tell you too, fostered of Zeus, if you wished, whatever good and evil has been done in your halls while you have been gone on your long and grievous way.’ So she spoke, and I answered: ‘Devise yourself the ambush for the divine old man, in case he sees me first, or knows of it beforehand, and escapes me. A god is hard for a mortal man to master.’

“And the shining goddess answered at once: ‘Then I will tell you quite exactly. When the sun bestrides mid-heaven, the unerring old man of the sea comes out of the water, under the West Wind's breath, hidden in the dark ruffle of the sea; and once out, he lies down to sleep in the hollow caves. Around him seals, the brood of the sea's fair daughter, sleep in a huddle, come up out of the grey water, breathing out the sharp smell of the deep brine. There I will bring you at break of day and lay you down in a row among them; and you must choose well three companions, the best you have by your well-benched ships. And I will tell you all the old man's tricks. First he will go along counting the seals; and when he has told them all off by fives and looked them over, he will lie down in their midst, like a shepherd among his flocks of sheep. The moment you see him settled, then summon up your strength and courage, and hold him there, however he struggles and strains to escape. He will try everything, and turn into everything that moves upon the earth, and into water, and into portentous blazing fire; but you must hold on unshakably and squeeze the harder. And when at last he questions you in his own shape, the shape he had when you saw him settling to sleep, then, hero, ease your grip and let the old man go, and ask him which of the gods is punishing you, and about your homecoming, how you are to go over the fish-filled sea.’ So saying, she plunged beneath the surging sea.

“And I went to my ships where they stood on the sand, and many things darkened my heart as I went. When I had come down to the ship and the sea, we made supper, and immortal night came on, and we lay down to sleep on the shore of the sea. When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, I walked along the shore of the wide-wayed sea, praying hard to the gods; and I took three companions, the ones I trusted most for any venture. She meanwhile had slipped beneath the broad bosom of the sea and brought up four sealskins from the deep, all freshly flayed — for she was plotting a snare for her father. She had scooped out beds in the sea-sand and sat waiting; and we came up close to her, and she laid us down in a row and threw a skin over each. That ambush would have been most horrible — the deadly stench of the sea-bred seals wore us down horribly; who would lie down beside a beast of the sea? — but she saved us, and devised a great relief: she brought ambrosia and set it under each man's nose, breathing sweetness, and it killed the smell of the beasts. All morning we waited with enduring hearts; and the seals came up out of the sea in a crowd, and lay down in rows along the break of the surf. At midday the old man came up out of the sea, and found his fat seals, and went along them all and counted them — and among the beasts he counted us first, and his heart never suspected the trick; and then he too lay down. Then we rushed on him with a shout and threw our arms around him — and the old man had not forgotten his cunning art. First he turned into a great-bearded lion, and then a serpent, and a leopard, and a huge boar; and he became running water, and a towering leafy tree. We held on unshakably, with enduring hearts.

“But when at last the old man of the baneful arts grew weary, he questioned me and spoke: ‘Which of the gods, son of Atreus, schemed with you, so that you could take me by ambush against my will? What do you want?’ So he spoke, and I answered: ‘You know, old man — why put me off with questions? — how long I have been penned on this island with no way out to be found, while the heart in me grows faint. Tell me — for the gods know everything — which of the immortals fetters me here and has bound me from my road; and tell me of my homecoming, how I am to go over the fish-filled sea.’ And he answered me at once: ‘Why, you should have made fair sacrifice to Zeus and the other gods before embarking, so as to come quickest to your own country over the wine-dark sea. For it is not your portion to see your dear ones and come to your well-built house and your own native land before you have gone back again to the waters of Egypt, the sky-fed river, and offered holy hecatombs to the immortal gods who hold wide heaven. Then the gods will grant you the road you desire.’ So he spoke, and my spirit broke within me, because he ordered me back again over the misty sea to Egypt, a long and grievous way.

“Yet even so I answered him: ‘All this I will do, old man, just as you command. But come, tell me this, and tell it exactly: did all the Achaeans come home unharmed with their ships, all whom Nestor and I left behind when we sailed from Troy? Or did any die a bitter death aboard his ship, or in his friends' arms, after he had wound up the war?’ So I spoke, and he answered me at once: ‘Son of Atreus, why do you ask me this? You have no need to know, nor to learn my mind; you will not be long dry-eyed, I tell you, once you have heard it all. Many of them were brought down, and many were left; but of the leaders of the bronze-clad Achaeans, two only died on the way home — as for the fighting, you were there yourself — and one, still living somewhere, is held back on the wide sea.

“‘Ajax was brought down among his long-oared ships. Poseidon first drove him onto the great rocks of Gyrae, and saved him out of the sea; and he would have escaped his doom, though Athena hated him, if he had not thrown out a word of mad arrogance: he said that in the gods' despite he had escaped the great gulf of the sea. Poseidon heard his loud boast, and at once took his trident in his massive hands and struck the rock of Gyrae and split it apart. One part held its place; the broken piece fell into the sea — the piece on which Ajax had been sitting when his wits were blinded — and it carried him down into the boundless surging deep. So he perished there, when he had drunk the salt water.

“‘Your brother escaped the fates, for the time, and slipped away in his hollow ships; queenly Hera saved him. But when he was on the point of rounding the steep height of Malea, a storm-wind snatched him up and carried him, groaning heavily, over the fish-filled sea, to the border of the country where Thyestes had his house in the old days, and where Thyestes's son Aegisthus lived now. But when from there too a safe return showed itself, and the gods turned the wind fair again, and they came home — then rejoicing he set foot on his fathers' soil, and touched and kissed his native earth, and many hot tears streamed from him, so glad he was to see his land. But from a lookout post a watchman saw him — a watchman treacherous Aegisthus had brought and posted there, promising him two talents of gold for wages; he had kept watch a full year, so that the king might not slip past unseen and remember his fighting strength. The man went to the palace to bring word to the shepherd of the people, and at once Aegisthus contrived his treacherous scheme: he picked the twenty best men in the land and set them in ambush, and on the other side of the hall he ordered a feast prepared; and then he went with horses and chariots to summon Agamemnon, shepherd of the people — his mind on unspeakable things. He brought him up unsuspecting of his death, and feasted him, and killed him — as a man cuts down an ox at the manger. Not one of the son of Atreus's companions was left alive, of those who followed him, and not one of Aegisthus's men either: they were all killed in the halls.’

“So he spoke, and my spirit broke within me. I sat down on the sands and wept, and my heart no longer wished to live and see the light of the sun. But when I had had my fill of weeping and writhing, the unerring old man of the sea said to me: ‘No more of this endless weeping, son of Atreus; we gain nothing by it. Rather, try with all speed how you may come to your own native land. Either you will find him still alive — or Orestes will have been beforehand and killed him, and you may arrive for the funeral feast.’ So he spoke, and my heart and proud spirit were warmed again in my breast for all my grief, and I spoke winged words to him: ‘These men, then, I know of. But name the third man — the one who is still alive and held back on the wide sea. Or is he dead? Grieved as I am, I want to hear it.’

“So I spoke, and he answered me at once: ‘It is the son of Laertes, whose home is in Ithaca. I saw him on an island, letting the big tears fall, in the halls of the nymph Calypso, who holds him there by force; and he cannot come to his own native land, for he has no ships with oars and no companions to carry him over the broad back of the sea. As for you, Menelaus, fostered of Zeus, it is not ordained that you die in horse-pasturing Argos and meet your fate there. The immortals will send you to the Elysian plain, at the ends of the earth, where fair-haired Rhadamanthus is — where life is easiest for men: no snow, no heavy winter, never any rain, but always Ocean sends up the breezes of the clear-blowing West Wind to refresh mankind — because you have Helen, and in the gods' eyes you are son-in-law to Zeus.’ So saying, he plunged beneath the surging sea.

“And I went to the ships with my godlike companions, and many things darkened my heart as I went. When we had come down to the ship and the sea, we made supper, and immortal night came on, and we lay down to sleep on the shore of the sea. When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, first of all we hauled our ships down into the bright water, and set the masts and sails in the trim vessels, and the men came aboard and sat down at the benches, and sitting in order they beat the grey water with their oars. Back to Egypt's sky-fed river I brought my ships, and offered full hecatombs; and when I had appeased the anger of the gods who live forever, I heaped up a burial mound for Agamemnon, so that his fame might never be quenched. That done, I set sail, and the immortals gave me a wind and brought me swiftly to my own dear country.

“But come — stay on now in my halls, until the eleventh day, or the twelfth; then I will send you off in style, and give you glorious gifts, three horses and a well-polished chariot; and besides I will give you a beautiful cup, so that you may pour libations to the immortal gods and remember me all your days.” Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Son of Atreus, do not keep me here long. For a year I could gladly sit in your presence, and no longing for home or parents would take me — such marvelous pleasure your tales and your talk give me. But my companions are already chafing in sacred Pylos, and you would keep me here a long while. As for the gift you would give me — let it be a treasure I can keep; but horses I will not take to Ithaca. I will leave them here for your own delight. You are lord of a wide plain, rich in clover and galingale, in wheat and spelt and broad-eared white barley. In Ithaca there are no wide courses, no meadowland at all. It is goat-pasture — and lovelier to me than horse-pasture. None of the islands that lean against the sea is fit for driving horses, or rich in meadows — Ithaca least of all.”

So he spoke, and Menelaus of the great war-cry smiled, and stroked him with his hand, and spoke to him by name: “You are of good blood, dear child — hear how you speak! Very well, I will exchange the gifts; I can do that. Of all the treasures that lie stored in my house I will give you the most beautiful and the most precious: I will give you a mixing-bowl of wrought metal, all silver, its rim finished in gold, the work of Hephaestus. The hero Phaedimus gave it to me, the king of the Sidonians, when his house sheltered me on my way home. This I would have you take.”

So they spoke such things to one another, while the banqueters came to the divine king's house, driving up sheep and bringing wine that strengthens men, and their fair-veiled wives sent bread with them. So they busied themselves with the meal in the halls of Menelaus.

But in front of Odysseus's palace, the suitors were amusing themselves as before, with all their old insolence, throwing the discus and the hunting-spear on the leveled ground. Antinous and godlike Eurymachus were sitting apart — the ringleaders of the suitors, far the best of them in prowess. Noemon, Phronius's son, came up to them and questioned Antinous: “Antinous, do we know at all, or not, when Telemachus is coming back from sandy Pylos? He went off with my ship — and now I need her, to cross over to broad Elis, where I have twelve brood mares, with hardy mules at the teat, still unbroken; I want to drive one off and break him in.” So he spoke, and their hearts were astonished: they had not imagined the boy had gone to Neleian Pylos — they thought he was somewhere on the estates, with the flocks or with the swineherd.

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, said to him: “Tell me the truth. When did he go, and what young men went with him? Picked men of Ithaca — or his own hired men and slaves? He could manage even that! And tell me this too, exactly, so that I know it: did he take the black ship from you by force, against your will, or did you give it freely, because he begged you?” Noemon, Phronius's son, answered him: “I gave it freely myself. What would anyone do, when such a man, with such cares on his heart, asks a favor? It would be hard to refuse the gift. The young men who went with him are the best in the land after ourselves; and I saw Mentor going aboard as their leader — or a god, but he was like Mentor in every way. And this is what puzzles me: I saw godlike Mentor here yesterday, at dawn — and yet he boarded the ship then, for Pylos.”

So he spoke, and went off to his father's house; and the proud hearts of the two were left aghast. They made the suitors sit down together and stop their games, and among them Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke, in anguish: his darkened heart was brimming full of fury, and his eyes were like blazing fire. “Damn it all — here is a great feat insolently carried off, this voyage of Telemachus. We said it would come to nothing — and the young boy has simply gone, in the teeth of all of us: launched a ship and picked the best crew in the land. He will begin to be a menace even beyond this. May Zeus destroy his strength before he reaches full manhood! Come — give me a swift ship and twenty men, so that I can watch and ambush him on his way back, in the strait between Ithaca and rugged Same, and make this sea-trip after his father end in misery.” So he spoke, and they all approved and urged it on. Then they rose at once and went into the house of Odysseus.

But Penelope was not long unaware of the schemes the suitors were brooding in their hearts; the herald Medon told her, for he had overheard their plans, standing outside the courtyard while they wove their plot within. He went through the house to bring word to Penelope, and as he crossed her threshold she said: “Herald, why have the lordly suitors sent you? To tell godlike Odysseus's servant-women to drop their work and prepare a feast for them? After all their courting and their gathering here, may they never gather anywhere again — may this be their last and latest supper! — you who crowd in here day after day and shear away our great livelihood, the property of shrewd Telemachus. Did you never hear from your fathers, in the old days when you were children, what kind of man Odysseus was to your parents — never doing an unjust thing, never saying one, in all the land? And that is the very way of divine kings: a king will hate one mortal and love another — but that man never wronged any man alive. No — your spirit shows itself, and your ugly deeds; and there is no gratitude left, afterward, for good done.”

Medon, a man of prudent understanding, answered her: “Queen, would that that were the worst of it. The suitors are contriving another thing, far greater and more grievous — may the son of Cronus never bring it to pass! They are set on killing Telemachus with the sharp bronze as he comes home. He has gone for news of his father, to sacred Pylos and shining Lacedaemon.” So he spoke; and her knees gave way where she stood, and her heart failed; for a long time speechlessness held her; her eyes filled with tears, and her full voice was choked. At last she answered him: “Herald, why has my son gone? He had no need to board any swift-running ship — those horses of the sea that men ride over the great waters. Was it so that not even his name should be left among men?” Medon, a man of prudent understanding, answered her: “I do not know whether some god stirred him, or whether his own heart moved him to go to Pylos, to learn of his father's return, or what fate he has met.” So saying, he went off through the house of Odysseus.

And heart-wasting grief poured around her. She could not bear to sit on a chair, though there were many in the house, but sank down on the threshold of her richly-wrought chamber, weeping piteously, and around her all her servant-women whimpered, all who were in the house, young and old. Sobbing hard, Penelope said to them: “Listen, friends: the Olympian has given me sorrows beyond all the women who were bred and born with me. Long ago I lost my noble, lion-hearted husband, graced with every excellence among the Danaans, that good man whose fame spreads wide through Hellas and the heart of Argos. And now again the storm-winds have snatched my well-loved son from the halls, without a word of fame, and I never heard that he was leaving. Cruel women! Not one of you thought to wake me from my bed, though your hearts knew well enough when he went down to the black hollow ship. Had I known he was setting out on that journey, he would have stayed, however set on going — or he would have left me dead in the house behind him. Quick, someone call old Dolius, my servant, whom my father gave me before ever I came here, who keeps my orchard of many trees — so that he can go at once and sit by Laertes and tell him all of this. Perhaps Laertes may weave some plan in his heart, and come out and appeal in tears to the people, who are minded to destroy his line, and the line of godlike Odysseus.”

Then the good nurse Eurycleia said: “Dear bride — kill me with the pitiless bronze or leave me in the house, but I will not hide the truth from you. I knew all of it. I gave him whatever he ordered, bread and sweet wine. And he took a great oath from me not to tell you before the twelfth day came, or until you missed him yourself and heard that he was gone, so that you would not mar your lovely skin with weeping. Now — bathe, and put clean clothes on your body, and go up to your upper room with your handmaids, and pray to Athena, daughter of aegis-bearing Zeus; for she may yet save him, even from death. And do not add grief to a grieving old man. I cannot think the blessed gods utterly hate the line of Arcesius; someone of it will yet live to hold the high-roofed halls and the rich fields far around.” So she spoke, and lulled Penelope's weeping, and stayed her eyes from tears. She bathed, and put clean clothes on her body, and went up to the upper room with her handmaids, and set barley-grains in a basket, and prayed to Athena: “Hear me, child of aegis-bearing Zeus, unwearied one. If ever resourceful Odysseus in these halls burned fat thigh-pieces of ox or sheep to you, remember it for me now: save my dear son, and ward off the suitors in their wicked insolence.” With that she raised the ritual cry, and the goddess heard her prayer.

But the suitors broke into uproar through the shadowy halls, and one of the arrogant young men would say: “So the much-courted queen is preparing our wedding at last — and has no idea that death has been arranged for her son.” So one and another would say; but it was they who had no idea how things had been arranged. And Antinous addressed them: “Are you mad? Keep clear of boastful talk of every kind, or someone may carry the word inside. Up now, in silence, and let us carry out the plan that pleased us all.” So saying he picked the twenty best men, and they went down to the swift ship and the shore of the sea. First of all they hauled the ship down into deep water, and set the mast and sails in the black ship, and fitted the oars in their leather slings, all in due order, and spread the white sails; and their eager squires brought them their weapons. They moored her well out in the roadstead, and came ashore, and there they took their supper, and waited for evening to come.

But she, up in her upper room, lay fasting, circumspect Penelope, touching no food and no drink, wondering whether her blameless son would escape death, or be brought down by the insolent suitors. As a lion caught in a crowd of men churns with fear when they draw their treacherous circle around him, so she churned with thought — until sweet sleep came over her, and she sank back and slept, and all her joints went loose.

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, thought of another thing. She made a phantom, shaped in the likeness of a woman — Iphthime, daughter of great-hearted Icarius, whom Eumelus married, whose home is in Pherae — and sent it to the house of godlike Odysseus, to still Penelope in her grieving and wailing, and stop her weeping and her tearful lament. Into the chamber it came, past the thong of the bolt, and stood above her head, and spoke to her: “Do you sleep, Penelope, your dear heart worn with sorrow? The gods who live at ease do not suffer you to weep or grieve. Your son is coming home yet — for in the gods' eyes he is no offender.” Circumspect Penelope answered her, slumbering very sweetly at the gates of dreams: “Why have you come here, sister? You have not been in the habit of coming before, since your home is far away. And you tell me to leave off the grief and the many pains that torment me in mind and heart. Long ago I lost my noble, lion-hearted husband, graced with every excellence among the Danaans, that good man whose fame spreads wide through Hellas and the heart of Argos; and now again my well-loved son has gone off in a hollow ship — a child, knowing nothing yet of hardships or of the world's assemblies. For him I grieve even more than for that other; for him I tremble and fear what may happen to him, whether among the people where he has gone, or on the sea. Enemies in plenty are plotting against him, set on killing him before he reaches his own native land.”

The dim phantom answered her: “Take heart; do not fear too greatly in your mind. Such an escort goes with him as other men have prayed to have stand at their side — for she has power — Pallas Athena. And she pities you in your grief; it is she who sent me now to tell you this.” Circumspect Penelope said to her: “If you are truly a god, and have heard a god's voice, then come, tell me of that other unhappy one as well — is he perhaps still alive somewhere, and looking on the light of the sun? Or is he dead already, in the house of Hades?” And the dim phantom answered her: “Of him I will not tell you the tale through to the end — whether he lives or is dead. It is an ill thing to speak words empty as wind.” With that it slipped away past the door-bolt into the breath of the winds. And the daughter of Icarius started up from sleep, and her heart was warmed, so vivid was the dream that had sped to her in the dead of night.

The suitors meanwhile had embarked and were sailing the watery ways, plotting sheer murder in their hearts for Telemachus. There is a rocky island in mid-sea, midway between Ithaca and rugged Same — Asteris, not large, but it has harbors in it fit for ships to lurk, with entrances on either side. There the Achaeans waited for him in ambush.

## Book V

Dawn rose from her bed beside lordly Tithonus, to bring light to the immortals and to mortal men; and the gods were sitting down in assembly, and among them Zeus who thunders on high, whose power is greatest of all. To them Athena was recounting the many sorrows of Odysseus, calling them to mind; for it troubled her that he was still in the nymph's house: “Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live forever — let no sceptered king ever again be kind and gentle from his heart, or know justice in his mind; let him rather be harsh always and work wickedness, since no one remembers godlike Odysseus, not one of the people he ruled, though he was gentle as a father. He lies on an island suffering hard pains, in the halls of the nymph Calypso, who holds him there by force; and he cannot come to his own native land, for he has no ships with oars and no companions to carry him over the broad back of the sea. And now, on top of that, men are bent on killing his beloved son as he sails home — he has gone for news of his father, to sacred Pylos and shining Lacedaemon.”

Zeus the cloud-gatherer answered her: “My child, what a word has escaped the fence of your teeth! Was it not you yourself who devised this plan — that Odysseus should come home and take vengeance on those men? As for Telemachus, guide him skillfully — you have the power — so that he reaches his own native land unscathed, and the suitors sail back in their ship with nothing done.”

Then he turned to Hermes, his dear son: “Hermes — you are our messenger in all else — go tell the fair-haired nymph our fixed decision: the homecoming of steadfast Odysseus, that he is to return with no escort of gods or of mortal men. On a raft made fast with many lashings, suffering pains, on the twentieth day he shall reach fertile Scheria, the land of the Phaeacians, who are near of kin to the gods. They will honor him with all their heart like a god, and send him in a ship to his own dear country, giving him bronze and gold in plenty and clothing — more than Odysseus would ever have won for himself out of Troy, even if he had come home unharmed with his fair share of the spoil. For this is his portion: to see his own people again and come to his high-roofed house and his native land.”

So he spoke, and the guide, the slayer of Argus, did not disobey. At once he bound beneath his feet the beautiful sandals, immortal and golden, that carried him over water and over the boundless earth alike, swift as the blowing wind; and he took up the wand with which he charms the eyes of men, of whomever he wishes, and wakes others from sleep. Holding it in his hands, the strong slayer of Argus flew. He touched down on Pieria, then dropped from the high air to the sea, and skimmed over the waves like a shearwater, which down the perilous gulfs of the barren sea hunts fish and wets its thick wings in the brine. In such a shape Hermes rode the crowding waves.

But when he reached the far-off island, he stepped from the violet sea onto dry land and went on until he came to the great cave where the fair-haired nymph made her home; and he found her within. A great fire was blazing on the hearth, and the scent of split cedar and juniper burning spread far across the island. Inside, she was singing with a lovely voice as she went to and fro at her loom, weaving with a golden shuttle. Around the cave a wood grew in full luxuriance — alder and black poplar and sweet-smelling cypress — where long-winged birds roosted, owls and hawks and long-tongued sea-crows, whose business is on the water. Over the arch of the hollow cave a garden vine had trailed itself, ripe and heavy with clusters. Four springs in a row flowed with bright water, close to one another, turned this way and that; and around them soft meadows of violet and wild celery bloomed. Even an immortal coming there would have gazed in wonder at the sight, and been glad at heart. There the guide, the slayer of Argus, stood and gazed.

When he had wondered at it all to his heart's content, he went straight into the wide cave; and Calypso, shining among goddesses, knew him at sight when she saw him — for the immortal gods are not strangers to one another, though one may live far apart. But great-hearted Odysseus he did not find within: he was sitting on the shore, weeping, where he always sat, tearing his heart with tears and groans and sorrows, looking out over the barren sea, letting the tears run down. And Calypso, shining among goddesses, questioned Hermes, seating him on a bright polished chair: “Why have you come to me, Hermes of the golden wand, honored friend and welcome? You have not been in the habit of coming before. Say what is in your mind; my heart bids me do it, if I can do it and it is a thing that can be done. But first come with me, and let me set hospitality before you.” So speaking, the goddess set a table beside him, filled with ambrosia, and mixed red nectar; and the guide, the slayer of Argus, drank and ate.

When he had dined and satisfied his heart with food, he answered her: “You ask me, goddess to god, why I have come; and I will tell you the truth of it, since you ask. Zeus ordered me here — I did not want to come. Who would willingly race across so vast a stretch of salt water? And no city of mortals anywhere near, to offer the gods sacrifices and choice hecatombs! But there is no way for another god to slip past or thwart the purpose of Zeus who bears the aegis. He says there is a man with you, the most wretched of all the men who fought nine years around Priam's city and in the tenth sacked it and set out for home — men who on the way home offended Athena, and she raised an evil wind and long seas against them. There all the rest of his good companions perished, but wind and wave carried him here. That man Zeus now orders you to send on his way, as quickly as may be. It is not his portion to die here, far from his own people: it is still his lot to see his dear ones again and come to his high-roofed house and his native land.”

So he spoke, and Calypso, shining among goddesses, shuddered, and spoke winged words to him: “You are cruel, you gods, jealous beyond all others! You begrudge goddesses their sleeping openly with men, whenever one makes a man her beloved husband. So it was when rosy-fingered Dawn took Orion: you gods who live at ease begrudged her, until chaste Artemis of the golden throne came at him in Ortygia with her gentle arrows and killed him. So it was when fair-haired Demeter, yielding to her own heart, lay with Iasion in love in a thrice-plowed field: Zeus was not long unaware of it, and killed him with a cast of his blazing bolt. And so now you begrudge me, gods, a mortal man at my side. I saved him — when he rode the keel alone, after Zeus had struck his swift ship with a blazing bolt and shattered it in the middle of the wine-dark sea. All his good companions perished there, but wind and wave carried him here. And I loved him and fed him, and I told him I would make him immortal and unaging all his days. But since there is no way for another god to slip past or thwart the purpose of Zeus who bears the aegis — let him go, if Zeus urges it and commands it, out over the barren sea. Only I will not convey him anywhere: I have no ships with oars, no companions, to carry him over the broad back of the sea. But I will counsel him openly, and hide nothing, so that he may reach his own native land unscathed.”

The guide, the slayer of Argus, answered her: “Send him off so, then — and beware the wrath of Zeus, or he may hold it against you and bear down on you hereafter.” So saying, the strong slayer of Argus departed; and the queenly nymph went to great-hearted Odysseus, since she had heard the message of Zeus. She found him sitting on the shore. His eyes were never dry of tears, and his sweet life was draining away in mourning for his homecoming — for the nymph no longer pleased him. By night, it is true, he would sleep beside her — by compulsion, in the hollow caves, unwilling beside the willing one; but by day he sat on the rocks and the sands, tearing his heart with tears and groans and sorrows, looking out over the barren sea, letting the tears run down.

Coming close, the shining goddess said to him: “Poor man — grieve here no longer, do not let your life waste away. I am ready now, quite ready, to send you off. Come: cut long timbers, and join them with bronze into a wide raft; and fasten half-decks on it, high up, so that it may carry you over the misty sea. I will stow in it bread and water and red wine, to your heart's content, to keep hunger from you, and I will clothe you, and send a wind behind you, so that you may reach your own native land unscathed — if the gods are willing, who hold wide heaven, and who are stronger than I to purpose and to accomplish.”

So she spoke, and much-enduring godlike Odysseus shuddered, and spoke winged words: “Something else is in your mind here, goddess — not my passage home — when you tell me to cross the great gulf of the sea on a raft: that terrible, grinding gulf, which not even the trim swift ships cross, for all their delight in the wind of Zeus. I will not set foot on any raft against your will — not unless you bring yourself, goddess, to swear me a great oath that you are not planning some new evil against me.” So he spoke, and Calypso, shining among goddesses, smiled, and stroked him with her hand, and said: “You are a rogue indeed — there is nothing feeble in your wits — that you thought to say such a thing! Now let earth be my witness, and the wide heaven above, and the down-flowing water of Styx — the greatest oath and the most dread among the blessed gods — that I am not planning some new evil against you. No: my thoughts and counsel for you are what I would devise for my own self, if ever such need came upon me. My mind is honest; the heart in my breast is not made of iron, but is compassionate.”

So speaking, the shining goddess led the way quickly, and he followed in the footsteps of the god. They came to the hollow cave, the goddess and the man, and he sat down on the chair from which Hermes had risen, and the nymph set out every kind of food for him, to eat and to drink, such things as mortal men eat; and she herself sat opposite godlike Odysseus, and her maids set ambrosia and nectar beside her. They reached out their hands to the good things lying ready. And when they had had their pleasure of food and drink, Calypso, shining among goddesses, began: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, resourceful Odysseus — so you really want to go home now, this very moment, to your own dear country? Well then, farewell, even so. But if you knew in your heart how much sorrow it is your fate to fill up before you reach your native land, you would stay right here with me and keep this house, and be immortal — however you long to see that wife of yours, whom you pine for all your days. And yet I claim I am not her inferior, in form or figure — it is not seemly, surely, for mortal women to rival immortals in body and beauty.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Queenly goddess, do not be angry with me over this. I know it all myself, entirely: circumspect Penelope is slighter than you to look upon, in beauty and in stature — she is mortal, and you are immortal and unaging. But even so I wish, and long every day, to go home, and to see my day of returning. And if some god wrecks me again on the wine-dark sea, I will endure it, with a heart in my breast schooled to sorrow. Much have I suffered already and much have I labored, in the waves and in war. Let this be added to the rest.” So he spoke; and the sun set and darkness came on; and the two of them went into the inmost recess of the hollow cave, and took their pleasure in love, and stayed the night beside one another.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, at once Odysseus dressed in cloak and tunic, and she — the nymph — put on a great shining-white mantle, fine-woven and graceful, and cast a beautiful golden girdle about her waist, and a veil over her head. Then she set about the sending of great-hearted Odysseus. She gave him a great axe of bronze, fitted to his hands, sharpened on both edges, with a beautiful olive-wood haft set firmly in it; and she gave him a polished adze besides. She led the way to the far edge of the island, where tall trees grew — alder and black poplar, and fir that reached the sky — long dry and well seasoned, that would float lightly for him. When she had shown him where the tall trees grew, Calypso, shining among goddesses, went back toward her house; and he set to cutting timbers, and the work went quickly under his hands. Twenty trees in all he felled, and trimmed them with the bronze, and dressed them expertly, and trued them to the line.

Meanwhile Calypso, shining among goddesses, brought him augers; and he bored all the pieces and fitted them to one another, and hammered the raft together with pegs and joints. As broad as a man well skilled in carpentry lays out the floor of a wide cargo ship, so broad Odysseus made his raft. He set up half-decks, fitting them to close-set ribs, and finished with long gunwale-planks. He made a mast in it, and a yard fitted to it, and a steering-oar besides, to keep her on course; and he fenced her round from stem to stern with wattles of osier, a bulwark against the wave, and heaped on a mass of brush. Meanwhile Calypso, shining among goddesses, brought him cloth to make a sail; and that too he fashioned expertly. He made fast the braces and halyards and sheets, and then hauled her down on rollers into the bright sea.

The fourth day came and all his work was done; and on the fifth shining Calypso sent him from the island, when she had bathed him and dressed him in fragrant clothes. The goddess put aboard one skin of dark wine, and a second, a great one, of water, and provisions too in a sack, and stowed in many good things to his heart's content; and she sent a fair warm wind for him, harmless and gentle. Glad at the wind, godlike Odysseus spread his sail; and he steered skillfully with the steering-oar as he sat, and sleep never fell on his eyelids as he watched the Pleiades, and late-setting Boötes, and the Bear, which men also call the Wagon, which turns in her place and watches Orion, and alone has no share in the baths of Ocean — for Calypso, shining among goddesses, had told him to keep her on his left hand as he crossed the sea. Seventeen days he sailed, crossing the deep; and on the eighteenth the shadowy mountains of the Phaeacians' land appeared, where it lay nearest to him — like a shield laid on the misty sea.

But the mighty Earthshaker, coming back from the Ethiopians, saw him far off, from the mountains of the Solymi; for Odysseus showed plain to him, sailing the sea. And his anger swelled the greater, and shaking his head he said to his own heart: “Well now! So the gods have changed their minds about Odysseus while I was among the Ethiopians — and here he is, close to the Phaeacians' land, where it is his portion to escape the great cord of misery that has bound him. Even so, I think I can still drive him deep enough into suffering.” With that he gathered the clouds, and gripping his trident in his hands he stirred up the sea, and roused all the blasts of every wind there is, and shrouded land and sea alike in cloud; and night sprang down from heaven. East Wind and South fell on together, and the hard-blowing West, and the North Wind born in the clear sky, rolling a great sea before him. Then Odysseus's knees went loose and his heart failed, and in anguish he spoke to his own great heart: “Wretch that I am — what will become of me at the last? I fear the goddess spoke nothing but truth when she said I should fill up my measure of sorrows on the sea before I reached my own country. Now it is all coming true. With what clouds Zeus is crowning the wide heaven! He has stirred up the sea, and the blasts of every wind are hard upon me. Now my sheer destruction is certain. Three times blessed those Danaans, four times, who died then in wide Troy doing the will of the sons of Atreus! Would that I had died so, and met my fate, on that day when the crowding Trojans hurled their bronze-tipped spears at me over the dead son of Peleus. Then I would have had my funeral rites, and the Achaeans would have carried my fame. Instead it was my doom to be caught by a miserable death.”

Even as he spoke, a great wave drove down on him from above, rushing terribly, and spun the raft around. Far from the raft he fell, and let the steering-oar fly from his hands; the mast snapped in the middle under the terrible gust of mingled winds, and sail and yard fell far off into the sea. The wave held him under a long time, and he could not come up quickly from beneath the great wave's rush, for the clothes shining Calypso had given him weighed him down. At long last he came up, spitting the bitter brine from his mouth, while it streamed in torrents from his head. But even so, worn as he was, he did not forget his raft: he lunged after her through the waves and caught hold of her, and sat down in the middle of her, dodging the finality of death; and the great wave carried her along the current, this way and that. As the autumn North Wind carries thistle-tufts across the plain, and they cling thick to one another, so the winds carried the raft over the sea, this way and that: now the South Wind would toss her to the North to carry, now the East would yield her to the West to chase.

But the daughter of Cadmus saw him — Ino of the beautiful ankles, the White Goddess, who once was a mortal with a mortal's voice, and now in the salt depths has her share of honor among the gods. She pitied Odysseus, driven and suffering, and rose from the water in the likeness of a diving bird on the wing, and settled on the raft of many lashings, and said: “Poor man — why is Poseidon the Earthshaker so monstrously enraged with you, that he sows so many evils for you? Yet he shall not destroy you, however he rages. Do just as I say — you do not seem to me to lack sense. Strip off these clothes, leave the raft for the winds to carry, and swim for it with your hands, and make for the Phaeacians' land, where it is your portion to escape. Here — take this veil and stretch it under your chest; it is immortal; there is no fear then of suffering or death. But the moment your hands touch the mainland, untie it and throw it back into the wine-dark sea, far from the shore, and turn your face away.” So speaking, the goddess gave him the veil, and herself plunged back into the surging sea, like a diving bird; and the dark wave closed over her.

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus pondered, and in anguish he spoke to his own great heart: “Ah me — let this not be some new snare one of the immortals is weaving for me, when she tells me to abandon the raft. I will not obey — not yet: with my own eyes I saw that the land where she said my refuge lies is far away. This is what I will do — it seems best to me: as long as the timbers hold in their joints, so long I will stay aboard and endure my sufferings; but when the wave has shaken the raft to pieces, then I will swim — there is nothing better left to plan.” While he was turning this over in his mind and heart, Poseidon the Earthshaker raised up a great wave, terrible and grinding, arching over him, and drove it down. As a violent wind tosses a heap of dry chaff and scatters it every way, so the wave scattered the raft's long timbers. But Odysseus got astride one timber, riding it like a race-horse, and stripped off the clothes shining Calypso had given him; and at once he stretched the veil under his chest, and flung himself into the sea head first, arms spread, striking out to swim. And the mighty Earthshaker saw him, and shaking his head he said to his own heart: “There — now wander the sea with all your sufferings, until you come among the god-nurtured Phaeacians. Even so, I do not think you will make light of your beating.” So saying, he whipped up his fair-maned horses and came to Aegae, where his famous halls are.

But Athena, daughter of Zeus, thought of another thing. She tied up the courses of the other winds and bade them all cease and be still, and roused a brisk North Wind and broke the waves before him, so that Zeus-born Odysseus might come among the oar-loving Phaeacians, having escaped death and the fates. Two nights and two days he was driven on the solid swell, and many times his heart looked destruction in the face. But when fair-haired Dawn brought round the third day, then the wind fell, and there came a breathless calm; and he caught sight of the land close by, peering keenly ahead as he was lifted on a great wave. As welcome as the sight of a father's life returning to his children, when he has lain in sickness suffering hard pains and wasting long away, while the hateful power bore down on him — and then, welcome beyond telling, the gods free him of his sickness — so welcome to Odysseus was the sight of land and trees. He swam on, straining to set foot on solid earth. But when he was as far offshore as a shout will carry, he heard the boom of sea against rock-teeth — for the great swell was crashing against the dry land, belching terribly, and everything was shrouded in salt spray. There were no harbors to hold ships, no roadsteads, but jutting headlands, and rock-teeth, and cliffs.

Then Odysseus's knees went loose and his heart failed, and in anguish he spoke to his own great heart: “Ah me — now that Zeus has granted me sight of land beyond hope, and I have cut my way across all this gulf, no way out of the grey sea shows itself anywhere. Offshore, sharp rocks, and around them the swell roars and rages; the cliff runs up sheer; the water inshore is deep; there is nowhere to stand on both feet and escape destruction. If I try to climb out, a great wave may snatch me and dash me on the stony rock, and my effort go for nothing. But if I swim along further, hoping to find shelving beaches and harbors of the sea, I fear the storm may snatch me up again and carry me, groaning heavily, out over the fish-filled deep; or some god may set a great sea-beast on me out of the water, of the many that famous Amphitrite breeds. For I know how the famous Earthshaker hates me.” While he was turning this over in his mind and heart, a great wave carried him onto the rugged shore. There his skin would have been stripped off and his bones crushed together, had not the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, put a thought in his mind: lunging in, he caught the rock with both hands, and clung to it groaning while the great wave went past. So he escaped that one; but the backwash leapt on him again and struck him and threw him far out to sea. As when an octopus is dragged out of its den and the pebbles cling thick to its suckers, so on the rocks the skin was stripped from his bold hands; and the great wave closed over him. Then, past his allotted portion, wretched Odysseus would have perished, had not bright-eyed Athena given him presence of mind. Coming up clear of the surf where it belches against the shore, he swam along outside it, looking toward the land, hoping to find shelving beaches and harbors of the sea. And when, swimming, he came to the mouth of a fair-flowing river, there the place seemed best to him, free of rocks, and there was shelter from the wind. He felt the river's current flowing out, and prayed in his heart: “Hear me, lord, whoever you are. I come to you, much prayed-for, fleeing out of the sea from Poseidon's threats. Even to the immortal gods that man is sacred who comes as a wanderer, as I come now to your stream and to your knees, after much suffering. Pity me, lord: I claim myself your suppliant.”

So he spoke, and at once the river stayed his current and held back his wave, and made a calm before him, and brought him safe into his outflow. And Odysseus bent both knees and let his strong hands fall, for the sea had crushed his heart. All his flesh was swollen, and the sea oozed in streams up through his mouth and nostrils; breathless and speechless he lay, barely alive, and a terrible weariness had come upon him. But when he got his breath again and his spirit gathered back into his heart, then he unbound the goddess's veil from his body and let it go into the river as it ran seaward; a great wave carried it back down the current, and Ino quickly caught it in her hands. And he, staggering clear of the river, sank down in the rushes and kissed the grain-giving earth.

And in anguish he spoke to his own great heart: “Ah me, what now? What will become of me at the last? If I keep watch through the wretched night here by the river, the evil frost and the soft dew together may finish me — weak as I am, my spirit gasping — and toward dawn the breeze off a river blows cold. But if I climb the slope to the shadowy wood, and lie down to sleep in the thick brush — supposing cold and weariness release me, and sweet sleep comes over me — I fear I may become prey and plunder for wild beasts.” As he thought it over, this seemed the better course: he made for the wood, and found it close by the water, in a clearing seen from every side. He crept under two bushes growing from a single stock — one wild olive, one the planted tree. Through them no force of wet-blowing winds could pass, no rays of the shining sun could strike, no rain pierce through, so thick they grew, interlaced with one another. Under these Odysseus crept, and at once heaped up a wide bed with his own hands; for there was a great drift of fallen leaves, enough to shelter two men or three in the winter season, however hard the weather. Seeing it, much-enduring godlike Odysseus was glad, and lay down in the middle, and poured the fallen leaves over himself. As a man buries a firebrand in dark ash, on a far-off farm with no neighbors near, saving the seed of fire so as not to have to kindle it from somewhere else, so Odysseus buried himself in leaves. And Athena poured sleep over his eyes, to shut his eyelids and release him quickest from his exhausting weariness.

## Book VI

So he slept there, much-enduring godlike Odysseus, worn out with sleep and weariness. Meanwhile Athena went to the land and city of the Phaeacians — a people who once lived in broad Hypereia, next to the Cyclopes, overbearing men who kept raiding them and were the stronger. Godlike Nausithous had led them up from there and settled them in Scheria, far from bread-eating men; he had run a wall around the city, built houses, made temples for the gods, and divided out the plowlands. But he, long since broken by fate, had gone down to Hades, and now Alcinous ruled, whose counsels came from the gods. To his house went the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, devising the homecoming of great-hearted Odysseus.

She went to the richly-wrought chamber where a girl lay sleeping, like the immortals in form and beauty: Nausicaa, daughter of great-hearted Alcinous; and near her two handmaids, with beauty from the Graces, one on either side of the doorposts, and the shining doors were shut. Like a breath of wind she swept to the girl's bed, and stood above her head, and spoke to her, taking the likeness of the daughter of Dymas, famed for ships — a girl her own age, dear to her heart. In that shape bright-eyed Athena said: “Nausicaa, how did your mother come to have so careless a daughter? Your shining clothes are lying about uncared-for — and your wedding is near, when you must dress beautifully yourself and provide the same for those who escort you. It is from things like this that a good name spreads among men, and a father and honored mother are made glad. Come, let us go washing at break of day; I will come with you as your work-fellow, so that you are ready the sooner — for you will not be a maiden much longer. Already the best men among all the Phaeacians are courting you, whose own family is noble too. Come, urge your famous father before dawn to have the mules and the wagon made ready, to carry the sashes and robes and shining coverlets. And it is far more becoming for you yourself to ride than to go on foot — the washing-pools are a long way from the city.” So speaking, bright-eyed Athena went away to Olympus, where they say the gods' seat stands fast forever: no winds shake it, no rain wets it, no snow comes near, but clear air spreads cloudless, and a white radiance runs over it; there the blessed gods take their pleasure all their days. There the bright-eyed one went, when she had given the girl her instructions.

At once fair-throned Dawn came and woke fair-robed Nausicaa; and she marveled at her dream, and went through the house to tell her parents, her dear father and mother. She found them within: her mother sat at the hearth with her serving-women, spinning yarn dyed with sea-purple; her father she met at the door as he was going out to join the famous princes in council, where the lordly Phaeacians summoned him. Standing close, she said to her dear father: “Papa dear, won't you have the wagon made ready for me — the tall one with the good wheels — so that I can take our fine clothes to the river to wash? They are lying dirty. And it is fitting that you yourself, when you sit in council with the first men, should have clean clothes on your body. And there are five dear sons born to you in the halls — two married, and three flourishing bachelors, and they always want fresh-washed clothes to wear to the dancing. All of this is on my mind.” So she spoke, for she was too shy to name marriage outright to her dear father. But he understood it all, and answered: “I grudge you neither the mules, child, nor anything else. Go; the servants will make ready the tall wagon with the good wheels, fitted with a body on top.” So saying, he called to the servants, and they obeyed. Outside they made ready the smooth-running mule wagon, and led the mules under the yoke and harnessed them; and the girl brought the shining clothing from her chamber and laid it in the polished wagon, and her mother packed a box with pleasing food of every kind, and put in delicacies, and poured wine into a goatskin. The girl climbed into the wagon; and her mother gave her soft olive oil in a golden flask, for herself and her handmaids after bathing. Nausicaa took the whip and the glossy reins, and touched the mules to start them; there was a clatter of hooves, and they pressed on untiring, carrying the clothing and the girl — not alone: her handmaids went along with her.

When they reached the beautiful stream of the river, where the washing-pools never fail — abundant clear water welling up through them, enough to clean the dirtiest clothes — they loosed the mules from the wagon and turned them along the eddying river to graze the honey-sweet river-grass. From the wagon they took the clothing in armfuls and carried it into the dark water, and trod it briskly in the pits, racing one another. When they had washed everything and rinsed away all the dirt, they spread the clothes in rows along the shore of the sea, where the waves washed the shingle cleanest against the land. Then they bathed, and rubbed themselves richly with olive oil, and took their meal on the banks of the river while they waited for the clothes to dry in the sun's blaze. And when they had had their pleasure of food, she and her handmaids, they threw off their headgear and fell to playing at ball; and white-armed Nausicaa led their singing. As arrow-pouring Artemis goes down the mountains, along high Taygetus or Erymanthus, delighting in boars and swift deer, and with her the nymphs of the countryside, daughters of aegis-bearing Zeus, join in her play — and Leto's heart is glad, for above them all her daughter carries her head and brows, and is easily known, though all are beautiful — so the unwed girl shone out among her handmaids.

But when she was about to yoke the mules and fold the fine clothes and set off home, then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, thought of another thing: that Odysseus should wake, and see the lovely-faced girl, and that she should lead him to the Phaeacians' city. So the princess threw the ball to one of her handmaids — and missed her, and threw it into a deep eddy; and they all shrieked aloud. Godlike Odysseus woke, and sitting up he debated in his mind and heart: “Ah me — whose land have I come to this time? Are they violent, savage, without justice? Or kind to strangers, with a god-fearing mind? A cry of girls came round me just now — of nymphs, perhaps, who hold the steep mountain crests and the springs of rivers and the grassy meadows. Or am I somewhere near men of human speech? Come — I will find out and see for myself.”

So saying, godlike Odysseus crept out from under the bushes, and with his thick hand broke a leafy branch from the dense growth to hold before his body and cover a man's nakedness. He came on like a mountain-bred lion trusting in his strength, who goes through rain and wind, his eyes blazing, in among the cattle or the sheep or after the wild deer — his belly drives him to try the flocks even inside the close-built fold: so Odysseus was about to come among the fair-haired girls, naked as he was; for need had come upon him. And he loomed terrible to them, crusted with brine, and they scattered in fright along the jutting spits of shore. Alone the daughter of Alcinous stayed, for Athena put courage in her heart and took the fear from her limbs. She stood her ground facing him. And Odysseus debated: should he clasp the knees of the lovely-faced girl and beg — or keep his distance and beg with soothing words, in hope she would show him the city and give him clothes? As he thought it over, this seemed the better course — to keep his distance and beg with soothing words, in case the girl take offense if he touched her knees. So at once he spoke, a speech soothing and shrewd:

“I am at your knees, queen — goddess or mortal, whichever you are. If you are one of the gods who hold wide heaven, then to Artemis, daughter of great Zeus, I liken you most nearly in beauty and stature and form; but if you are one of the mortals who dwell on earth, then thrice-blessed your father and honored mother, thrice-blessed your brothers — surely their hearts are forever warmed with delight because of you, when they watch such a young shoot stepping into the dance. But most blessed of all, beyond everyone, the man who prevails with his wedding-gifts and leads you home. For my eyes have never seen such a mortal, man or woman; awe holds me as I look. Once, in Delos, by Apollo's altar, I saw something like you — a young palm shoot springing up; for I went there too, with a great host at my back, on that journey which was to bring me such bitter sorrows. Seeing that tree I stood amazed a long while, for no such stem ever rose from the earth — as now, lady, I wonder at you and stand amazed, and fear terribly to touch your knees. But hard grief has come upon me. Yesterday, on the twentieth day, I escaped the wine-dark sea; all that time the waves and the driving storms carried me from the island of Ogygia, and now some power has cast me up here — to suffer more evil here as well, I suppose; for I do not think it will stop: the gods will accomplish much upon me first. Pity me, queen. After many trials it is to you first that I have come; I know not one of the people who hold this city and land. Show me the town, and give me a rag to throw about me, if you had any wrapping for the linen when you came here. And may the gods grant you all that your heart desires — a husband and a home, and may they bestow the fine gift of like-mindedness; for nothing is stronger or better than when a man and a woman keep a household with one mind: much pain for their enemies, and joy for their friends — and they themselves know it best.”

White-armed Nausicaa answered him: “Stranger — since you seem neither base nor witless — it is Olympian Zeus himself who deals out fortune to men, to good and bad, to each as he wills; and this, I suppose, is what he has given you, and you must bear it even so. But now, since you have come to our city and land, you shall not go wanting for clothing or anything else that is due a hard-tried suppliant when he presents himself. I will show you the town, and tell you the name of the people: the Phaeacians hold this city and land, and I am the daughter of great-hearted Alcinous, on whom the power and might of the Phaeacians depend.” Then she called to her fair-haired handmaids: “Stop, my women! Where are you running at the sight of a man? Surely you don't take him for an enemy? There is no mortal alive, nor will be, who could come bearing war to the land of the Phaeacians — we are too dear to the immortals. We live far apart, in the surging sea, at the world's edge, and no other mortals mix with us. This is some unlucky wanderer come here, whom we must care for now. From Zeus come all strangers and beggars, and a gift, though small, is dear. Come, my women, give the stranger food and drink, and bathe him in the river where there is shelter from the wind.”

So she spoke, and they stopped, and urged one another on, and set Odysseus down in the shelter, as Nausicaa daughter of great-hearted Alcinous had ordered. They laid out clothing beside him, a mantle and a tunic, and gave him soft olive oil in the golden flask, and told him to bathe in the river's streams. Then godlike Odysseus said to the handmaids: “Stand off there, my women, while I wash the brine from my shoulders myself, and rub myself with oil — it is long enough since oil came near my skin. But I will not bathe in front of you; I am ashamed to go naked among fair-haired girls.” So he spoke; they went apart, and told the girl. And godlike Odysseus washed his body clean in the river of the brine that caked his back and broad shoulders, and scrubbed from his head the crust of the barren sea. When he had washed all over and rubbed himself with oil, and put on the clothes the unwed girl had provided, then Athena, born of Zeus, made him taller to look at and sturdier, and made the curling locks flow from his head like the hyacinth flower. As when a craftsman overlays silver with gold — a skilled man whom Hephaestus and Pallas Athena have taught every art, and he finishes graceful work — so she poured grace on his head and shoulders. Then he went apart and sat on the shore of the sea, shining with beauty and grace; and the girl gazed at him.

Then she said to her fair-haired handmaids: “Listen, my white-armed women, while I say something. Not against the will of all the gods who hold Olympus does this man come among the godlike Phaeacians. Before, he seemed to me a sorry sight; but now he is like the gods who hold wide heaven. Would that such a man might be called my husband, living here — that it might please him to stay! But come, my women, give the stranger food and drink.” So she spoke, and they heard and obeyed, and set food and drink beside Odysseus. And much-enduring godlike Odysseus drank and ate ravenously, for he had gone long without food.

Then white-armed Nausicaa thought of another thing. She folded the clothes and laid them in the fine wagon, yoked the strong-hoofed mules, and climbed up herself; then she called to Odysseus and spoke to him: “Rouse yourself now, stranger, to go to the city, so that I may set you on the way to my wise father's house, where, I promise you, you shall meet the best of all the Phaeacians. Only do just as I say — you seem to me no fool. As long as we are passing through fields and farmlands, walk briskly with the handmaids behind the mules and the wagon, and I will lead the way. But when we are about to enter the city — around it runs a high wall, and a fine harbor lies on either side of the town, and the entrance is narrow, and the curved ships are drawn up along the road, for every man has his own slip; there too is their gathering-place, around the fine temple of Poseidon, fitted with quarried stones set deep; and there they attend to the tackle of their black ships, cables and canvas, and shave down their oars. For the Phaeacians care nothing for bow or quiver, but for masts and ships' oars and the trim ships themselves, in which they delight as they cross the grey sea — there I would avoid their harsh talk, in case someone jeer at me behind my back; there are insolent men enough in the town, and one of the baser sort might say, meeting us: ‘Who is this following Nausicaa, this tall handsome stranger? Where did she find him? A husband for her, no doubt! Some castaway she has taken up, off his ship, a man from foreign parts — since no one lives near us. Or some god has come down from heaven in answer to her prayers, hers to keep all her days!
