Better so, if she has gone round and found herself a husband from elsewhere — since she scorns the Phaeacians here in their own land, though many fine men court her.’ So they will talk, and it would become a reproach against me. I myself would blame any girl who did such things — who against the will of her dear father and mother went with men before coming to open marriage.

“Stranger, mark my words now, so that you may soonest win escort and homecoming from my father. You will find a splendid grove of Athena near the road — poplars; a spring flows in it, and a meadow lies around it; there is my father's estate, and his flourishing orchard, as far from the city as a shout will carry. Sit down there and wait a while, until we have come into the town and reached my father's house. Then, when you judge that we have reached the house, go into the Phaeacians' city and ask for the house of my father, great-hearted Alcinous. It is easily known — a mere child could guide you — for the Phaeacians' houses are not built like the house of the hero Alcinous. But when the buildings and the courtyard have taken you in, go quickly straight through the great hall until you come to my mother. She sits at the hearth in the firelight, spinning yarn dyed with sea-purple — a wonder to behold — leaning against a pillar, and her serving-women sit behind her. There too my father's chair is propped beside hers, where he sits and drinks his wine like an immortal. Pass him by, and throw your arms about my mother's knees, if you want to see your day of homecoming quickly, and with joy, however far you have come. If she is friendly to you in her heart, then there is hope you will see your own people again, and come to your well-built house and your native land.”

So speaking, she touched the mules with the shining whip, and they quickly left the river's streams behind; well they trotted, well they swung their legs, and she drove with care, so that the handmaids and Odysseus could follow on foot, laying on the whip with judgment. The sun set as they reached the famous grove sacred to Athena. There godlike Odysseus sat down, and at once he prayed to the daughter of great Zeus: “Hear me, child of aegis-bearing Zeus, unwearied one. Hear me now at last, since before you never heard me when I was wrecked, when the famous Earthshaker wrecked me. Grant that I come among the Phaeacians as one to be welcomed and pitied.” So he prayed, and Pallas Athena heard him; but she did not yet appear to him face to face, out of respect for her father's brother, who raged furiously against godlike Odysseus until he reached his own land.

## Book VII

So much-enduring godlike Odysseus prayed there, while the strength of the two mules carried the girl toward the town. When she reached her father's famous house, she halted at the gateway, and her brothers, men like the immortals, gathered round her: they loosed the mules from the wagon and carried the clothing inside. She herself went to her chamber, where an old Apeiraean woman, the chambermaid Eurymedusa, kindled a fire for her. Long ago the curved ships had brought her from Apeira, and men had picked her out as a prize of honor for Alcinous, because he ruled all the Phaeacians and the people listened to him as to a god; she had nursed white-armed Nausicaa in the palace. Now she kindled the fire and set out supper in the room.

Then Odysseus rose to go to the city; and Athena, with kindly thoughts for him, poured a thick mist around him, so that no great-hearted Phaeacian meeting him should jeer at him and ask who he was. And just as he was about to enter the lovely town, there the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, met him in the likeness of a young girl carrying a pitcher. She stopped in front of him, and godlike Odysseus asked her: “Child, would you not guide me to the house of a man called Alcinous, who is lord among these people? I am a hard-tried stranger, come here from far away, from a distant land, and I know no one of the people who hold this city and land.” The goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered: “Then I will show you the house you ask for, stranger father, since he lives close by my own blameless father. Only go in silence, just so, and I will lead the way; and do not look at anyone or ask questions. These people have no great patience with strangers, and give no loving welcome to a man from elsewhere. Trusting to their swift running ships they cross the great gulf, since the Earthshaker has granted it to them; and their ships are fast as a wing, or as a thought.”

So speaking, Pallas Athena led the way quickly, and he followed in the footsteps of the god. And the Phaeacians, famed for their ships, never noticed him as he went through the town among them: fair-haired Athena, that dread goddess, would not allow it, but poured a wondrous mist over him, with kindly thoughts for him in her heart. And Odysseus marveled at the harbors and the trim ships, at the meeting-places of the heroes themselves, and the long walls, high, fitted with palisades, a wonder to behold. When they reached the king's famous house, the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, spoke first: “Here, stranger father, is the house you asked me to show you. You will find the kings, fostered of Zeus, at their feast. Go inside, and let your heart fear nothing: a bold man proves the better in everything, even if he comes from a foreign land. First you will come upon the mistress in the hall — Arete is the name she is called, and she comes of the same parents as the king, Alcinous. Nausithous first was born of Poseidon the Earthshaker and Periboea, loveliest of women, youngest daughter of great-hearted Eurymedon, who once was king over the insolent Giants — but he destroyed his reckless people, and was destroyed himself. With her Poseidon lay, and fathered a son, great-hearted Nausithous, who ruled among the Phaeacians; and Nausithous fathered Rhexenor and Alcinous. Rhexenor, still without a son, newly wed, Apollo of the silver bow struck down in his hall, leaving one only child, Arete. Alcinous made her his wife, and honored her as no other woman on earth is honored, of all who now keep house under their husbands. So heartfelt is the honor she has, and has always had, from her dear children and from Alcinous himself and from the people, who look on her as on a god and greet her with blessings when she walks through the town. For she has no lack of good understanding herself: for those to whom she is well-disposed — even men — she resolves disputes. If she is friendly to you in her heart, then there is hope you will see your own people again, and come to your high-roofed house and your native land.”

So speaking, bright-eyed Athena departed over the barren sea; she left lovely Scheria, and came to Marathon and wide-wayed Athens, and entered the close-built house of Erechtheus. And Odysseus went toward the famous house of Alcinous; and his heart pondered much as he stood there, before he reached the bronze threshold. For a radiance like the sun's or the moon's filled the high-roofed house of great-hearted Alcinous. Bronze walls ran this way and that, from the threshold to the inmost room, with a frieze of blue enamel round about; golden doors closed the close-built house within; silver doorposts stood on the bronze threshold, a silver lintel above, and a golden handle. On either side stood dogs of gold and silver, which Hephaestus had fashioned with knowing skill to guard the house of great-hearted Alcinous, deathless and unaging all their days. Within, seats were fixed along the wall this way and that, from the threshold to the inmost room in an unbroken line, and over them were thrown light finely-spun coverings, the work of women. There the leaders of the Phaeacians sat drinking and eating, for they had abundance the year round. And golden youths stood on well-built pedestals, holding blazing torches in their hands, lighting the nights through the house for the banqueters. And fifty serving-women belong to the house: some grind the apple-yellow grain at the millstone, others weave at the looms and twirl the spindles as they sit, restless as the leaves of a tall poplar; and from the close-woven linen the soft olive oil runs off. As far as the Phaeacian men are skilled beyond all others at driving a swift ship on the sea, so are their women skilled at the loom; for Athena has granted them beyond others knowledge of beautiful handiwork, and good wits.

Outside the courtyard, near the doors, is a great orchard of four acres, with a fence driven round it on both sides. There tall trees grow in full leaf — pears and pomegranates and apple trees with shining fruit, and sweet figs, and olives in full leaf. Their fruit never perishes and never fails, winter or summer, the year round; but always the West Wind's breath quickens some to life and ripens others. Pear mellows on pear, apple on apple, grape-cluster on grape-cluster, fig on fig. There too his fruitful vineyard is rooted, one part of which — a warm, level spot — is drying in the sun, while other grapes they are gathering, and others they are treading; in front are unripe grapes shedding their blossom, and others just darkening. There too, by the last row of vines, grow trim garden-beds of every kind, gleaming the year round; and in it are two springs — one is scattered through the whole garden, while the other, opposite, runs under the courtyard threshold toward the high house, and from it the townspeople draw their water. Such were the gods' splendid gifts in the house of Alcinous.

There much-enduring godlike Odysseus stood and gazed. And when he had wondered at it all to his heart's content, he stepped quickly over the threshold into the house. He found the leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians pouring libations from their cups to the sharp-sighted slayer of Argus — to him they poured last of all, when they were minded to go to bed. But much-enduring godlike Odysseus went on through the hall, wrapped in the thick mist Athena had poured about him, until he came to Arete and King Alcinous. And Odysseus threw his arms about Arete's knees — and at that moment the divine mist streamed away from him. They fell silent throughout the hall at the sight of the man, and marveled as they looked. And Odysseus made his plea: “Arete, daughter of godlike Rhexenor — to your husband, and to your knees, I come after many trials, and to these banqueters. May the gods grant them prosperous life, and may each hand down to his children the wealth in his halls and the honors the people have given him. But for me — speed my sending, that I may come to my own country, and quickly; for long now, far from my own people, I have been suffering sorrows.”

So saying, he sat down in the ashes on the hearth, by the fire; and they all stayed hushed in silence. At last the old hero Echeneus spoke — an elder among the Phaeacian men, a master of speech, who knew many things of old. With good will he addressed them: “Alcinous, this is not the finer way, and it is not fitting, that a stranger should sit on the ground, on the hearth, in the ashes; these others are holding back, waiting on your word. Come, raise the stranger up and seat him on a silver-studded chair, and order the heralds to mix wine, so that we may pour to Zeus who delights in thunder, who walks beside worthy suppliants; and let the housekeeper give the stranger supper from what is within.”

When the hallowed prince Alcinous heard this, he took by the hand Odysseus, the shrewd man of many devices, raised him from the hearth and set him on a shining chair, making his son rise — kindly Laodamas, who sat next to him and whom he loved best. A servant brought water in a fine golden pitcher and poured it over a silver basin for him to wash, and drew up a polished table beside him; the grave housekeeper set out bread and many good things, giving freely of her store. And much-enduring godlike Odysseus drank and ate. Then the princely Alcinous said to the herald: “Pontonous, mix a bowl and serve wine to everyone in the hall, so that we may pour to Zeus who delights in thunder, who walks beside worthy suppliants.” So he spoke, and Pontonous mixed the honey-hearted wine and served a first portion to all, into every cup. And when they had poured and drunk what their hearts desired, Alcinous addressed them: “Hear me, leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians, while I say what the heart in my breast bids me. Now that you have feasted, go home and sleep; in the morning we will call more of the elders together, and entertain the stranger in the halls, and offer fine sacrifices to the gods; and after that we will think about his sending, so that without toil or trouble this stranger may come to his own native land under our escort, and quickly, and with joy, however far away it is — and suffer no harm or hurt midway before he sets foot on his own soil. There, afterward, he will suffer whatever Destiny and the grim Spinners spun into their thread for him at his birth, when his mother bore him. But if he is one of the immortals come down from heaven, then this is some new thing the gods are devising; for always in time past the gods have shown themselves to us plainly, whenever we offer glorious hecatombs, and they feast among us, sitting where we sit. Even if a lone traveler of ours meets them on the road, they use no concealment, for we are close of kin to them — as close as the Cyclopes and the wild tribes of the Giants.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Alcinous, let another thought be in your heart. I am not like the immortals who hold wide heaven, not in body or stature, but like mortal men. Whomever you know among mankind who carry the heaviest load of misery — to them I would liken myself in sorrows. Yes, and I could tell a still longer tale of all the evils I have labored through by the gods' will. But let me eat my supper, troubled as I am; for there is nothing more shameless than the accursed belly, which commands a man to remember it whether he will or no, worn as he may be, with grief in his heart — just as I have grief in my heart, and yet it keeps bidding me eat and drink, and blots out all I have suffered, and insists on being filled. But you — bestir yourselves at break of day, to set me, unlucky as I am, on my own soil, even after all my sufferings. Let life leave me once I have seen my property, my servants, and my great high-roofed house.” So he spoke, and they all approved, and urged that the stranger be sent home, since he had spoken rightly. And when they had poured and drunk what their hearts desired, the others went home to sleep, each man to his own house.

But godlike Odysseus was left in the hall, and beside him sat Arete and godlike Alcinous, while the serving-women cleared away the dishes of the feast. White-armed Arete began — for seeing them she had recognized the mantle and tunic, the fine clothes she herself had made with her serving-women — and she spoke winged words: “Stranger, this first will I ask you myself: who are you, and from where among men? Who gave you these clothes? Did you not say you came here wandering over the sea?” Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “It is a hard thing, queen, to tell my sorrows through to the end, for the gods of heaven have given me many. But this I will tell you, since you ask and inquire. There is an island, Ogygia, lying far off in the sea, where the daughter of Atlas lives, guileful Calypso of the lovely hair, a dread goddess; and no one keeps company with her, god or mortal man. But some power brought me, wretched as I am, to her hearth — alone, for Zeus had struck my swift ship with his blazing bolt and shattered it in the middle of the wine-dark sea. There all the rest of my good companions perished, but I got my arms around the keel of my curved ship and was carried for nine days; on the tenth, in black night, the gods brought me to the island of Ogygia, where Calypso lives, the fair-haired dread goddess. She took me in and loved me devotedly and fed me, and kept saying she would make me immortal and unaging all my days; but she never persuaded the heart in my breast. Seven years I remained there without a break, and forever wetted with my tears the immortal clothes Calypso gave me. But when the eighth year came round in its circling, then she roused me and bade me go — whether by some message from Zeus, or because her own mind had changed. She sent me off on a raft of many lashings, and gave me much — bread and sweet wine — and dressed me in immortal clothes, and sent a fair warm wind ahead, harmless and gentle. Seventeen days I sailed, crossing the deep; on the eighteenth the shadowy mountains of your land appeared, and my heart was glad — ill-fated as I was; for I was yet to keep company with misery in plenty, sent upon me by Poseidon the Earthshaker. He roused the winds against me and barred my way, and stirred up a sea past all telling; nor would the waves let me be carried on my raft as I groaned without cease. The storm shattered the raft at last; and I swam, cutting my way across that gulf, until wind and water carried me to your shore. There, as I tried to land, the waves would have overpowered me against the dry land, hurling me on the great rocks in a joyless place; but I gave way, swam back, and came at last to a river, where the place seemed best to me, free of rocks, and sheltered from the wind. I staggered out and lay gathering my spirit, and immortal night came on. I climbed out and away from the sky-fed river, and lay down to sleep in the bushes, heaping the leaves over me; and a god poured boundless sleep upon me. There in the leaves, my heart worn with sorrow, I slept all night, and through the dawn, and to midday; the sun was westering when sweet sleep released me. And then I saw your daughter's handmaids playing on the shore, and among them herself, like the goddesses. To her I made my prayer — and she did not fail in right understanding, in a way you would not expect of a young person met by chance, for the young are always thoughtless. She gave me bread in plenty and sparkling wine, and had me bathed in the river, and gave me these clothes. That, grieved as I am, is the truth told out.”

Then Alcinous answered him and said: “Stranger, in this one thing my child did not judge rightly: she did not bring you to our house with her handmaids — though it was to her first that you made your prayer.” Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Hero, do not scold your blameless daughter for my sake. She did tell me to follow with her handmaids; but I would not, out of fear and shame — in case your heart darkened when you saw it; for we are quick to jealousy, we tribes of men upon the earth.” Alcinous answered him and said: “Stranger, the heart in my breast is not the kind to take offense at nothing; due measure is better in all things. O father Zeus and Athena and Apollo — being such a man as you are, thinking as I myself think, would that you might have my daughter, and be called my son-in-law, and stay here! I would give you house and possessions, if you stayed willingly. But against your will no Phaeacian shall hold you: Zeus father forbid it! And your sending I appoint for a fixed time — know it now — for tomorrow. You will lie mastered by sleep while they row you over calm water, until you come to your own country and your house, or wherever else you please — even if it is much farther than Euboea, which those of our people who saw it call the farthest land of all, when they carried fair-haired Rhadamanthus to visit Tityus, the son of Earth. They went there and back without weariness, and finished the journey home on the same day. And you will know it in your own heart: my ships are the finest, and my young men the best, at tossing up the sea with the oar-blade.”

So he spoke, and much-enduring godlike Odysseus was glad, and he prayed aloud and said: “Father Zeus, may Alcinous accomplish all he has said! Then his fame would never be quenched over the grain-giving earth — and I would come to my native land.” So they spoke such things to one another. And white-armed Arete told her handmaids to set out a bedstead under the portico, to lay fine purple blankets on it, spread rugs over them, and put on fleecy cloaks for covering. They went from the hall with torches in their hands; and when they had briskly made up the close-piled bed, they came and roused Odysseus, saying: “Up now, stranger, and come to your rest; your bed is made.” So they spoke, and welcome it seemed to him to lie down and sleep. So much-enduring godlike Odysseus slept there, on the corded bedstead under the echoing portico. And Alcinous lay down in the inner chamber of the high house, and beside him his lady wife made ready his bed and his rest.

## Book VIII

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, the hallowed prince Alcinous rose from his bed, and up rose Zeus-born Odysseus, sacker of cities. The hallowed prince Alcinous led the way to the Phaeacians' assembly-place, built for them beside the ships, and there they sat down on the polished stones, side by side. Meanwhile Pallas Athena went through the city in the likeness of wise Alcinous's herald, devising the homecoming of great-hearted Odysseus, and standing beside each man she said: "Come along, leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians — to the assembly, to hear about the stranger who has newly come to wise Alcinous's house after wandering the sea, a man like the immortals in build." So saying she stirred the spirit and heart of every man, and quickly the seats of the assembly filled with men gathering. And many marveled at the sight of Laertes's wise son; for Athena poured a wondrous grace over his head and shoulders, and made him taller and sturdier to look at, so that he might be welcome to all the Phaeacians, and awesome, and worthy of respect, and might accomplish the many trials with which the Phaeacians would test him.

When they had gathered and were all together, Alcinous addressed them: "Hear me, leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians, while I say what the heart in my breast bids me. This stranger — I do not know who he is — has come wandering to my house, whether from the men of the east or of the west. He presses for his sending, and begs that it be assured. Let us, as always before, speed his convoy; for no one, no one who ever comes to my house, stays here long grieving for want of escort. Come: let us haul a black ship down to the bright sea for her first voyage, and let fifty-two young men be picked from the town, those who have always been the best. Lash the oars well at the benches, then come ashore, and afterward come to my house and set to a quick feast — I will provide well for everyone. That is my order for the young men. And you others, the sceptered kings, come to my fine house, so that we may entertain the stranger in the halls. Let no one refuse. And call the divine singer, Demodocus; for to him beyond all others the god has given song, to give delight wherever his heart moves him to sing."

So saying he led the way, and the sceptered kings followed, while a herald went for the divine singer. And the fifty-two picked youths went, as he ordered, to the shore of the barren sea; and when they had come down to the ship and the sea, they hauled the black ship down into deep water, set the mast and sails in her, fitted the oars in their leather slings, all in due order, and spread the white sails; they moored her well out in the roadstead, and then made their way to wise Alcinous's great house. The porticoes and courts and rooms were filled with men gathering, many of them, young and old. For them Alcinous sacrificed twelve sheep, eight white-tusked pigs, and two shambling oxen; these they flayed and dressed, and made ready a lovely feast.

The herald came near, leading the trusty singer, whom the Muse loved above all men, and gave him both good and evil: she robbed him of his eyes, but gave him sweet song. For him Pontonous set a silver-studded chair in the middle of the feasters, leaning it against a tall pillar; and the herald hung the clear-toned lyre on a peg just above his head, and showed him how to reach it with his hands, and set beside him a basket and a fine table, and a cup of wine to drink whenever his heart bade him. And they reached out their hands to the good things lying ready.

When they had put away their desire for drink and food, the Muse moved the singer to sing the famous deeds of men, from that lay whose fame had then reached wide heaven: the quarrel of Odysseus and Achilles, Peleus's son — how once they contended at a rich feast of the gods with terrible words, and Agamemnon, king of men, was glad at heart that the best of the Achaeans were quarreling; for so Phoebus Apollo had told him in prophecy at sacred Pytho, when he crossed the stone threshold to consult the god — for then the beginning of disaster was rolling toward Trojans and Danaans alike, through the counsels of great Zeus.

This the famous singer sang. But Odysseus took his great purple cloak in his sturdy hands, drew it over his head, and hid his handsome face; for he was ashamed to let the Phaeacians see the tears falling from under his brows. Whenever the divine singer paused, Odysseus would wipe his tears, take the cloak from his head, and pouring from his two-handled cup make libation to the gods; but whenever he began again — and the Phaeacian nobles urged him on, for they delighted in the tale — Odysseus would cover his head again and sob. From all the others he hid his falling tears; only Alcinous marked him and noticed, sitting beside him, and heard his heavy groaning. At once he said to the oar-loving Phaeacians: "Hear me, leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians. We have now had our fill of the shared feast, and of the lyre, companion of the rich banquet. Let us go out and try our hands at every contest, so that the stranger may tell his friends when he is home again how far we surpass other men in boxing and wrestling and jumping and speed of foot."

So saying he led the way, and they followed. The herald hung the clear-toned lyre on its peg, took Demodocus by the hand, and led him from the hall, along the same way the Phaeacian nobles had gone to marvel at the games. They came to the assembly-place, and a great crowd followed, thousands; and many fine young men stood up. Up rose Acroneus and Ocyalus and Elatreus, Nauteus and Prymneus and Anchialus and Eretmeus, Ponteus and Proreus, Thoon and Anabesineus, and Amphialus, son of Polyneus, Tecton's son; up rose too Euryalus, a match for man-destroying Ares, the son of Naubolus, the finest in face and build of all the Phaeacians after blameless Laodamas. And up stood the three sons of blameless Alcinous: Laodamas, Halius, and godlike Clytoneus.

First they tried the footrace. A course was stretched for them from the turning-post, and they all flew off together at speed, raising the dust of the plain; and blameless Clytoneus was far the best at running — by as much as a team of mules gains on oxen in fallow land, by so much he outran the others back to the crowd, and left them behind. Then they tried hard wrestling, and Euryalus outdid all the best men; at the jump Amphialus was first of all; at the discus Elatreus was far the strongest; and at boxing, Laodamas, Alcinous's good son.

When they had all pleased their hearts with the games, Laodamas, son of Alcinous, spoke among them: "Come, friends, let us ask the stranger whether he knows and has learned any sport. He is not ill-built, surely — those thighs, those calves, both arms above, the massive neck, the great strength. He does not lack youth's vigor either; he has only been broken by many hardships. For I say there is nothing worse than the sea for crushing a man, however strong he may be." Euryalus answered him: "Laodamas, well and rightly spoken. Now go yourself, call him out, and say your word." When Alcinous's good son heard this, he went and stood in the middle and said to Odysseus: "Come, stranger father, you too — try the games, if you have skill in any. And it is fitting you should know sport: for there is no greater fame for a man, as long as he lives, than what he achieves with his own hands and feet. Come, try — and scatter the cares from your heart. Your journey will not be delayed much longer; the ship is already launched, and the crew stands ready."

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: "Laodamas, why do you all press me with this mockery? Cares fill my heart, not games. I have suffered much before now and labored much; and now I sit in your assembly hungry for home, a suppliant to your king and all the people." Then Euryalus answered and taunted him to his face: "No, stranger — I would never take you for a man skilled in games, of the kind men everywhere practice. More like some master of sailors and traders, who plies to and fro in his many-benched ship, a man mindful of his cargo, with an eye on his freight and his grasping profits. You do not look like an athlete."

Looking at him from under his brows, resourceful Odysseus said: "Stranger, that was not well said; you seem an insolent man. So true it is that the gods do not give their graces all together to all men — not build and brains and eloquence alike. One man is slighter in looks, but a god crowns his shape with words, and men look on him delighted as he speaks steadily on with winning modesty, conspicuous through the gathering, and when he walks the city they gaze at him like a god. Another again is like the immortals in looks, but no grace is set as a crown upon his words. So with you: your looks are striking — a god could not better them — but your mind is empty. You have stirred the heart in my breast with your unmannerly talk. I am no novice at games, whatever you say; I counted among the first, I think, while I could trust my youth and these hands. Now I am held fast by misery and pain, for I have endured much, cutting my way through wars of men and the grinding waves. But even so, after all my sufferings, I will try your games. Your word bit deep; you have provoked me with your speech."

He sprang up, cloak and all, and seized a discus — a bigger one, thick, more than a little heavier than those the Phaeacians used to throw among themselves. He spun and let it fly from his sturdy hand, and the stone hummed; the Phaeacians of the long oars, those famous seamen, ducked to the ground under the rush of the stone. It flew past the marks of all the rest, running lightly from his hand; and Athena, in the shape of a man, set the mark and spoke to him by name: "Even a blind man, stranger, could pick out your mark by feeling for it — it is not mixed in with the crowd, but far in front of them all. Take heart, for this contest at least: no Phaeacian will reach it, let alone pass it." So she spoke, and much-enduring godlike Odysseus was glad, rejoicing to find one true friend in the gathering. Then with a lighter heart he said to the Phaeacians: "Reach that now, young men — and soon, I think, I will send another after it, as far or farther. As for the rest of you — whoever's heart and spirit bids him, step up and be tried, since you have thoroughly angered me: boxing, wrestling, running, I refuse none of it, with any of the Phaeacians — except Laodamas himself. He is my host: who would fight the man who befriends him? Only a fool or a nobody challenges his own host at games in a foreign land — he cuts away all his own prospects. But of the others I refuse no one and think light of no one; I want to know them, and try them face to face. I am not bad at any sport men practice. I know well how to handle a polished bow: I would be first to strike a man with my arrow in the enemy press, however many companions stood beside me shooting. Only Philoctetes surpassed me with the bow in the land of the Trojans, whenever we Achaeans shot; of all the others I claim to be far the best, of mortals now alive who eat bread on the earth. With men of the past I will not set myself to strive — with Heracles, or Eurytus of Oechalia, who strove with the very immortals at archery. That is why great Eurytus died before his time, and no old age came upon him in his halls: Apollo was angered and killed him, because Eurytus had challenged him to shoot. And I throw the spear farther than any other man an arrow. Only at running I fear one of you might outstrip me: I was shamefully mauled among all those waves, since there was no steady keeping aboard my ship, and my knees have gone slack."

So he spoke, and they were all hushed in silence. Only Alcinous answered him: "Stranger, since nothing you say among us is said ungraciously — you simply want to show the prowess that attends you, angry that this man stood up in the gathering and taunted you, in a way no mortal would fault your prowess who knew in his heart how to speak sense — come now, mark my word, so that you may tell it to another hero someday, when you feast in your own halls beside your wife and children and remember our prowess: what feats Zeus has laid on us, continuously, from our fathers' days till now. We are not flawless boxers, nor wrestlers; but we run swiftly on our feet, and with our ships we are the best; and always dear to us are the feast, and the lyre, and dances, and changes of clothing, and warm baths, and bed. Come then, all you best dancers of the Phaeacians, dance — so that the stranger may tell his friends when he is home how far we surpass others in seamanship and speed of foot, in dance and song. And let someone go quickly and fetch Demodocus the clear-toned lyre, which lies somewhere in our halls."

So spoke godlike Alcinous, and a herald rose to fetch the hollow lyre from the king's house. And nine chosen stewards stood up, public officials who managed everything at the games: they leveled the dancing-floor and cleared a fine wide ring. The herald came near, bringing Demodocus the clear-toned lyre; and he went into the middle, and around him stood boys in their first youth, skilled in the dance, and they beat the divine dancing-floor with their feet. And Odysseus gazed at the flashing of their feet, and marveled in his heart.

Then, playing the lyre, the singer struck up a lovely song about the love of Ares and fair-crowned Aphrodite: how first they lay together, secretly, in the house of Hephaestus; and Ares gave many gifts, and shamed the bed of lord Hephaestus. But straightaway a messenger came to Hephaestus — Helios, the Sun, who had seen the two of them lying in love. When Hephaestus heard the heart-stinging tale, he went to his forge brooding evil deep in his heart, and set the great anvil on the block, and hammered out bonds that could not break or loosen, to hold them fast where they were. And when, in his fury at Ares, he had wrought his snare, he went to the chamber where his dear bed stood, and around the bedposts everywhere he spun the bonds in a circle, and many hung down from above, from the roof-beam — fine as spider's webs, which no one could so much as see, not even the blessed gods: so cunningly were they made. And when he had spun all his snare about the bed, he made as if to go to Lemnos, that well-built citadel, dearest of all lands to him. And Ares of the golden reins kept no blind watch: he saw Hephaestus the famous craftsman going, and went to the great god's house, hungry for the love of fair-crowned Cytherea. She was newly come from her father, the mighty son of Cronus, and had just sat down; and Ares came inside, and clamped his hand on hers, and spoke: "Come, my dear, to bed; let us take our pleasure. Hephaestus is no longer in the country — gone already, I think, to Lemnos, to the wild-tongued Sintians."

So he spoke, and to her it seemed a welcome thing to lie down. The two of them went to the bed and lay down; and around them poured the cunning bonds of shrewd Hephaestus, and there was no moving a limb, no raising themselves. Then indeed they understood: there was no more escaping. And the famous strong-armed god came near — for Helios had kept watch for him and told him the tale — and he turned back before he ever reached Lemnos, and came home with a grieving heart, and stood in the doorway, and wild anger took him. He gave a terrible shout, calling to all the gods: "Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live forever, come see a laughable business — and past all bearing: how Aphrodite, Zeus's daughter, forever slights me because I am lame, and gives her love to destroying Ares, because he is handsome and sound of limb, while I was born crippled. And for that no one else is to blame but my two parents — I wish they had never begotten me! But look where the two of them have climbed into my bed and sleep in love, while I look on in misery. I do not think they will care to lie so much longer, however great their love; soon enough neither will want to sleep. But the snare and the bond will hold them, until her father pays me back every one of the bridal gifts I handed over for his shameless daughter. His daughter is beautiful — but without self-command."

So he spoke, and the gods gathered at the house with its bronze floor: Poseidon came, the earth-holder; Hermes the helper came; lord Apollo the far-worker came. The goddesses stayed at home, each one, for modesty. There in the doorway stood the gods, the givers of good things, and unquenchable laughter rose among the blessed gods as they gazed at the arts of shrewd Hephaestus. And glancing at his neighbor a god would say: "Evil deeds do not prosper. The slow overtakes the swift — look how Hephaestus, slow as he is, has caught Ares, swiftest of the gods who hold Olympus: lame, but by craft. Now the adulterer's fine is owed." So they spoke to one another. But lord Apollo, son of Zeus, said to Hermes: "Hermes, son of Zeus, guide, giver of good things — would you be willing, pinned in strong bonds, to sleep in that bed beside golden Aphrodite?" And the guide, the slayer of Argus, answered: "If only it might be so, lord far-shooting Apollo! Let three times as many bonds pin me, bonds without end, and all you gods look on, and all the goddesses too — I would still sleep beside golden Aphrodite." So he spoke, and laughter rose among the immortal gods. But laughter did not take Poseidon: he kept begging Hephaestus, the famous smith, to set Ares free, and spoke winged words: "Release him. I promise you he will pay whatever is right, as you ask, before all the immortal gods." The famous strong-armed god answered him: "Do not ask this of me, Poseidon, earth-holder. Wretched are the pledges pledged for wretches. How could I hold you bound before the immortal gods, if Ares slipped away, free of both debt and bond?" And Poseidon the Earthshaker answered: "Hephaestus, if Ares dodges the debt and escapes, I will pay it you myself." Then the famous strong-armed god said: "It cannot be, and it would not be right, to refuse your word." With that the strength of Hephaestus loosed the bond; and the two of them, freed from that bond, strong as it was, sprang up at once, and he went off to Thrace, while she, laughter-loving Aphrodite, came to Paphos in Cyprus, where her precinct is, and her altar fragrant with incense. There the Graces bathed her and anointed her with immortal oil, such as blooms upon the gods who live forever, and dressed her in lovely clothing, a wonder to behold.

This the famous singer sang; and Odysseus delighted in his heart as he listened, and so did the others, the Phaeacians of the long oars, those famous seamen. Then Alcinous ordered Halius and Laodamas to dance alone, since no one could rival them. They took in their hands the beautiful purple ball that skilled Polybus had made them: one would bend back and throw it up toward the shadowy clouds, and the other, springing high off the ground, would catch it easily before his feet touched earth again. And when they had tried their skill with the ball thrown straight up, the two danced on the nourishing earth, passing it rapidly back and forth, while the other youths stood in the ring beating time, and a great din rose up. Then godlike Odysseus said to Alcinous: "Lord Alcinous, most renowned of all your people — you promised your dancers were the best, and here is the proof. Wonder takes me as I look."

So he spoke, and the hallowed prince Alcinous was glad, and at once said to the oar-loving Phaeacians: "Hear me, leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians: this stranger seems to me a man of excellent sense. Come, let us give him a guest-gift, as is fitting. Twelve distinguished kings hold rule in this land, and I myself am the thirteenth: let each of you bring a fresh-washed mantle and a tunic and a talent of precious gold. And let us bring it all at once, so that the stranger may take it in his hands and go in to supper glad at heart. And let Euryalus make his peace with the man himself, with words and with a gift, since what he said was not said rightly." So he spoke, and they all approved and gave the order, and each sent his herald to fetch the gifts. And Euryalus answered him: "Lord Alcinous, most renowned of all your people, I will indeed make my peace with the stranger, as you bid. I will give him this sword, all of bronze, with a silver hilt, and a scabbard of new-sawn ivory turned about it; it will be worth much to him." So saying he put the silver-studded sword into Odysseus's hands, and spoke winged words: "Hail, stranger father. If any harsh word was blurted out, may the storm-winds snatch it up and carry it away; and may the gods grant you to see your wife again and come to your own country, since you have suffered sorrows so long, far from your own people." Resourceful Odysseus answered him: "And to you, friend, a hearty welcome; may the gods give you happiness; and may you never miss this sword you have given me, with the words of peace to match." So saying, he slung the silver-studded sword about his shoulders.

The sun set, and the glorious gifts were there before him: the lordly heralds carried them to Alcinous's house, and the sons of blameless Alcinous received the beautiful gifts and set them beside their honored mother. The hallowed prince Alcinous led the way in, and they came and sat down on the high seats; and then the princely Alcinous said to Arete: "Bring here a splendid chest, wife, the best we have, and lay in it yourself a fresh-washed mantle and a tunic; and heat a bronze cauldron over the fire and warm water, so that the stranger, having bathed, and having seen all the gifts of the blameless Phaeacians well stowed, may take pleasure in the feast and in hearing the singer's lay. And I will give him this beautiful cup of mine, of gold, so that he may remember me all his days as he pours libation in his hall to Zeus and to the other gods."

So he spoke, and Arete told her serving-women to set a great three-legged cauldron on the fire at once. They set the bath-cauldron on the blazing fire, poured in water, and kindled the wood beneath; the fire lapped the belly of the cauldron, and the water warmed. Meanwhile Arete brought out from the chamber a beautiful chest for the stranger, and laid in it the fine gifts, the clothing and the gold the Phaeacians had given; and she herself added a mantle and a fine tunic, and spoke winged words to him: "See to the lid yourself now, and quickly tie a knot upon it, in case someone robs you on the voyage, when later you sleep your sweet sleep aboard the black ship." When much-enduring godlike Odysseus heard this, at once he fitted the lid and cast a swift, intricate knot upon it, which queenly Circe had once taught him. And directly the housekeeper bade him go to the tub and bathe; and he saw the hot bath with a glad heart, for comfort had not been common with him since he left the house of fair-haired Calypso — though while there, he had been cared for continually, like a god. When the serving-women had bathed him and rubbed him with oil, and put a fine cloak and tunic about him, he stepped from the bath and went to join the men at their wine.

And Nausicaa, with her beauty from the gods, stood by the doorpost of the strong-built hall, and marveled at Odysseus as her eyes took him in, and she spoke winged words to him: "Farewell, stranger. When you are in your own country, remember me sometimes — for to me first you owe the saving of your life." Resourceful Odysseus answered her: "Nausicaa, daughter of great-hearted Alcinous — may Zeus grant it so, the loud-thundering husband of Hera, that I come home and see my day of returning; then there, too, I would pray to you as to a god, all my days forever. For you gave me my life, girl."

He spoke, and went to sit on a chair beside King Alcinous. They were already serving the portions and mixing the wine; and the herald came near, leading the trusty singer Demodocus, honored among the people, and seated him in the middle of the feasters, leaning his chair against a tall pillar. Then resourceful Odysseus called the herald — cutting off a portion of the chine of a white-tusked pig, with rich fat on either side, and more was left over: "Herald, take this portion of meat to Demodocus, that he may eat; and I will greet him warmly, grieved though I am. For among all men on the earth singers have their share of honor and reverence, since the Muse has taught them the pathways of song, and loves the tribe of singers." So he spoke, and the herald carried it over and placed it in the hands of the hero Demodocus; and he received it, and was glad at heart. And they reached out their hands to the good things lying ready.

When they had put away their desire for drink and food, then resourceful Odysseus said to Demodocus: "Demodocus, I praise you beyond all mortal men. Either the Muse taught you, Zeus's daughter, or Apollo; for all too rightly you sing the fate of the Achaeans — all they did and suffered, all their labors — as if you had been there yourself, or heard it from one who was. But come now, change your theme: sing the building of the wooden horse, which Epeius made with Athena's help, and which godlike Odysseus brought by cunning into the citadel, filled with the men who sacked Ilium. If you tell me that tale rightly, in its due order, then I will proclaim at once to all mankind that a gracious god has granted you divine song."

So he spoke; and the singer, stirred by the god, began, and made his song appear, taking it up where the Argives had set fire to their huts, boarded their well-benched ships, and were sailing away — while the others already sat in the Trojans' meeting-place, hidden in the horse beside famous Odysseus; for the Trojans themselves had dragged it up into their citadel. So it stood there, while the people sat around it talking endlessly, and could not decide; three counsels found favor among them: to split the hollow timber with the pitiless bronze; or to drag it to the height and hurl it down the rocks; or to let it stand, a great offering to appease the gods — which was the way it was fated to end. For it was their destiny to perish when the city took within its walls the great wooden horse, where all the best of the Argives sat, bringing death and doom to the Trojans. And he sang how the sons of the Achaeans poured out of the horse, leaving their hollow ambush, and sacked the city; he sang how, scattered this way and that, they ravaged the steep town, and how Odysseus went like Ares to the house of Deiphobus with godlike Menelaus. There, he sang, Odysseus dared the grimmest fighting of all — and won in the end, through great-hearted Athena.

This the famous singer sang. But Odysseus melted; the tears from under his eyelids wetted his cheeks. As a woman wails, falling on the body of her dear husband, who has fallen before his city and his people, warding off the pitiless day from his town and his children — she sees him dying, gasping his last, and flinging herself around him she shrieks aloud, while the enemy behind her beat her back and shoulders with their spear-butts and lead her off into slavery, to labor and misery, and her cheeks waste with the most pitiful of griefs — so Odysseus let pitiful tears fall from under his brows. From all the others he hid his falling tears; only Alcinous marked him and noticed, sitting beside him, and heard his heavy groaning. At once he said to the oar-loving Phaeacians: "Hear me, leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians — and let Demodocus now still his clear-toned lyre; for what he sings pleases not everyone alike. Since we began supper and the divine singer rose, our guest has not once ceased from bitter grieving; great sorrow, surelyly, has closed round his heart. Let the singer stop, so that we may all take pleasure together, hosts and guest alike; that is far the finer way. For it is for our honored guest's sake that all this has been made ready — the convoy, and the friendly gifts we give him out of love. To any man with even a little right feeling, a stranger and suppliant is as good as a brother.

"And so, do not you, either, hide behind shrewd evasions what I am about to ask; it is finer to speak out. Tell me the name you are called at home by your mother and father, and the others in your city and round about. For no man is wholly nameless, base or noble, once he has been born: parents lay names on all their children when they bear them. And tell me your land, your people, your city, so that our ships may carry you there, plotting the course in their minds. For the Phaeacians have no steersmen, no steering-oars such as other ships carry: the ships themselves know the thoughts and minds of men, and know all men's cities and rich fields, and swiftest of all things they cross the gulf of the salt sea, wrapped in mist and cloud; and they have no fear, ever, of damage or of wreck. Yet this I once heard my father Nausithous say: he said Poseidon was jealous of us, because we give safe convoy to all comers; and he said that one day, as a well-made Phaeacian ship came home over the misty sea from such a convoy, the god would smite her, and would fold a great mountain round our city. So the old man said. And these things the god may accomplish, or may leave undone, as pleases his heart. But come, tell me this, and tell it truly: where have you wandered, and to what countries of men have you come? Tell me of the peoples and their well-built cities — those who are harsh and savage and without justice, and those who are kind to strangers, with a god-fearing mind. And tell me why you weep and grieve at heart when you hear the fate of the Argive Danaans and of Ilium. That the gods wrought; they spun destruction for those men, so that there might be a song for those yet to be born. Did some kinsman of yours die before Ilium — a good man, a son-in-law or a wife's father, those nearest to us after our own blood and stock? Or perhaps a companion, a fine man, one who knew your heart? For a companion with an understanding heart is no whit less than a brother."

## Book IX

Then resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Lord Alcinous, most renowned of all your people, truly it is a fine thing to listen to a singer such as this man is, like the gods in voice. For I myself say there is no fulfillment more delightful than when good cheer holds a whole people, and feasters up and down the halls sit in rows listening to a singer, while beside them the tables are laden with bread and meat, and the cupbearer draws wine from the mixing-bowl and carries it round and pours it into the cups. This, to my mind, is the loveliest thing there is.

But your heart has turned to asking about my grievous sorrows, so that I may weep and groan still more. What shall I tell first, then, and what last? For the gods of heaven have given me sorrows in plenty. Now first I will tell you my name, so that you too may know it, and so that afterward, if I escape the pitiless day, I may be your guest-friend, though my home is far away. I am Odysseus, son of Laertes, known among all men for my cunning schemes, and my fame reaches heaven. I live in clear-skied Ithaca. On it is a mountain, Neriton, covered with quivering leaves, conspicuous from afar; and around it lie many islands close to one another, Doulichion and Same and wooded Zacynthus. Ithaca itself lies low, farthest out in the sea toward the dark of evening, while the others lie apart, toward the dawn and the sun — a rough land, but a good nurse of young men. For my part, I can see nothing sweeter than one's own country.

Calypso, shining among goddesses, kept me there with her in her hollow caves, wanting me for her husband; and in the same way Circe of Aeaea, that crafty one, held me back in her halls, wanting me for her husband; but they never persuaded the heart in my breast. So true it is that nothing is sweeter than a man's own country and his own parents, even if he lives in a rich house far away, in a foreign land, apart from his parents. But come, let me tell you of my journey home and all its sorrows, which Zeus laid upon me as I came from Troy.

From Ilium the wind carried me to the Cicones, to Ismarus. There I sacked the city and destroyed the men; and taking their wives and many possessions out of the city, we divided them among us, so that no one, as far as I could help it, should go cheated of an equal share. Then indeed I urged that we flee on quick feet, but the men, in their great folly, did not listen. There much wine was drunk, and they slaughtered many sheep along the shore, and shambling, crooked-horned cattle. Meanwhile the Cicones who had got away went calling to other Cicones, their neighbors, more numerous and braver men, who lived inland and knew how to fight men from chariots, and, where need be, on foot. They came then, as many as the leaves and flowers that come in their season, in the early morning; and then an evil fate from Zeus stood beside us, doomed as we were, so that we should suffer many pains. They formed up and fought a battle beside the swift ships, and the two sides cast at each other with bronze-tipped spears. As long as it was morning and the sacred day was growing, so long we stood our ground and beat them off, though they were more; but when the sun turned toward the hour of unyoking oxen, then the Cicones broke the Achaeans and overwhelmed them. Six well-greaved companions from each ship were killed; the rest of us escaped death and doom.

From there we sailed on, grieving at heart, glad to have escaped death, though we had lost our dear companions. But I did not let my curved ships go on until we had called three times on each of those poor companions who died on the plain, cut down by the Cicones. Then Zeus who gathers the clouds stirred up the North Wind against our ships in a tremendous storm, and covered land and sea alike with clouds; and night rushed down from heaven. The ships were driven along sideways, and the force of the wind tore their sails into three and four pieces. We lowered the sails into the ships, fearing destruction, and rowed the ships hurriedly toward land. There for two nights and two days on end we lay, eating our hearts out with weariness and grief together. But when fair-haired Dawn brought the third day, we set up the masts, hoisted the white sails, and took our seats, and the wind and the helmsmen steered the ships. And now I would have come home unscathed to my own native land, but as I was rounding Malea the waves and the current and the North Wind pushed me off course, and drove me wandering past Cythera.

From there for nine days I was carried by deadly winds over the teeming sea; on the tenth we set foot on the land of the Lotus-eaters, who eat a flowery food. There we went ashore and drew water, and my companions quickly took their meal beside the swift ships. When we had tasted food and drink, I sent companions ahead to go and learn what men lived in that land, eaters of bread — choosing two men, and sending a third with them as herald. They went off at once and mingled with the Lotus-eaters; and the Lotus-eaters did not plot destruction for our companions, but gave them lotus to taste. Whoever of them ate the honey-sweet fruit of the lotus no longer wished to bring back word or to return, but wanted to stay there among the Lotus-eaters, feeding on lotus, and to forget his homecoming. These men I brought back to the ships by force, weeping, and dragged them into the hollow ships and bound them under the benches; then I ordered the rest of my loyal companions to hurry and board the swift ships, so that no one else might eat of the lotus and forget his homecoming. They came aboard at once and sat down at the oarlocks, and sitting in order they struck the gray sea with their oars.

From there we sailed on, grieving at heart, and came to the land of the Cyclopes, an overbearing and lawless people, who put their trust in the immortal gods and never plant anything with their hands or plow; everything grows for them unsown and unplowed — wheat, and barley, and vines that bear wine from heavy clusters, and the rain of Zeus makes it grow for them. They have no assemblies for taking counsel and no laws; they live on the crests of high mountains, in hollow caves, and each one lays down the law for his own children and wives, and they care nothing for one another.

Now a fertile island stretches across outside the harbor, neither close to the land of the Cyclopes nor far off, a wooded island where wild goats breed beyond number; for no coming and going of men keeps them away, nor do hunters range over it, men who suffer hardships in the forest as they follow the crests of the mountains. It is not taken up with flocks or plowed fields either; unsown and unplowed all its days, empty of men, it feeds only the bleating goats. For the Cyclopes have no red-cheeked ships, and no shipwrights among them to build well-benched ships that could serve their every need, sailing to the cities of other men, as men so often cross the sea in ships to one another — men who would have made the island, too, a well-built settlement. For it is not a poor island at all; it would bear everything in season. There are meadows along the shores of the gray sea, soft and well watered; vines there would never fail. There is level plowland, and they could reap a deep harvest at every season, for the soil beneath is very rich. And there is a harbor with good anchorage, where there is no need of moorings — no need to throw out anchor-stones or make fast stern cables; you can simply beach your ship and wait until the sailors' hearts stir them and the winds blow fair. And at the head of the harbor bright water flows, a spring beneath a cave, with poplars growing all around.

There we sailed in, and some god guided us through the murky night, for there was nothing to be seen: a thick mist lay around the ships, and the moon showed no light from heaven, but was shut in by clouds. So no one's eyes made out the island, nor did we see the long waves rolling toward the shore, before our well-benched ships ran aground. When the ships were beached we took down all the sails and stepped out ourselves onto the surf-line of the sea; and there we fell asleep and waited for shining Dawn.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, we wandered through the island, marveling at it. And the nymphs, daughters of Zeus who bears the aegis, started the mountain goats, so that my companions might have their meal. At once we took our curved bows and long-socketed goat-spears from the ships, and dividing into three bands we began to shoot; and the god quickly gave us game to our hearts' content. Twelve ships followed me, and to each ship nine goats fell by lot, while for me alone they picked out ten. So then all day long until the setting of the sun we sat feasting on endless meat and sweet wine; for the red wine in the ships was not yet spent — there was still some left, since each crew had drawn off a great store in jars when we took the sacred citadel of the Cicones. And we looked across at the land of the Cyclopes, who were near, and saw their smoke, and heard the sound of their voices and of their sheep and goats. When the sun set and darkness came on, we lay down to sleep on the surf-line of the sea.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, I called an assembly and spoke among them all: “The rest of you stay here now, my loyal companions, while I with my own ship and my own crew go and find out about these men, who they are — whether they are violent, savage, and lawless, or hospitable to strangers, with minds that fear the gods.” So speaking I went aboard my ship, and ordered my companions to come aboard themselves and cast off the stern cables. They came aboard at once and sat down at the oarlocks, and sitting in order they struck the gray sea with their oars.

When we reached the place, which was not far, there on the edge of the land we saw a cave close to the sea, high, overhung with laurels. There many flocks, sheep and goats alike, were penned for the night; and around it a high courtyard was built of stones sunk deep in the earth, and of tall pines and high-crowned oaks. There a monstrous man had his sleeping-place, who pastured his flocks alone and far apart; he did not go about among the others, but kept apart, and his heart knew no law. For he was built as a monstrous wonder, not like a man who eats bread, but like a wooded peak of the high mountains that stands out alone, apart from the rest.

Then I ordered the rest of my loyal companions to stay there by the ship and guard it, while I picked the twelve best of my companions and set out. I took with me a goatskin of dark, sweet wine, which Maron had given me — the son of Euanthes, priest of Apollo, the god who watches over Ismarus — because we had protected him, with his child and his wife, out of reverence; for he lived in the wooded grove of Phoebus Apollo. He gave me splendid gifts: he gave me seven talents of well-wrought gold, and he gave me a mixing-bowl all of silver, and then wine, drawing it off into twelve jars in all, sweet and unmixed, a drink for the gods. Not one of the slaves or serving-women in his house knew of it — only himself and his dear wife, and one housekeeper alone. Whenever they drank that honey-sweet red wine, he would fill one cup and pour it into twenty measures of water, and a sweet fragrance, a marvel, would rise from the mixing-bowl; then, truly, holding back would have been no pleasure. Of this wine I filled a great skin and brought it along, and provisions too in a leather sack; for my proud spirit had a sudden foreboding that a man would come against me clothed in great strength, a savage, knowing nothing of justice or law.

We came quickly to the cave, but did not find him inside; he was pasturing his fat flocks in the fields. Going into the cave, we gazed in wonder at everything: crates loaded down with cheeses, and pens crowded with lambs and kids, each sort shut in separately — the firstborn apart, the middle ones apart, and the newborns apart again. Every vessel swam with whey, the pails and the bowls, all well made, into which he milked. Then my companions begged me, first of all, to take some of the cheeses and go back, and then to drive the kids and lambs quickly out of their pens down to the swift ship, and sail away over the salt water. But I would not listen — though it would have been far better — because I wanted to see the man himself, and whether he would give me guest-gifts. As it turned out, his appearing was to be no joy to my companions.

There we lit a fire and made an offering, and took some of the cheeses ourselves and ate, and sat inside waiting for him, until he came back from his herding. He carried a huge load of dry wood, to serve him at supper-time, and flung it down inside the cave with a crash; and in terror we shrank back into the innermost corner of the cave. Then he drove his fat flocks into the wide cave — all the ones he milked — leaving the males, the rams and the he-goats, outside in the deep courtyard. Then he lifted high a great door-stone and set it in place, a mighty thing; twenty-two good four-wheeled wagons could not have pried it from the ground — such a towering mass of rock he set in the doorway. Then he sat and milked the ewes and bleating goats, all in due order, and put her young under each. At once he curdled half the white milk, gathered it, and stored it in woven baskets, and the other half he left standing in vessels, so that he might have it to take and drink, and it might serve him at supper. When he had busied himself over his tasks and finished them, he lit the fire, and caught sight of us, and asked: “Strangers, who are you? Where do you sail from, over the watery ways? Is it on some business, or do you rove at random over the sea like pirates, who wander risking their lives and bringing harm to men of other lands?”

So he spoke, and our hearts broke within us, terrified by his deep voice and by the monster himself. But even so I answered him and said: “We are Achaeans driven off course from Troy by all manner of winds, over the great gulf of the sea. Making for home, we came by another way, on other paths; so, I suppose, Zeus chose to devise it. We claim to be the men of Agamemnon, son of Atreus, whose fame is now the greatest under heaven, so great a city did he sack, so many men did he destroy. And we, for our part, have come to your knees as suppliants, in hopes that you might offer us hospitality, or even give us some gift besides, as is the right of strangers. Respect the gods, mighty one. We are your suppliants, and Zeus is the avenger of suppliants and strangers — Zeus the god of guests, who walks beside strangers who deserve respect.”

So I spoke, and he answered me at once with a pitiless heart: “You are a fool, stranger, or you have come from far away, telling me to fear the gods or shun their anger. The Cyclopes care nothing for Zeus who bears the aegis, nor for the blessed gods, since we are far stronger than they. I would not spare you or your companions to avoid the enmity of Zeus — not unless my own heart bade me. But tell me where you moored your well-built ship when you came — at the far end of the shore, or near? — so that I may know.” So he spoke, testing me; but he did not catch me — I knew too much — and I answered him back with crafty words: “Poseidon the earth-shaker smashed my ship to pieces: he drove it against the rocks at the border of your land, bringing it close to the headland, and the wind carried it in from the sea. But I, with these men here, escaped sheer destruction.”

So I spoke, and out of his pitiless heart he answered nothing, but sprang up and laid hands on my companions. He snatched two at once and dashed them against the ground like puppies; their brains ran out onto the earth and soaked the ground. He tore them limb from limb and made his supper of them, and ate like a mountain-bred lion, leaving nothing — entrails and flesh and marrowy bones. And we wept and lifted up our hands to Zeus at the sight of such monstrous work, and helplessness gripped our hearts. When the Cyclops had filled his great belly, eating human flesh and drinking unmixed milk on top of it, he lay down inside the cave, stretched out among his flocks. Then I made a plan in my great-hearted spirit: to go up close, draw my sharp sword from beside my thigh, and stab him in the chest, where the midriff holds the liver, feeling for the place with my hand. But a second thought held me back: there in the cave we too would have perished in sheer destruction, for we could never have pushed back with our hands the mighty stone he had set in the high doorway. So then, groaning, we waited for shining Dawn.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, he lit his fire again and milked his fine flocks, all in due order, and put her young under each. When he had busied himself over his tasks and finished them, again he snatched two men at once and made his meal of them. And when he had eaten, he drove his fat flocks out of the cave, lifting away the great door-stone with ease; then he set it back again, as a man might set the lid on a quiver. With loud whistling the Cyclops turned his fat flocks toward the mountain; and I was left behind, brooding on evil in the depths of my heart, in case I might somehow take vengeance, and Athena grant me that glory.

And this was the plan that seemed best to my mind. Beside one of the pens lay the Cyclops's great club, of green olive wood; he had cut it to carry once it dried. Looking at it, we judged it as big as the mast of a black ship of twenty oars, a broad merchantman that crosses the great gulf — so long, so thick it looked. Standing over it, I cut off about a fathom's length, and set it beside my companions and told them to smooth it down. They made it even, and I stood by and sharpened it at the tip, and then took it at once and hardened it in the blazing fire. Then I laid it carefully away, hiding it under the dung that lay in great heaps through the cave. And I told the others to cast lots for who would dare to lift the stake with me and grind it into his eye when sweet sleep came over him. The lot fell on the very men I would have chosen myself, four of them, and I counted myself in with them as the fifth.

In the evening he came back, herding his fine-fleeced flocks; and at once he drove the fat flocks into the wide cave, every one, and left none outside in the deep courtyard — whether from some foreboding, or because a god so ordered it. Then he lifted high the great door-stone and set it in place, and sat and milked the ewes and bleating goats, all in due order, and put her young under each. When he had busied himself over his tasks and finished them, again he snatched two men at once and made his supper of them.

Then I went up close to the Cyclops and spoke to him, holding in my hands an ivy-wood bowl of dark wine: “Here, Cyclops, drink wine, now that you have eaten human flesh, so that you may know what kind of drink our ship carried. I was bringing it to you as a libation, in hopes that you might take pity on me and send me home; but your fury is past all bearing. Hard man, how will any other of the many men there are ever come to you again, when you act with such lawlessness?” So I spoke, and he took it and drank it off, and he was mightily pleased with the sweet drink, and asked me for a second cup: “Give me more, and gladly, and tell me your name, here and now, so that I may give you a guest-gift to make you glad. The grain-giving earth bears wine from heavy clusters for the Cyclopes too, and the rain of Zeus makes it grow for them; but this — this is a stream of ambrosia and nectar.” So he spoke, and I handed him the gleaming wine again. Three times I brought it and gave it, and three times he drained it in his folly. And when the wine had gone round the Cyclops's wits, then I spoke to him with honeyed words: “Cyclops, you ask my famous name, and I will tell it to you; and you give me my guest-gift, just as you promised. Nobody is my name; Nobody is what I am called by my mother and my father and all my companions.” So I spoke, and he answered me at once with a pitiless heart: “Nobody I will eat last among his companions, the others before him — that shall be my guest-gift to you.”

And with that he sank back and fell flat, and lay with his thick neck bent to one side, and sleep that conquers all took hold of him; and from his gullet gushed wine and gobbets of human flesh, as he vomited in his drunkenness. Then I drove the stake under the deep bed of embers to heat it, and heartened all my companions with my words, so that no one might flinch and hang back on me. When the olive stake, green though it was, was on the point of catching fire, and glowed through terribly, then I brought it near out of the fire, and my companions stood around me; and some god breathed great courage into us. They took the olive stake, sharp at the tip, and drove it into his eye, while I threw my weight on it from above and spun it — as when a man bores a ship's timber with a drill, and the men below keep it spinning with a strap they hold at either end, and the drill runs on without stopping. So we took the fire-pointed stake and spun it in his eye, and the blood flowed hot around it. The blast of the burning eyeball singed all his lids and brows, and the roots of the eye crackled in the flame. As when a smith dips a great axe or an adze in cold water to temper it, and it hisses loudly — for that is what gives iron its strength — so his eye hissed around the olive stake.

He gave a great and terrible scream, and the rock rang with it, and we shrank back in terror. He wrenched the stake from his eye, all fouled with streaming blood, and flung it from him with frantic hands, and shouted loudly for the Cyclopes who lived around him in caves along the windy heights. Hearing his cries they came crowding from every side, and standing around the cave they asked what troubled him: “What ails you so, Polyphemus, that you cry out like this through the immortal night and keep us from sleep? Surely no mortal is driving off your flocks against your will? Surely no one is killing you by trickery or by force?” And out of the cave mighty Polyphemus answered them: “Friends, Nobody is killing me — by trickery, not by force.” And they answered him with winged words: “Well, if nobody is doing you violence, and you are alone, there is no escaping the sickness that comes from great Zeus; pray instead to our father, lord Poseidon.”

So they spoke as they went away, and my heart laughed within me at how my name and my flawless cunning had tricked him. But the Cyclops, groaning and racked with agonies, groped with his hands and lifted the stone from the doorway, and sat himself in the entrance with both arms spread wide, hoping to catch anyone who tried to slip out the door among the sheep — for so foolish, I suppose, he hoped in his heart I would be. And I set to planning how it might all turn out for the best, if I could find some deliverance from death for my companions and for myself. I wove every kind of trick and scheme, as a man will when his life is at stake; for great evil was very near.

And this was the plan that seemed best to my mind. There were rams there, well fed and thick-fleeced, fine big animals, with wool dark as violet. Quietly I bound them together with the twisted withies on which the Cyclops slept, that lawless monster — three at a time. The middle one of each three carried a man, while the two on either side went along keeping my companions safe. So three sheep carried each man. As for me — there was a ram, far the best of the whole flock — I took hold of his back and curled myself under his shaggy belly, and lay there, gripping the marvelous fleece with my hands, twisted round beneath him, holding on with a patient heart. So then, groaning, we waited for shining Dawn.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, the males of the flock hurried out to pasture, while the females bleated round the pens unmilked, their udders bursting full. Their master, worn down with cruel pains, felt along the backs of all the sheep as they stood upright before him; and the fool never saw that my men were bound beneath the breasts of his own fleecy sheep. Last of the flock the ram walked toward the door, weighed down by his wool and by me with my close-woven thoughts. Feeling along his back, mighty Polyphemus said to him: “Dear ram, why are you last of the flock coming through the cave like this? Before now you were never left behind by the sheep — always far in front, striding long, you were first to graze the tender flowers of the grass, first to reach the flowing rivers, first to want to come back to the fold in the evening. Now you are last of all. Are you grieving, then, for your master's eye, which an evil man put out with his miserable companions, after he had overcome my wits with wine — Nobody, who I tell you has not yet escaped destruction? If only you could feel as I do, and could speak, to tell me where that man is skulking from my fury! Then his brains would be dashed against the floor and spattered this way and that through the cave, and my heart would find some relief from the miseries this good-for-nothing Nobody has brought me.” So saying, he sent the ram out through the doorway.

When we had gone a little way from the cave and the courtyard, first I loosed myself from under the ram, and then I set my companions free. Quickly we drove off those long-legged sheep, rich with fat, turning to look behind us again and again, until we reached the ship. And a welcome sight we were to our dear companions — we who had escaped death; for the others they began to groan and weep. But I would not allow it: with a frown of my brows I signed to each man not to weep, and ordered them instead to throw the many fine-fleeced sheep aboard quickly and sail out over the salt water. They came aboard at once and sat down at the oarlocks, and sitting in order they struck the gray sea with their oars.

But when I was as far off as a man's shout will carry, then I called to the Cyclops with jeering words: “Cyclops, it was no weakling, after all, whose companions you meant to eat by brute force in your hollow cave. Your evil deeds were bound to find you out, hard man, since you did not shrink from eating guests in your own house. For that, Zeus and the other gods have paid you back.” So I spoke, and the anger in his heart grew fiercer still. He tore off the peak of a great mountain and hurled it, and it fell just in front of our dark-prowed ship, barely missing the tip of the steering-oar. The sea surged up as the rock came down, and the backwash, like a flood-tide out of the deep, carried the ship swiftly back toward the land and drove it onto the shore.

But I seized a long pole in my hands and pushed her off and clear, and roused my companions, ordering them with a nod of my head to throw their weight on the oars, so that we might escape disaster; and they bent forward and rowed. When we had crossed twice as much sea and were that far away, again I made to call to the Cyclops; but all around me my companions, one from this side, one from that, tried to hold me back with soothing words: “Reckless man, why do you want to provoke that savage? Just now he threw his missile into the sea and drove the ship back to land, and we thought we were finished then and there. If he had heard any of us so much as utter a sound, he would have smashed our heads and our ship's timbers together with a jagged rock — such power is in his throw.” So they spoke, but they could not persuade my great-hearted spirit, and I called back to him with anger in my heart: “Cyclops, if any mortal man ever asks you about the shameful blinding of your eye, tell him it was Odysseus, sacker of cities, who blinded you — the son of Laertes, whose home is in Ithaca.”

So I spoke, and he groaned and answered me: “Ah, then the old prophecies have come home to me indeed! There was once a seer here, a fine man and a great one, Telemus, son of Eurymus, who excelled in prophecy and grew old among the Cyclopes as a prophet. He told me all this would come to pass in time — that I would lose my sight at the hands of Odysseus. But I always expected some big, handsome man to come here, clothed in great strength; and now a puny, worthless weakling has blinded my eye, after overcoming me with wine. Come here, Odysseus, and let me set out guest-gifts for you, and urge the famous earth-shaker to grant you passage home. I am his son, and he declares himself my father. He himself will heal me, if he is willing — no one else will, of the blessed gods or of mortal men.”
