So he spoke, and I answered him: “Would that I could strip you of life and breath and send you down to the house of Hades, as surely as not even the earth-shaker will heal that eye of yours!” So I spoke, and he prayed then to lord Poseidon, stretching his hands up to the starry heaven: “Hear me, Poseidon, earth-holder, dark-haired god! If I am truly yours, and you declare yourself my father, grant that Odysseus, sacker of cities, may never reach his home — the son of Laertes, whose home is in Ithaca. But if it is his portion to see his own people and come to his well-built house and his native land, may he come late and come wretchedly, having lost all his companions, on a ship not his own, and may he find trouble in his house.” So he spoke in prayer, and the dark-haired god heard him. Then once more he lifted a stone, a far bigger one, and swung it round and hurled it, putting his measureless strength behind it; and it fell just behind our dark-prowed ship, barely missing the tip of the steering-oar. The sea surged up as the rock came down; but this time the wave carried the ship forward and drove it on to the farther shore.

When we reached the island where our other well-benched ships were waiting all together, with our companions sitting around them in tears, watching for us without cease, we ran our ship up onto the sand there, and stepped out ourselves onto the surf-line of the sea. We took the Cyclops's sheep out of the hollow ship and divided them, so that no one, as far as I could help it, should go cheated of an equal share. But the ram my well-greaved companions gave to me alone, over and above my share, when the flocks were divided; and on the shore I sacrificed him to Zeus of the dark clouds, son of Cronus, who is lord of all, and burned the thigh-pieces. But Zeus took no heed of my sacrifice; he was pondering instead how all my well-benched ships and my loyal companions might be destroyed.

So then all day long until the setting of the sun we sat feasting on endless meat and sweet wine. When the sun set and darkness came on, we lay down to sleep on the surf-line of the sea. And when early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, I roused my companions and ordered them to come aboard themselves and cast off the stern cables. They came aboard at once and sat down at the oarlocks, and sitting in order they struck the gray sea with their oars. From there we sailed on, grieving at heart, glad to have escaped death, though we had lost our dear companions.

## Book X

We came next to the Aeolian island, where Aeolus son of Hippotas lived, dear to the immortal gods, on a floating island; all around it runs a wall of unbreakable bronze, and the rock rises up sheer. Twelve children of his live in his halls, six daughters and six sons in the prime of youth, and he has given his daughters to his sons as wives. They feast forever beside their dear father and devoted mother, with good things beyond counting set before them; all day the house is rich with the smell of roasting and rings with sound, out through the courtyard, and at night they sleep beside their modest wives on blankets and on corded bedsteads. To their city and their beautiful house we came, and for a full month he entertained me and questioned me about everything—Ilium, the ships of the Argives, the return of the Achaeans—and I recounted it all to him in due order.

But when at last I asked leave to go, and told him to send me on my way, he did not refuse; he prepared my sending. He gave me a bag made from the flayed hide of a nine-year-old ox, and in it he bound fast the paths of the blustering winds; for the son of Cronus had made him keeper of the winds, to still or to rouse whichever one he wishes. In my hollow ship he tied the bag down with a shining silver cord, so that not even the smallest breath could slip out; but for me he sent out the breeze of the West Wind to blow, to carry the ships and the men along. Yet it was not to be: we were destroyed by our own folly.

Nine days we sailed on, night and day alike, and on the tenth our native fields were already in sight—we were so near that we could see men tending their fires. Then sweet sleep came over me, for I was worn out: I had handled the sheet of the ship the whole way myself, giving it to none of my companions, so that we might come the sooner to our own land. But my companions began talking to one another, saying that I was bringing home gold and silver, gifts from great-hearted Aeolus son of Hippotas. And glancing at the man beside him, one of them would say: “Look at this—how loved and honored this man is by all men, whatever city or land he comes to! He is carrying home a store of beautiful treasure from the plunder of Troy, while we, who have made the very same journey, come home with empty hands. And now Aeolus has given him this too, as a gift of friendship. Quick, let us see what it is—how much gold and silver there is in that bag.”

So they talked, and the evil counsel of my companions won out. They opened the bag, and all the winds burst free; and in an instant the storm seized them and swept them out to sea, weeping, away from their native land. And I, waking, debated in my blameless heart whether to throw myself from the ship and perish in the sea, or to endure in silence and remain among the living. I endured, and stayed; covering my head, I lay in my ship, while the ships were carried by the evil blast of the storm back to the Aeolian island, and my companions groaned.

There we went ashore and drew water, and my companions quickly took their meal beside the swift ships. When we had put away our desire for food and drink, I took with me a herald and one companion and went to the famous house of Aeolus; I found him feasting beside his wife and children. We came into the house and sat down on the threshold by the doorposts, and they were astonished at heart, and questioned us: “How have you come back, Odysseus? What evil god attacked you? Surely we sent you off with every care, so that you might reach your own country and your home and whatever place was dear to you.” So they spoke, and I answered with grieving heart: “My wretched companions ruined me—they, and cruel sleep besides. But set it right, friends; the power is yours.” So I spoke, plying them with gentle words; but they fell silent, and the father answered: “Get off this island at once, you most contemptible of living men! It is not lawful for me to help or to send on his way a man whom the blessed gods detest. Out!—for you come here as one the immortals hate.” So saying he sent me from his house, groaning heavily.

From there we sailed on, grieved at heart. The men's spirit was worn down by the painful rowing—our own folly's doing, for no fair wind showed itself any more. Six days we sailed on, night and day alike, and on the seventh we came to the steep citadel of Lamus, Telepylus of the Laestrygonians, where herdsman hails herdsman as he drives his flock in, and the one driving his out answers. There a man who never slept could have earned a double wage, one for herding cattle and one for pasturing white sheep; for the paths of night and of day lie close together.

There we came into the famous harbor, around which sheer cliff runs unbroken on both sides, while jutting headlands, facing each other, thrust out at the mouth, and the entrance is narrow. The others all steered their curved ships inside, and moored them close together within the hollow harbor; for no wave ever swelled in it, great or small, but a white calm lay all about. I alone kept my black ship outside, at the very edge, making my cables fast to a rock; and I climbed to a rugged lookout and stood there. From it no works of oxen or of men could be seen; we saw only smoke rising up from the land.

Then I sent companions ahead to find out who the men were who ate bread in this land—two men I chose, and sent a third with them as herald. They went ashore and took a smooth road, along which wagons used to carry wood down to the city from the high mountains. Before the city they met a girl drawing water, the sturdy daughter of Laestrygonian Antiphates. She had come down to the fair-flowing spring Artacia, from which they carried water to the town; and the men came up to her and spoke, asking who was king of this people and who they were that he ruled. She at once pointed out her father's high-roofed house.

But when they entered the famous house, they found his wife there—big as a mountain peak—and the sight of her filled them with horror. She called famous Antiphates, her husband, straight from the assembly-place, and he plotted grim destruction for my men: he snatched up one of my companions on the spot and made his meal of him. The other two sprang away and fled back to the ships. Then he raised a war-cry through the city, and hearing it the mighty Laestrygonians came thronging from every side, thousands of them, not like men but like Giants. From the cliffs they hurled down rocks each as heavy as a man could lift, and at once an evil din rose along the ships—the din of men dying and of ships being crushed together. Spearing them like fish, they carried them off, a joyless feast.

While they were slaughtering the men inside the deep harbor, I drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh and cut the cables of my dark-prowed ship, and at once I called to my companions and ordered them to throw their weight on the oars, so that we might escape disaster; and in terror of destruction they all churned up the sea together. Gladly my ship fled the overhanging cliffs and reached the open water; but the others were destroyed there, all together. From there we sailed on, grieved at heart, glad to have escaped death, but with our dear companions lost.

We came to the island of Aeaea, where fair-braided Circe lived, a dread goddess with a human voice, own sister of murderous-minded Aeetes; both were children of Helios, who gives light to mortals, and of their mother Perse, whom Ocean bore. There we brought the ship silently in to shore, into a harbor safe for ships, and some god guided us in. We went ashore and lay there two days and two nights, eating our hearts out with weariness and grief. But when fair-braided Dawn brought the third day to its birth, I took my spear and my sharp sword and climbed quickly up from the ship to a viewpoint, hoping I might see the works of men or catch the sound of voices. I climbed to a rugged lookout and stood there, and smoke appeared to me, rising from the broad-wayed earth in Circe's halls, through the dense brush and the woods. Then I debated in mind and heart whether to go and find out, now that I had seen the glowing smoke. And as I turned it over, this seemed the better course: to go first to the swift ship and the shore of the sea, give my companions their meal, and then send them out to learn more.

But as I came near my curved ship, some god took pity on me, alone as I was, and sent a great high-antlered stag right into my path. He was coming down from his woodland pasture to the river to drink, for the sun's force lay heavy on him. As he stepped out I struck him on the spine, in the middle of the back, and the bronze spear went clean through; he fell in the dust with a cry, and his spirit flew away. Setting my foot on him, I drew the bronze spear from the wound and laid it down on the ground; then I pulled up brushwood and withies and plaited a rope, well twisted at both ends, a fathom long, and bound together the feet of the monstrous beast. I went down to the black ship carrying him slung across my neck, leaning on my spear, for there was no way to carry him on one shoulder with a single hand—he was a very great beast.

I threw him down in front of the ship and roused my companions, standing beside each man with soothing words: “Friends, grieve as we may, we will not go down to the house of Hades before our fated day comes. Come—while there is food and drink in the swift ship, let us think of eating, and not waste away with hunger.” So I spoke, and they quickly obeyed my words. Uncovering their heads, there by the shore of the barren sea they marveled at the stag—he was a very great beast. When they had taken their pleasure in gazing at him, they washed their hands and prepared a glorious feast. So all that day until the sun went down we sat feasting on meat past counting and sweet wine; and when the sun set and darkness came on, we lay down to sleep by the breaking waves of the sea.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, I called an assembly and spoke among them all: “Hear my words, companions, for all your suffering. Friends, we no longer know where the darkness lies or where the dawn, where the sun that lights mortal men goes down beneath the earth, or where he rises. Let us think, and quickly, whether any plan is left to us—though I do not believe there is. For I climbed to a rugged lookout and saw that this is an island, and around it the boundless sea lies like a crown. The island itself lies low, and in the middle of it I saw smoke with my own eyes, through the dense brush and the woods.” So I spoke, and their hearts broke within them, as they remembered the deeds of Laestrygonian Antiphates and the violence of the great-hearted Cyclops, the eater of men. They wept aloud, shedding big tears; but no good came of their weeping. Then I counted off all my well-greaved companions into two bands, and gave each band a leader: I commanded one, and godlike Eurylochus the other. Quickly we shook lots in a bronze helmet, and out leapt the lot of great-hearted Eurylochus. So he set out, and twenty-two companions with him, weeping; and they left us behind in tears as well.

In a forest glen they found the house of Circe, built of polished stone, in a place with a clear view all around. About it were mountain wolves and lions, creatures she herself had bewitched by giving them evil drugs. They did not spring at the men, but rose up and fawned about them, wagging their long tails. As when dogs fawn around their master as he comes from a feast, for he always brings some tidbit to please them, so the strong-clawed wolves and lions fawned around my men; but the men were afraid at the sight of the dread creatures. They stood in the gateway of the fair-braided goddess, and from inside they could hear Circe singing with a beautiful voice as she went to and fro at her great immortal loom, weaving such work as goddesses make, fine and graceful and shining. Then Polites, a leader of men, spoke first among them—of all my companions the dearest to me and most devoted: “Friends, someone inside is moving to and fro at a great loom and singing beautifully—the whole floor echoes with it—goddess or woman. Let us call out to her at once.”

So he spoke, and they cried out and called to her. She came out at once and opened the shining doors and invited them in; and in their innocence they all followed her—all but Eurylochus, who stayed behind, suspecting a trap. She led them in and seated them on chairs and couches, and mixed for them a dish of cheese and barley meal and pale honey in Pramnian wine; but into the food she stirred grim drugs, to make them wholly forget their native land. When she had served it and they had drunk it down, she struck them at once with her wand and penned them in the pigsties. They had the heads of pigs now, the voice, the bristles, the body—but their minds remained steadfast, just as before. So they were shut in, weeping; and Circe flung them acorns and mast and cornel berries to eat, such things as wallowing swine always feed on.

Eurylochus came straight back to the swift black ship, to bring word of his companions and their bitter fate. He could not get out a single word, for all his trying, so stricken was his heart with great grief; his eyes brimmed with tears, and his spirit could think only of wailing. But when in our amazement we had all pressed him with questions, at last he told us how the others had been destroyed: “We went up through the brush, as you ordered, glorious Odysseus. In a forest glen we found a beautiful house, built of polished stone, in a place with a clear view all around. There someone—goddess or woman—was moving at a great loom and singing clearly, and the men cried out and called to her. She came out at once and opened the shining doors and invited them in, and in their innocence they all followed her; but I stayed behind, suspecting a trap. And they vanished, all of them together; not one of them came out again, though I sat a long time watching.”

So he spoke, and I slung my silver-studded sword about my shoulders, the great bronze sword, and my bow with it, and ordered him to lead me back by the same road. But he clasped my knees with both hands and begged me, and in his grief he spoke winged words: “Do not take me back there against my will, nurtured of Zeus—leave me here. I know you will never come back yourself, nor bring back a single one of your companions. Let us flee, and quickly, with these men who are left; we might still escape the evil day.” So he spoke, and I answered him: “Eurylochus, stay here then, where you are, eating and drinking beside the hollow black ship. But I am going. Hard necessity is upon me.” So saying, I went up from the ship and the sea.

But as I went up through the sacred glens and was on the point of reaching the great house of Circe of the many drugs, Hermes of the golden wand met me as I came toward the house. He had taken the likeness of a young man with the first down on his lip, youth at its loveliest. He took my hand and spoke to me by name: “Where now, unlucky man, are you going alone through these hills, knowing nothing of the country? Your companions are penned up there in Circe's house, like pigs crowded into their sties. Have you come to set them free? You will not come back yourself, I tell you—you will only stay where the others are. But come, I will release you from this evil and save you. Here—take this potent herb with you when you go into Circe's house; it will keep the evil day from your head. And I will tell you all the deadly arts of Circe. She will mix you a potion, and put drugs into the food; but even so she will not be able to bewitch you, for the potent herb I am going to give you will not allow it. And I will tell you exactly what to do. When Circe strikes you with her long wand, then draw your sharp sword from beside your thigh and rush at her as though you meant to kill her. She will shrink back in fear and invite you to her bed. Do not refuse the bed of the goddess then, if you want her to free your companions and care for you as her guest; but make her swear the great oath of the blessed gods that she will plot no other evil harm against you, so that when you are stripped she does not leave you weak and unmanned.”

So speaking, the slayer of Argus gave me the herb, drawing it out of the ground, and showed me its nature. At the root it was black, but its flower was like milk. Moly, the gods call it; and it is hard for mortal men to dig up—but the gods can do all things. Then Hermes went away to high Olympus, across the wooded island, and I went on toward the house of Circe, my heart brooding darkly on many things as I walked.

I stood at the doors of the fair-braided goddess; standing there I called out, and the goddess heard my voice. She came out at once and opened the shining doors and invited me in, and I followed her, grieved at heart. She led me in and seated me on a beautiful chair studded with silver, richly worked, with a footstool beneath for my feet; and she prepared me a potion in a golden cup, for me to drink, and dropped a drug into it, with evil purpose in her heart. When she had given it to me and I had drunk it down—and it did not bewitch me—she struck me with her wand and spoke to me: “Off to the sty now, and lie down with the rest of your companions.” So she spoke; but I drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh and rushed at Circe as though I meant to kill her. With a loud shriek she ducked beneath the blade and clasped my knees, and weeping she spoke winged words to me: “Who are you, and from where among men? Where is your city, and your parents? Amazement takes hold of me: you drank these drugs, and you were not bewitched. No other man has ever withstood these drugs once he drank them, once they passed the barrier of his teeth; but you have a mind in your breast that no spell can touch. Surely you are resourceful Odysseus, the man of many turns, who the golden-wanded slayer of Argus always told me would come here in his swift black ship on his way home from Troy. Come, sheathe your sword, and let the two of us go up to my bed, so that, joined there in love, we may come to trust each other.”

So she spoke, and I answered her: “Circe, how can you ask me to be gentle with you, when in your halls you have turned my companions into pigs, and now, holding me here, with treachery in your heart you invite me into your chamber and your bed, so that when I am stripped you may leave me weak and unmanned? I will not consent to come to your bed unless you bring yourself, goddess, to swear a great oath that you will plot no other evil harm against me.” So I spoke, and she swore at once, as I demanded. And when she had sworn and completed the oath, then I went up to Circe's beautiful bed.

Meanwhile four handmaids were busy about the halls, the women who do the work of her house. They are born of the springs and the groves and the sacred rivers that flow down to the sea. One of them spread beautiful purple coverlets over the chairs, laying linen underneath; another drew up silver tables before the chairs and set golden baskets on them; the third mixed honey-hearted wine, sweet wine, in a silver bowl, and set out golden cups; and the fourth brought water and kindled a great fire beneath a huge tripod, and the water grew warm. When the water boiled in the gleaming bronze, she sat me in a bath and washed me with water from the great tripod, tempering it to my liking, pouring it over my head and shoulders, until she had drawn the heart-wearying weariness from my limbs. And when she had bathed me and rubbed me richly with oil, she dressed me in a fine cloak and tunic, and led me in and seated me on a beautiful chair studded with silver, richly worked, with a footstool beneath for my feet. A servant brought water in a fine golden pitcher and poured it over a silver basin, for my hands to be washed, and drew up a polished table beside me. The grave housekeeper brought bread and set it before me, adding many good things, generous with what she had; and she urged me to eat. But my heart was not in it. I sat with my thoughts far away, and my spirit foreboded evil.

When Circe noticed that I sat there without reaching out my hands to the food, and that a heavy grief had hold of me, she came and stood close to me and spoke winged words: “Why do you sit like this, Odysseus, like a man struck dumb, eating your heart out, touching neither food nor drink? Do you suspect some further trick? You have no need to fear: I have already sworn you a mighty oath.” So she spoke, and I answered her: “Circe, what man with any decency in him could bear to taste food or drink before his companions were set free and he had seen them with his own eyes? If you mean it kindly, this urging to eat and drink, then free them, and let me see my trusty companions with my own eyes.”

So I spoke, and Circe went out through the hall, her wand in her hand, and opened the doors of the sty, and drove them out, looking like nine-year-old hogs. They stood there facing her, and she went among them, anointing each one with another drug; and the bristles fell away from their limbs—the bristles that the deadly drug queenly Circe had first given them had made grow—and they became men again, younger than they were before, and far handsomer and taller to look upon. They knew me, and each man clung to my hands; and a sweet longing for weeping came over them all, and the house rang terribly about us. The goddess herself was moved to pity.

Then she came and stood close to me, shining among goddesses, and said: “Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, resourceful Odysseus, go now to your swift ship and the shore of the sea. First of all, drag the ship up onto dry land, and stow your goods and all the tackle in the caves; then come back yourself, and bring your trusty companions.” So she spoke, and my proud heart consented. I went to the swift ship and the shore of the sea, and there by the swift ship I found my trusty companions weeping pitifully, shedding big tears. As when calves in a farmyard skip about the herd of cows returning to the yard when they have grazed their fill—all together they frisk to meet them, and the pens can no longer hold them, but lowing without cease they run circles around their mothers—so those men, when their eyes fell on me, pressed around me weeping; and it seemed to their hearts as though they had come home to their own country, to the very city of rugged Ithaca, where they were born and raised.

And weeping they spoke winged words to me: “At your return, nurtured of Zeus, we rejoice as though we had come back to Ithaca, our native land. But come, tell us how the rest of our companions were destroyed.” So they spoke, and I answered them with gentle words: “First of all let us drag the ship up onto dry land, and stow our goods and all the tackle in the caves; then rouse yourselves, all of you, and come with me, so that you may see your companions in the sacred house of Circe, eating and drinking—for they have plenty, and it does not run out.” So I spoke, and they quickly obeyed my words.

Only Eurylochus tried to hold all my companions back, and he spoke winged words to them: “Poor fools, where are we going? Why are you so eager for these evils—to go down into Circe's hall, where she will turn us all into pigs or wolves or lions, to stand guard over her great house whether we will or not? Just so did the Cyclops, when our companions came into his yard, and bold Odysseus went with them—it was through that man's recklessness that they too were destroyed.” So he spoke, and I debated in my mind whether to draw the long-edged sword from beside my sturdy thigh, strike off his head, and send it to the ground, near kinsman of mine by marriage though he was; but my companions, one from this side, one from that, held me back with soothing words: “Sprung from Zeus, let us leave this man here, if you so order it, to stay by the ship and guard the ship; and lead the rest of us to the sacred house of Circe.” So speaking, they went up from the ship and the sea. Nor was Eurylochus left behind by the hollow ship: he came too, for he feared my terrible rebuke.

Meanwhile in her house Circe had bathed the rest of my companions with kindly care, and rubbed them richly with oil, and dressed them in fleecy cloaks and tunics; and we found them all feasting well in the halls. When the men saw one another and knew each other face to face, they wept and lamented, and the house rang with their sobbing. Then the goddess came and stood close to me, shining among goddesses, and said: “No more of this swelling grief now. I know myself how many pains you have suffered on the sea full of fish, and how much harm hostile men have done you on land. Come now, eat your food and drink your wine, until you get back the spirit in your breasts that you had when you first left your native land of rugged Ithaca. Now you are withered and heartless, forever brooding on your hard wanderings; your hearts have known no gladness for a long time, for truly you have suffered much.”

So she spoke, and our proud hearts consented. So there, day after day for a full year, we sat feasting on meat past counting and sweet wine. But when the year was out, and the seasons turned as the months waned and the long days came round again, my trusty companions called me aside and said: “Strange man, remember your native land now at last, if it is indeed fated that you be saved, and come to your high-roofed house and your own country.” So they spoke, and my proud heart consented.

So all that day until the sun went down we sat feasting on meat past counting and sweet wine; and when the sun set and darkness came on, the others lay down to sleep through the shadowed halls. But I went up to Circe's beautiful bed and clasped her knees in supplication, and the goddess heard my voice; and I spoke winged words to her: “Circe, make good the promise you gave me, to send me home. My heart is eager now to be gone, and so are the hearts of my companions, who wear away my own heart, grieving around me whenever you are elsewhere.”

So I spoke, and the shining goddess answered at once: “Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, resourceful Odysseus, stay no longer in my house against your will. But there is another journey you must accomplish first. You must go to the house of Hades and of dread Persephone, to consult the soul of Theban Tiresias, the blind prophet, whose mind is still unshaken; to him alone, even in death, Persephone has granted understanding—he alone has his wits, while the others flit about as shadows.” So she spoke, and my heart broke within me. I sat on the bed and wept, and my heart no longer wished to live and look upon the light of the sun. But when I had had my fill of weeping and writhing, then I answered her and said: “Circe, who will guide us on that journey? No one has ever yet reached Hades in a black ship.”

So I spoke, and the shining goddess answered at once: “Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, resourceful Odysseus, do not trouble yourself over a pilot for your ship. Set up the mast, spread the white sail, and sit down; the breath of the North Wind will carry her. But when in your ship you have crossed the stream of Ocean, you will find a low shore and the groves of Persephone, tall poplars and willows that let their fruit fall unripened. Beach the ship there, beside deep-eddying Ocean, and go yourself into the dank house of Hades. There Pyriphlegethon flows into Acheron, and Cocytus too, which is a branch of the water of Styx; and there is a rock where the two roaring rivers meet. Draw near to that place, hero, as I bid you, and dig a pit a cubit long and a cubit wide, and around it pour a drink-offering to all the dead—first with milk and honey, then with sweet wine, and the third time with water; and sprinkle white barley meal over it.

“Then entreat the strengthless heads of the dead with many prayers: vow that when you come to Ithaca you will sacrifice in your halls a barren heifer, the best you have, and heap the pyre with fine things; and to Tiresias, apart from the rest, you will offer a ram all black, the one that stands out among your flocks. And when with your prayers you have entreated the famous nations of the dead, then sacrifice a ram and a black ewe, turning their heads toward Erebus, while you yourself turn away and face the streams of the river; then many souls of the dead who are gone will come. Then urge your companions on, and order them to flay the sheep that lie there slaughtered by the pitiless bronze and burn them, and to pray to the gods, to mighty Hades and dread Persephone. And you yourself, drawing your sharp sword from beside your thigh, sit there, and do not let the strengthless heads of the dead come near the blood until you have questioned Tiresias. Then the prophet will come to you quickly, leader of men, and he will tell you your road, and the measures of your journey, and your homecoming—how you are to go back over the sea full of fish.”

So she spoke, and straightaway came Dawn of the golden throne. The nymph dressed me in a cloak and tunic, and she herself put on a great silvery mantle, fine and graceful, and cast a beautiful golden belt about her waist, and set a veil upon her head. And I went through the house rousing my companions, standing beside each man with soothing words: “No more sleeping now, no more drowsing in sweet slumber. Let us go: queenly Circe has told me the way.” So I spoke, and their proud hearts consented.

Yet not even from there could I lead my companions away unharmed. There was one, Elpenor, the youngest of them all, not remarkably brave in battle nor steady in his wits, who had lain down apart from his companions in Circe's sacred house, heavy with wine and wanting the cool air, high up on the roof. When he heard the noise and bustle of his companions stirring, he sprang up suddenly, and it went clean out of his mind to come back down by way of the long ladder; he fell headlong from the roof, his neck was broken from the spine, and his soul went down to Hades.

As the men were setting out, I said to them: “You think, no doubt, that you are going home to your own dear native land; but Circe has marked out another journey for us—to the house of Hades and of dread Persephone, to consult the soul of Theban Tiresias.” So I spoke, and their hearts broke within them; they sat down where they were, and wailed, and tore their hair. But no good came of their lamenting. And while we were going down, grieving, to the swift ship and the shore of the sea, shedding big tears, Circe had gone ahead of us and tethered a ram and a black ewe beside the black ship, slipping past us with ease. Who could see a god with his own eyes, going this way or that, if the god does not wish to be seen?

## Book XI

But when we had come down to the ship and the sea, first of all we dragged the ship into the bright salt water, and set the mast and sails in the black ship, and took the sheep and put them aboard, and ourselves embarked, grieving, shedding big tears. And behind our dark-prowed ship Circe of the lovely hair, dread goddess with human speech, sent a favorable wind that filled the sail, a good companion. We made each piece of tackle fast throughout the ship and sat down, and the wind and the helmsman kept her straight. All day long her sail was stretched taut as she ran over the sea; then the sun set and all the ways grew dark. And she came to the limits of deep-flowing Ocean. There lie the land and city of the Cimmerians, shrouded in mist and cloud; never does the shining sun look down on them with his rays, neither when he climbs toward the starry heaven nor when he turns back again from heaven to earth, but deadly night is spread over those wretched mortals. There we ran the ship ashore and took out the sheep, and we ourselves walked along the stream of Ocean until we came to the place Circe had described.

There Perimedes and Eurylochus held the victims, while I drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh and dug a pit a cubit long and a cubit wide, and around it I poured a drink-offering to all the dead — first with milk and honey, then with sweet wine, and the third time with water; and over it I sprinkled white barley meal. And I entreated the strengthless heads of the dead with many prayers: that when I came to Ithaca I would sacrifice in my halls a barren cow, the best I had, and heap the pyre with good things, and that to Tiresias alone, apart from the rest, I would offer a ram all black, the one that stands out among our flocks. When I had implored the tribes of the dead with vows and prayers, I took the sheep and cut their throats over the pit, and the dark-clouded blood ran down. And the souls of the dead who are gone came gathering up out of Erebus.

Brides, and unmarried young men, and old men who had endured much, and tender girls with hearts new to grief, and many men wounded by bronze-tipped spears, men slain in battle, still wearing their blood-stained armor. From every side they came crowding around the pit with an unearthly cry, and pale fear took hold of me. Then I urged my companions and told them to flay and burn the sheep that lay there, slaughtered by the pitiless bronze, and to pray to the gods, to mighty Hades and dread Persephone; while I myself drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh and sat there, and would not let the strengthless heads of the dead come near the blood until I had questioned Tiresias.

The first soul to come was that of my companion Elpenor, for he had not yet been buried under the wide-wayed earth: we had left his body behind in Circe's hall, unwept and unburied, since other work drove us on. I wept when I saw him, and pitied him in my heart, and I spoke winged words to him: “Elpenor, how did you come here beneath the misty gloom? You have arrived faster on foot than I in my black ship.” So I spoke, and with a groan he answered me: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, Odysseus of many devices, an evil doom sent by some god was my undoing, and too much wine. I had lain down to sleep in Circe's house, and did not think to go back down by the long ladder — I fell straight from the roof, and my neck was broken from the spine, and my soul came down to the house of Hades. Now I beg you by those we left behind, who are not here with us — by your wife, and by your father, who raised you when you were small, and by Telemachus, whom you left an only child in your halls: I know that when you leave here, from the house of Hades, you will put in with your well-built ship at the island of Aeaea. There, my lord, I beg you to remember me. Do not sail on and leave me behind unwept, unburied, turning your back on me, or I may become a cause of the gods' anger against you. Burn me with all the armor that is mine, and heap up a mound for me on the shore of the gray sea — a memorial of an unlucky man, for men to come to know of. Do this for me, and plant on my tomb the oar I rowed with while I lived among my companions.”

So he spoke, and I answered him: “All this, unhappy man, I will accomplish and do.” So the two of us sat exchanging our joyless words, I on one side holding my sword over the blood, while on the other side the phantom of my companion spoke on and on. And then there came the soul of my dead mother, Anticleia, daughter of great-hearted Autolycus, whom I had left alive when I went to sacred Ilium. I wept when I saw her, and pitied her in my heart; but even so, deep as my grief was, I would not let her come near the blood before I had questioned Tiresias.

Then came the soul of Theban Tiresias, holding a golden staff, and he knew me and spoke: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, Odysseus of many devices, why now, unhappy man, have you left the light of the sun and come here to see the dead and this joyless place? Draw back from the pit, and hold your sharp sword away, so that I may drink of the blood and speak the truth to you.” So he spoke, and I drew back and thrust my silver-studded sword into its sheath. And when he had drunk the dark blood, then the flawless seer spoke to me:

“It is a honey-sweet homecoming you are seeking, glorious Odysseus, but a god will make it hard for you. I do not think you will escape the notice of the earth-shaker, who has stored up anger against you in his heart, enraged that you blinded his dear son. Even so, you may yet come home, though suffering hardships, if you are willing to hold back your own appetite and your companions' from the moment you first bring your well-built ship to the island of Thrinacia, escaping the violet sea, and find grazing there the cattle and fat flocks of Helios, who sees all things and hears all things. If you leave them unharmed and keep your mind on your homecoming, you may all yet reach Ithaca, though you will suffer; but if you harm them, then I foretell destruction for your ship and your companions. And even if you yourself escape, you will come home late and in misery, with all your companions lost, on a ship belonging to others, and you will find trouble in your house — overbearing men who devour your livelihood, courting your godlike wife and offering wedding-gifts. Yet you will make them pay for their violence when you come.

“But when you have killed the suitors in your halls, whether by guile or openly with the sharp bronze, then take up a well-fitted oar and go, until you come to men who do not know the sea and eat no food mixed with salt — men who know nothing of red-cheeked ships, or of the well-fitted oars that are the wings of ships. And I will tell you a very clear sign, which will not escape you: when another traveler falls in with you and says you are carrying a winnowing-fan on your fine shoulder, then plant the well-fitted oar in the earth, and offer fine sacrifices to lord Poseidon — a ram, and a bull, and a boar that mounts sows — and then go home and offer holy hecatombs to the immortal gods who hold the wide heavens, to all of them in order. And death will come to you yourself from the sea, the gentlest of deaths, and take you when you are worn down by a sleek old age, with your people prosperous around you. All this I tell you truly.”

So he spoke, and I answered him: “Tiresias, all this, no doubt, the gods themselves have spun. But come, tell me this, and speak truly: I see here the soul of my dead mother. She sits in silence near the blood, and cannot bring herself to look her own son in the face, or to speak to him. Tell me, lord — how may she know me for the man I am?” So I spoke, and he answered me at once: “An easy thing I will tell you and set in your mind. Whichever of the dead who are gone you allow to come near the blood will speak the truth to you; whichever one you refuse will draw back and go away again.”

So spoke the soul of lord Tiresias, and went back into the house of Hades, when he had told out his prophecies. But I stayed steadily where I was until my mother came up and drank the dark-clouded blood. At once she knew me, and grieving she spoke winged words to me: “My child, how did you come beneath the misty gloom while you are still alive? It is hard for the living to look upon all this. For between us lie great rivers and terrible streams — Ocean first of all, which no one can cross on foot, but only with a well-built ship. Have you come here wandering from Troy, after all this time, with your ship and your companions? Have you not yet been to Ithaca, and not yet seen your wife in your halls?”

So she spoke, and I answered her: “Mother, need brought me down to Hades, to consult the soul of Theban Tiresias. For I have not yet come near Achaea, not yet set foot on our own land, but I have wandered in misery without end, ever since I first followed godlike Agamemnon to Ilium of the fine horses, to fight the Trojans. But come, tell me this, and speak truly: what doom of grievous death overcame you? Was it a long illness? Or did Artemis the archeress come and kill you with her gentle arrows? And tell me of my father, and of the son I left behind — is my honor still safe in their keeping, or does some other man hold it now, and do they say I will never come home? And tell me the mind and purpose of my wedded wife — does she stay with the boy and keep everything safe, or has whoever is best of the Achaeans already married her?”

So I spoke, and my honored mother answered at once: “Indeed she stays, with an enduring heart, in your halls; and her nights and her days waste away in misery, in tears. No one yet holds your fine honor: Telemachus tends your estates undisturbed, and shares in the equal feasts it is fitting for a man who deals judgment to attend, for all men invite him. But your father stays out there in the country and does not come down to the city. He has no proper bed, no bedding, no cloaks or shining coverlets; through the winter he sleeps where the servants sleep in the house, in the dust near the fire, and wears poor clothes on his body; and when summer comes, and the ripe harvest-season, then anywhere along the rising ground of his vineyard-plot a low bed of fallen leaves is strewn for him. There he lies grieving, and the sorrow grows great in his heart as he longs for your homecoming, and hard old age has come upon him. So it was, too, that I perished and met my fate: it was not the keen-sighted archeress who came and killed me in the halls with her gentle arrows, nor did any illness come over me, of the kind that most often takes the life from the limbs with hateful wasting. No — it was longing for you, and for your counsels, glorious Odysseus, and for your gentleness, that took my honey-sweet life from me.”

So she spoke, and I, pondering it in my heart, longed to embrace the soul of my dead mother. Three times I started toward her, and my heart urged me to hold her; three times she flew out of my hands like a shadow or a dream. And the pain grew sharper still in my heart, and I spoke winged words to her: “Mother, why do you not stay for me when I long to hold you, so that even in the house of Hades we two may throw our arms around each other and take our fill of chill lamentation? Or is this some phantom that dread Persephone has sent me, to make me grieve and groan still more?”

So I spoke, and my honored mother answered at once: “Ah me, my child, ill-fated beyond all men — Persephone the daughter of Zeus is not deceiving you; this is the way of mortals, when anyone dies. The sinews no longer hold the flesh and the bones together: the strong force of blazing fire consumes them, as soon as the life leaves the white bones, and the soul flies away like a dream and flutters off. But press on toward the light with all speed — and remember all these things, so that hereafter you may tell them to your wife.”

So the two of us exchanged our words; and then the women came, for dread Persephone sent them — all those who had been the wives and daughters of great men. They gathered in a throng around the dark blood, and I considered how I might question each one. And this seemed to my mind the best plan: I drew my long-edged sword from beside my sturdy thigh, and would not let them all drink the dark blood at once. So they came forward one by one, and each declared her lineage, and I questioned them all.

There the first I saw was highborn Tyro, who said she was the daughter of flawless Salmoneus, and said she had been the wife of Cretheus, son of Aeolus. She fell in love with a river, divine Enipeus, far the most beautiful of the rivers that flow upon the earth, and she used to haunt Enipeus's lovely streams. But the earth-holder, the earth-shaker, took the river's likeness and lay beside her at the mouths of the eddying stream; and a dark wave stood up around them, high as a mountain, arching over, and hid the god and the mortal woman. He loosed her virgin girdle, and shed sleep upon her. And when the god had finished the work of love, he took her hand and spoke and named her name: “Be happy, lady, in this love. As the year comes round you will bear splendid children, for the beds of the immortals are not barren; and you must care for them and raise them. Now go to your house, and hold your peace, and do not name me — but know that I am Poseidon the earth-shaker.” So speaking he sank beneath the surging sea. And she conceived, and bore Pelias and Neleus, who both became strong servants of great Zeus: Pelias dwelt in spacious Iolcus, rich in flocks, and the other in sandy Pylos. And her other sons this queen among women bore to Cretheus — Aeson and Pheres and Amythaon, fighter from the chariot.

After her I saw Antiope, daughter of Asopus, who boasted that she had slept in the very arms of Zeus; and she bore two sons, Amphion and Zethus, who first founded the seat of seven-gated Thebes, and walled it — for unwalled they could not live in spacious Thebes, strong though they were. After her I saw Alcmene, wife of Amphitryon, who lay in the arms of great Zeus and bore Heracles, the lion-hearted, steadfast in battle; and I saw Megara, daughter of proud Creon, whom the tireless son of Amphitryon had for his wife.

And I saw the mother of Oedipus, beautiful Epicaste, who in the ignorance of her mind did a monstrous thing: she married her own son. He had killed his father, and he married her; and soon the gods made it known among men. But he, suffering agonies in lovely Thebes, ruled on over the Cadmeans through the gods' deadly designs; while she went down to the house of Hades, the mighty warder of the gate, fastening a noose sheer from the high roof-beam, gripped by her grief; and to him she left behind sufferings past counting, all that a mother's Furies bring to pass.

And I saw lovely Chloris, whom Neleus once married for her beauty, when he had brought wedding-gifts past counting — the youngest daughter of Amphion, son of Iasus, who once ruled by might in Minyan Orchomenus. She was queen of Pylos, and bore him splendid children — Nestor and Chromius and lordly Periclymenus. And after them she bore stately Pero, a marvel to men, whom all the princes round about came courting; but Neleus would give her to no one who could not drive off from Phylace the broad-browed, spiral-horned cattle of mighty Iphicles — a dangerous task. Only the flawless seer undertook to drive them off; but the hard doom of a god fettered him — cruel bonds, and the rough herdsmen of the field. Yet when the months and the days were fulfilled, as the year came round again and the seasons returned, then mighty Iphicles set him free, when he had spoken all his prophecies; and the will of Zeus was accomplished.

And I saw Leda, the wife of Tyndareus, who bore to Tyndareus two strong-hearted sons, Castor the tamer of horses and Polydeuces the boxer. Both of them the life-giving earth holds, and yet they live: even beneath the earth they have honor from Zeus, and they are alive on alternate days, each in turn, and dead on the others; and they have won honor equal to the gods'.

After her I saw Iphimedeia, wife of Aloeus, who said that she had lain with Poseidon; and she bore two sons — short-lived they were — godlike Otus and far-famed Ephialtes, the tallest men the grain-giving earth ever reared, and by far the most beautiful after famous Orion. At nine years old they were nine cubits across the shoulders, and in height they had grown nine fathoms. They threatened to raise the din of furious war against the very immortals on Olympus: they strained to pile Ossa on Olympus, and on Ossa Pelion with its quivering leaves, so that heaven might be scaled. And they would have done it, had they reached the full measure of manhood; but the son of Zeus, whom fair-haired Leto bore, destroyed them both before the down bloomed beneath their temples and covered their chins with flowering beard.

And I saw Phaedra and Procris and beautiful Ariadne, daughter of grim Minos, whom Theseus once tried to carry from Crete to the hill of sacred Athens; but he had no joy of her, for before that Artemis killed her on sea-girt Dia, on the testimony of Dionysus. And I saw Maera and Clymene, and hateful Eriphyle, who took precious gold as the price of her own husband's life. But I could not tell of them all, nor name them — all the wives and daughters of heroes that I saw; before I finished, the immortal night itself would wear away. And now it is time to sleep, whether I go to the swift ship and my companions or stay here; my sending-home will be the concern of the gods, and of you.

So he spoke, and they were all hushed in silence, held spellbound through the shadowy hall. Then white-armed Arete was the first to speak among them: “Phaeacians, how does this man seem to you — in looks, in stature, and in the balanced mind within him? He is my guest, moreover, though each of you shares in that honor. So do not be hasty to send him on his way, and do not cut short your gifts to one in such need; for by the gods' good will there is much treasure lying in your halls.”

Then the old hero Echeneus spoke among them, who was the eldest of the Phaeacian men: “Friends, our wise queen speaks nothing wide of the mark, nothing short of what we ourselves expect. Do as she says. But on Alcinous here depend both word and deed.” And Alcinous answered him: “This word shall stand, as surely as I live and rule the oar-loving Phaeacians. Let our guest, for all his longing for home, endure to stay until tomorrow, until I can make our whole gift complete. His sending-home will be the concern of the men, of all of them, and of me above all; for mine is the power in this land.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Lord Alcinous, most renowned of all your people, if you urged me to stay here even for a year, and arranged my sending and gave me splendid gifts, I would choose that too, and it would be far better for me to come to my own dear country with a fuller hand; I should be more respected and better loved by all who saw me come home to Ithaca.”

Then Alcinous answered him: “Odysseus, looking at you we can in no way take you for a cheat and a liar, such as the black earth breeds in such numbers, men scattered far and wide, patching lies together out of things no man could even see. There is a grace of words upon you, and a sound mind within; and you have told your tale with all the skill of a singer — the grim sorrows of all the Argives, and your own. But come, tell me this, and speak truly: did you see any of your godlike companions, who went with you to Ilium and met their fate there? This night is very long — endless; it is not yet time to sleep in the hall. Tell me these wondrous deeds. I could hold out until bright dawn, if only you could bear to tell me your sorrows here in the hall.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Lord Alcinous, most renowned of all your people, there is a time for many words, and a time also for sleep. But if you long to hear still more, I would not grudge you the telling of other things more pitiable still — the sorrows of my companions, who perished afterward: men who escaped the Trojans' woeful battle-cry, only to perish on the way home through the will of one evil woman.”

When holy Persephone had scattered the souls of the women this way and that, there came the soul of Agamemnon son of Atreus, grieving; and around him others were gathered, all those who had died with him in Aegisthus's house and met their fate. He knew me at once, when he had drunk the dark blood, and he wept aloud, shedding big tears, stretching out his hands toward me, straining to reach me. But there was no firm strength left in him, none of the force that once filled his supple limbs. I wept when I saw him, and pitied him in my heart, and I spoke winged words to him: “Most glorious son of Atreus, Agamemnon, lord of men, what doom of grievous death overcame you? Did Poseidon overcome you in your ships, raising a cruel blast of hard winds? Or did hostile men cut you down on dry land, while you were driving off their cattle and their fine flocks of sheep, or fighting over a city and its women?”

So I spoke, and he answered me at once: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, Odysseus of many devices, Poseidon did not overcome me in my ships by raising a cruel blast of hard winds, nor did hostile men cut me down on dry land. It was Aegisthus who contrived my death and doom and killed me — with my accursed wife — after inviting me to his house and feasting me, as a man cuts down an ox at the manger. So I died a most pitiful death; and around me my other companions were killed without pause, like white-tusked pigs slaughtered in the house of some rich and powerful man for a wedding, or a shared banquet, or a lavish feast. You have been present at the killing of many men, cut down singly or in the press of hard battle; but you would have grieved most of all in your heart to see that sight — how we lay in the hall among the mixing-bowl and the laden tables, and the whole floor ran with blood.

“And the most pitiable cry I heard was the voice of Priam's daughter, Cassandra, whom scheming Clytemnestra killed beside me; and I, lifting my hands, beat them on the ground as I lay dying with a sword through me — and that bitch turned her back on me, and though I was on my way to Hades she could not bring herself to close my eyes with her hands or to shut my mouth. So true it is that nothing is more dreadful or more shameless than a woman who puts such deeds in her heart — the kind of monstrous thing she plotted, contriving the murder of her wedded husband. And to think I believed I would come home welcome to my children and my household! But she, with her mind set on utter horror, has poured shame on herself, and on all women to come, even on one who does right.”

So he spoke, and I answered him: “Alas — how terribly far-seeing Zeus has hated the line of Atreus from the very beginning, through the schemes of women! For Helen's sake many of us perished, and for you Clytemnestra laid her trap while you were far away.” So I spoke, and he answered me at once: “Never be gentle, then, even you, with your wife; do not tell her the whole of everything you know, but tell her one part and keep another hidden. Yet for you, Odysseus, death will not come from your wife: the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, is far too sensible, too sound in the counsels of her heart. She was a young bride when we left her to go to the war, and a boy was at her breast, an infant, who by now must sit and be counted among men. Happy boy! His dear father will see him when he comes, and he will embrace his father, as is right and proper. But my wife did not let me so much as fill my eyes with the sight of my son: before that, she killed me, her own husband. And I will tell you another thing — lay it up in your heart: bring your ship in to your own dear country secretly, not openly; for there is no trusting in women any more.

“But come, tell me this, and speak truly: do you hear of my son still living, perhaps in Orchomenus, or in sandy Pylos, or perhaps with Menelaus in wide Sparta? For godlike Orestes has not yet died upon the earth.” So he spoke, and I answered him: “Son of Atreus, why do you ask me this? I do not know at all whether he is alive or dead; and it is wrong to speak words as empty as wind.”

So the two of us stood exchanging our joyless words, grieving, shedding big tears. And there came the soul of Achilles, son of Peleus, and of Patroclus, and of flawless Antilochus, and of Ajax, who was the finest in face and form of all the Danaans after the flawless son of Peleus. The soul of the swift-footed grandson of Aeacus knew me, and grieving he spoke winged words: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, Odysseus of many devices — rash man, what deed will you plot in your heart still greater than this? How could you bear to come down to Hades, where the dead dwell without their wits, mere phantoms of worn-out mortals?” So he spoke, and I answered him: “Achilles, son of Peleus, far the greatest of the Achaeans, I came out of need, to consult Tiresias, in the hope that he might tell me some plan for reaching rocky Ithaca. For I have not yet come near Achaea, not yet set foot on my own land — I have trouble without end. But you, Achilles — no man before you was ever more blessed, and none will be hereafter. Before, while you lived, we Argives honored you like the gods; and now that you are here, you rule mightily among the dead. So do not grieve at having died, Achilles.”

So I spoke, and he answered me at once: “Do not try to make death sweet to me, glorious Odysseus. I would rather be bound to the soil, the hired man of another — some landless man, with little enough to live on — than be lord over all the perished dead. But come, tell me news of my noble son: did he follow to the war to be a champion, or not? And tell me of flawless Peleus, if you have heard anything — does he still have his honor among the many Myrmidons, or do they slight him through Hellas and Phthia, because old age has bound his hands and feet? For I am no longer there under the rays of the sun to help him, being such a man as once I was on the wide plain of Troy, when I cut down the best of their host in defense of the Argives. If I could come, even for a little while, such a man as that, to my father's house, I would make my strength and my invincible hands a horror to any of those who do him violence and bar him from his honor.”

So he spoke, and I answered him: “Of flawless Peleus I have heard nothing; but of your dear son Neoptolemus I will tell you the whole truth, as you ask. I myself brought him from Scyros in my hollow, well-balanced ship to join the well-greaved Achaeans. Whenever we took counsel around the city of Troy, he was always the first to speak, and his words never missed the mark; godlike Nestor and I alone surpassed him. And whenever we fought with the bronze on the Trojan plain, he never hung back in the mass of men or in the throng, but ran far out in front, yielding to no one in his fury; and he killed many men in dread combat. I could not tell of them all, nor name them — all the host he killed in defense of the Argives — but what a man was that son of Telephus he brought down with the bronze, the hero Eurypylus! And many of his companions, the Ceteians, were killed around him, all because of a woman's gifts. He was the most beautiful man I ever saw, after godlike Memnon.

“And when we, the best of the Argives, were going down into the horse that Epeius made — and everything was laid on me, when to open our close-packed ambush and when to shut it — then the other leaders and counselors of the Danaans kept wiping away tears, and each man's limbs trembled beneath him; but him my eyes never once saw grow pale in his handsome face, or wipe a tear from his cheeks. Again and again he begged me to let him out of the horse, and he kept gripping his sword-hilt and his bronze-heavy spear, bent on doing the Trojans harm. And when we had sacked the steep city of Priam, he went aboard his ship with his share of the spoil and a fine prize, unscathed — never struck by the sharp bronze, never wounded in the close fighting, as happens so often in war; for Ares rages blindly.”

So I spoke, and the soul of the swift-footed grandson of Aeacus went away with long strides over the meadow of asphodel, rejoicing that I had said his son was a man of mark. The other souls of the dead who are gone stood there grieving, and each asked after those dear to them. Only the soul of Ajax, son of Telamon, stood apart, still angry over the victory I had won against him in the judgment beside the ships, over the arms of Achilles. His honored mother had set them as the prize, and the sons of the Trojans and Pallas Athena gave the judgment. Would that I had never won such a contest — for over those arms the earth closed on such a head: Ajax, who in beauty and in deeds surpassed all the other Danaans, after the flawless son of Peleus.

I spoke to him with gentle words: “Ajax, son of flawless Telamon, could you not, even in death, forget your anger at me over those accursed arms? The gods made them a curse for the Argives, for in you they lost such a tower of strength; at your death we Achaeans grieved without ceasing, as much as for the life of Achilles son of Peleus. No one else is to blame: it was Zeus who so terribly hated the army of Danaan spearmen, and who laid this fate upon you. Come here, my lord, and hear our word and our speech; and master your fury and your proud heart.” So I spoke; but he answered me nothing, and went his way toward Erebus, after the other souls of the dead who are gone. Then, angry as he was, he might still have spoken to me, or I to him; but the heart in my breast wanted to see the souls of the others who were dead.

There I saw Minos, splendid son of Zeus, holding a golden scepter and giving judgment among the dead, seated; and around the lord they pleaded their cases, sitting and standing through the wide-gated house of Hades. And after him I noticed huge Orion, driving wild beasts together over the meadow of asphodel — the very beasts he himself had killed on the lonely mountains — holding in his hands a club of solid bronze, forever unbroken. And I saw Tityus, son of glorious Earth, lying on the ground; he stretched over nine acres, and a pair of vultures, sitting on either side, tore at his liver, plunging their beaks deep into his bowels; and he could not fend them off with his hands. For he had laid violent hands on Leto, the honored consort of Zeus, as she went toward Pytho through Panopeus with its lovely dancing-grounds.

And I saw Tantalus in hard torment, standing in a pool, and the water lapped at his chin. He strained with thirst, but he could not take a drop to drink: as often as the old man bent down, desperate to drink, so often the water was swallowed away and vanished, and around his feet the black earth showed, for a god dried it up. And trees in high leaf poured down fruit above his head — pears and pomegranates and apple trees with shining fruit, and sweet figs and olives in full growth; but whenever the old man reached out his hands to grasp them, the wind tossed them up toward the shadowing clouds. And I saw Sisyphus in hard torment, heaving an enormous stone with both hands. Bracing himself with hands and feet, he kept thrusting the stone up toward the crest of a hill; but every time, just as he was about to heave it over the top, its overpowering weight would turn it back, and down again to the plain rolled the pitiless stone. And again he would strain and push it, the sweat streaming from his limbs, and the dust rising from his head.

And after him I noticed the mighty Heracles — his phantom only; for the man himself is with the immortal gods, delighting in their feasts, with lovely-ankled Hebe for his wife, the daughter of great Zeus and Hera of the golden sandals. Around him rose a clamor of the dead, like birds scattering in terror in every direction; and he, like dark night, with his bow bare and an arrow on the string, glared terribly about him, forever like a man on the point of shooting. Fearsome was the belt across his chest, a golden baldric on which wondrous works were wrought — bears, and wild boars, and lions with gleaming eyes, and combats and battles and killings and slayings of men. May the craftsman who took that baldric into his art and made it never make another such thing!

He knew me the moment his eyes fell on me, and grieving he spoke winged words to me: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, Odysseus of many devices — poor man, do you too drag out some evil doom, like the one I bore under the rays of the sun? I was a son of Zeus the son of Cronus, and yet my misery was without end; for I was made subject to a man far worse than I, who laid hard labors on me. Once he even sent me here, to fetch the hound of Hades — for he could think of no labor harder for me than that. But I carried the hound up and brought him out of Hades; Hermes was my escort, and bright-eyed Athena.”

So speaking, he went back into the house of Hades; but I stayed steadily where I was, in case any more of the men of heroic times should come. And I would have seen still earlier men, the ones I longed to see — Theseus and Pirithous, glorious children of the gods. But before that the tribes of the dead came gathering in their countless thousands, with an unearthly cry, and pale fear took hold of me, that dread Persephone might send up out of Hades the Gorgon head of that terrible monster. Then at once I went to the ship and told my companions to embark themselves and loose the stern-cables. They went aboard quickly and sat down at the benches; and the wave of the stream carried her down the river Ocean, first with our rowing, and afterward with a fair breeze.

## Book XII

Now when our ship had left the stream of the river Ocean and come out onto the swell of the wide-wayed sea, and to the island of Aeaea, where early-born Dawn has her house and her dancing-floors, and where the Sun has his risings, we ran the ship up onto the sands when we arrived, and stepped out ourselves onto the shore of the sea; and there we fell asleep and waited for bright Dawn.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, I sent my companions ahead to the house of Circe to bring back the body of dead Elpenor. Quickly cutting logs, we buried him where the headland juts farthest out, grieving, letting the warm tears fall. And when the corpse was burned, and the dead man's armor with it, we heaped up a mound, dragged a gravestone onto it, and planted his well-shaped oar on the very top of the barrow.

So we saw to each of these things. And Circe did not fail to notice that we had come back from the house of Hades; she came very quickly, dressed and ready, and her handmaids came with her carrying bread and abundant meat and gleaming red wine. Standing in our midst, the shining goddess said: “Reckless men, who went down alive into the house of Hades—twice-dead, when other men die only once! Come now, eat this food and drink this wine, here, all day long; and at daybreak you shall sail. I myself will show you the way and point out every danger, so that you may not suffer harm and pain, on sea or on land, through some wretched miscalculation.”

So she spoke, and our proud hearts consented. So then all day long until the setting of the sun we sat feasting on meat past counting and sweet wine. And when the sun went down and darkness came on, the others lay down to sleep by the stern cables of the ship; but she took me by the hand, sat me down apart from my dear companions, lay down beside me, and asked me about everything; and I recounted it all to her, point by point, as was fitting. Then queenly Circe spoke these words to me: “All that, then, has been brought to its end. Now listen to what I tell you—and a god himself will bring it back to your mind.

“First you will come to the Sirens, who enchant all men, whoever comes their way. Whoever in his ignorance draws near and hears the Sirens' voice—his wife and little children never stand beside him at his homecoming and rejoice; the Sirens enchant him with their clear-toned song, sitting in a meadow, and around them is a great heap of the bones of rotting men, and the skin shrivels on the bones. Row past them, and knead honey-sweet wax and stop up your companions' ears with it, so that none of the others may hear. But if you yourself wish to listen, have them bind you hand and foot in the swift ship, upright in the mast-step, with the ropes made fast to the mast itself, so that you may hear the two Sirens' voice and take your delight in it. And if you beg your companions and order them to release you, let them bind you then with still more bonds.

“When your companions have driven the ship past them, I will not tell you exactly, once and for all, which of two ways your course will lie; you must weigh it in your own heart. But I will tell you both. On the one side are overhanging rocks, and against them roars the great swell of dark-eyed Amphitrite; the blessed gods call them the Wandering Rocks. That way not even winged things can pass, not even the timorous doves that carry ambrosia to father Zeus—even of them the smooth rock always takes one away, and the Father sends in another to make up the number. That way no ship of men has ever escaped once it came there; the waves of the sea and squalls of deadly fire carry off ships' planks and men's bodies together. One seafaring ship alone has sailed past, the Argo, whose name is on all men's lips, on her voyage home from Aeetes; and the wave would quickly have flung even her against the great rocks, but Hera sent her through, because Jason was dear to her.

“On the other side are two cliffs. One reaches the wide heaven with its sharp peak, and a dark cloud wraps around it; the cloud never draws away, and clear sky never holds that peak, in summer or in autumn. No mortal man could climb it or set foot on its top, not if he had twenty hands and twenty feet, for the rock is smooth, as if polished all around. In the middle of the cliff is a murky cave, facing the gloom of the west, toward Erebus—the very way you will steer your hollow ship, glorious Odysseus. Not even a strong man shooting an arrow from a hollow ship could reach that vaulted cave. Inside it lives Scylla, yelping horribly. Her voice, it is true, is no louder than a newborn puppy's, but she herself is an evil monster; no one would rejoice to look at her, not even a god who crossed her path. She has twelve feet, all of them dangling, and six necks, immensely long, and on each neck a hideous head, and in each head three rows of teeth, thick-set and crowded, full of black death. Up to her middle she is sunk in the hollow cave, but she holds her heads out of that dreadful chasm, and there she fishes, sweeping hungrily around the cliff for dolphins and dogfish and any larger creature she can catch, of the countless beasts that loud-moaning Amphitrite breeds. No sailors have ever yet boasted of slipping past her unscathed with their ship: with each head she carries off a man, snatched from the dark-prowed vessel.

“The other cliff, you will see, Odysseus, lies lower—they are close to each other; you could shoot an arrow across. On it is a great fig tree in full leaf, and beneath it divine Charybdis sucks down the black water. Three times a day she spouts it up, and three times she sucks it down—terribly. May you not be there when she sucks it down, for no one could save you from that ruin, not even the earth-shaker. No—keep close to Scylla's cliff, and drive your ship past at speed; it is far better to mourn six companions from your ship than all of them together.”

So she spoke, and I answered her: “Come, goddess, tell me this truly: is there some way I might escape deadly Charybdis, and yet fight the other off when she preys on my companions?” So I spoke, and the shining goddess answered at once: “Rash man! Even now your mind is set on deeds of war and struggle. Will you not yield even to the immortal gods? She is no mortal thing; she is an immortal evil—dreadful, grim, savage, and not to be fought. There is no defense; the bravest course is flight. If you delay by the rock to arm yourself, I fear she will dart out a second time and catch you with just as many heads and seize just as many men. Row past with all your strength, and call on Crataiis, Scylla's mother, who bore her to be a plague to mortals; she will stop her from darting out again.

“Then you will come to the island of Thrinacia. There the many cattle and sturdy sheep of Helios graze—seven herds of cattle and as many fine flocks of sheep, fifty in each. They bear no young, and they never die. Goddesses are their shepherds, fair-haired nymphs, Phaethusa and Lampetie, whom shining Neaera bore to Helios Hyperion. When their queenly mother had borne and reared them, she settled them on the island of Thrinacia to live far away, guarding their father's sheep and his crooked-horned cattle. If you leave these unharmed and keep your mind on your homecoming, you may all yet reach Ithaca, though you suffer much on the way. But if you harm them, then I foretell destruction for your ship and your companions; and even if you yourself escape, you will come home late and in misery, having lost all your companions.”

So she spoke, and at once golden-throned Dawn came. Then the shining goddess went away up the island; and I went to the ship and roused my companions to come aboard themselves and loose the stern cables. They embarked quickly and took their places at the benches, and sitting in order they struck the grey sea with their oars. And behind our dark-prowed ship, Circe of the lovely hair, dread goddess with a mortal's voice, sent a favorable wind that filled the sail, a good companion for our voyage. When we had seen to all the tackle throughout the ship, we sat down, and the wind and the helmsman kept her on her course.

Then, grieved at heart, I spoke among my companions: “Friends, it is not right that only one man, or two, should know the prophecies that Circe, shining among goddesses, told me. I will tell you, so that knowing them we may either die, or turn death and doom aside and escape. First she bids us shun the voice of the wondrous Sirens and their flowering meadow. Me alone she bade listen to their song; but you must bind me in hard bonds, so that I stay fixed where I am, upright in the mast-step, with the ropes made fast to the mast itself. And if I beg you and order you to release me, then you must press me down with still more bonds.”

So I went through each thing, telling my companions all of it. And meanwhile the well-built ship swiftly reached the island of the two Sirens, for a fair, harmless wind was driving her. Then all at once the wind dropped, and there came a windless calm, and some god lulled the waves to sleep. My companions rose, furled the ship's sails and stowed them in the hollow ship, then sat to the oars and whitened the water with the polished pine blades. And I cut a great round of wax into small pieces with my sharp bronze and kneaded them in my strong hands; the wax quickly grew soft, worked by my great strength and the rays of lord Helios, Hyperion's son, and one by one I stopped the ears of all my companions with it. Then they bound me in the ship, hand and foot together, upright in the mast-step, and made the ropes fast to the mast itself; and sitting down they struck the grey sea with their oars.

But when we were as far away as a man's shout will carry, driving swiftly on, the sea-quick ship did not slip past them unnoticed as she sped near, and they raised their clear-toned song: “Come here, famed Odysseus, great glory of the Achaeans! Bring your ship in, so that you may hear the voice of us two. No man has ever yet rowed past this place in his black ship until he has heard the honey-sweet voice from our lips; then he goes on his way delighted, and knowing more than he knew. For we know all that the Argives and Trojans suffered on the broad plain of Troy by the will of the gods, and we know all that comes to pass on the nourishing earth.” So they sang, sending out their beautiful voice, and my heart longed to listen; I commanded my companions to set me free, signaling with my brows. But they threw themselves forward and rowed on, and at once Perimedes and Eurylochus rose and bound me with more ropes and pressed them tighter. And when they had rowed past the Sirens, and we could no longer hear their voice or their song, my trusty companions quickly took out the wax I had stopped their ears with, and released me from my bonds.

But when we had left the island behind, I suddenly saw smoke and a great wave, and heard a booming. The oars flew from my terrified companions' hands and splashed trailing in the current, and the ship stopped where she was, since their hands no longer drove the tapered oar-blades. And I went through the ship rousing my companions with soothing words, standing beside each man in turn: “Friends, we are no strangers to trouble by now; and surely this evil upon us is no greater than when the Cyclops penned us in his hollow cave by brute force. Even from there, by my courage and my counsel and my wits, we escaped—and I think we shall remember all this too, one day. Come now, let us all do as I say. You keep to your benches and strike the deep surf of the sea with your oar-blades, and perhaps Zeus will grant that we slip away and escape this destruction. And to you, helmsman, I give this order—lay it up in your heart, since you handle the steering-oar of our hollow ship: keep the ship well clear of that smoke and surf, and hug the cliff, so that she does not swerve away to the other side before you know it, and throw us all into ruin.”

So I spoke, and they quickly obeyed my words. But Scylla I did not mention—that hopeless misery—for fear my companions might take fright, drop their rowing, and huddle together down in the hold.
