And then I let Circe's hard command slip from my mind, for she had told me not to arm myself at all: I put on my glorious armor, took two long spears in my hands, and went up onto the foredeck of the ship, for it was from there I expected rocky Scylla to show herself first, she who was bringing ruin on my companions. But I could not catch sight of her anywhere, and my eyes grew weary with peering every way along the misty rock.

So we sailed up the narrow strait, lamenting. On one side was Scylla; on the other, divine Charybdis terribly sucked down the salt water of the sea. Whenever she vomited it forth, she would seethe and churn all through like a cauldron over a great fire, and the spray flew high and fell on the tops of both cliffs; but whenever she gulped the salt sea-water down, the whole churning depth of her stood revealed, the rock around roared terribly, and at the bottom the sea-floor showed dark with sand. Pale fear took hold of my men. We were looking toward her, dreading destruction—and in that moment Scylla snatched out of the hollow ship six of my companions, the best of them in strength of arm.

Turning to look at the swift ship and after my men, I saw their feet and hands already above me as they were swung aloft; and they cried out and called to me by name, for the last time, in anguish of heart. As when a fisherman on a jutting rock, with his long pole, casts down morsels as a lure for the little fish, letting the horn of a field-ox down into the sea, and then, catching one, flings it writhing up onto the land—so they were swung writhing up toward the rocks. There in her doorway she devoured them, shrieking, stretching out their hands to me in that terrible death-struggle. That was the most pitiful thing my eyes ever saw in all my sufferings as I searched out the paths of the sea.

Now when we had escaped the rocks and dread Charybdis and Scylla, we came soon after to the god's flawless island, where the fine broad-browed cattle and the many sturdy sheep of Helios Hyperion were kept. While I was still at sea in the black ship, I heard the lowing of cattle being driven to their yards, and the bleating of sheep; and there fell upon my heart the word of the blind seer, Theban Teiresias, and of Aeaean Circe, who charged me over and over to shun the island of Helios, who brings joy to mortals. Then, grieved at heart, I spoke among my companions: “Hear my words, companions, for all your suffering, so that I may tell you the prophecies of Teiresias and of Aeaean Circe, who charged me over and over to shun the island of Helios, who brings joy to mortals; for there, she said, lay the most dreadful evil for us. Drive the black ship on past the island.”

So I spoke, and their hearts broke within them; and at once Eurylochus answered me with hateful words: “You are a hard man, Odysseus. Your strength is beyond us all, your limbs never tire; truly you must be made entirely of iron, if you will not let your companions, worn out with weariness and lack of sleep, set foot on land, on this sea-girt island where we might once again make ourselves a good supper—but instead you order us to wander on, just as we are, through the swift night, driven off from the island over the misty sea. It is out of the night that the hard winds come, the wreckers of ships. How would any of us escape sheer destruction, if a sudden squall of wind should come, of the South Wind or the hard-blowing West, the winds that most often shatter a ship, whatever the ruling gods may will? No—for now let us yield to black night and prepare our supper, staying by the swift ship; and at dawn we will go aboard and put out onto the wide sea.”

So spoke Eurylochus, and the other companions approved. Then I knew for certain that some god was plotting evil, and I spoke winged words to him: “Eurylochus, you force my hand, for I am one man alone. But come now, every one of you swear me a mighty oath: if we find any herd of cattle or great flock of sheep, no man among you will kill a single cow or sheep in wicked recklessness. Sit quiet, and eat the food that immortal Circe gave us.” So I spoke, and they swore at once, as I commanded. And when they had sworn and completed the oath, we moored the well-built ship in a hollow harbor, near sweet water, and my companions went ashore from the ship and skillfully prepared their supper. When they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, they remembered and wept for their dear companions, the men Scylla had snatched from the hollow ship and eaten; and as they wept, sweet sleep came over them. But in the third watch of the night, when the stars had wheeled past their height, Zeus who gathers the clouds stirred up a raging wind with a tremendous storm, and hid earth and sea alike in cloud; and night rushed down from heaven.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, we dragged the ship up and made her fast inside a hollow cave, where the nymphs had their fair dancing-floors and seats. Then I called my men together and spoke among them: “Friends, there is food and drink in the swift ship; let us keep our hands off these cattle, or we shall suffer for it. These are the cattle and sturdy sheep of a dread god, Helios, who sees all things and hears all things.” So I spoke, and their proud hearts consented.

But for a whole month the South Wind blew without ceasing, and no other wind arose but the East and the South. So long as my men still had bread and red wine, they kept their hands off the cattle, for they wanted to live. But when all the stores in the ship were used up, and they were forced to go roaming in search of game—fish and birds, whatever came to their hands—with bent hooks, while hunger gnawed at their bellies, then I went away up the island to pray to the gods, in hope that one of them might show me a way home. And when, crossing the island, I had left my companions well behind, I washed my hands in a spot sheltered from the wind and prayed to all the gods who hold Olympus; and they poured sweet sleep over my eyelids.

Meanwhile Eurylochus was opening his evil counsel to my companions: “Hear my words, companions, for all your suffering. All deaths are hateful to wretched mortals, but the most pitiful of all is to die of hunger and so meet one's doom. Come then, let us drive off the best of Helios's cattle and sacrifice them to the immortals who hold the wide heaven. And if we ever reach Ithaca, our native land, we will at once build a rich temple to Helios Hyperion, and set up in it many fine offerings. But if, in anger over his straight-horned cattle, he chooses to destroy our ship, and the other gods follow him, then I would rather gulp down the wave once and for all and lose my life, than waste slowly away on a desert island.” So spoke Eurylochus, and the other companions approved.

At once they drove off the best of Helios's cattle—from close by, for the fine broad-browed cattle with their curling horns grazed not far from the dark-prowed ship. They stood in a circle around them and prayed to the gods, plucking tender leaves from a high-crowned oak, for they had no white barley aboard the well-benched ship. When they had prayed, they slaughtered and flayed the cattle, cut out the thigh-pieces and wrapped them in fat, making a double fold, and laid raw flesh upon them. They had no wine to pour over the burning sacrifice, so they made their libations with water as they roasted all the entrails. And when the thighs were burned up and they had tasted the inner parts, they cut up the rest and pierced the pieces on spits.

Then sweet sleep sped from my eyelids, and I set out for the swift ship and the shore of the sea. But as I drew near the curved ship, the sweet smell of hot fat came drifting around me; and I groaned aloud and cried out to the immortal gods: “Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live forever—it was for my ruin that you lulled me in that pitiless sleep, while my companions, left behind, contrived this monstrous deed.” Swiftly long-robed Lampetie went as messenger to Helios Hyperion, to tell him we had killed his cattle. And at once, angered at heart, he spoke among the immortals: “Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live forever, take vengeance on the companions of Odysseus, son of Laertes, who have insolently killed my cattle—the cattle I delighted to see whenever I climbed into the starry heaven, and whenever I turned back again from heaven toward the earth. If they do not pay me a fitting recompense for the cattle, I will go down into Hades and shine among the dead.” Zeus who gathers the clouds answered him: “Helios, shine on among the immortals and among mortal men on the grain-giving earth. As for those men, I will soon strike their swift ship with my bright thunderbolt and shatter it to splinters in the middle of the wine-dark sea.” All this I heard from fair-haired Calypso, and she said she had heard it herself from Hermes the guide.

When I came down to the ship and the sea, I went from man to man and rebuked them; but we could find no remedy—the cattle were already dead. And soon the gods began showing portents to my men: the hides crawled, the meat on the spits lowed, both roasted and raw, and there was a sound like the voice of cattle. For six days my trusty companions feasted on the best of Helios's cattle they had driven off. But when Zeus son of Cronus brought the seventh day, then at last the wind ceased its stormy raging, and we quickly went aboard and put out onto the wide sea, setting up the mast and hoisting the white sail.

But when we had left the island behind, and no other land was in sight, only sky and sea, then the son of Cronus set a dark cloud over the hollow ship, and the sea grew black beneath it. She ran on for no long time; for soon came the shrieking West Wind, raging with a great storm, and the blast of wind snapped both forestays of the mast. The mast fell backward, and all the tackle spilled down into the bilge; and in the stern of the ship the mast struck the helmsman's head and crushed all the bones of his skull together. Like a diver he dropped from the deck, and his proud spirit left his bones. In the same moment Zeus thundered and hurled his bolt into the ship; she shuddered from end to end, struck by the bolt of Zeus, and filled with the smell of sulfur, and my companions fell from the ship. Like sea-crows they were carried on the waves around the black ship, and the god took away their homecoming.

But I kept moving through the ship until the surge tore the sides loose from the keel, and the wave carried the bare keel along, and snapped the mast off close against it. Over the mast, though, the backstay had been flung, a rope made of oxhide; with this I lashed the two together, keel and mast alike, and sitting astride them I was carried along by the deadly winds. Then the West Wind ceased its stormy raging, and the South Wind came on fast, bringing anguish to my heart—that I must measure my way back again to deadly Charybdis. All night long I was carried, and at sunrise I came to Scylla's cliff and to dread Charybdis. She was just sucking down the salt water of the sea; but I, swung up high toward the tall fig tree, caught hold of it and clung there like a bat. There was nowhere I could plant my feet firmly, or climb up onto the tree, for its roots were far below and its branches out of reach overhead, long and great, overshadowing Charybdis. So I clung on without letting go, until she should vomit back the mast and keel again; and to my longing eyes they came, though late. At the hour when a man rises from the assembly for his supper, after judging the many quarrels of young men who bring their cases to him, at that very hour the timbers reappeared out of Charybdis. I let go from above, feet and hands together, and dropped with a splash into the water beside the long timbers; and sitting on them, I rowed myself along with my hands. As for Scylla, the father of gods and men did not let her catch sight of me again—otherwise I should never have escaped sheer destruction.

From there I was carried for nine days, and on the tenth night the gods brought me to the island of Ogygia, where Calypso of the lovely hair lives, dread goddess with a mortal's voice, who took me in and cared for me. But why tell that tale over again? Only yesterday I told it in this house, to you and to your noble wife; and it is hateful to me to tell again a tale that has once been plainly told.

## Book XIII

So he spoke, and they all fell silent and still, held spellbound through the shadowy halls. Then Alcinous answered him and said: “Odysseus, since you have come to my bronze-floored, high-roofed house, I do not think you will be driven wandering off course again before you reach home, however much you have suffered. And to each man of you I say this, laying it upon you — all of you who in my halls forever drink the gleaming wine of the elders and listen to the singer: clothing for our guest lies already in the polished chest, with finely wrought gold and all the other gifts the counselors of the Phaeacians have brought here. Come, let us give him besides a great tripod and a cauldron, every man of us; and we in our turn will gather from the people and repay ourselves — for it is a hard thing for one man to give so freely with nothing in return.”

So spoke Alcinous, and his word pleased them, and they went each to his own house to sleep. When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, they hurried down to the ship, carrying the bronze that gladdens a man. The hallowed prince Alcinous himself went through the ship and stowed it all carefully beneath the benches, so that it would not hamper any of the crew at their rowing when they bent hard to the oars. Then they went to the house of Alcinous and prepared a feast. For them the hallowed prince Alcinous sacrificed an ox to Zeus of the dark clouds, the son of Cronus, who is lord over all. They burned the thigh-pieces and shared the glorious feast, rejoicing; and among them sang the divine singer, Demodocus, honored by the people. But Odysseus kept turning his head toward the blazing sun, willing it to set, for now he longed to be on his way. As a man longs for his supper when all day long his two wine-dark oxen have dragged the jointed plow through fallow ground, and the sun's light goes down most welcome to him, so that he can go to his supper, and his knees ache under him as he walks — so welcome to Odysseus was the setting of the sun's light.

At once he spoke among the oar-loving Phaeacians, and to Alcinous above all he declared his word: “Lord Alcinous, most renowned of all the people, pour your libations and send me on my way unharmed — and farewell to you all. For now everything my heart desired has been accomplished: passage home, and gifts of friendship, and may the gods of heaven make them prosper for me. May I return and find my blameless wife at home, with my dear ones safe and sound; and may you, remaining here, bring joy to your wedded wives and your children. May the gods grant you every excellence, and may no evil come upon your people.”

So he spoke, and they all approved, and urged that the stranger be given passage, since he had spoken rightly. Then the princely Alcinous said to the herald: “Pontonous, mix a bowl of wine and serve it to everyone in the hall, so that with a prayer to father Zeus we may send our guest to his own native land.” So he spoke, and Pontonous mixed the honey-hearted wine and served it to all in turn; and from where they sat they poured libations to the blessed gods who hold wide heaven. Then godlike Odysseus rose, placed the two-handled cup in Arete's hand, and spoke winged words to her: “Farewell, my queen, through all your days, until old age and death come, which are the lot of every human being. I go on my way now; and may you have joy in this house, in your children and your people and Alcinous the king.”

So saying, godlike Odysseus stepped over the threshold, and the hallowed prince Alcinous sent a herald with him, to lead him to the swift ship and the shore of the sea. And Arete sent serving women along with him — one carrying a freshly washed cloak and a tunic, another she charged with bringing the sturdy chest, and a third carried bread and red wine. When they came down to the ship and the sea, the noble escorts promptly took it all and stowed it in the hollow ship, all the food and drink; and for Odysseus they spread a rug and a linen sheet on the deck of the hollow ship, at the stern, so that he might sleep unbroken. Then he too went aboard and lay down in silence, and they took their places at the oarlocks, each in order, and loosed the cable from the pierced stone. And the moment they leaned back and tossed up the sea with their oar-blades, a sweet sleep fell upon his eyelids — an unwaking sleep, most sweet, very like to death.

And the ship — as on a plain four yoked stallions spring forward all together under the strokes of the lash, and lifting high they swiftly devour their course — so her stern lifted, and behind her the great dark wave of the loud-roaring sea came surging on. She ran on unswerving and sure; not even the circling hawk, the swiftest of winged things, could have kept pace with her. So she ran on swiftly, cutting through the waves of the sea, carrying a man whose counsels were like the gods', a man who before had suffered many pains in his heart, passing through the wars of men and the grievous waves, and who now slept without stirring, all his sufferings forgotten. And when the brightest of stars rose, the one that comes above all others to herald the light of early-born Dawn, then the seafaring ship was drawing near the island.

There is a harbor of Phorcys, the old man of the sea, in the land of Ithaca. Two jutting headlands, sheer on the seaward side, slope down toward the harbor and shelter it from the great waves the hard-blowing winds raise outside; within, well-benched ships ride without mooring once they have come inside the anchorage. At the head of the harbor stands a long-leafed olive tree, and near it a lovely, shadowy cave, sacred to the nymphs who are called Naiads. In it are mixing bowls and two-handled jars of stone, and there the bees store their honey. In it too are long looms of stone, where the nymphs weave webs of sea-purple, a wonder to behold; and there are ever-flowing springs. It has two doors: one, toward the North Wind, by which men may go down; the other, toward the South Wind, is for the gods — men do not enter by that way, for it is the path of the immortals.

There they rowed in, knowing the place from before; and the ship, at full speed, ran up onto the land for as much as half her length — such was the force of the rowers' arms. They stepped from the well-benched ship onto the shore, and first they lifted Odysseus out of the hollow ship, linen sheet and shining rug and all, and laid him down on the sand, still overcome by sleep. Then they lifted out the treasures the noble Phaeacians had given him as he set out for home, thanks to great-hearted Athena. These they piled together at the foot of the olive tree, off the path, for fear some passer-by might come upon them and plunder them before Odysseus awoke. Then they set off for home again.

But the earth-shaker had not forgotten the threats he had made at the first against godlike Odysseus, and he asked the will of Zeus: “Father Zeus, I shall no longer have any honor among the immortal gods, when mortals honor me not at all — the Phaeacians, who are of my own blood. I said just now that Odysseus should suffer many evils before he came home; his return I never took from him altogether, once you had promised it and bowed your head. But now they have carried him asleep over the sea in their swift ship and set him down in Ithaca, and given him gifts past counting — bronze and gold in plenty and woven clothing, more than Odysseus would ever have carried away from Troy, had he come home unscathed with his fair share of the spoil.”

Zeus the cloud-gatherer answered him: “Well now, wide-ruling earth-shaker, what a thing to say! The gods do not dishonor you at all; it would be a hard thing to fling dishonor at the eldest and the best of us. And if any man, giving in to his own strength and violence, fails to honor you, vengeance is yours, always, ever after. Do as you wish, and as your heart desires.” Then Poseidon the earth-shaker answered him: “I would have acted at once, lord of the dark clouds, just as you say; but I always stand in awe of your anger, and shrink from it. Now, then, I mean to smash that beautiful ship of the Phaeacians on the misty sea as she comes back from her convoy, so that at last they hold back and give up escorting men — and to fling a great mountain around their city.” And Zeus the cloud-gatherer answered him: “Friend, here is what seems best to my own mind: when all the people are watching from the city as she drives in, turn her to stone close to shore — a stone in the shape of a swift ship, so that all mankind may marvel — and fling the great mountain around their city.”

When Poseidon the earth-shaker heard this, he went off to Scheria, where the Phaeacians live, and there he waited. And she came in close, the seafaring ship, running lightly on her way; and the earth-shaker came close upon her and turned her to stone, and rooted her to the sea-floor with one downward stroke of the flat of his hand. And he was gone. But the Phaeacians of the long oars, men famous for their ships, spoke winged words to one another; and thus a man would say, glancing at his neighbor: “Ah me, who has bound our swift ship fast on the sea as she was running for home? Just now she was in full view.”

So a man would say; but they did not know how these things had come about. Then Alcinous addressed them and said: “Ah, now the old prophecies come home to me indeed — my father's words. He used to say that Poseidon bore a grudge against us, because we give safe passage to all comers; and he said that one day, as a beautiful ship of Phaeacian men was coming back from a convoy on the misty sea, the god would smash her, and would fling a great mountain around our city. So the old man said; and now it is all coming to pass. Come then, let us all obey what I say: give up your escorting of mortals, whenever anyone comes to our town; and let us sacrifice twelve chosen bulls to Poseidon, in hope that he may take pity, and not fling a towering mountain around our city.” So he spoke, and they were afraid, and made the bulls ready.

So the leaders and counselors of the Phaeacian people stood around the altar and prayed to lord Poseidon. And godlike Odysseus awoke out of his sleep in his own native land — and did not know it, he had been away so long; for the goddess Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus, had poured a mist around him, so that she might make him unrecognizable and tell him everything first, and so that neither his wife nor his townsmen nor his friends should know him before the suitors had paid in full for all their transgressions. And so everything looked strange to its own lord — the long unbroken footpaths, the harbors with their safe anchorage, the sheer rocks and the trees in their bloom.

He sprang to his feet and stood gazing at his native land; then he groaned aloud, struck both his thighs with the flats of his hands, and cried out in lament: “Ah me, whose country have I come to this time? Are they violent men, savage and lawless, or kind to strangers, with hearts that fear the gods? Where am I to carry all this treasure? And where am I to wander myself? Would that I had stayed there among the Phaeacians, and come instead to some other of the mighty kings, who would have welcomed me and sent me on my way home. As it is, I do not know where to put all this — and I cannot leave it here, or it may fall as plunder to other men. Ah, so they were not entirely wise or just after all, those leaders and counselors of the Phaeacians, who have carried me off to a strange land! They said they would bring me to far-seen Ithaca, and they have not done it. May Zeus, the god of suppliants, repay them — Zeus who watches over all men and punishes whoever goes astray. But come, let me count my goods and look them over, in case they carried something off with them in their hollow ship.”

So saying, he counted the beautiful tripods and the cauldrons, the gold and the fine woven clothing; and of it all he found nothing missing. But he mourned for his native land, dragging himself along the shore of the loud-roaring sea, weeping bitterly. And Athena came near him, in the form of a young man, a herder of sheep, delicate as the sons of kings are, wearing a fine double-folded cloak about her shoulders; she had sandals under her shining feet, and a javelin in her hand. Odysseus rejoiced at the sight of her, and came up to her and spoke winged words: “Friend, since you are the first I have met in this land, greetings — and may you come to me with no ill will in your heart. Save these goods, and save me; for I pray to you as to a god, and I come to your dear knees. And tell me this truly, so that I may know it well: what land is this, what people, what men are born here? Is it one of the far-seen islands, or some headland of the rich mainland sloping down into the sea?”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “You are a simple man, stranger, or you have come from far away, if you ask about this land. It is not so nameless as all that; a great many know it, both those who dwell toward the dawn and the sun, and those behind, toward the misty dark. It is rough country, true, no place for driving horses; yet it is not too poor, though it is not broad either. Grain grows on it past telling, and wine as well; the rain never fails it, nor the fresh dew; it is good pasture for goats and for cattle; there is wood of every kind, and watering places that never run dry. And so, stranger, the name of Ithaca has reached even to Troy — which, they say, is far indeed from Achaean land.”

So she spoke, and much-enduring godlike Odysseus rejoiced, glad in his own native land, at the word of Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus who bears the aegis; and he spoke winged words to her. Yet he did not speak the truth — he caught the word back, always plying the shrewd mind in his breast to his own advantage.

“I heard of Ithaca even in broad Crete, far over the sea; and now I have come here myself with these goods. I left as much again behind for my children when I fled — for I killed Idomeneus's dear son, swift-footed Orsilochus, who in broad Crete outran all bread-winning men with his quick feet, because he wanted to strip me of all my Trojan spoil, for which I had suffered pains in my heart, passing through the wars of men and the grievous waves — all because I would not oblige his father and serve under him in the land of Troy, but led my own company of comrades. I struck him with my bronze-tipped spear as he came in from the country, lying in wait with a comrade near the road; a pitch-dark night covered the sky, no man saw us, and I took his life unseen. When I had killed him with the sharp bronze, I went straight to a ship and begged the lordly Phoenicians, giving them a satisfying share of the spoil; I told them to carry me to Pylos and set me ashore there, or in shining Elis, where the Epeans hold power. But the force of the wind drove them off from there, much against their will — they had no wish to cheat me — and beaten off course we came here by night. We rowed hard into the harbor, and no one gave a thought to supper, though we sorely needed to take it; we all simply came off the ship and lay down as we were. Then sweet sleep came over me in my weariness, and they took my goods out of the hollow ship and set them down where I myself lay on the sand. Then they went aboard and left for well-peopled Sidon; and I was left behind, my heart aching.”

So he spoke, and the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, smiled, and stroked him with her hand; and now her form was that of a woman, beautiful and tall and skilled in glorious handiwork, and she spoke winged words to him: “Cunning he would have to be, and a master thief, who could get past you in all your tricks — even if it were a god who met you. Incorrigible man, subtle-minded, never tired of deceit — so not even in your own land were you going to give up the cheating and the thieving tales that you love from the bottom of your heart! But come, no more of this between us. We are both old hands at cunning: you are far the best of all mortals in counsel and in speech, and I among all the gods am famed for wit and craft. And yet you did not know Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus — I who always stand beside you and guard you in every labor, I who made you dear to all the Phaeacians. Now I have come here again, to weave a plan with you, and to hide the goods the noble Phaeacians gave you when you set out for home — by my own design and counsel — and to tell you all the troubles it is your fate to endure in your well-built house. You must bear them, for there is no choice; and tell no one, not one man or woman among them all, that you have come back from your wanderings. Suffer your many pains in silence, and submit to the violence of men.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “It is hard, goddess, for a mortal who meets you to know you, however wise he may be — for you take whatever likeness you please. But this I know well, that you were kind to me before, while we sons of the Achaeans were fighting at Troy. Yet after we had sacked Priam's steep city and gone aboard our ships, and a god scattered the Achaeans, I never saw you again, daughter of Zeus; I never noticed you coming aboard my ship to ward off any pain from me. No — I wandered on, with the heart in my breast forever torn, until the gods delivered me from my misery; not until I was in the rich land of the Phaeacian men did you hearten me with your words and lead me into the city yourself. Now I implore you by your father — for I do not believe I have come to far-seen Ithaca; it is some other land I am walking in, and I think you say all this in mockery, to lead my mind astray — tell me if it is really true that I have come to my own dear country.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “Always the same mind in that breast of yours! And that is why I cannot abandon you in your misfortune — because you are courteous, and quick-witted, and steady. Any other man coming home from his wanderings would have hurried gladly to his house to see his children and his wife; but you are not yet willing to know or to ask anything until you have tested your wife — who sits in your halls just as she always did, while her nights and her days waste away in sorrow as she weeps. As for me, I never doubted; I knew in my heart that you would come home, though with all your companions lost. But you must understand, I had no wish to fight Poseidon, my father's brother, who laid up anger against you in his heart, enraged that you blinded his dear son. Come now, I will show you the ground of Ithaca itself, so that you may believe. This is the harbor of Phorcys, the old man of the sea; here at the head of the harbor is the long-leafed olive; and near it is the lovely, shadowy cave, sacred to the nymphs who are called Naiads — that arching cavern is the very one where you used to offer the nymphs so many perfect hecatombs; and there is Mount Neriton, clothed in its forests.”

As she spoke, the goddess scattered the mist, and the land appeared. Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus rejoiced, glad in his own land, and he kissed the grain-giving earth. And at once he prayed to the nymphs, lifting up his hands: “Naiad nymphs, daughters of Zeus, I thought I should never see you again! Now I greet you with gentle prayers — and we will give you gifts as well, as in the old days, if the daughter of Zeus, the driver of the spoil, graciously grants that I myself may live, and brings my dear son to manhood.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, said to him: “Take heart; do not let such things trouble your mind. Rather let us stow the goods this very moment in a corner of the wondrous cave, so that they stay safe for you; and then let us consider together how everything may turn out for the best.” So speaking, the goddess went down into the shadowy cave, searching out its hiding places, while Odysseus brought everything up close — the gold and the unwearying bronze and the well-made clothing the Phaeacians had given him. He stowed it all carefully, and Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus who bears the aegis, set a stone against the entrance.

Then the two of them sat down at the foot of the sacred olive and plotted death for the arrogant suitors. The goddess, bright-eyed Athena, was first to speak: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, resourceful Odysseus, think how you are to lay your hands on the shameless suitors, who for three years now have lorded it in your hall, courting your godlike wife and offering bridal gifts; while she, forever mourning in her heart for your return, holds out hope to them all and makes promises to every man, sending out messages — but her mind is set on other things.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Ah — then I was to have perished in my own halls by the same evil fate as Agamemnon, son of Atreus, if you, goddess, had not told me everything as it truly stands. Come then, weave a plan for me to pay them back; and stand beside me yourself, filling me with bold courage, as on the day we loosed the shining battlements of Troy. If you would stand by me with that same fury, bright-eyed one, I would fight three hundred men — with you, great goddess, if in your grace you would come to my aid.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “I will be with you, never doubt it; you will not be out of my sight when we set about that work — and I think one and another of those suitors who devour your substance will spatter the wide floor with his blood and brains. But come, I will make you unrecognizable to every mortal: I will shrivel the fine skin on your supple limbs, strip the fair hair from your head, and wrap you in rags that a man would shudder to see on any human being; and I will dim those eyes of yours, so beautiful before, so that you will look wretched to all the suitors, and to your wife, and to the son you left in your halls. And you yourself — go first of all to the swineherd who keeps your pigs, whose heart is loyal to you, and who loves your son and steadfast Penelope. You will find him sitting by his swine; they are pasturing near the Rock of Corax and the spring Arethusa, eating acorns to their hearts' content and drinking the black water — the things that fatten pigs to rich abundance. Stay there; sit with him and ask him about everything, while I go to Sparta, the land of beautiful women, to summon Telemachus, your own dear son, Odysseus — for he has gone to spacious Lacedaemon, to Menelaus, seeking news of you, whether you were still alive anywhere.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Why then did you not tell him, you whose mind knows all things? So that he too might wander in misery over the barren sea, while other men devour his living?”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “Do not let him weigh too heavily on your heart. I myself gave him escort, so that he might win a good name by going there; he has no hardship at all, but sits at his ease in the house of the son of Atreus, with abundance all around him. It is true that young men lie in wait for him with a black ship, bent on killing him before he reaches his native land; but I do not think it will come to that — sooner the earth will close over one or another of those suitors who devour your substance.”

So speaking, Athena touched him with her wand. She shriveled the fine skin on his supple limbs, stripped the fair hair from his head, and set the skin of an aged old man over all his limbs; and she dimmed his eyes, so beautiful before. She threw around him different clothing — a foul rag and a tunic, tattered and filthy, grimed with reeking smoke — and over it all she dressed him in the great hide of a swift deer, worn bare of hair; and she gave him a staff and a wretched pouch, full of holes, slung on a twisted cord. So the two made their plans and parted; and she went off to shining Lacedaemon, to fetch the son of Odysseus.

## Book XIV

But Odysseus went up from the harbor along a rough path, through wooded country and over the heights, to where Athena had shown him the noble swineherd lived, the man who cared for his livelihood best of all the servants godlike Odysseus had acquired. He found him sitting in the porch of his lodge, where his courtyard was built up high, in a spot with a clear view all around, fine and large, with an open run about it. The swineherd had built it himself for the pigs of his absent master, without help from his mistress or old Laertes, out of hauled stones, and coped it with wild pear. Outside he had driven a line of stakes the whole way round, close-set and thick, split from the dark heart of oak; and inside the courtyard he had made twelve sties, close by one another, as beds for the pigs. In each of them fifty ground-sleeping sows were penned, breeding females; the boars slept outside, and were far fewer, for the godlike suitors kept thinning them by eating them, since the swineherd was forever sending in the best of all the well-fattened hogs; there were three hundred and sixty of them. Beside them four dogs, savage as wild beasts, always slept, which the swineherd, a leader of men, had reared. He himself was fitting sandals to his feet, cutting them from a good-colored oxhide, while the others had gone off, each a different way with the herded pigs — three of them; the fourth he had sent to town, forced to it, to bring a hog for the arrogant suitors, so that they could slaughter it and glut their appetite with meat.

Suddenly the baying dogs caught sight of Odysseus. They rushed at him, barking; but Odysseus had the cunning to sit down, and the staff dropped from his hand. Then and there, at his own farmstead, he would have suffered a shameful hurt; but the swineherd came quickly after them on swift feet, darting through the gateway, and the hide fell from his hand. He shouted at the dogs and scattered them this way and that with a shower of stones, and said to his master: “Old man, another moment and the dogs would have torn you to pieces, and you would have poured shame all over me. And the gods have given me pains and griefs enough besides: here I sit, mourning and grieving for a godlike master, and fatten hogs for other men to eat, while he, longing perhaps for a meal, wanders among the lands and towns of men of alien speech — if indeed he still lives somewhere and sees the light of the sun. But come with me, old man, let us go into the hut, so that you too, when you have had your fill of bread and wine, may tell me where you come from and all the troubles you have endured.”

So speaking, the noble swineherd led him to the hut, brought him in, and gave him a seat, piling thick brushwood underneath and spreading over it the skin of a shaggy wild goat, his own bedding, big and thick. Odysseus was glad to be welcomed so, and he spoke and addressed him: “May Zeus grant you, stranger, and the other immortal gods, whatever you most desire, since you have received me with such good will.” And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Stranger, it would not be right for me to slight a stranger, not even if one came worse off than you; for all strangers and beggars are from Zeus, and a gift from folk like us is small, but given with love. That is the way of servants, always afraid when the masters over them are young men. For truly the gods have barred the homecoming of that man who would have loved me devotedly and given me possessions of my own — a house, a plot of land, a much-courted wife — such things as a kind-hearted master gives the servant who has labored long for him and whose work a god makes prosper, as this work of mine prospers, that I hold to. For all this my master would have rewarded me richly, had he grown old here. But he is gone — and would that the whole breed of Helen had perished, root and branch, since she loosened the knees of so many men! For he too went, for the sake of Agamemnon's honor, to Ilium, land of fine horses, to fight the Trojans.”

So saying, he quickly belted up his tunic with his girdle and went off to the sties, where the tribes of young pigs were penned. He picked out two, brought them in, and slaughtered them both; he singed them, cut them up, and pierced the pieces on spits. When he had roasted it all, he carried it hot and set it before Odysseus, on the spits themselves, and sprinkled white barley meal over it; then he mixed honey-sweet wine in an ivy-wood bowl, sat down opposite him, and urged him on, saying: “Eat now, stranger, what a servant has to offer — young pork; the fatted hogs the suitors eat, with no thought in their hearts of the gods' watching eyes, and no pity. But the blessed gods do not love ruthless deeds; they honor justice and the righteous acts of men. Even cruel and lawless raiders, who land on foreign soil and Zeus grants them plunder, and they fill their ships and set off for home — even on their hearts falls a strong fear of the gods' vengeance. But these men must know something; they must have heard some god's voice telling of his wretched death, seeing that they will not court her honorably, nor go back to their own homes, but sit at their ease and devour his possessions with arrogance, and there is no sparing. Every night and every day that comes from Zeus, they slaughter victims — never one only, nor two only; and they draw off his wine and waste it just as arrogantly. For truly his livelihood was past telling. No lord had so much, not on the dark mainland nor on Ithaca itself; not twenty men together have wealth so great. I will count it out for you: twelve herds of cattle on the mainland; as many flocks of sheep; as many droves of pigs; as many wide-ranging herds of goats, grazed for him by foreigners and by his own herdsmen alike. And here, at the island's far end, eleven wide-ranging herds of goats in all are pastured, with good men watching over them. Every one of those men, day after day, drives a beast in for the suitors, whichever of the well-fed goats looks best. And I guard and keep these sows, and pick out the best of the pigs with care and send it to them.”

So he spoke, while Odysseus ate his meat hungrily and drank his wine, ravenous, in silence, sowing evil for the suitors. When he had dined and satisfied his heart with food, Eumaeus filled the cup he himself drank from and gave it to him brimming with wine; and he accepted it, glad at heart, and spoke winged words to him: “Friend, who was the man who bought you with his own wealth, a man as rich and powerful as you describe? You say he perished for the sake of Agamemnon's honor. Tell me — perhaps I may know him, if he was such a man. Zeus, I suppose, and the other immortal gods know whether I have seen him and could bring you news of him; for I have wandered far.”

Then the swineherd, a leader of men, answered him: “Old man, no wanderer who came here with news of him could persuade his wife and his dear son. Drifters in need of keep tell lies at random and have no wish to speak the truth. Every vagrant who makes his way to the land of Ithaca goes to my mistress and spins deceitful tales; and she receives him kindly, treats him well, and asks about every detail, while the tears fall from her eyelids as she grieves, as is the way of a wife whose husband has died far away. You too, old man, would be quick enough to stitch a story together, if someone would give you a cloak and tunic to wear. But him — by now the dogs and the swift birds must have torn the skin from his bones, and the life has gone out of him; or the fish have eaten him in the sea, and his bones lie on some shore, wrapped in deep sand. So he has perished out there, and grief is left behind for all his friends, and for me above all; for never again shall I find a master so gentle, wherever I go — not even if I came back to the house of my father and mother, where I was born and where they raised me themselves. Nor is it for them that I mourn so much any more, though I long to see them with my own eyes and be in my native land; no, it is longing for Odysseus, gone as he is, that takes hold of me. Even in his absence, stranger, I am shy of speaking his bare name, so much did he love me and care for me in his heart; I call him my honored friend, far away though he is.”

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus said to him: “Friend, since you deny it altogether, and insist that man will never come again, and your heart is fixed in disbelief — then I will not merely say it, I will say it on oath: Odysseus is coming home. And let my reward for the good news be paid the moment he arrives at his own house: dress me then in a cloak and tunic, fine clothing. Before that, however great my need, I will accept nothing; for hateful to me as the gates of Hades is the man who yields to poverty and spins deceitful tales. Now let Zeus be my witness, first among the gods, and this table of hospitality, and the hearth of blameless Odysseus to which I have come: truly all this shall be accomplished just as I say. Within this very year Odysseus will be here. Between the waning of this moon and the rising of the next, he will come home, and he will take vengeance on whoever dishonors his wife and his glorious son.”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Old man, I shall never pay that reward for good news, and Odysseus will never come home again. No — drink in peace, and let us turn our minds to other things; do not remind me of all this, for truly the heart in my breast aches whenever anyone speaks of my good master. As for the oath, we will let it be; but may Odysseus come, as I wish he would, and Penelope, and old Laertes, and godlike Telemachus. Now it is for the boy that I grieve without ceasing, for Telemachus, whom Odysseus fathered. The gods made him grow like a young sapling, and I used to think he would stand among men no whit behind his own dear father, admirable in build and looks; but some immortal — or some man — has damaged the balanced mind within him. He has gone for news of his father to sacred Pylos, and the lordly suitors are lying in ambush for him on his way home, so that the line of godlike Arceisius may be wiped out of Ithaca without a name. But let him be, too — whether he is caught, or whether he escapes and the son of Cronus stretches a hand over him. Come now, old man, tell me your own troubles. And tell me this truly, so that I may know it well: who are you, and where are you from? Where is your city, who are your parents? What kind of ship did you come on, and how did the sailors bring you to Ithaca? Who did they claim to be? For I do not imagine you came here on foot.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Very well, I will tell you all this quite truly. Would that the two of us had food and sweet wine enough to last a while, here inside the hut, to feast on quietly while others did the work; then I might easily go on for a whole year and still not finish the tale of my heart's sorrows — all that I have toiled through by the will of the gods. I claim my birth from broad Crete, the son of a wealthy man. Many other sons were born and raised in his hall, true-born sons of his wife; but the mother who bore me was bought, a concubine. Yet Castor son of Hylax — his stock I claim to be — honored me equally with his lawful sons. In those days he was honored like a god among the Cretans, for his good fortune, his wealth, and his glorious sons. But the death-spirits came and carried him off to the house of Hades; and his high-hearted sons divided up his living and cast lots for it, and to me they gave a very small share, with a house to go with it. I took a wife from a family of many holdings, winning her by my own worth, for I was no fool and no shirker in battle. Now all that is gone; yet even so, looking at the stubble, I think you can tell what the grain once was — though trouble past measure has hold of me now.

“Ares and Athena gave me courage, and the power that breaks the ranks of men. Whenever I picked the best men for an ambush, sowing evil for the enemy, my proud heart never looked ahead to death; far the first of all I would leap out and take my man with the spear, whichever of the enemy gave way before me in flight. Such a man I was in war. But field work was never dear to me, nor the care of a household, which rears fine children; what I always loved were oared ships, and wars, and polished javelins, and arrows — grim things, that make other men shudder. But I loved what a god, I suppose, had put into my heart; for one man delights in one kind of work, another in another.

“Before the sons of the Achaeans ever set foot on Troy, nine times I led men and swift-faring ships against foreign peoples, and a great deal fell into my hands. Out of the spoil I would pick what pleased me, and much came to me afterward by lot; so my house grew rich quickly, and from then on I was feared and respected among the Cretans. But when far-seeing Zeus devised that hateful expedition which loosened the knees of so many men, then they pressed me and famous Idomeneus to lead the ships to Ilium; and there was no way to refuse, for the hard voice of the people held us. There we sons of the Achaeans fought for nine years, and in the tenth we sacked Priam's city and set off homeward with our ships; and a god scattered the Achaeans. But for me, unlucky man, Zeus the counselor was devising evil. One month only I stayed, taking pleasure in my children, my wedded wife, and my possessions; and then my heart urged me to fit out ships and sail to Egypt with my godlike companions. Nine ships I fitted out, and the crews gathered quickly.

“For six days then my trusty companions feasted, and I provided victims in plenty, for sacrifice to the gods and for their own table. On the seventh we went aboard and sailed from broad Crete before a fine, fresh North Wind, easily, as if we were running downstream; not one of my ships came to harm, but we sat unscathed and free of sickness, while the wind and the steersmen kept them on course. On the fifth day we reached the fair-flowing river of Egypt, and in the river of Egypt I moored my curved ships. Then I ordered my trusty companions to stay right there by the ships and guard them, and I sent scouts out to the lookout posts. But my men gave way to insolence, and following their own impulse they at once began to ravage the beautiful fields of the Egyptians, carrying off the women and little children and killing the men. The cry soon reached the city, and at the first showing of dawn the townsmen came, hearing the shouting; the whole plain was filled with foot-soldiers and horses and the flash of bronze. And Zeus who delights in thunder cast an evil panic among my companions, and not one dared stand and face the enemy, for ruin ringed us round on every side. Then they killed many of us with the sharp bronze, and others they led away alive, to work for them by compulsion.

“But as for me, Zeus himself put this thought into my mind — would that I had died instead and met my fate right there in Egypt, for sorrow was still waiting to receive me! At once I took the well-made helmet from my head and the shield from my shoulders, and let the spear drop from my hand, and went straight toward the king's chariot, and clasped his knees and kissed them. He spared me and took pity on me, and seated me in his chariot and brought me, weeping, to his home. Many men indeed came rushing at me with their ash-wood spears, eager to kill me, for they were bitterly angry; but he kept them off, in awe of the wrath of Zeus, the god of strangers, who above all others is angered by evil deeds.

“There I stayed seven years, and gathered much wealth among the Egyptians, for they all gave to me. But when the eighth circling year came round, then there came a Phoenician, a man skilled in deceit, a greedy knave, who had already done men a great deal of harm. He won me over with his cunning and took me with him, until we came to Phoenicia, where his house and his goods lay. There I stayed with him a full year. But when the months and days were fulfilled, and the year had rolled round and the seasons came on again, he put me aboard a sea-going ship bound for Libya, with lying schemes: I was to help him convey a cargo, but in truth he meant to sell me there and take an enormous price. I went with him aboard, suspecting all of it, but I had no choice. The ship ran before a fine, fresh North Wind, on the mid-sea course above Crete; and Zeus was devising their destruction. When we had left Crete behind, and no other land was in sight, only sky and sea, then the son of Cronus set a blue-black cloud over the hollow ship, and the sea darkened beneath it. Zeus thundered and hurled his bolt into the ship at the same instant; struck by the bolt of Zeus she shivered from end to end, and filled with sulphur; and every man fell overboard. Like sea-crows they were carried on the waves around the black ship, and the god took away their homecoming. But as for me, in all the anguish of my heart, Zeus himself put the huge mast of the dark-prowed ship into my hands, so that I might still escape ruin. I wound my arms around it and was borne along by the deadly winds. Nine days I was carried; on the tenth, in the black night, a great rolling wave brought me to the land of the Thesprotians.

“There the king of the Thesprotians, the hero Pheidon, took me in and asked no ransom; for his dear son had come upon me, beaten down by cold and exhaustion, and raised me up by the hand and led me home, until he reached his father's house; and he clothed me in a cloak and tunic. It was there that I heard of Odysseus. The king said he had hosted him and treated him kindly as he passed on his way to his native land, and he showed me all the treasure Odysseus had gathered — bronze and gold and iron wrought with much labor. Truly it would feed a man's line to the tenth generation, so great was the store of it lying in the king's halls. Odysseus himself, he said, had gone to Dodona, to hear the will of Zeus from the god's high-crowned oak — how he should return to the rich land of Ithaca after so long away, openly or in secret. And he swore to me, to my face, pouring a libation in his house, that a ship was drawn down to the sea and a crew standing ready to convey him to his own dear country.

“But me he sent off first; for a ship of Thesprotian men happened to be leaving for Dulichium, rich in wheat. He told them to bring me there with all care to king Acastus. But an evil plan about me found favor in their hearts, so that I should sink into the very depths of misery. When the sea-going ship was well away from land, they set about contriving the day of slavery for me. They stripped me of my clothing, my cloak and tunic, and threw around me instead this wretched rag and this tunic, all tatters, which you see now with your own eyes; and in the evening they reached the fields of far-seen Ithaca. There they bound me fast in the well-benched ship with a twisted rope, and themselves went ashore and hurriedly took their supper by the edge of the sea. But the gods themselves loosened my bonds easily; and wrapping the rag about my head, I slid down the smooth lading-plank, brought my chest to the sea, and then struck out with both arms, swimming, and very quickly I was out of the water and clear of them. I climbed up to where a thicket of flowering woodland stood, and lay there crouching. They ranged about, groaning loudly; but when there seemed no profit in searching further, they went back again aboard their hollow ship. The gods themselves hid me easily, and led me on and brought me to the farmstead of an understanding man; for it is still my portion to live.”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Poor stranger, truly you have stirred my heart, telling all this — all you have suffered and all your wandering. But one part of it, I think, is not in order, and you will not persuade me: what you said about Odysseus. Why should a man like you tell useless lies? I know only too well, myself, about my master's homecoming: how he was hated utterly by all the gods, since they did not bring him down among the Trojans, nor in the arms of his friends after he had wound up the war. Then the whole body of Achaeans would have built him a grave-mound, and he would have won great glory for his son as well, for time to come. But now the storm-spirits have snatched him away without glory. As for me, I keep apart, here with my pigs; I do not go to town, unless perhaps circumspect Penelope urges me to come, when news arrives from somewhere. Then they all sit around the newcomer and question him on every point — both those who grieve for their long-absent master, and those who rejoice in eating up his livelihood unpunished. But I have no taste for asking and probing, ever since an Aetolian fooled me with his story — a man who had killed someone, and wandered over much of the earth, and came to my house; and I welcomed him warmly. He said he had seen Odysseus among the Cretans, at the house of Idomeneus, repairing the ships that storms had shattered; and he said he would come home either by summer or by harvest-time, bringing much treasure, with his godlike companions. And you, old man of many sorrows, since some spirit has brought you to me — do not try to please me with lies, do not try to charm me. It is not for that that I will respect you and treat you kindly, but out of fear of Zeus, the god of strangers, and out of pity for you yourself.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Truly there is a heart in your breast that will not be persuaded — since not even with an oath could I win you over or convince you. Come then, let us make a bargain, and hereafter let the gods who hold Olympus be witnesses for us both. If your master comes home to this house, you shall clothe me in a cloak and tunic and send me on my way to Dulichium, where my heart desires to be. But if your master does not come as I say, set your men on me and throw me from a great cliff, so that the next beggar may think twice before he tries deception.”

And the noble swineherd answered him: “Stranger, a fine name and reputation for goodness I would win among men, now and for ever after, if, having brought you into my hut and given you a guest's welcome, I then killed you and took your dear life! With a clean heart, after that, I could pray to Zeus, son of Cronus! But now it is time for supper. I hope my companions will soon be in, so that we can make ready a savory meal in the hut.”

So they spoke to one another; and now the pigs and the men who herd them came near. They shut the sows into their accustomed pens to sleep, and an immense squealing rose from the pigs as they were folded. Then the noble swineherd called to his companions: “Bring the best of the hogs, so that I may slaughter it for my guest from a far country — and we shall have some good of it ourselves, we who have long borne toil and misery for the sake of these white-tusked pigs, while other men eat up our labor unpunished.”

So saying, he split firewood with the pitiless bronze, while the others brought in a hog, five years old and very fat, and stood it by the hearth. Nor did the swineherd forget the immortals, for he was a man of good understanding: he began the rite by casting bristles from the head of the white-tusked hog into the fire, and prayed to all the gods that wise Odysseus might return to his own home. Then, drawing himself up, he struck the hog with a billet of oak he had left over from his splitting, and the life went out of it. The others cut its throat and singed it, and quickly divided it up; and the swineherd laid raw first-pieces from all the limbs in the rich fat, and cast them into the fire, sprinkling barley meal over them; and they cut up the rest, and pierced it on spits, and roasted it all carefully, and drew it off, and heaped it together on platters. Then the swineherd stood up to carve, for his heart knew what was fair, and he cut it all up and divided it into seven portions. One he set aside, with a prayer, for the nymphs and for Hermes, son of Maia, and the rest he distributed to each man; but Odysseus he honored with the whole length of the chine of the white-tusked hog, and made his master's heart glad.

Then resourceful Odysseus spoke to him, saying: “Eumaeus, may you be as dear to father Zeus as you are to me, since you honor a man in my state with good things.” And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Eat, my strange guest, and take pleasure in what is here, such as it is. A god will give one thing and let another go, just as his heart pleases; for he can do all things.” So he spoke, and burned the first-offerings to the gods who live forever; and when he had poured a libation of gleaming wine, he set the cup in the hands of Odysseus, sacker of cities, and sat down by his own portion.

And bread was served to them by Mesaulius, a man the swineherd had acquired by himself while his master was away, without help from his mistress or old Laertes, buying him from the Taphians with his own goods. They reached out their hands to the good things spread before them. And when they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, Mesaulius cleared away the bread, and they hurried off to their beds, filled with bread and meat.

Now night came on, foul and moonless; Zeus rained the whole night through, and the great West Wind, always heavy with rain, kept blowing. Then Odysseus spoke among them, testing the swineherd, to see whether he would strip off his own cloak and give it to him, or tell one of his companions to, since he cared for him so much.

“Listen now, Eumaeus, and all you other companions, and I will tell you a boastful tale; the wine commands me — witless wine, which sets even a very sensible man to singing and soft laughter, and stirs him up to dance, and brings out some word that were better left unsaid. But since I have once opened my mouth, I will not hide it. Would that I were young, and my strength as firm, as when we made ready an ambush and led it under the walls of Troy. Odysseus led it, and Menelaus, son of Atreus, and I was third in command with them, for so they themselves bade me. When we came up to the city and the steep wall, we lay around the town in the thick brushwood, among the reeds and the marsh, crouched under our arms; and a foul night came on as the North Wind dropped, freezing; snow came down on us from above like hoarfrost, bitter cold, and ice crusted thick on our shields. There all the others had cloaks and tunics, and slept in peace, their shoulders wrapped in their shields; but I, when I set out, had left my cloak behind with my companions, like a fool — I never thought I would be cold at all — and had come along with only my shield and my bright waist-guard.

“But when it was the third watch of the night, and the stars had crossed over, then I spoke to Odysseus, who was close beside me, nudging him with my elbow; and he listened at once: ‘Zeus-born son of Laertes, resourceful Odysseus, I shall not be much longer among the living. This cold is finishing me: I have no cloak. Some spirit fooled me into wearing only my tunic, and now there is no way out.’ So I spoke; and he then formed this plan in his heart — such a man he was for counsel and for battle. Speaking in a low voice, he said to me: ‘Quiet now, before some other Achaean hears you.’ Then he propped his head on his elbow and spoke out: ‘Listen, friends: a divine dream came to me in my sleep. We have come very far from the ships; will someone go and tell Agamemnon, son of Atreus, shepherd of the people, in hopes he may order more men out from the ships?’ So he spoke, and Thoas, son of Andraemon, sprang up quickly, threw off his crimson cloak, and set out running for the ships; and I lay in his cloak gladly, until Dawn of the golden throne appeared. Would that I were young now, as I was then, and my strength as firm; then one of the swineherds in this farmstead would give me a cloak, both out of kindness and out of respect for a good man. As it is, they scorn me for the wretched clothes on my skin.”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Old man, that is a blameless tale you have told, and not a word of it so far has been off the mark or without its profit. So you shall lack neither clothing nor anything else that is fitting for a hard-tried suppliant who crosses one's path — for tonight; but in the morning you will shake out your own rags again. There are not many cloaks here, or changes of tunic to put on — just one apiece for each man. But when the dear son of Odysseus comes, he himself will give you a cloak and tunic for clothing, and send you wherever your heart and spirit bid you go.”

So saying, he sprang up and set a bed for him near the fire, and threw onto it skins of sheep and goats. There Odysseus lay down, and over him Eumaeus threw a cloak, thick and large, which he kept by him as a spare, to put on whenever some terrible storm arose.

So Odysseus slept there, and beside him slept the young men. But the swineherd had no liking for a bed in that place, sleeping away from his pigs, and he made ready to go outside; and Odysseus was glad that he took such care of his absent master's livelihood. First he slung a sharp sword over his sturdy shoulders, then wrapped himself in a cloak to keep off the wind, very thick, and took up the fleece of a large, well-fed goat; and he took a sharp javelin, to ward off dogs and men. Then he went out to lie down where the white-tusked boars slept, under a hollow rock, in the lee of the North Wind.

## Book XV

Pallas Athena, meanwhile, went to spacious Lacedaemon, to remind the glorious son of great-hearted Odysseus of his homecoming and stir him to set out. She found Telemachus and Nestor's splendid son sleeping in the porch of glorious Menelaus's house — the son of Nestor, at least, overcome by soft sleep; but sweet sleep did not hold Telemachus, and all through the immortal night cares for his father kept his heart awake. Standing close beside him, bright-eyed Athena spoke:

“Telemachus, it is no longer good for you to wander far from home, leaving your possessions behind, and men in your house so overbearing. Take care they do not divide up and devour everything you own, while your journey comes to nothing. Rouse Menelaus of the loud war cry as quickly as you can to send you on your way, so that you may still find your blameless mother at home. For already her father and brothers are urging her to marry Eurymachus, since he outdoes all the suitors in his gifts and keeps raising the bride-price; take care she does not carry some treasure out of the house against your will. You know what kind of heart a woman has in her breast: she wants to enrich the house of the man who marries her, and of her former children and her dear wedded husband, once he is dead, she thinks no more, nor asks after them. Go yourself, then, and put everything in the charge of whichever of the serving-women seems best to you, until the gods reveal to you a noble bride.

“And another thing I will tell you — lay it up in your heart. The best men among the suitors are lying in wait for you, deliberately, in the strait between Ithaca and rugged Same, bent on killing you before you reach your native land. But I do not think it will come to that; sooner will the earth close over more than one of the suitors who are eating up your livelihood. Keep your well-made ship well clear of the islands, and sail by night as well as by day; that one of the immortals who guards and protects you will send a following wind behind you. And when you reach the first shore of Ithaca, send the ship and all your companions on to the city, but go yourself, before anything else, to the swineherd who keeps watch over your pigs and is loyal to you as ever. Spend the night there, and send him into the city to carry word to wise Penelope that you are safe and have come back from Pylos.”

So she spoke, and departed to high Olympus. But Telemachus woke the son of Nestor from his sweet sleep, nudging him with his foot, and said to him: “Wake up, Peisistratus, son of Nestor. Bring out the single-hoofed horses and yoke them to the chariot, so we can get on with our journey.” Then Peisistratus, Nestor's son, answered him: “Telemachus, however eager we are for the road, there is no driving through the dark of night — and dawn will soon be here. Wait, rather, until the hero son of Atreus, spear-famed Menelaus, brings his gifts and sets them in the chariot, and sends us off with kind words of farewell. For a guest remembers all his days the host who has shown him kindness.”

So he spoke, and at once Dawn came on her golden throne. And Menelaus of the loud war cry came up to them, risen from bed, from the side of fair-haired Helen. When the dear son of Odysseus saw him, he hurriedly drew his shining tunic about him and threw his great cloak over his sturdy shoulders, and went out to the door; and standing beside him, Telemachus, dear son of godlike Odysseus, said: “Menelaus, son of Atreus, fostered of Zeus, leader of peoples, send me back now at last to my own dear country; for now my heart longs to be home.”

Menelaus of the loud war cry answered him: “Telemachus, I will not keep you here long, when you are longing for home. I would blame any other host as well who went too far, either in kindness or in unkindness; due measure is better in all things. It is equal wrong to push a guest out the door who does not want to go, and to hold back one who is eager to leave. Treat a guest kindly while he is with you, and send him off when he wishes. But wait until I bring fine gifts and set them in your chariot — you shall see them with your own eyes — and until I tell the women to prepare a meal in the halls from the plenty stored within. It is honor and glory both, and a benefit besides, to dine before setting out over the wide, boundless earth. And if you would like to turn aside through Hellas and the heart of Argos, I will come with you myself; I will yoke horses for you and guide you to the cities of men, and no one will send us away just as we came — each will give us at least one thing to carry off, a fine bronze tripod or a cauldron, or a pair of mules, or a golden cup.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Menelaus, son of Atreus, fostered of Zeus, leader of peoples, I would rather go straight home now; for when I left I set no one to watch over my possessions. I fear I may perish myself while searching for my godlike father, or some fine treasure may vanish from my halls.”

When Menelaus of the loud war cry heard this, at once he told his wife and her maids to prepare a meal in the halls from the plenty stored within. And Eteoneus, son of Boethous, came up to him, just risen from bed, for he lived not far away; Menelaus of the loud war cry told him to light a fire and roast some meat, and he heard and did not disobey. Menelaus himself went down into his fragrant storeroom — not alone; Helen went with him, and Megapenthes. When they came to where his treasures lay, the son of Atreus took up a two-handled cup, and told his son Megapenthes to carry a mixing-bowl of silver. Helen stood by the chests where her richly embroidered robes lay, robes she had worked with her own hands. One of these Helen, shining among women, lifted out and carried — the one that was largest and most beautiful in its embroidery; it shone like a star, and lay beneath all the others.

They went on through the house until they came to Telemachus; and fair-haired Menelaus said to him: “Telemachus, may Zeus, the loud-thundering husband of Hera, bring your homecoming to pass just as your heart desires it. And of all the gifts that lie as treasures in my house, I will give you the one that is most beautiful and most precious. I will give you a mixing-bowl of wrought work: it is all silver, and its rims are finished with gold, the work of Hephaestus; the hero Phaedimus gave it to me, the king of the Sidonians, when his house sheltered me on my journey home. This I wish to give to you.” So saying, the hero son of Atreus put the two-handled cup into his hands; and strong Megapenthes carried the shining mixing-bowl of silver and set it before him; and Helen of the lovely cheeks stood by with the robe in her hands, and spoke and called him by name: “I too have a gift for you, dear child — this, a keepsake of Helen's hands, for your bride to wear on the longed-for day of your wedding; until then let it lie in your dear mother's keeping in her hall. And may you come with joy to your well-built house and your own native land.” So saying, she put it in his hands, and he received it gladly.

The hero Peisistratus took the gifts and stowed them in the chariot's basket, marveling at them all in his heart. Then fair-haired Menelaus led them into the house, and the two took their seats on couches and chairs. A servant brought water in a fine golden pitcher and poured it over a silver basin for their hands to be washed, and drew up a polished table beside them. The grave housekeeper brought bread and set it before them, adding many good things, generous with what she had; nearby the son of Boethous carved the meat and shared out the portions, and the son of glorious Menelaus poured the wine. And they reached out their hands to the good things spread before them. When they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, then Telemachus and Nestor's splendid son yoked the horses, mounted the inlaid chariot, and drove out of the gateway and the echoing colonnade. After them came the son of Atreus, fair-haired Menelaus, carrying honey-hearted wine in a golden cup in his right hand, so that they might pour a libation before they went. He stood in front of the horses, and pledged them, and said: “Farewell, young men — and give my greetings to Nestor, shepherd of the people; for truly he was gentle as a father to me, while we sons of the Achaeans made war at Troy.” Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Indeed, fostered of Zeus, we will tell him all this when we come, just as you say. And would that I were as sure, when I return to Ithaca, of finding Odysseus at home, to tell him how I came away from you having met with every kindness, bringing with me many fine treasures.”

Even as he spoke, a bird flew by on his right — an eagle, carrying in its talons a huge white goose, a tame bird from the yard, with men and women running after it, shouting. It swept close by them and darted to the right in front of the horses; and at the sight their hearts lifted, and the spirit warmed in every breast. Peisistratus, Nestor's son, was the first to speak: “Consider, Menelaus, fostered of Zeus, leader of peoples, whether the god showed this sign for the two of us, or for you yourself.” So he spoke, and Menelaus, dear to Ares, pondered how he should think it through and answer rightly. But long-robed Helen was before him, and spoke: “Hear me, and I will prophesy as the immortals put it in my heart, and as I believe it will come to pass. Just as this eagle came from the mountain, where its kin and its birthplace are, and snatched up the goose fattened in the house, so Odysseus, after many sufferings and many wanderings, will come home and take his revenge — or he is home already, and is sowing evil for all the suitors.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “May Zeus grant it so, the loud-thundering husband of Hera; then I would pray to you there as to a god.” He spoke, and laid the whip on the horses; and they sprang forward eagerly, racing through the city toward the plain. All day long they shook the yoke they bore between them. The sun set and all the ways grew dark; and they came to Pherae, to the house of Diocles, son of Ortilochus, whom Alpheius fathered. There they spent the night, and he set the gifts of hospitality before them. When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, they yoked the horses, mounted the inlaid chariot, and drove out of the gateway and the echoing colonnade. He touched the horses with the whip, and the pair flew on, nothing loath. Soon they came to the steep citadel of Pylos.

Then Telemachus spoke to the son of Nestor: “Son of Nestor, will you make me a promise, and carry it through as I ask? We claim to be guest-friends from of old, through our fathers' friendship, and we are of one age besides; and this journey will bind us still closer in mind. Do not take me past my ship, fostered of Zeus, but leave me there — or the old man will keep me in his house against my will, in his eagerness to show me kindness; and I must get home quickly.” So he spoke, and the son of Nestor debated in his heart how he might rightly promise it and perform it. And as he thought, this seemed to him the better way: he turned the horses toward the swift ship and the seashore, and unloaded the beautiful gifts into the ship's stern, the clothing and the gold that Menelaus had given him; and urging Telemachus on, he spoke winged words: “Hurry aboard now, and order all your crew to do the same, before I reach home and tell the old man. For I know this well in my mind and heart: so overbearing is his spirit, he will not let you go — he will come down here himself to summon you, and I tell you he will not go back empty-handed; for come what may, he will be very angry.” So saying, he drove the fair-maned horses back to the city of the Pylians, and quickly reached the palace. And Telemachus called to his companions and gave his orders: “Set the gear in order, comrades, in the black ship, and let us go aboard ourselves, so we can get on with our journey.” So he spoke, and they listened well and obeyed; quickly they went aboard and sat down at the oarlocks.

So he was busy with all this, and was praying and sacrificing to Athena by the ship's stern, when a man came up to him from a far country, in flight from Argos because he had killed a man — a seer, of the lineage of Melampus. Melampus in earlier days had lived in Pylos, mother of flocks, a rich man with a house among the Pylians second to none; but then he came to a land of other men, fleeing his homeland and great-hearted Neleus, most lordly of living men, who had seized his great wealth and held it by force for a full year. All that while Melampus lay bound in cruel bonds in the halls of Phylacus, suffering bitter pains for the sake of Neleus's daughter, and for the heavy madness that the goddess, the house-wrecking Fury, had laid upon his mind. But he escaped his fate, and drove the loud-lowing cattle from Phylace to Pylos, and paid back godlike Neleus for the shameful deed, and brought the woman home to be his brother's wife. He himself went to a land of other men, to horse-pasturing Argos; for there it was fated for him to live, ruling over many Argives.

There he married a wife and built a high-roofed house, and fathered Antiphates and Mantius, two strong sons. Antiphates fathered great-hearted Oicles, and Oicles fathered Amphiaraus, rouser of armies, whom Zeus who bears the aegis and Apollo loved from the heart with every kind of love; yet he did not reach the threshold of old age, but perished at Thebes because of a woman's gifts. To him were born sons, Alcmaeon and Amphilochus.
