Mantius in his turn fathered Polypheides and Cleitus; but Cleitus golden-throned Dawn snatched away for his beauty, that he might dwell among the immortals; and high-hearted Polypheides Apollo made a seer, far the best among mortals, once Amphiaraus was dead. He moved away to Hyperesia in anger at his father, and lived there, prophesying to all mankind.

It was his son who now came up, and Theoclymenus was his name. He found Telemachus pouring libations and praying beside his swift black ship, and he spoke winged words to him: “Friend, since I find you making offerings in this place, I beg you by your offerings and by the god, and then by your own life and the lives of the companions who follow you: tell me truly what I ask, and hide nothing. Who are you, and from where among men? Where is your city, who are your parents?”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Well then, stranger, I will tell you quite truly. Ithaca is my country by birth, and my father is Odysseus — if he ever lived; but by now he has surely perished by some grim death. That is why I took my companions and a black ship, and came to seek news of my father, so long gone.” Then godlike Theoclymenus said to him: “So I too am out of my country, for killing a man of my own kin; and he has many brothers and kinsmen in horse-pasturing Argos, with great power over the Achaeans. I am fleeing to escape death and black fate at their hands, since now it is my lot to wander among men. Take me aboard your ship, since in my flight I have come to you as a suppliant — or they will kill me; for I believe they are in pursuit.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “I will not turn you away from my trim ship when you wish to come. Follow, then; and there you will be made welcome with such as we have.” So saying, he took the man's bronze spear from him and laid it along the deck of the curved ship; and he himself stepped aboard the seagoing vessel, sat down in the stern, and seated Theoclymenus beside him; and the crew cast off the stern lines. Telemachus called to his companions and told them to lay hands on the tackle, and they obeyed with a will. They raised the pine mast and set it upright in the hollow mast-box, made it fast with the forestays, and hauled up the white sails with the well-twisted ox-hide ropes. And bright-eyed Athena sent them a favoring wind, rushing hard through the clear sky, so that the ship might run her course most swiftly through the salt water of the sea. They ran past Crouni and Chalcis of the lovely streams. The sun set and all the ways grew dark; and the ship bore down on Pheae, driven by the wind of Zeus, and on past bright Elis, where the Epeians hold power. From there he steered her toward the pointed islands, wondering whether he would escape death or be caught.

Meanwhile, in the hut, Odysseus and the noble swineherd were at supper, and the other men supped beside them. When they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, Odysseus spoke among them, testing the swineherd, whether he would still show him warm kindness and press him to stay there at the farm, or send him on to the city: “Listen now, Eumaeus, and all you other companions. At dawn I want to go off to the town to beg, so as not to wear you down, you and your men. Only give me good directions, and a good guide with me to take me there; and through the city I will drift on my own, as need drives me, in hope someone may hand me a cup and a crust. And I would go to the house of godlike Odysseus, and give my news to wise Penelope, and mingle with the overbearing suitors, to see whether they might give me a dinner out of the endless good things they have. I could serve them well at once, in whatever they wanted. For I will tell you something — mark it and listen: by the favor of Hermes the guide, who lends grace and glory to all men's work, no other mortal could rival me in service — laying a fire well, splitting dry wood, carving and roasting and pouring the wine, all the things that lesser men do in the service of the great.”

Then, deeply troubled, you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Ah, stranger, how did such a notion ever get into your head? You must be set on perishing there on the spot, if you really mean to plunge into that crowd of suitors, whose insolence and violence reach the iron sky. Their serving-men are nothing like you: young men, well dressed in cloaks and tunics, their heads always sleek and their faces handsome — those are the ones who wait on them; and the polished tables groan under bread and meat and wine. No — stay here. No one is troubled by your being here, not I nor any other of the companions who are with me. And when Odysseus's dear son comes, he will clothe you in a cloak and tunic, and send you on wherever your heart and spirit bid you go.”

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus answered him: “Eumaeus, may you be as dear to father Zeus as you are to me, for putting an end to my wandering and my dreadful misery. There is nothing worse for mortals than a roving life; it is for the sake of the cursed belly that men suffer such bitter cares, when wandering and hardship and pain come upon them. But now, since you keep me here and tell me to wait for him, come, tell me about the mother of godlike Odysseus, and his father, whom he left on the threshold of old age when he went away. Are they perhaps still alive under the rays of the sun, or dead by now and in the house of Hades?”

Then the swineherd, leader of men, answered him: “Well then, stranger, I will tell you quite truly. Laertes still lives, but he prays to Zeus forever that the life may waste out of his limbs in his own halls; for he grieves beyond measure for his son who is gone, and for his wise wedded wife, whose death hurt him more than anything and brought him to a raw old age. She died of grief for her glorious son — a miserable death; may no one die so who lives here as a friend to me and does me kindness. As long as she was alive, for all her sorrow, it was always dear to me to ask after her and question her, because she brought me up herself, together with long-robed Ctimene, her stately daughter, the youngest of the children she bore; with her I was raised, and her mother honored me little less.

“But when we both reached the lovely prime of youth, they gave her in marriage to a man of Same, and got countless bridal gifts; and me her mother dressed in a cloak and tunic, fine clothes indeed, gave me sandals for my feet, and sent me out to the farm — but she loved me all the more in her heart. Now I go without all that; yet the blessed gods make the work prosper that I keep to, and from it I have eaten and drunk and given to those who deserve respect. But from the mistress there is no kind word or kind deed to be had any more, since that evil fell upon the house — those overbearing men; and servants miss it sorely, speaking face to face with their mistress and hearing all the news, and eating and drinking, and then carrying something back to the farm besides — the sort of things that always warm a servant's heart.”

And resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Well now, swineherd Eumaeus — so you were only a little child when you were carried so far from your country and your parents! Come, tell me this, and tell me truly: was the wide-streeted city of your people sacked, the city where your father and honored mother lived? Or were you alone with the sheep or the cattle when hostile men took you in their ships and sold you overseas to this man's house — and he paid a fair price for you?” Then the swineherd, leader of men, answered him: “Stranger, since you ask me this and want to know, listen now in silence, and take your pleasure, and sit and drink your wine. These nights are endless. There is time enough to sleep, and time to listen and enjoy it; you need not lie down before the hour comes — too much sleep is a weariness itself. As for the rest of you, whoever's heart and spirit move him, let him go out and sleep; and at daybreak, after a meal, let him go out with the master's pigs. But we two will drink and feast in the hut, and take our pleasure in each other's bitter sorrows as we recall them; for afterward a man finds pleasure even in his pains, when he has suffered much and wandered far. So I will tell you what you ask me and want to know.

“There is an island called Syria — you may have heard of it — above Ortygia, where the turnings of the sun are. It is not so very thickly peopled, but it is a good land, rich in cattle and rich in sheep, full of wine and full of wheat. Famine never comes into that country, nor does any other hateful sickness fall upon wretched mortals; but when the tribes of men grow old there in the city, Apollo of the silver bow comes with Artemis, and visits them with his gentle arrows and lays them low. There are two cities on it, and everything is divided between them; and over both my father was king, Ctesius, son of Ormenus, a man like the immortals.

“There Phoenicians came, famous seamen, greedy rogues, bringing countless trinkets in their black ship. Now in my father's house there was a Phoenician woman, tall and beautiful and skilled in fine handiwork; and the wily Phoenicians set about beguiling her. First, while she was washing clothes, one of them lay with her beside the hollow ship in the bed of love — the thing that beguiles the wits of womankind, even one who does her work well. Then he asked her who she was and where she came from; and at once she pointed out my father's high-roofed house: ‘I claim my birth from Sidon, rich in bronze, and I am the daughter of Arybas, a man of flowing wealth; but Taphian pirates snatched me away as I was coming in from the country, brought me here and sold me into this man's house — and he paid a fair price for me.’

“Then the man who had lain with her in secret said: ‘Would you come home again with us, then, to see the high-roofed house of your father and mother, and see them too? For they are still alive, and are called wealthy.’ And the woman answered him: ‘Even that might be, if you sailors were willing to bind yourselves by oath to bring me home unharmed.’ So she spoke, and they all swore as she asked. And when they had sworn and completed the oath, the woman spoke among them again: ‘Silence now — let none of your crew say a word to me if he meets me in the street or perhaps at the spring, in case someone should go to the house and tell the old man, and he should grow suspicious and bind me in cruel bonds, and plot destruction for you. Keep the plan in your minds, and press on with the buying of your homeward cargo. But when the ship is full of goods, then let word come to me quickly at the house; for I will bring gold too, whatever comes under my hand. And there is something else I would gladly give as my passage-fee: I nurse a child of my noble master's in the house, a clever little thing, who runs along beside me out of doors. I would bring him aboard, and he would fetch you an enormous price, wherever you sold him among men of a foreign tongue.’

“So saying, she went away to the fair house. And the Phoenicians stayed among us a whole year, and gathered much livelihood by trade into their hollow ship. But when the hollow ship was laden for their voyage home, then they sent a messenger to bring word to the woman. A man deep in cunning came to my father's house with a golden necklace, strung here and there with amber beads. In the hall the serving-women and my honored mother were handling it and gazing at it with their eyes, and offering him a price; and he nodded to the woman in silence. When he had given his nod, he went off to the hollow ship, and she took me by the hand and led me out through the doors of the house. In the forecourt she found the cups and tables of the banqueters who attended on my father; they had gone out to the session and the people's debate. Quickly she hid three goblets in the fold of her dress and carried them off; and I followed along in my child's innocence.

“The sun set and all the ways grew dark. Going quickly, we came to the famous harbor, where the Phoenicians' swift-sailing ship lay. They went aboard then, took the two of us up, and sailed the watery ways; and Zeus sent a following wind. Six days we sailed on, night and day alike; but when Zeus, son of Cronus, brought the seventh day, then Artemis who showers arrows struck the woman, and she dropped with a thud into the bilge, like a sea tern. They threw her overboard to be prey for the seals and fish; and I was left behind, grieving at heart. Wind and water bore them on and brought them to Ithaca, and there Laertes bought me with his own wealth. And that is how my eyes first looked on this land.”

Then Odysseus, sprung from Zeus, answered him: “Eumaeus, truly you have stirred the heart in my breast, telling over all these sorrows your heart has suffered. Yet surely Zeus has set good beside the evil for you, since after much toil you came to the house of a kind man, who gives you food and drink with true care, and you live a good life; but I come here only after wandering through many cities of men.” So the two of them talked with each other, then lay down to sleep — not for long, only a little while; for soon fair-throned Dawn came.

Meanwhile, off the shore, the companions of Telemachus struck the sails and lowered the mast smartly, and rowed the ship in to her mooring. They cast out the anchor-stones and made fast the stern lines, and themselves stepped out onto the surf-line of the sea, and prepared their meal and mixed the gleaming wine. When they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, thoughtful Telemachus was the first to speak: “Row the black ship on to the city now; I myself will go to the fields and the herdsmen, and in the evening, when I have looked over my lands, I will come down to town. And in the morning I will set before you the wages of the journey, a good feast of meat and sweet wine.” Then godlike Theoclymenus said to him: “And where am I to go, dear child? To whose house shall I come, of the men who lord it over rocky Ithaca? Shall I go straight to your mother and your own home?” Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “At any other time I would tell you to go to our house — there is no lack of hospitality there. But as things are it would be the worse for you, since I shall be away and my mother will not see you; she does not often show herself to the suitors in the house, but keeps apart from them in the upper room, weaving at her loom. But I will name you another man you may go to: Eurymachus, splendid son of wise Polybus, whom the men of Ithaca now look upon as a god. He is far the best man among them, and the most eager to marry my mother and take the honors of Odysseus. But Olympian Zeus, who dwells in the sky, knows whether before any wedding he will bring an evil day down upon them.”

Even as he spoke, a bird flew by on his right — a hawk, Apollo's swift messenger. In its talons it held a dove and was plucking it, and it shed the feathers on the ground midway between the ship and Telemachus himself. Theoclymenus called him apart from his companions, clasped his hand, and spoke and called him by name: “Telemachus, not without a god's will did that bird fly by on your right — I knew it for a bird of omen the moment I looked at it. There is no line more kingly than yours in the land of Ithaca; yours is the power forever.” Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Would that this word of yours might be fulfilled, stranger; then you would soon know my friendship, and gifts in plenty from me, so that anyone who met you would call you blessed.”

And he spoke to Peiraeus, his trusted companion: “Peiraeus, son of Clytius, of all the companions who followed me to Pylos, you obey me most in everything; so now take this stranger to your house, and treat him with kindness and honor until I come.” Then Peiraeus, famed for his spear, answered him: “Telemachus, even if you should stay out here a long while, I will look after this man, and he shall have no lack of hospitality.” So saying, he went aboard the ship, and told his companions to embark themselves and cast off the stern lines; and they went quickly aboard and sat down at the oarlocks. But Telemachus bound the fine sandals beneath his feet, and took his strong spear, tipped with sharp bronze, from the ship's deck; and the crew cast off the stern lines. They pushed off and sailed for the city, as Telemachus, dear son of godlike Odysseus, had ordered; and his feet carried him swiftly striding on, until he reached the yard where his pigs past counting were kept, and where the swineherd slept among them, a good man, loyal in heart to his masters.

## Book XVI

Meanwhile, in the hut, Odysseus and the godlike swineherd had kindled a fire and were preparing breakfast at dawn, and had sent the herdsmen out with the droves of pigs. As Telemachus approached, the baying dogs fawned around him and did not bark. Godlike Odysseus noticed the dogs fawning, and the sound of footsteps reached him, and at once he spoke winged words to Eumaeus: “Eumaeus, someone is surely coming — a companion of yours, or someone else you know, for the dogs are not barking but fawning about, and I hear the thud of feet.”

The word was not yet fully spoken when his own dear son stood in the doorway. The swineherd sprang up in amazement, and the vessels he was busy with, mixing the gleaming wine, fell from his hands. He went to meet his master, and kissed his head and both his beautiful eyes and both his hands, and a swelling tear fell from him. As a father with a loving heart embraces his son when he comes home from a distant land in the tenth year — an only son, his darling, for whom he has suffered many pains — so then did the godlike swineherd clasp godlike Telemachus and kiss him all over, as one escaped from death.

And weeping, he spoke winged words to him: “You have come, Telemachus, sweet light! I thought I would never see you again, once you had gone by ship to Pylos. Come in now, dear child, come in, so that my heart can take its delight in looking at you, newly back from abroad and here in my house. For you do not come often to the farm or the herdsmen; you stay in town — as if it pleased your heart to look at that destroying crowd of suitors.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “So be it, old friend. It is for your sake I have come here, to see you with my own eyes and hear your news — whether my mother still remains in the halls, or some other man has married her by now, and the bed of Odysseus lies empty of sleepers, hung with foul cobwebs.” And the swineherd, a leader of men, replied: “She remains, and more than remains — with an enduring heart she stays in your halls, and in misery her nights and her days all waste away in weeping.”

So speaking, he took the bronze spear from him, and Telemachus went in and stepped over the stone threshold. As he came, his father Odysseus rose to yield him his seat, but Telemachus from where he stood checked him and said: “Sit, stranger. We will find a seat elsewhere in our farmstead; here is the man who will set one out.” So he spoke, and Odysseus went back and sat down; and the swineherd strewed green brushwood underneath and a fleece on top of it, and there the dear son of Odysseus seated himself. Then the swineherd set before them platters of roasted meat, left over from what they had eaten the day before, and briskly heaped bread in baskets and mixed honey-sweet wine in an ivy-wood bowl, and himself sat down facing godlike Odysseus. They reached out their hands to the good things spread before them. And when they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, Telemachus said to the godlike swineherd: “Old friend, where did this stranger come from? How did sailors bring him to Ithaca? Who did they claim to be? For I do not imagine he came here on foot.”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Well then, my child, I will tell you the whole truth. He claims his birth is from broad Crete, and says he has wandered as a drifter through many cities of mortals; so a god has spun that thread for him. Just now he ran off from a ship of Thesprotian men and came to my farmstead, and I put him in your hands. Do with him as you will; he declares himself your suppliant.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Eumaeus, this word of yours cuts me to the heart. How am I to receive the stranger in my house? I myself am young, and I cannot yet trust my hands to defend a man when someone picks a quarrel first. And my mother's heart is torn two ways in her breast — whether to stay here with me and keep the house, respecting her husband's bed and the talk of the people, or to go off at last with whichever of the Achaeans wooing her in the halls is the best man and offers the most. But as for the stranger, since he has come to your house, I will dress him in a cloak and tunic, fine clothing, and give him a two-edged sword and sandals for his feet, and send him wherever his heart and spirit bid him go. Or, if you are willing, keep him here and look after him at the farmstead; I will send clothes here, and all his food to eat, so that he is not a drain on you and your men. But go in among the suitors I will not let him — their insolence is too reckless by far. They would jeer at him, and that would be a terrible grief to me. It is hard for one man to get anything done against many, however strong he is; they are far too powerful.”

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus said to him: “Friend — since surely it is right for me to answer as well — truly my heart is torn apart as I listen, when you two tell of the reckless things the suitors contrive in the halls, against the will of a man like you. Tell me: do you submit to it willingly, or do the people through the land hate you, following the voice of a god? Or is it brothers you have to blame — the men a warrior trusts to stand by him in the fight, even when a great quarrel arises? Would that I were as young as I am willing in spirit — the son of blameless Odysseus, or the man himself! Then let a stranger cut the head from my shoulders on the spot, if I did not go to the hall of Odysseus son of Laertes and make myself an evil to every one of them. And if they crushed me by their numbers, one man alone, I would rather die cut down in my own halls than go on watching this disgrace forever — strangers knocked about, serving-women dragged shamefully through the beautiful house, wine drawn off and squandered, and men devouring my bread idly, endlessly, in a business that will never be brought to an end.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Well then, stranger, I will tell you quite exactly. The people as a whole bear me no hatred or grudge, nor do I have brothers to blame — the men a warrior trusts to stand by him in the fight, even when a great quarrel arises. For the son of Cronus has made our line run single: Arceisius fathered Laertes an only son, and Laertes in turn fathered Odysseus an only son, and Odysseus fathered only me, and left me in the halls, and had no joy of me. And so now there are enemies past counting in the house. All the noblest men who hold power in the islands — in Dulichium and Same and wooded Zacynthus — and all who lord it in rocky Ithaca, all these woo my mother and wear away my house. And she neither refuses the hateful marriage nor can bring herself to make an end of it; and they eat my house away and waste it. Soon they will tear me to pieces as well. But all that lies on the knees of the gods. Old friend, go now, quickly, and tell prudent Penelope that I am safe and have come back from Pylos. I will wait here; come back here yourself once you have given her the message — her alone. Let none of the other Achaeans hear of it, for there are many plotting evil against me.”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “I know, I understand — you are telling this to a man who was thinking it already. But come, tell me this, and tell me exactly: shall I go the same way with the news to Laertes, poor man? For a while, though he grieved terribly for Odysseus, he still used to oversee the farm work, and would eat and drink with the servants in the house whenever the heart in his breast moved him; but now, ever since you went by ship to Pylos, they say he no longer eats and drinks even so much as that, nor looks to the work, but sits moaning and lamenting, grieving, and the flesh wastes away around his bones.” Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “That is the harder news — but even so we must let him be, though it grieves us. If everything could be had for the mere wishing, we mortals would choose first my father's day of homecoming. No — give your message and come straight back; do not go wandering over the fields after him. But tell my mother to send her housekeeper to him quickly, and secretly; she can carry the news to the old man.” So he spoke, and roused the swineherd, who took his sandals in his hands, bound them under his feet, and set off for the city.

Nor did it escape Athena that Eumaeus the swineherd had gone from the farmstead. She came near, in the likeness of a woman, beautiful and tall and skilled in glorious handiwork, and she stood facing the doorway of the hut, showing herself to Odysseus. Telemachus did not see her before him or notice her at all — for the gods do not appear plainly to everyone — but Odysseus saw her, and the dogs did too, and they did not bark, but slunk whimpering in fear to the far side of the farmstead. She signaled with her brows; godlike Odysseus understood, and came out of the room past the great wall of the yard and stood before her. And Athena said to him: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, resourceful Odysseus, now is the time — speak your word to your son and do not hide it, so that the two of you may plan the suitors' death and doom, and then go to the far-famed town. And I myself will not be long away from you; I am eager for the fight.” So she spoke, and Athena touched him with her golden wand.

First she laid a well-washed cloak and a tunic about his chest, and she increased his stature and his youth. His dark color came back, his cheeks filled out, and the beard grew dark again on his chin. When she had done this she went away, and Odysseus went back into the hut. His dear son was astonished at him, and turned his eyes away in fear that it might be a god, and he spoke winged words to him: “Stranger, you look different to me now than you did before — you have other clothes, and your skin is no longer the same. Surely you are one of the gods who hold the wide heaven. Be gracious to us, then, and we will give you pleasing sacrifices and finely wrought gifts of gold — only spare us.”

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus answered him: “I am no god. Why do you liken me to the immortals? I am your father — the man for whose sake you groan and suffer so many pains, and bow beneath the violence of men.” So saying, he kissed his son, and a tear ran down his cheeks and fell to the ground; until then he had held it back, relentlessly, always. But Telemachus — for he could not yet believe that this was his father — answered him again with words: “You are not Odysseus, not my father; some god is bewitching me, to make me groan and grieve still more. No mortal man could contrive such things by his own wits, unless a god himself came and by his own will made him easily young or old. Just now you were an old man, and shabbily dressed; now you are like the gods who hold the wide heaven.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Telemachus, it is not right, when your own father is here in the house, to marvel beyond measure or stand amazed. No other Odysseus is ever going to come here. I am he — this man you see, who after suffering evils and long wandering have come in the twentieth year to my native land. And this is the work of Athena, driver of the spoil, who makes me whatever she wishes — for she has the power — now like a beggar, and now again like a young man with fine clothes on his body. It is easy for the gods who hold the wide heaven both to glorify a mortal man and to bring him low.”

So saying, he sat down; and Telemachus threw his arms around his noble father and wept, letting the tears stream, and in both of them rose the desire for lamentation. They cried aloud, shrill and thick, more incessantly than birds of prey — sea-eagles, or vultures with hooked talons, whose young the country folk have taken from the nest before they were fledged; so piteously did the tears fall from beneath their brows. And the light of the sun would have set on their weeping, had Telemachus not suddenly spoken to his father: “But in what kind of ship, dear father, did sailors bring you here to Ithaca? Who did they claim to be? For I do not imagine you came here on foot.”

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus said to him: “Well then, my child, I will tell you the truth. The Phaeacians brought me, those famous seamen, who give passage to other men too, whoever comes to them. They carried me asleep in a swift ship over the sea and set me down in Ithaca, and they gave me splendid gifts — bronze and gold in plenty, and woven clothing. By the gods' will it all lies stored in caves. And now I have come here at Athena's prompting, so that we may plan the slaughter of our enemies. Come, count the suitors off for me and name them, so that I may know how many men they are, and who; then I will turn it over in my blameless heart and decide whether the two of us can stand against them alone, without others, or whether we must look for help.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Father, I have always heard of your great fame — a spearman mighty with his hands, and shrewd in counsel — but what you say now is too much. Awe takes hold of me. Two men could not fight so many, and strong men at that. The suitors are not just ten, or twice ten — they are many more, and you shall soon have the count. From Dulichium, fifty-two chosen young men, with six serving-men in their train; from Same, twenty-four men; from Zacynthus, twenty young Achaeans; and from Ithaca itself twelve, all of them the best, and with them Medon the herald, and the divine singer, and two attendants skilled in carving. If we go up against all of these inside, I fear the vengeance you come to take for their violence will prove bitter and terrible. No — if you can think of some defender, consider who might stand by the two of us with a willing heart.”

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus said to him: “Well then, I will tell you — mark it and listen to me. Consider: will Athena, with father Zeus, be enough for us two, or should I think of some other defender?” And thoughtful Telemachus answered: “Good defenders indeed, those two you name, though they sit high among the clouds; they rule over other men and over the immortal gods.”

Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus said to him: “Those two will not stand long apart from the great battle-din, when the strength of Ares is put to the test in my halls between the suitors and us. But for now, go home at the showing of dawn and mingle with the arrogant suitors; the swineherd will bring me to the city later, looking like a wretched old beggar. And if they treat me shamefully in the house, let the heart in your breast endure it while I suffer — even if they drag me by the feet through the hall to the door, or pelt me with missiles: look on, and hold yourself in. Tell them to stop their follies, yes, winning at them with gentle words; but they will not listen at all, for their day of doom stands close beside them.

“And I will tell you something else — lay it up in your heart. When Athena of many counsels puts it in my mind, I will nod to you with my head; when you see it, take up all the weapons of war that lie in the hall, and stow them away, every one, in a corner of the high store-room. And when the suitors miss them and ask you about them, put them off with soft words: ‘I have laid them away out of the smoke, since they are no longer like what Odysseus left behind when he went to Troy, but tarnished wherever the breath of the fire has reached them. And besides, the son of Cronus has put a greater fear in my mind — that in your wine you might raise a quarrel among yourselves and wound one another, and shame the feast and the wooing; for iron of itself draws a man on.’ But for the two of us alone leave two swords and two spears and two oxhide shields ready to hand, so that we can rush upon them and seize them; and then Pallas Athena and Zeus the counselor will bewitch the suitors.

“And I will tell you something else — lay it up in your heart. If you are truly mine, and of our blood, then let no one hear that Odysseus is home. Let Laertes not know it, nor the swineherd, nor any of the household, nor Penelope herself; you and I alone will learn which way the women lean. And we might also test some of the serving-men — which of them honors us and fears us in his heart, and which cares nothing and slights you, fine as you are.”

His glorious son answered him: “Father, you will come to know my spirit hereafter, I think; no slackness has hold of me. But I do not believe this plan will profit either of us, and I urge you to think again. You will be a long time going about uselessly, testing each man on the farms, while they sit at their ease in the halls and devour our goods with a high hand, and spare nothing. The women, yes — I would have you find out which of them dishonor you and which are guiltless. But the men about the farmsteads — I would rather we not test them now. Let that be work for later, if you truly know some sign from Zeus who bears the aegis.” So they spoke such things to one another.

Meanwhile the well-built ship that had carried Telemachus and all his companions from Pylos was putting in to Ithaca. When they had come inside the deep harbor, they hauled the black ship up on the shore, and their proud attendants carried off the gear and bore the beautiful gifts straight to the house of Clytius. And they sent a herald ahead to the house of Odysseus, to bring word to prudent Penelope that Telemachus was in the country and had ordered the ship to sail round to the city, so that the noble queen might not take fright in her heart and let the soft tears fall. So the herald and the godlike swineherd met, going on the same errand, to bring the lady the news. And when they reached the house of the godlike king, the herald spoke out in the midst of the maidservants: “Now, O queen, your dear son has come home.” But the swineherd stood close by Penelope and told her everything her dear son had bidden him say; and when he had delivered the whole of his charge, he went off to his pigs, and left the courts and the hall.

The suitors were dismayed, and downcast at heart. They came out of the hall past the great wall of the yard, and sat down there in front of the gates. Eurymachus, son of Polybus, was first to speak among them: “Friends, a great deed has been insolently brought off — this journey of Telemachus. We swore it would never be carried through. Come, let us launch a black ship, the best we have, and gather seafaring men for rowers, to carry word quickly to the others out there to come home at once.” He had not finished speaking when Amphinomus, turning where he sat, saw a ship inside the deep harbor, and men furling the sails and holding the oars in their hands. He laughed out merrily and said to his companions: “No need now to send any message — here they are, home. Either some god told them, or they themselves saw the other ship go past and could not catch her.”

So he spoke, and they rose and went down to the shore of the sea. Quickly the crew hauled the black ship up on the land, and proud attendants carried off the gear; and the suitors themselves went in a body to the assembly-place, and let no one else, young or old, sit among them. Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them: “Curse it all — see how the gods have delivered this man out of harm's way! By day, watchmen sat on the windy heights, always one relay after another; and at sunset we never spent a night ashore, but rode the sea in our swift ship, waiting for bright Dawn, lying in ambush for Telemachus, to take him and destroy him. And all the while some god has brought him home. So let us here and now contrive a grim end for Telemachus — let him not slip through our hands; for while he lives, I tell you, this business of ours will never be brought off.

“He himself is shrewd in counsel and in mind, and the people no longer show us any favor at all. Come then, before he gathers the Achaeans to assembly — for he will not hold back, I tell you: he will keep his anger hot, and he will stand up before them all and tell how we stitched sheer murder for him and failed to catch him; and they will not applaud when they hear of evil deeds. Take care they do not do us some harm and drive us out of our own land, so that we end up in the country of strangers. No — let us strike first, and take him in the fields far from the city, or on the road. His livelihood and his possessions we will keep ourselves, dividing them fairly among us, and the house we will give to his mother, and to whatever man marries her. But if this word displeases you, and you would rather he live and keep all his father's goods — then let us no longer gather here and eat up his store of pleasant things, but let each man do his wooing from his own hall, seeking her with bridal gifts; and she then may marry the man who gives most, and who comes as her fated husband.”

So he spoke, and they all fell silent and still. Then Amphinomus rose and spoke among them — the glorious son of lord Nisus, Aretias's son. He was the leader of the suitors from Dulichium, rich in wheat and in grass, and his talk was the most pleasing to Penelope, for he had a good mind. With good will toward them he spoke out: “Friends, I for one would not choose to kill Telemachus. It is a fearful thing to kill a man of royal blood. First let us ask the will of the gods. If the decrees of great Zeus approve it, I will kill him myself, and urge on all the others; but if the gods turn us from it, I bid you hold your hands.” So spoke Amphinomus, and his word pleased them. They rose at once and went to the house of Odysseus, and going in, they sat down on the polished chairs.

Then prudent Penelope thought of another thing: to show herself to the suitors in all their overweening insolence. For she had learned of the plot against her son's life in the halls — Medon the herald had told her, for he had overheard their counsels. She went down to the hall with her waiting-women, and when that shining among women came to the suitors, she stood by the pillar of the strongly built roof, holding her glistening veil before her cheeks, and she rebuked Antinous, and spoke and called him by name: “Antinous — insolence itself, contriver of evil! And yet they say that in the land of Ithaca you are the best of all your age-mates in counsel and in speech. You never were any such man. Madman! Why do you stitch death and doom for Telemachus, and pay no heed to suppliants, whose witness is Zeus himself? It is unholy to stitch evil against one another. Or do you not know of the day your father came here in flight, in terror of the people? For they were furious with him, because he had joined Taphian pirates and harried the Thesprotians, who were in league with us. They wanted to destroy him, to tear the dear heart out of him, and to swallow up his great and pleasing livelihood; but Odysseus held them back and stopped them, eager as they were. That is the man whose house you now eat up without payment, whose wife you woo, whose son you would murder — and me you grieve beyond all measure. Stop, I tell you; and make the others stop.”

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered her: “Daughter of Icarius, prudent Penelope, take heart; do not let these things trouble your mind. The man does not exist, nor will exist, nor ever be born, who shall lay hands on Telemachus your son while I live and have my eyes open upon the earth. For I will say this outright, and it will surely be accomplished: in an instant that man's dark blood would stream around my spear. Many a time did Odysseus, sacker of cities, set me on his knees and put roast meat in my hands and hold red wine to my lips. And so Telemachus is by far the dearest of all men to me, and I tell him he has no need to tremble at death — not from the suitors, at least; from the gods there is no escaping.” So he spoke to reassure her — while he himself was contriving the boy's destruction.

She went up to her bright upper chamber, and there she wept for Odysseus, her dear husband, until bright-eyed Athena cast sweet sleep upon her eyelids. In the evening the godlike swineherd came back to Odysseus and his son. They were busy getting supper ready, having slaughtered a year-old pig. But Athena had come close to Odysseus son of Laertes, and striking him with her wand she made him an old man once more, and put miserable rags about his body, so that the swineherd should not know him at sight and go carry the news to prudent Penelope, unable to hold the secret in his heart. Telemachus spoke to him first: “You are back, godlike Eumaeus. What news is there in the town? Are the proud suitors home again from their ambush, or are they still out there watching for me on my way home?”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “I had no mind to ask and pry about that as I went down through the city. My heart urged me to give my message and come back here as fast as I could. But a swift messenger from your companions joined me on the way — a herald, and he was the first to tell your mother the word. And this much more I do know, for I saw it with my own eyes: I was already above the city, where the hill of Hermes stands, on my way back, when I saw a swift ship coming down into our harbor. There were many men in her, and she was laden with shields and double-edged spears. I thought it was they — but I do not know for certain.”

So he spoke, and the hallowed prince Telemachus smiled, catching his father's eye — but he kept his glance away from the swineherd. And when they had finished their work and made the meal ready, they feasted, and no man's heart went short of a fair share of the feast. And when they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, they thought of rest, and took the gift of sleep.

## Book XVII

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, Telemachus, the dear son of godlike Odysseus, bound the beautiful sandals beneath his feet and took up his sturdy spear, well fitted to his grip, eager for the town, and spoke to his swineherd: “Old friend, I am going to the city, so that my mother may see me; for I do not think she will stop her bitter weeping and tearful lamentation until she sees me in the flesh. But this is my charge for you: take this unlucky stranger to the city, so that he may beg his meal there; whoever wishes will give him a crust and a cupful. I cannot take on every man myself, with the sorrows I have in my heart. And if the stranger is angry at that, so much the worse for him; I like to speak the truth plainly.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Friend, I have no wish myself to be kept here. Better for a beggar to beg his meal in town than in the fields; whoever wishes will give to me. I am no longer of an age to stay at a farmstead and obey a master's every order. Go on; this man will lead me, as you have told him, as soon as I have warmed myself at the fire and the day grows hot. These clothes I wear are terribly poor, and the morning frost might be too much for me; and you say the town is a long way off.”

So he spoke, and Telemachus strode off through the farmstead, stepping quickly, sowing the seeds of evil for the suitors. When he reached the pleasant house, he carried his spear and set it against a tall pillar, and himself went in, crossing the stone threshold. His nurse Eurycleia was far the first to see him, as she was spreading fleeces over the ornate chairs; she burst into tears and came straight toward him, and the other maidservants of steadfast Odysseus gathered around him, and kissed his head and shoulders in loving welcome. Then circumspect Penelope came from her chamber, looking like Artemis or golden Aphrodite, and threw her arms around her dear son, weeping, and kissed his head and both his beautiful eyes, and through her sobs she spoke winged words: “You have come, Telemachus, sweet light! I thought I would never see you again, after you went by ship to Pylos—secretly, against my will—for news of your dear father. Come now, tell me everything you saw.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Mother, do not stir up my grief or rouse the heart in my chest—I have only just escaped sheer destruction. Bathe, put clean clothes on your body, go up to your upper room with your serving women, and vow to all the gods that you will offer full hecatombs, if only Zeus may some day bring deeds of requital to pass. I am going to the assembly place, to fetch a stranger who came along with me on my way here. I sent him ahead with my godlike companions, and told Piraeus to take him home and treat him with kindness and honor until I come.” So he spoke, and his word stayed with her, wingless. She bathed, put clean clothes on her body, and vowed to all the gods that she would offer full hecatombs, if only Zeus might some day bring deeds of requital to pass.

Then Telemachus went out through the hall, spear in hand, and two swift dogs went with him. Athena shed a marvelous grace upon him, and all the people gazed at him in wonder as he came. The arrogant suitors gathered around him, speaking fair words while they brooded evil deep in their hearts. He avoided their thick crowd and went instead to where Mentor sat, and Antiphus, and Halitherses, who had been his father's friends from the beginning, and sat down there; and they asked him about everything. Then Piraeus, famed for his spear, came up, leading the stranger through the town to the assembly place; and Telemachus did not stand apart from his guest for long, but went to his side. Piraeus spoke first: “Telemachus, quickly send women to my house, so that I can pass on to you the gifts Menelaus gave you.” Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Piraeus, we do not know how these matters will turn out. If the arrogant suitors kill me by stealth in my own halls and divide up all my father's possessions, I would rather you keep those things and enjoy them than any of these men; but if I sow death and doom for them, then bring them gladly to my house, to a glad man.”

So saying, he led the travel-worn stranger home. When they reached the pleasant house, they laid their cloaks on the chairs and couches, stepped into the polished baths, and washed. When the maids had bathed them and rubbed them with oil, and thrown woolly cloaks and tunics around them, they came out of the baths and sat down on the chairs. A servant brought water in a fine golden pitcher and poured it over a silver basin for them to wash their hands, and drew up a polished table beside them. The grave housekeeper brought bread and set it before them, adding many good things, generous with what she had. His mother sat opposite him by the doorpost of the hall, leaning back in her chair, spinning fine yarn on her distaff. They reached out their hands to the good things spread before them; and when they had satisfied their desire for food and drink, circumspect Penelope was the first to speak: “Telemachus, I am going up to my room, to lie down on the bed that has become a bed of sorrows for me, always wet with my tears, since Odysseus went to Ilium with the sons of Atreus. You could not bring yourself, before the arrogant suitors came into this house, to tell me plainly of your father's return, if you heard anything of it.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Very well, mother, I will tell you the truth. We went to Pylos, to Nestor, shepherd of the people. He received me in his high house and welcomed me with kindness, as a father welcomes his own son newly come home after a long time abroad; so kindly did he care for me, along with his glorious sons. But of steadfast Odysseus, alive or dead, he said he had heard nothing from anyone on earth. He sent me on to the son of Atreus, Menelaus famed for his spear, with horses and a well-joined chariot. There I saw Argive Helen, for whose sake Argives and Trojans suffered so much by the will of the gods. Menelaus, good at the war cry, asked me at once what need had brought me to shining Lacedaemon, and I told him the whole truth. Then he answered me with these words:

“‘Shameful! So those weaklings want to lie in the bed of a strong-hearted man! As when a deer beds down her newborn suckling fawns in a mighty lion's thicket lair and goes ranging the mountain slopes and grassy valleys to graze, and then the lion comes back to his own lair and lets an ugly doom loose on both her young—so Odysseus will let an ugly doom loose on those men. Would that—father Zeus, Athena, Apollo!—he might come among the suitors in the strength he showed long ago in well-built Lesbos, when he stood up and wrestled Philomeleides in a challenge and threw him down hard, and all the Achaeans rejoiced. Every one of them would find a swift death and a bitter marriage. As for what you ask and beg of me, I will not swerve aside and speak of other things, nor will I deceive you; of all that the unerring old man of the sea told me, I will not hide or hold back a single word. He said he had seen Odysseus on an island, suffering hard pains, in the halls of the nymph Calypso, who keeps him there by force; and he cannot come to his own native land, for he has no ships with oars and no companions to carry him over the broad back of the sea.’ So spoke the son of Atreus, Menelaus famed for his spear. When all that was done I set out for home; and the immortals gave me a fair wind and brought me swiftly to my own dear country.”

So he spoke, and stirred the heart in her breast. Then godlike Theoclymenus spoke among them: “Honored wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, truly Menelaus does not know it plainly—but mark my word, for I will prophesy to you exactly, and hide nothing. Zeus be my witness first among the gods, and this hospitable table, and the hearth of blameless Odysseus to which I have come: Odysseus is already in his native land, sitting still or moving about, learning of these evil deeds, and he is sowing evil for all the suitors. So clear was the bird-sign I observed as I sat on the well-benched ship, and I called it out to Telemachus.” Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Ah, stranger, would that this word of yours might be fulfilled! Then you would quickly know my friendship, and many gifts from me—anyone who met you would call you blessed.”

So they spoke such things to one another. Meanwhile the suitors, in front of Odysseus's hall, were amusing themselves with throwing discuses and hunting spears on the leveled ground where they always played, insolent as ever. But when it was dinner time, and the flocks came in from the fields on every side, driven by the men who always drove them, then Medon spoke to them—of all the heralds he was the one who pleased them most, and always attended their feasts: “Young men, now that you have all enjoyed your games, come to the house so we can prepare the meal. There is nothing wrong with taking dinner at the proper hour.” So he spoke, and they rose and went, obeying his word. When they reached the pleasant house, they laid their cloaks on the chairs and couches, and slaughtered great sheep and fat goats, slaughtered fattened pigs and a cow from the herd, and made their meal ready.

Meanwhile Odysseus and the noble swineherd were preparing to go from the country to the town. The swineherd, leader of men, spoke first: “Stranger, since you are set on going to town today, as my master ordered—though I myself would rather have left you here to guard the farmstead; but I respect him and fear he may scold me later, and a master's rebukes are hard to bear—come, let us go now. The best of the day is already gone, and toward evening you will find it colder.” Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “I know, I understand; you are telling this to a man who sees it himself. Let us go, and you lead the way from start to finish. And give me a staff to lean on, if you have one cut anywhere, since you say the path is treacherous.” With that he slung his shabby pouch over his shoulders, all tattered and torn, with a twisted cord for a strap, and Eumaeus gave him a staff to his liking. The two of them set out, and the dogs and the herdsmen stayed behind to guard the farmstead; and so the swineherd led his master toward the city in the likeness of a wretched old beggar, propped on a staff, with miserable clothing on his body.

But when, going down the rugged path, they were near the town and reached the fountain, fair-flowing and built of stone, where the townspeople drew their water—Ithacus had made it, with Neritus and Polyctor; around it was a grove of water-nourished poplars, circling it on every side, and the cold water ran down from the rock above, and on top an altar had been built to the nymphs, where all who passed made their offerings—there Melanthius, son of Dolius, met them, driving goats that were the pick of all the herds for the suitors' dinner, and two herdsmen followed with him. When he saw them he broke into abuse, and spoke a word outrageous and shameful, and it stirred the heart of Odysseus: “Look at this—the vile leading the vile! How the god always brings like and like together! Where are you taking this glutton, you miserable swineherd, this tiresome beggar, this licker of feast-scraps? He is the kind to stand rubbing his shoulders on many doorposts, whining for crusts—not for swords or cauldrons. If you gave him to me to guard my farmstead, to muck out the pens and carry fresh shoots to the kids, then he could drink whey and put some meat on that thigh of his. But no—since he has learned only bad ways, he will not stir himself to work; he would rather go cringing through the district, begging to feed that bottomless belly of his. But I will tell you this, and it will surely come to pass: if he comes to the house of godlike Odysseus, many a footstool from men's hands will fly around his head, and his ribs will be worn down as he is pelted through the house.”

So he spoke, and as he passed he lunged and kicked Odysseus on the hip, like a fool; yet he did not knock him off the path, and Odysseus stood firm. And Odysseus debated whether to spring after him and take his life with the staff, or lift him off the ground and dash his head against the earth. But he endured it, and held himself in check. The swineherd looked the man in the face and rebuked him, and lifted his hands and prayed aloud: “Nymphs of the fountain, daughters of Zeus, if ever Odysseus burned thigh-pieces for you, wrapped in rich fat, of lambs and of kids, grant me this wish: may that man come home, and may some god lead him. Then he would scatter all these fine airs you now carry about so insolently, forever loitering around the town while bad herdsmen ruin the flocks.”

Melanthius the goatherd answered him: “Listen to that—how the dog talks, with his mind full of mischief! One day I will carry him far from Ithaca on a black well-benched ship, and he will fetch me a good living. Would that Apollo of the silver bow might strike Telemachus down today in his halls, or the suitors bring him down, as surely as the day of return has perished for Odysseus far away.” So saying, he left them there walking slowly on, while he strode off and quickly reached the master's house. He went straight in and sat down among the suitors, opposite Eurymachus, whom he liked best of all. The serving men set a portion of meat before him, and the grave housekeeper brought bread and set it beside him to eat.

Odysseus and the noble swineherd came near and stopped, and around them rose the sound of the hollow lyre, for Phemius was striking up his song for the company. Odysseus took the swineherd by the hand and said: “Eumaeus, surely this is the beautiful house of Odysseus; it would be easy to pick out at a glance among many. One building follows on another; its courtyard is finished with wall and coping, and its double doors are well fenced—no man could take it by force. And I can tell that many men are feasting inside, for the savor of roasting meat rises from it, and the lyre rings out, which the gods have made the companion of the feast.”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “You saw it easily—you are not slow-witted in anything else either. But come, let us think how this business is to go. Either you go first into the pleasant house and mingle with the suitors, and I will stay behind here; or, if you like, wait here, and I will go in ahead of you. But do not linger long, or someone may notice you outside and throw something at you or strike you. Think on it, I tell you.” Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus answered him: “I know, I understand; you are telling this to a man who sees it himself. Go ahead of me, and I will stay behind here. I am no stranger to blows and things thrown at me; my heart is patient, for I have suffered many evils in the waves and in war—let this one be added to the rest. But there is no hiding a hungry belly, that cursed thing, which brings so many evils on men; for its sake, too, the well-benched ships are fitted out to cross the barren sea, carrying evil to enemies.”

So they spoke such things to one another. And a dog lying there lifted his head and pricked up his ears: Argos, the dog of steadfast Odysseus, whom he himself had once raised, though he had no joy of him before he went off to sacred Ilium. In earlier days the young men would take him out after wild goats and deer and hares; but now, with his master gone, he lay neglected in the deep dung of mules and cattle that was heaped in front of the doors until the servants of Odysseus could cart it off to manure his great estate. There lay the dog Argos, covered with dog-ticks. And now, when he sensed that Odysseus was near, he wagged his tail and dropped both his ears, but he no longer had the strength to come closer to his master. Odysseus looked away and wiped off a tear, easily hiding it from Eumaeus, and at once he asked him: “Eumaeus, this is a strange thing, this dog lying in the dung. His build is fine—though I cannot tell for certain whether he had speed to match his looks, or whether he was just one of those table-dogs men keep, that their masters groom for show.”

And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “That is the dog of a man who died far away, and no mistake. If he were still what he was, in body and in action, when Odysseus left him behind and went to Troy, you would soon marvel to see his speed and strength. No creature that he chased could escape him in the depths of the thick woods, for he was a master at tracking too. But now he is in a bad way; his master has perished far from his own country, and the women take no care of him, careless as they are. Servants, once their masters no longer rule them, have no more will to do their proper work; for wide-seeing Zeus takes away half a man's worth on the day slavery takes hold of him.” So saying, he went into the pleasant house and walked straight down the hall among the proud suitors. But Argos, the moment he had seen Odysseus again in the twentieth year, was seized by the doom of black death.

Godlike Telemachus was far the first to see the swineherd as he came through the house, and quickly nodded and called him over. Eumaeus looked around and took a stool that stood nearby, where the carver used to sit when he carved great quantities of meat for the suitors feasting through the hall; he carried it to Telemachus's table and set it opposite him, and there he sat down, and a herald took a portion and served it to him, with bread lifted from the basket. Close behind him Odysseus came into the house in the likeness of a wretched old beggar, propped on a staff, with miserable clothing on his body. He sat down on the ash-wood threshold just inside the doors, leaning against the cypress doorpost, which a carpenter had once planed skillfully and trued to the line.

Telemachus called the swineherd to him, and taking a whole loaf from the beautiful basket, and as much meat as his cupped hands could hold, he said: “Take this and give it to the stranger, and tell him to go around and beg from all the suitors in turn. Shame is no good companion for a man in need.” So he spoke, and the swineherd went when he heard the order, and standing close he spoke winged words: “Telemachus gives you this, stranger, and tells you to go around and beg from all the suitors in turn. Shame, he says, is no good thing in a beggar.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Lord Zeus, grant that Telemachus be blessed among men, and that all his heart desires may come to pass.” So he spoke, and took the food in both hands and set it down before his feet on his shabby pouch, and ate while the singer sang in the hall; when he had finished his meal, the divine singer was ending too. The suitors filled the hall with their noise. And Athena came close to Odysseus, son of Laertes, and urged him to gather crusts among the suitors, and learn which of them were decent and which lawless—though even so she was not going to save a single one of them from destruction. So he set out, begging from each man in turn from left to right, stretching out his hand on every side, as if he had been a beggar all his life. They took pity and gave, and wondered at him, asking one another who he was and where he had come from.

Then Melanthius the goatherd spoke among them: “Hear me, suitors of the famous queen, about this stranger. I have seen him before—it was the swineherd who led him here; but the man himself I do not know for certain, or where he claims his birth.” So he spoke, and Antinous turned on the swineherd with abuse: “You notorious swineherd, why did you bring this fellow to town? Do we not have vagrants enough already, tiresome beggars, lickers of feast-scraps? Is it nothing to you that men gather here and eat up your master's living—must you invite this one too?” And you answered him, swineherd Eumaeus: “Antinous, noble though you are, that was not well said. Who ever goes and invites a stranger from abroad himself, unless it is one of those who serve the public good—a seer, or a healer of sickness, or a builder in wood, or an inspired singer who delights with his song? Those are the men who are invited over the boundless earth; no one would invite a beggar, to eat him out of house and home. But you are always harsh, beyond all the other suitors, to the servants of Odysseus, and to me above all. Yet I care nothing for it, so long as prudent Penelope lives in the halls, and godlike Telemachus.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Quiet—do not trade long speeches with that man. Antinous has a way of provoking people cruelly with harsh words, and he sets the others on as well.” And he spoke winged words to Antinous: “Antinous, how kindly you look after me—like a father for his son!—ordering me to drive this stranger from the hall with a word of force. May the god never bring that to pass. Take something and give it to him; I do not grudge it—I command it. And have no fear on that score of my mother, or of any of the servants in the house of godlike Odysseus. But no—there is no such thought in your breast: you would much rather eat it yourself than give it to another.”

Antinous answered him: “Telemachus, you high-flown speaker with your unbridled temper, what a thing to say! If all the suitors handed him as much as I will, this house would keep him at a distance for three months.” So he spoke, and seized the footstool on which he propped his shining feet as he feasted, and brought it out from under the table into view. But all the others gave, and filled the pouch with bread and meat; and Odysseus was on the point of going back to the threshold to taste, free of charge, what the Achaeans had given, when he stopped beside Antinous and spoke to him:

“Give, friend. You do not seem to me the meanest of the Achaeans but the noblest, for you look like a king; so you ought to give even more generously than the others—bread, at least—and I will spread your fame over the boundless earth. I too once lived in a house of my own among men, a fortunate man in a wealthy home, and I often gave to a wanderer, whoever he might be and whatever need he came in. I had servants past counting, and everything else in plenty by which men live well and are called rich. But Zeus, son of Cronus, stripped it all away—such, no doubt, was his will—when he sent me off with roving pirates to Egypt, a long voyage, to my ruin.

“I moored my curved ships in the river of Egypt. Then I ordered my trusty companions to stay there by the ships and guard them, and sent scouts out to the lookout places; but my men gave in to their own insolence and, following their impulse, at once began plundering the beautiful fields of the Egyptians, carrying off the women and little children and killing the men. The alarm soon reached the city, and hearing the cry the townsmen came out at daybreak; the whole plain filled with foot soldiers and horses and the flash of bronze. Zeus who delights in the thunderbolt cast a vile panic among my companions, and not one dared stand and face the enemy, for evil hemmed us in on every side. There they killed many of us with the sharp bronze, and others they led up alive, to work for them as slaves. Me they gave to a stranger who happened to meet them, to take to Cyprus—Dmetor, son of Iasus, who ruled Cyprus by force. From there I have come here, suffering hardships.”

Antinous answered him: “What god has brought this plague here to sour our feast? Stand off there in the middle, away from my table, or you may soon find yourself in a bitter Egypt and a bitter Cyprus—what a bold, shameless beggar you are! You stand by every man in turn, and they give recklessly, since there is no restraint and no scruple in being generous with other men's goods, when each has plenty before him.”

Resourceful Odysseus drew back and answered him: “Well, well—so your wits do not match your looks after all. You would not give so much as a grain of salt from your own house to a suppliant, you who sit at another man's table and cannot bring yourself to take a piece of bread and hand it to me—and there is plenty before you.” So he spoke, and Antinous grew angrier still at heart, and looking at him from under his brows he spoke winged words: “Now I do not think you will get out of this hall in one piece, since you add insults too.” With that he seized the footstool and threw it, and struck Odysseus on the right shoulder, at the base of the back. But he stood firm as a rock; the blow of Antinous did not stagger him. He only shook his head in silence, brooding evil deep within.

He went back to the threshold and sat down, laid down his well-filled pouch, and spoke among the suitors: “Hear me, suitors of the famous queen, while I say what the heart in my breast commands. There is no grief in the mind, no great pain, when a man is struck fighting for his own possessions, for his cattle or his white sheep. But Antinous struck me on account of my wretched belly, that cursed thing, which brings so many evils on men. If there are gods and Furies anywhere for beggars, may the end of death find Antinous before his wedding day.”

Antinous, son of Eupeithes, answered him: “Sit still and eat, stranger, or take yourself elsewhere—or for talk like that the young men will drag you through the house by hand or foot and flay you raw.” So he spoke, but all the others were bitterly indignant, and one of the proud young men would say: “Antinous, it was not well done to strike the unlucky wanderer. Doomed man—what if he should be some god come down from heaven? The gods do take on the likeness of strangers from far countries, in every kind of shape, and go from city to city, watching over the violence and the good order of men.” So the suitors spoke, but he paid no heed to their words. And Telemachus nursed a great grief in his heart at the blow, yet he let no tear fall from his eyelids to the ground; he only shook his head in silence, brooding evil deep within.

But when circumspect Penelope heard that a man had been struck in the hall, she said among her serving women: “May Apollo, famed for his bow, strike you just so yourself!” And the housekeeper Eurynome said to her: “If only our prayers could be answered, not one of these men would live to see fair-throned Dawn.” Circumspect Penelope answered her: “Nurse, they are all hateful, for they all plot evil; but Antinous above all is like black death itself. Some unlucky stranger is wandering through the house, begging of the men, for want drives him; the others all filled his pouch and gave to him, but that man struck him with a footstool on the right shoulder, at the base of the back.” So she talked among her serving women, sitting in her chamber, while godlike Odysseus was eating his meal.

Then she called the noble swineherd to her and said: “Go, noble Eumaeus, and tell the stranger to come here, so that I may greet him and ask whether he has perhaps heard of steadfast Odysseus, or seen him with his own eyes; he looks like a man who has wandered far.” And you answered her, swineherd Eumaeus: “My queen, if only the Achaeans would be quiet! The tales he tells would charm your very heart. Three nights I had him, three days I kept him in my hut, for he came to me first when he ran away from a ship, and even so he had not finished the story of his sufferings. As a man gazes at a singer who has learned from the gods to sing words of longing for mortals, and they yearn to listen to him without end whenever he sings—so that man held me spellbound, sitting beside me in my house. He says he is a guest-friend of Odysseus's family, and lives in Crete, where the race of Minos is. From there he has come here, suffering hardships, driven on and on; and he insists he has heard of Odysseus nearby, in the rich land of the Thesprotians—alive, and bringing many treasures home.”

Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Go, call him here, so that he can tell it to my face. As for those men, let them amuse themselves sitting in the doorway or here through the house, since their hearts are merry. Their own possessions lie untouched at home, bread and sweet wine, with only their households to eat it, while they haunt our house day after day, slaughtering our cattle and sheep and fat goats, feasting and drinking the sparkling wine with never a thought—and most of it wasted. For there is no man here such as Odysseus was, to keep ruin from the house. But if Odysseus were to come, back to his native land, he and his son would soon take revenge for these men's violence.” So she spoke—and Telemachus sneezed, a great sneeze, and the whole house rang terribly around them. Penelope laughed, and quickly spoke winged words to Eumaeus: “Go, please, and call the stranger here to me. Do you not see that my son sneezed at every word I said? So death shall not go unaccomplished for the suitors—for every one of them; not a man shall escape death and the fates. And I will tell you another thing—lay it up in your heart: if I find that everything he says is true, I will dress him in fine clothes, a cloak and a tunic.”

So she spoke, and the swineherd went when he heard the order, and standing close to Odysseus he spoke winged words: “Stranger, father, circumspect Penelope calls you, the mother of Telemachus. Her heart moves her to ask about her husband, though she has suffered many sorrows. And if she finds that everything you say is true, she will dress you in a cloak and a tunic, which you need most of all; and as for bread, you can beg through the district and feed your belly—whoever wishes will give to you.” Much-enduring godlike Odysseus answered him: “Eumaeus, I would gladly tell the whole truth at once to the daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope, for I know well about that man—he and I have shouldered the same misery. But I am afraid of this crowd of hard suitors, whose insolence and violence reach the iron sky. Even just now, when that man struck me and hurt me as I walked through the house doing no harm, neither Telemachus nor anyone else did a thing to prevent it. So tell Penelope to wait in her halls until sunset, however eager she is; then let her ask me about her husband and his day of return, giving me a seat closer to the fire—for my clothes are miserable, as you know yourself, since it was to you I first came as a suppliant.”

So he spoke, and the swineherd went when he had heard the message. As he crossed the threshold, Penelope said to him: “You have not brought him, Eumaeus? What is the wanderer thinking? Is he too afraid of someone, or is he simply ashamed here in the house? A bashful beggar makes a poor beggar.” And you answered her, swineherd Eumaeus: “He speaks with good sense; anyone else would think as he does, in trying to keep clear of these overbearing men's insolence. He asks you to wait until sunset. And it is far better for you too, my queen, to speak to the stranger alone, and hear him alone.” Circumspect Penelope answered him: “The stranger is no fool; he sees how it might well be. For I do not think there are any mortal men anywhere who plot such reckless outrage as these men do.”

So she spoke, and the noble swineherd went back into the crowd of suitors, when he had told her everything. At once he spoke winged words to Telemachus, holding his head close so that the others would not hear: “Dear boy, I am going back to guard the pigs and everything there, your living and mine. You look after all things here. Keep yourself safe first of all, and take care in your heart that nothing happens to you; many of the Achaeans are plotting evil—may Zeus destroy them before any harm comes to us.” Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “So it shall be, old friend. Go when you have had your supper; and in the morning come again, and bring fine beasts for sacrifice. All the rest will be my concern, and the immortals'.” So he spoke, and the swineherd sat down again on the polished stool; and when he had satisfied his heart with food and drink, he went off to his pigs, leaving the courtyard and the hall full of feasters; and they were taking their pleasure in dance and song, for by now the day had drawn on to evening.

## Book XVIII

Now there came along a public beggar, who used to beg his way through the town of Ithaca, notorious for his greedy belly, eating and drinking without end. He had no strength or muscle in him, but to look at he was very big. Arnaeus was his name—his honored mother had given it to him at birth—but all the young men called him Irus, because he would carry messages whenever anyone told him to. This man came and tried to drive Odysseus from his own house, and jeering at him he spoke winged words: “Clear off the doorway, old man, before you get dragged out by the foot. Don’t you see how they are all winking at me, urging me to drag you? Still, I would be ashamed to. Up with you, then, before our quarrel comes to fists.”

Glaring at him from under his brows, resourceful Odysseus said: “Friend, I am doing you no harm in deed or word, and I do not grudge anyone giving to you, however much he hands over. This threshold will hold the two of us, and you have no business being jealous over what belongs to other people. You look to me like a vagrant, the same as I am, and it is the gods who deal out fortune. Only do not challenge me too far with those fists of yours, or you will make me angry, and old as I am I will smear your chest and lips with blood. Then I would have all the more peace tomorrow, for I do not think you would come back a second time to the hall of Odysseus, son of Laertes.”

At that the vagrant Irus grew angry and answered: “Listen to that—how glibly the greedy pig runs on, like some old crone at her oven! I could do him real mischief, hammering with both hands, and knock every tooth from his jaws onto the ground, like a sow’s caught rooting in the crops. Belt up your rags, then, so all these men can watch us fight—though how could you stand up to a younger man?” So, on the polished threshold before the high doors, the two of them whetted their tempers with all their hearts.

The princely might of Antinous took notice of them, and laughing out loud with delight he called to the suitors: “Friends, nothing like this has ever happened before—such entertainment as the god has brought to this house! The stranger and Irus are squabbling and want to fight it out with their fists. Quick, let us set them at each other.” So he spoke, and they all sprang up laughing and crowded around the two ragged beggars, and Antinous, son of Eupeithes, said to them: “Hear me, proud suitors, while I say a word. There are goat paunches lying at the fire, which we set aside for supper, stuffed with fat and blood. Whichever of the two wins and proves the stronger man, let him stand up and take his pick of them; and from now on he shall always eat with us, and we will let no other beggar inside to join us and ask for alms.”

So spoke Antinous, and his proposal pleased them. Then, with his own cunning purpose, resourceful Odysseus said: “Friends, there is no way an old man, worn down by hardship, can fight a younger one; but this belly of mine, that maker of trouble, drives me on to be beaten down with blows. Come then, all of you swear me a strong oath, that no one, doing Irus a favor, will foul me with a heavy fist and beat me down by force for him.” So he spoke, and they all swore as he asked. And when they had sworn and completed the oath, the princely might of Telemachus spoke among them again: “Stranger, if your heart and your proud spirit urge you to stand up to this man, then fear none of the other Achaeans—whoever strikes you will have many to fight. I am your host here, and the kings approve, Antinous and Eurymachus, both men of sense.”

So he spoke, and they all agreed. Then Odysseus belted his rags up around his loins, and bared his thighs, fine and massive; his broad shoulders came into view, his chest, his brawny arms—and Athena, standing close by, filled out the limbs of the shepherd of the people. The suitors were all astonished beyond measure, and one would say, with a glance at the man next to him: “Irus will soon be Un-Irus, with a beating he brought on himself—look at the thigh the old man shows under those rags!” So they spoke, and Irus’s heart was shaken badly. Even so the serving-men belted him up and led him out by force, terrified, the flesh trembling on his limbs. And Antinous rounded on him and spoke to him sharply: “Better for you now, you great ox, that you had never lived nor been born at all, if you shake and stand in such dread of this man—an old man, worn down by the misery that has come upon him. But I will tell you this, and it will be carried out: if this man beats you and proves the stronger, I will throw you into a black ship and send you to the mainland, to King Echetus, mutilator of all mankind, who will slice off your nose and ears with the pitiless bronze, and tear out your parts and give them raw to the dogs to eat.” So he spoke, and a still worse trembling seized Irus’s limbs. They led him into the middle, and both men put up their fists.
