Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus debated whether to hit him so hard that the life would leave him where he fell, or to strike him gently and only stretch him on the ground. As he weighed it, this seemed the better course: to strike gently, so the Achaeans would take no particular note of him. So they put up their guard, and Irus punched at the right shoulder, but Odysseus struck him on the neck below the ear and crushed the bones inside; at once red blood filled his mouth, and he went down in the dust bleating, grinding his teeth together and drumming his feet on the ground—and the lordly suitors threw up their hands and nearly died laughing. But Odysseus took him by the foot and dragged him out through the doorway, across the courtyard to the gates of the portico; there he propped him sitting against the courtyard wall, pushed a stick into his hand, and spoke winged words to him: “Sit there now and keep off the pigs and the dogs, and stop setting yourself up as lord of strangers and beggars, wretch that you are, or something even worse may come your way.”

With that he slung his shabby leather pouch over his shoulders, full of holes, with a twisted cord for a strap, and going back to the threshold he sat down. The suitors went inside laughing merrily, and they greeted him: “May Zeus, stranger, and the other immortal gods give you whatever you most desire and whatever is dear to your heart, for putting a stop to that insatiable fellow’s begging around the town. We will soon ship him over to the mainland, to King Echetus, mutilator of all mankind.” So they spoke, and godlike Odysseus rejoiced at the good omen of their words. Antinous set the great paunch beside him, stuffed with fat and blood, and Amphinomus took two loaves from a basket and set them before him, and drank his health from a golden cup, saying: “Your health, father stranger. May good fortune be yours in the days to come, though for now you are gripped by many hardships.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Amphinomus, you strike me as a man of real sense—as your father is too, for I have heard his good name: Nisus of Doulichion, a fine man, they say, and a rich one. You are said to be his son, and you seem a courteous man. So I will tell you something—mark it, and hear me out. Of all the things that breathe and creep upon the earth, the earth breeds nothing feebler than man. As long as the gods grant him vigor and his knees are quick, he thinks no evil can ever touch him in time to come; but when the blessed gods bring sorrows on him instead, these too he must bear, unwilling, with an enduring heart. For the minds of men on this earth are such as the day the father of gods and men brings upon them. I too was once set to be a man of fortune among men, but I gave in to my strength and power and did many reckless things, counting on my father and my brothers. Let no man, then, ever be lawless in anything, but keep in silence whatever gifts the gods may give. What recklessness I see the suitors plotting here—wasting the possessions and dishonoring the wife of a man who, I tell you, will not be long away from his friends and his native land; he is very near. May some god steer you home out of his path, and may you not cross him when he comes back to his own dear country. For once he comes under his own roof, I do not think the suitors and that man will be parted without blood.”

So he spoke, and pouring a libation he drank the honey-sweet wine, then set the cup back in the hands of the marshal of the people. But Amphinomus went off through the hall with a heavy heart, shaking his head, for his spirit sensed evil coming. Even so he did not escape his doom: Athena had bound him too, to be brought down by force under the hands and spear of Telemachus. He went back and sat down again in the chair from which he had risen.

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, put a thought in the mind of the daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope: to show herself to the suitors, so as to set their hearts fluttering wide open, and to become more honored than before in the eyes of her husband and her son. She gave an awkward little laugh and said: “Eurynome, my heart longs—as it never has before—to show myself to the suitors, hateful to me as they are. And I would say a word to my son that would be for his good: that he should not keep company so constantly with these overbearing suitors, who speak fairly to his face and mean evil behind his back.”

Then Eurynome the housekeeper answered her: “Yes, child, all of this you have said rightly. Go then, speak your word to your son and hide nothing—but first wash your skin and put a gloss on your cheeks. Do not go down with your face all streaked with tears like this; unbroken, endless grieving only makes things worse. Your son is now the age you prayed hardest to the immortals to see him reach—a bearded man.” And circumspect Penelope answered her: “Eurynome, do not coax me to this, for all your kindness—to wash my skin and anoint myself with oil. Whatever radiance I had, the gods who hold Olympus destroyed it the day that man embarked in the hollow ships. But tell Autonoe and Hippodameia to come and stand beside me in the hall. I will not go among men alone; I would be ashamed.”

So she spoke, and the old woman went off through the hall to carry word to the women and hurry them along. Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, thought of something else. Over the daughter of Icarius she poured sweet sleep, and she slept, leaning back, every joint of her loosened, right there on the couch; and all the while the shining goddess was giving her immortal gifts, so that the Achaeans would look at her in wonder. First she cleansed her lovely face with the ambrosial balm of beauty, the very kind fair-crowned Cytherea anoints herself with when she goes to the lovely dance of the Graces; she made her taller and fuller to look upon, and made her whiter than sawn ivory. That done, the shining goddess departed.

The white-armed maids came in from the hall, chattering as they approached, and sweet sleep let her go. She rubbed her cheeks with her hands and said: “Soft indeed was the slumber that wrapped me, for all my bitter suffering. Would that pure Artemis would give me a death as soft as that, this very moment, so that I need no longer wear my life away grieving at heart, aching for my dear husband and his every excellence—for he stood out above all the Achaeans.” So saying, she went down from the bright upper rooms—not alone, for two maids went with her. And when she reached the suitors, that shining woman, she stopped beside a pillar of the strongly built roof, holding her glistening veil before her cheeks, and a trusted maid stood on either side of her. The suitors’ knees went weak on the spot; desire bewitched their hearts, and every man of them prayed to lie beside her in bed.

But she spoke to Telemachus, her dear son: “Telemachus, your mind and your judgment are no longer steady. When you were still a boy you kept shrewder thoughts in your head; but now that you are grown, now that you have come to the full measure of manhood—now, when a stranger looking at your height and your good looks would call you the son of a fortunate man—your mind and judgment are no longer what they should be. What a thing has been done in these halls, that you let the stranger be manhandled so! What if a stranger sitting in our house were to come to real harm from such rough dragging-about? On you would fall the shame and disgrace of it among men.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Mother, I cannot resent your being angry over this. But I do take notice in my heart, and I understand each thing, the good and the bad alike; before, I was still a child. Yet I cannot manage to think everything through wisely—these men rattle me, sitting around me on every side with their evil designs, and I have no one to back me. Still, this bout between the stranger and Irus did not turn out as the suitors wished: the stranger proved the stronger man. O father Zeus, and Athena, and Apollo, would that the suitors in our house were beaten down and hanging their heads just so, some in the courtyard, some inside the hall, the limbs of every one of them unstrung—the way that Irus now sits out by the courtyard gates, head lolling like a drunk, unable to stand upright on his feet or make his way home, wherever home may be, since his own limbs are undone.”

So the two of them talked with one another. But Eurymachus addressed Penelope: “Daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope, if all the Achaeans throughout Iasian Argos could see you, still more suitors would be feasting in your halls from tomorrow’s dawn, for you surpass all women in beauty and stature and in the well-balanced mind within you.”

Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Eurymachus, whatever excellence I had of face and form, the immortals destroyed it when the Argives embarked for Ilium, and with them went my husband, Odysseus. If that man were to come home and care for this life of mine, my fame would be greater for it, and fairer. As it is, I can only grieve—so many are the evils some god has driven upon me. Ah, when he left his native land behind, he took my right hand at the wrist and said: ‘Wife, I do not think the well-greaved Achaeans will all come home from Troy safe and unharmed. They say the Trojans too are fighting men—spearmen, archers who draw the bow, riders of swift horses—the kind who most quickly tip the balance in the great struggle of a war that could go either way. So I do not know whether the god will bring me home, or whether I shall be lost there at Troy. Let everything here be in your care. Remember my father and mother in the halls as you do now, or even more, while I am far away. But when you see our son with a beard on his cheek, then marry whatever man you choose, and leave your house.’

“So he spoke, and now all of it is coming to pass. There will come a night when a hateful marriage falls to my lot—the lot of an accursed woman, whose happiness Zeus has stripped away. But here is a bitter grief that comes over my heart and spirit: this was never the way of suitors in the old days. Men who wish to court a good woman, a rich man’s daughter, and compete with one another for her—such men bring cattle and fat sheep of their own, a feast for the bride’s people, and they give splendid gifts. They do not devour someone else’s livelihood and pay nothing.”

So she spoke, and much-enduring godlike Odysseus was glad, because she was drawing gifts out of them, charming their hearts with honeyed words while her mind was set on other things. Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, answered her: “Daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope, as for gifts—whichever of the Achaeans wishes to bring them here, accept them; it is not gracious to refuse a gift. But we are not going back to our estates, or anywhere else, until you marry whichever of the Achaeans is the best man.” So spoke Antinous, and his proposal pleased them, and each man sent off a herald to fetch his gifts. For Antinous the herald brought a great and beautiful robe, richly embroidered; fastened on it were twelve brooches, all of gold, fitted with well-curved clasps. For Eurymachus he promptly brought a chain of elaborate workmanship, gold strung with amber beads, bright as the sun. For Eurydamas two servants brought a pair of earrings, each with three drops clustered like mulberries, and great was the grace that shone from them. Out of the house of lord Peisander, son of Polyctor, a servant brought a necklace, an ornament of great beauty. And so one Achaean after another brought his own fine gift. Then that shining woman went up to her upper rooms, and her maids carried the beautiful gifts up after her.

The suitors turned to dancing and the delights of song, and took their pleasure, waiting for evening to come on; and as they made merry, dark evening came upon them. At once they set up three braziers in the hall to give light, and heaped dry kindling around them, long-seasoned, bone-dry, freshly split with the bronze, and mixed pine torches in among it; and the serving-women of steadfast Odysseus took turns keeping the flames alive. Then Zeus-born resourceful Odysseus himself spoke to them: “Serving-women of Odysseus, of your master so long away—go along to the rooms where your honored queen is. Sit with her in her chamber and cheer her, twirling the spindle at her side or carding wool with your hands. I will keep the light going for all these men myself. Even if they choose to sit it out until fair-throned Dawn, they will not outlast me. I am a man of very great endurance.”

So he spoke, and they burst out laughing, glancing at one another. And fair-cheeked Melantho abused him shamefully—Dolius’s daughter, whom Penelope had brought up and cherished like her own child, giving her every plaything her heart could want; yet even so she felt no grief for Penelope’s sorrows, but slept with Eurymachus and was his lover. She railed at Odysseus with insulting words: “Wretched stranger, you must be some kind of madman, knocked clean out of your wits. You will not go off to sleep at the smithy or in some public shelter—no, you stand here running your mouth, bold as you please in front of all these men, without a flicker of fear in your heart. Either the wine has gone to your head, or your mind is always like this, and that is why you babble such nonsense. Or are you giddy with yourself because you beat that vagrant Irus? Take care some better man than Irus does not stand up against you soon, to batter your head with heavy fists and throw you out of the house dripping with blood.”

Glaring at her from under his brows, resourceful Odysseus said: “I will go straight over to Telemachus, you shameless dog, and tell him how you talk, so he can cut you limb from limb on the spot.” So speaking, he sent the women scattering in terror; they fled through the house, and the knees of every one of them went slack with fright, for they thought he meant exactly what he said. But he took his stand by the blazing braziers, tending the light, watching them all—while his heart within was brooding on other things, things that would not go unaccomplished.

And Athena would by no means let the proud suitors hold back from heart-stinging insolence, so that the pain might sink still deeper into the heart of Odysseus, son of Laertes. Eurymachus, son of Polybus, began speaking among them, baiting Odysseus and raising a laugh among his companions: “Hear me, suitors of our far-famed queen, while I say what the heart in my chest commands. Not without some god’s will has this man come to the house of Odysseus. In any case, the torchlight seems to me to be coming straight off the man himself—off that head of his, since there is not a hair on it, not even a wisp.” Then he turned to Odysseus, sacker of cities: “Stranger, if I took you on, would you be willing to work for hire on an outlying farm of mine—your wages would be assured—laying up stones for walls and planting tall trees? There I would keep you in bread the year round, and put clothes on your back and give you sandals for your feet. But no—now that you have learned your idle tricks, you will not want to set your hand to work; you would rather go cringing through the town, so you can feed that bottomless belly of yours.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Eurymachus, I only wish the two of us could have a working contest, in the spring season, when the days grow long—out in a hay meadow, I with a well-curved scythe in my hands and you with one just like it, so we could test each other at the work, fasting till deep dusk, with grass still standing to cut. Or would that there were oxen to drive—the very best, glossy and big, both well filled with grass, matched in age and matched in pulling, their strength not easily worn down—and a four-acre field whose soil yields to the plow: then you would see whether I could cut a furrow clean and straight to the end. Or again, would that the son of Cronus stirred up a war somewhere, this very day, and that I had a shield and two spears and a helmet all of bronze, fitted close to my temples: then you would see me in the thick of the foremost fighters, and you would not stand there talking and jeering at this belly of mine. But you are all insolence, and your mind is hard; no doubt you fancy yourself someone great and powerful, because the company you keep is small, and none of it any good. If Odysseus were to come back and reach his native land, those doors—wide as they are—would suddenly be far too narrow for you as you bolted out through the doorway.”

So he spoke, and Eurymachus grew angrier still at heart, and glaring at him from under his brows he spoke winged words: “You wretch, I will soon make you pay for talking like this, bold as you please in front of all these men, without a flicker of fear in your heart. Either the wine has gone to your head, or your mind is always like this, and that is why you babble such nonsense. Or are you giddy with yourself because you beat that vagrant Irus?” And with that he snatched up a footstool. But Odysseus ducked down at the knees of Amphinomus of Doulichion, in fear of Eurymachus, and the stool struck the cupbearer on his right hand; the wine jug clattered to the floor, and the man groaned and fell backward in the dust. The suitors broke into an uproar through the shadowy halls, and one would say, with a glance at the man next to him: “Would that this stranger had died in his wanderings somewhere else before ever he came here—then he would never have brought all this din among us. Now here we are brawling over beggars, and there will be no pleasure in a good feast, since the worse is getting the upper hand.”

And the princely might of Telemachus spoke among them: “Strange men, you are out of your minds—you can no longer hide how much you have eaten and drunk. Some god must be goading you. No—now that you have feasted well, go home and go to bed, whenever the spirit moves you. Not that I am driving anyone out.” So he spoke, and they all bit their lips, astonished at Telemachus, that he spoke so boldly.

Then Amphinomus rose and spoke among them, the illustrious son of Nisus, lord Aretias’s grandson: “Friends, when a thing has been fairly said, no one should take offense and lash back with hostile words. Do not strike this stranger, nor any of the servants in the house of godlike Odysseus. Come, let the cupbearer pour the first drops into the cups, so we can make our libation and go home to bed; and let us leave the stranger here in the halls of Odysseus, in Telemachus’s care, for it is to his house that the man has come.” So he spoke, and his word was welcome to them all. The hero Moulius, a herald from Doulichion and Amphinomus’s own attendant, mixed the bowl for them and served every man in turn; and they poured libations to the blessed gods and drank the honey-sweet wine. And when they had poured and drunk to their hearts’ content, they went off to rest, each man to his own house.

## Book XIX

But godlike Odysseus was left behind in the hall, plotting with Athena the slaughter of the suitors. At once he spoke winged words to Telemachus: “Telemachus, we must stow the weapons of war inside, every one of them. And when the suitors miss them and question you, put them off with soft words: tell them you have laid them away out of the smoke, since they no longer look like the arms Odysseus left behind when he went to Troy, but are tarnished wherever the breath of the fire has reached them; and that besides, some god has put this greater fear in your mind—that heated with wine you might raise a quarrel among yourselves and wound one another, and disgrace the feast and the wooing. For iron of itself draws a man on.”

So he spoke, and Telemachus obeyed his dear father. He called out the nurse Eurycleia and said to her: “Nurse, come, shut the women in their quarters for me, until I can lay away my father's fine weapons in the storeroom. All this time, with my father gone, they have stood neglected about the house, and the smoke has been dulling them—I was still a child then. Now I want to store them where the breath of the fire will not reach.” The dear nurse Eurycleia answered him: “Ah, child, would that you would finally take up the good sense to care for the house and guard all its possessions! But come, who then will go along and carry the light for you? The maids would have lit your way, but you will not let them come out.” Thoughtful Telemachus replied: “This stranger here. I will not have any man stand idle who eats from my grain-measure, however far he has come.” So he spoke, and for her his word stayed wingless; and she locked the doors of the stately hall.

Then the two of them sprang up, Odysseus and his glorious son, and carried in the helmets, the bossed shields, and the sharp-pointed spears; and before them Pallas Athena, holding a golden lamp, made a beautiful light. Suddenly Telemachus said to his father: “Father, here is a great wonder my eyes are seeing! The walls of the hall, the fine crossbeams, the fir rafters, and the tall pillars that hold them up all shine before my eyes as if from blazing fire. Surely some god is in the house, one of those who hold the wide heavens.” Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Hush—hold your thoughts to yourself and ask no questions. This is the way of the gods who hold Olympus. Go now to your bed, and I will stay behind here, to stir up the maids and your mother a little further; in her grief she will ask me about everything.” So he spoke, and Telemachus went out through the hall by the light of blazing torches to lie down in his chamber, where he always slept when sweet sleep came over him; there he lay down now as well, and waited for the bright Dawn. But godlike Odysseus was left behind in the hall, plotting with Athena the slaughter of the suitors.

Then circumspect Penelope came from her chamber, looking like Artemis or golden Aphrodite. Beside the fire they set her accustomed chair, inlaid with ivory and silver, which the craftsman Icmalius had made long ago, fitting to it a footstool of one piece with the chair itself; and a great fleece was spread upon it. There circumspect Penelope took her seat. White-armed serving-women came from the hall and cleared away the heaped bread, the tables, and the cups from which the overbearing men had been drinking; they raked the fire out of the braziers onto the floor and piled fresh wood on them in plenty, for light and for warmth.

Then Melantho began scolding Odysseus a second time: “Stranger, are you still going to plague us here all through the night, prowling about the house and eyeing the women? Get outside, you wretch, and be content with your dinner—or you will soon go out the door with a firebrand flying after you.” Looking at her darkly, resourceful Odysseus replied: “Strange woman, why do you go at me with such an angry heart? Is it because I am dirty and wear poor clothes on my body, and beg through the town? Necessity drives me to it; that is what beggars and wandering men are. I too once lived in a house of my own among men, a fortunate man in a wealthy home, and I often gave to a wanderer, whoever he might be and in whatever need he came. I had servants past counting, and everything else in plenty by which men live well and are called rich. But Zeus, son of Cronus, shattered it all—such, I suppose, was his will. So take care, woman, that you too do not one day lose all the glory that sets you now above the other maids—that your mistress does not turn on you in anger, or that Odysseus does not come home. For there is still a portion of hope. And even if he has perished as you think, and will never return, there is his son now by Apollo's grace, Telemachus—such a son that no woman's misbehavior in these halls escapes him, for he is no longer of an age to overlook it.”

So he spoke, and circumspect Penelope heard him, and rebuked the servant, calling her out by name: “Make no mistake, you bold, shameless dog—none of your outrageous doings escape me, and you will wipe them off on your own head. You knew perfectly well, for you heard it from my own lips, that I meant to question the stranger here in my halls about my husband; for I am worn deep with grief.” Then she said to Eurynome the housekeeper: “Eurynome, bring a bench, and a fleece to lay on it, so that the stranger may sit and speak with me and hear me out; I want to question him fully.” So she spoke, and the housekeeper briskly brought a polished bench and set it down, and threw a fleece over it; and there much-enduring godlike Odysseus sat down. Circumspect Penelope began: “Stranger, I will ask you this first myself: who are you, and where are you from? Where is your city, who are your parents?”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Lady, no mortal on the boundless earth could find fault with you. Truly your fame reaches the wide heavens, like the fame of some blameless king who, fearing the gods and ruling over many strong men, upholds just judgments; and for him the black earth bears wheat and barley, the trees hang heavy with fruit, the flocks lamb without fail, the sea provides fish—all from his good leadership—and under him the people prosper. So ask me anything else, here in your house, but do not ask about my family and my native land, or you will fill my heart with still more pain as I remember; I am a man of many sorrows. And it is not right for me to sit weeping and wailing in another's house—grieving without end and without measure only makes things worse—and one of the maids might take offense at me, or you yourself, and say that I swim in tears because my wits are heavy with wine.”

Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Stranger, whatever excellence I had of face or figure the immortals destroyed when the Argives embarked for Ilium, and with them went my husband Odysseus. If that man could come back and watch over my life, my fame would be greater and finer for it. As it is, I only grieve, so many are the evils some god has driven upon me. All the chief men who rule the islands, Doulichion, Same, and wooded Zacynthus, and those who dwell around clear-skied Ithaca itself, are courting me against my will and wearing away the house. So I pay no heed to strangers or suppliants, nor to heralds, who do the people's business; I only melt my heart away with longing for Odysseus. These men press for the marriage, and I wind out my schemes.

“First a god breathed it into my mind to set up a great loom in my halls and weave a robe, fine-threaded and very wide; and I said to them at once: Young men, my suitors, since godlike Odysseus is dead, be patient, however eager you are for my marriage, until I finish this cloth—let my spinning not be wasted and come to nothing—a shroud for the hero Laertes, against the day when the ruinous fate of death that lays men low takes him; so that no Achaean woman among the people may hold it against me if a man who won so much should lie without a winding-sheet. So I spoke, and their proud hearts consented. Then day after day I would weave at the great loom, and by night I would unravel it, once I had torches set beside me. For three years I kept my secret so, and persuaded the Achaeans; but when the fourth year came, as the seasons rolled around, the months waning and the long days running their course, then, through my maids—careless dogs that they are—the suitors came upon me and caught me, and cried out against me. So I finished it, unwillingly, under compulsion. And now I can neither escape the marriage nor find any other scheme. My parents press me hard to marry, and my son chafes as these men devour his livelihood—and he understands it now, for he is by now a man fully able to care for a house, the kind of man to whom Zeus grants honor. But even so, tell me your family, and where you come from; for you were not born from an oak of ancient story, or from a rock.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Honored wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, will you never stop asking me about my lineage? Well then, I will tell you—though you will be giving me over to sorrows more than those I already hold. That is the way of it, when a man has been away from his country as long as I have, wandering through the many cities of men and suffering pain. Even so, I will tell you what you ask and inquire. There is a land called Crete, in the middle of the wine-dark sea, beautiful and rich, ringed by water; in it live many men, past counting, and ninety cities. Language mixes with language there: there are Achaeans, great-hearted native Cretans, Cydonians, Dorians in their three tribes, and godlike Pelasgians. Among the cities is Knossos, a great city, where Minos reigned in spans of nine years, the confidant of great Zeus—Minos, the father of my father, great-hearted Deucalion. Deucalion fathered me and the lord Idomeneus. Idomeneus went off to Ilium in the curved ships with the sons of Atreus; my own famous name is Aethon, and I am the younger by birth—he was the elder and the better man.

“There I saw Odysseus, and gave him guest-gifts. The force of the wind had carried him down to Crete as he was making for Troy, driving him off course past Maleia; he anchored at Amnisus, where the cave of Eileithyia is, in those difficult harbors, and barely rode out the storm. He came up to the town at once and asked for Idomeneus, saying he was his dear and honored guest-friend; but by then it was the tenth or the eleventh dawn since Idomeneus had gone with his curved ships to Ilium. So I took him to my house and entertained him well, showing him every kindness out of the plenty that was within; and for the rest of the companions who followed him I gathered barley-meal from the public store, and gleaming wine, and cattle for sacrifice, so that their hearts might be satisfied. Twelve days the godlike Achaeans stayed there, for a great north wind penned them in and would not let them so much as stand upon the land—some hard spirit had roused it. On the thirteenth day the wind fell, and they put out to sea.”

He made all the many falsehoods of his tale seem like truth, and as she listened her tears flowed, and her face melted. As snow melts on the high mountains, snow the West Wind poured down and the East Wind thaws, and as it melts the running rivers fill—so her lovely cheeks melted with the tears she shed, weeping for her husband, who was sitting at her side. And Odysseus in his heart pitied his weeping wife, but his eyes stood fixed as horn or iron, unmoving in their lids; by guile he kept his tears hidden. When she had taken her fill of tearful lamentation, she spoke to him again in answer: “Now, stranger, I think I will put you to the test, and see whether you truly entertained my husband in your halls with his godlike companions, as you say. Tell me what sort of clothes he wore on his body, what kind of man he was himself, and the companions who followed him.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Lady, it is hard to say, after so long a parting; it is now the twentieth year since he went from there and left my country. But I will tell you how my heart pictures him. Godlike Odysseus wore a thick purple cloak, folded double; the brooch on it was made of gold, with double sheaths for the pins, and on its face was fine craftsmanship: a hound holding a dappled fawn in its forepaws, pinning it fast as it struggled. Everyone marveled at it, how—the two of them being gold—the hound gripped the fawn and throttled it, while the fawn writhed with its feet, straining to escape. And I noticed the tunic on his body, gleaming like the skin of a dried onion—so smooth it was, and bright as the sun. Many women, I can tell you, gazed at him in wonder. And another thing I will tell you—lay it up in your heart: I do not know whether Odysseus wore these clothes from home, or whether some companion gave them to him as he boarded the swift ship, or perhaps some guest-friend, since Odysseus was dear to many men; few of the Achaeans were his equal. I myself gave him a bronze sword, and a fine purple cloak of double fold, and a fringed tunic, and sent him off with honor on his well-benched ship. And a herald attended him, a little older than himself; I will describe him too, what he was like: round in the shoulders, dark-skinned, curly-haired, and Eurybates was his name. Odysseus honored him above his other companions, because their minds ran alike.”

So he spoke, and stirred in her a still greater desire to weep, as she recognized the sure tokens Odysseus had described. When she had taken her fill of tearful lamentation, she answered him: “Now, stranger, though you had my pity before, you shall be a dear and honored guest in my halls. It was I who gave him those very clothes you speak of; I folded them and brought them from the storeroom, and I pinned on the shining brooch, to be an ornament for him. And now I shall never welcome him back again, come home to his own dear country. It was an evil fate that took Odysseus off in his hollow ship, to look on that accursed Ilion I will not name.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Honored wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, mar your lovely skin no longer, and do not melt your heart away weeping for your husband. Not that I blame you at all: any woman mourns when she loses the wedded husband whose love she has known and whose children she has borne—even a lesser man than Odysseus, who they say was like the gods. But cease your weeping, and attend to what I say; for I will tell you truly and hide nothing. I have already heard of the homecoming of Odysseus, close by, in the rich land of the Thesprotians—alive, and bringing home many fine treasures, gathered as he begs his way among the people. But his trusty companions and his hollow ship he lost on the wine-dark sea, on the way from the island of Thrinacia; for Zeus and Helios were angry with him, because his companions had killed the cattle of the Sun. They all perished in the surging sea; but him the wave threw up on dry land, clinging to the keel of his ship, in the country of the Phaeacians, who are near kin to the gods. They honored him with all their heart like a god, gave him many gifts, and were themselves willing to bring him home unharmed. Odysseus would have been here long ago; but it seemed to his heart the more profitable course to travel far and wide gathering wealth—so far does Odysseus surpass all mortal men in knowing where profit lies; no man alive could rival him at it.

“All this Pheidon, king of the Thesprotians, told me. And he swore to me in person, pouring a libation in his house, that a ship had been drawn down to the sea and a crew stood ready, to convey him to his own dear country. But me he sent off first, for a ship of the Thesprotians happened to be leaving for Doulichion, rich in wheat. And he showed me all the possessions Odysseus had gathered—treasure enough to feed a man's line to the tenth generation, so much lay stored for him in that king's halls. Odysseus himself, he said, had gone to Dodona, to hear the will of Zeus from the god's high-crowned oak: how he should return to his own dear country after so long away, openly or in secret. So he is safe, and he will come—very soon now; he will not be far from his friends and his native land much longer. And I will give you my oath on it besides. Zeus be my witness first, highest and best of the gods, and this hearth of blameless Odysseus to which I have come: all this will be accomplished exactly as I say. Within this very year Odysseus will be here—as this moon wanes and the next comes on.”

Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Would that this word of yours might be fulfilled, stranger! Then you would quickly know my kindness, and many gifts from me—enough that anyone who met you would call you blessed. But this is how my heart forebodes it, and so it will be: Odysseus will not come home again, and you will get no passage hence; for there are no masters in this house such as Odysseus was among men—if he ever truly lived—to receive honored strangers and send them on their way. Even so—my maids, wash him, and lay out a bed for him, a bedstead with cloaks and shining blankets, so that he may lie warm until Dawn of the golden throne; and at first light bathe him and rub him with oil, so that here inside, sitting at Telemachus's side in the hall, he may think of breakfast. And the worse for any of those men who spitefully torments him—he will accomplish nothing here ever again, however furious his rage. For how would you learn of me, stranger, whether I surpass other women at all in mind and thoughtful counsel, if you sat down to dinner in my halls unwashed and dressed in rags? Men are creatures of a day. To the man who is harsh himself and knows harsh ways, all mortals pray that sorrows come while he lives, and when he is dead all men mock him; but of the man who is blameless himself and knows blameless ways, strangers carry the fame far and wide among all mankind, and many call him noble.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Honored wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, cloaks and shining blankets have been hateful to me ever since I first left the snowy mountains of Crete behind me, sailing in my long-oared ship. I will lie tonight as I have lain through sleepless nights before; many a night I have spent on a rough bed, waiting for the bright fair-throned Dawn. Nor does my heart take any pleasure in footbaths; no woman of those who serve about your house shall touch my foot—unless there is some old woman, aged and faithful-hearted, who has endured in her heart as much as I have. Such a one I would not begrudge the touching of my feet.”

Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Dear stranger—never has any man so discerning, of all the guests from far countries, come to my house more welcome, so wise and well-judged is everything you say. I have just such an old woman, her mind full of careful thoughts, who nursed and reared that unhappy man, taking him in her arms the day his mother bore him. She will wash your feet, feeble with age though she is. Come, get up now, circumspect Eurycleia, and wash the age-mate of your master. Odysseus by now must be such as this man is, in his feet and in his hands alike; for in misery mortals grow old quickly.”

So she spoke, and the old woman covered her face with her hands, let fall hot tears, and spoke words of grief: “Alas, my child, and I helpless for you! Surely Zeus hated you beyond all men, god-fearing though your heart was. No mortal ever burned so many rich thigh-pieces to Zeus who delights in the thunder, or such choice hecatombs, as you gave him, praying that you might reach a sleek old age and rear your glorious son; and now from you alone he has utterly taken the day of your homecoming. Just so, I suppose, the women mocked him too, in some far country, when he came to someone's famous house, as these dogs here all mock you—and it is to escape their insults and their many taunts that you will not let them wash you. But the daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope, bids me do it, and I am not unwilling. So I will wash your feet, both for Penelope's own sake and for yours, since the heart within me is stirred with sorrows. And now mark what I say: many travel-worn strangers have come here, but I tell you I have never yet seen any man so like Odysseus as you are—in build, in voice, in your very feet.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Old mother, so say all who have set eyes on the two of us—that we are very like each other, just as you yourself so shrewdly observe.” So he spoke, and the old woman took the shining basin she used for washing feet, poured in cold water in plenty, and then added the hot. Odysseus had been sitting at the hearth, but now he turned quickly toward the darkness, for a sudden fear came over his heart that as she handled him she might notice the scar, and everything would come out into the open. She came close and began to wash her master; and at once she knew the scar, the wound a boar had dealt him long ago with its white tusk, when he went to Parnassus to visit Autolycus and his sons.

Autolycus was his mother's noble father, who surpassed all men in thievery and in the oath; the god Hermes himself had given him that gift, for Autolycus burned pleasing thigh-pieces of lambs and kids to him, and Hermes went with him as a ready friend. Once, coming to the rich land of Ithaca, Autolycus found a child newly born to his daughter; and Eurycleia laid the baby on his knees as he was finishing his supper, and said to him: “Autolycus, find a name yourself to give your daughter's child; he is a child of many prayers.” And Autolycus answered her: “My son-in-law and my daughter, give him the name I say. Inasmuch as I have come here a man of wrath, angered at many, men and women alike across the nourishing earth, let his name be Odysseus, the child of wrath. And for my part, when he is grown and comes to his mother's great house at Parnassus, where my possessions are, I will give him a share of them and send him home rejoicing.”

It was for those gifts that Odysseus came, so that Autolycus might give him the splendid presents. Autolycus and the sons of Autolycus welcomed him with open arms and gentle words, and his mother's mother, Amphithea, threw her arms around Odysseus and kissed his head and both his beautiful eyes. Autolycus called on his renowned sons to prepare the meal, and they heard his urging: at once they led in a five-year-old bull, which they flayed and dressed, dividing the whole carcass; they cut up the meat skillfully, pierced the pieces with spits, roasted them with care, and portioned out the shares. So all day long until the sun went down they feasted, and no one's heart lacked its fair share of the feast; and when the sun set and darkness came on, they lay down and took the gift of sleep.

When early-born, rosy-fingered Dawn appeared, they set out for the hunt, the hounds and the sons of Autolycus together, and with them went godlike Odysseus. They climbed the steep mountain of Parnassus, clothed in forest, and soon reached its windy folds. The sun was just striking the fields, rising out of the soft-gliding, deep-flowing stream of Ocean, when the hunters came into a glen. Ahead of them went the hounds, casting for a scent, and behind came the sons of Autolycus, and with them godlike Odysseus, close upon the hounds, brandishing his long-shadowed spear. There, in a dense thicket, a great boar lay couched. No force of the wet-blowing winds pierced that lair, no shining rays of the sun ever struck it, no rain broke through it, so thick it was, and a great drift of fallen leaves lay deep within.

The thud of the men's and the dogs' feet came about the boar as they pressed in on him, and out of his lair he came to face them, the bristles stiff along his back and fire glaring in his eyes, and stood at bay close before them. First of all Odysseus rushed in, his long spear raised high in his heavy hand, eager to strike; but the boar was quicker, and gashed him above the knee, charging in aslant, and its tusk tore away a long strip of flesh, though it did not reach the man's bone. But Odysseus struck home, hitting its right shoulder, and the point of the bright spear went clean through; the boar dropped in the dust with a scream, and its life flew away. The dear sons of Autolycus busied themselves with the carcass, and skillfully bound up the wound of blameless godlike Odysseus, staunching the dark blood with a chant; and soon they came back to their dear father's house. There Autolycus and the sons of Autolycus healed him well, gave him splendid gifts, and quickly sent him home rejoicing to his own dear country, to Ithaca. His father and his honored mother were glad at his return, and asked him about everything—about the scar, and what had happened to him; and he told them the whole story, how in the hunt a boar had gashed him with its white tusk, when he went to Parnassus with the sons of Autolycus.

That scar the old woman, taking his leg in the flat of her hands, knew at a touch, and she let his foot drop. His leg fell into the basin; the bronze rang, the basin tipped over, and the water spilled out on the floor. Joy and pain seized her heart together, her eyes filled with tears, and her full voice caught in her throat. She reached for his chin and said to Odysseus: “Truly you are Odysseus, dear child—and I did not know you, not until I had touched all my master's body!” And she glanced toward Penelope, wanting to tell her that her dear husband was in the house. But Penelope could neither meet her look nor take her meaning, for Athena had turned her thoughts elsewhere. Then Odysseus, feeling for the old woman's throat, gripped it with his right hand, and with the other drew her closer to him and said: “Nurse, why would you destroy me? You nursed me yourself at your own breast; and now, after suffering many pains, I have come in the twentieth year to my native land. But since you have found me out, and a god has put it into your heart, be silent, and let no one else in the house learn of it. For I will tell you this, and it will surely be accomplished: if a god brings down the proud suitors at my hands, I will not spare even you, my nurse though you are, on the day I kill the other serving-women in my halls.”

Circumspect Eurycleia answered him: “My child, what a word has escaped the fence of your teeth! You know how firm my spirit is, how it never yields; I will hold like solid stone or iron. And I will tell you another thing—lay it up in your heart: if a god brings down the proud suitors at your hands, then I will name for you the women of the house, which ones dishonor you and which are guiltless.” Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Nurse, why should you name them? There is no need. I will observe them well enough myself, and come to know each one. Just keep the matter silent, and leave the rest to the gods.” So he spoke, and the old woman went out through the hall to fetch water for his feet, for the first had all been spilled. When she had washed him and rubbed him richly with oil, Odysseus drew his bench nearer the fire again to warm himself, and covered the scar with his rags.

Circumspect Penelope began speaking again: “Stranger, there is one small thing more I will ask you myself; for soon it will be the hour for pleasant rest—for anyone, at least, whom sweet sleep will take, whatever his cares. But to me some god has given grief beyond all measure. By day my one comfort is to mourn and lament, while I look to my own tasks and my women's about the house; but when night comes and sleep takes hold of everyone else, I lie in my bed, and thick about my beating heart the sharp cares crowd and goad me as I grieve. As when the daughter of Pandareus, the nightingale of the greenwood, sings her lovely song at the first coming of spring, perched in the thick leaves of the trees, and turning her song this way and that pours out her many-toned voice, mourning her child, her dear Itylus, the son of lord Zethus, whom she killed with the bronze one day in her folly—so my heart too is torn, and swings this way and that: do I stay beside my son and keep everything safe as it is, my property, my serving-women, and this great high-roofed house, respecting my husband's bed and the voice of the people, or do I now go with whichever of the Achaeans is noblest among those who court me in the halls, offering bride-gifts past counting? My son, while he was still a child and light of mind, would not let me marry and leave my husband's house; but now that he is grown tall and has reached the full measure of manhood, he even begs me to go back out of this hall, so vexed is he for the property the Achaeans keep devouring.

“But come now, hear this dream of mine, and interpret it for me. I have twenty geese about the house; they come out of the water to eat their wheat, and I warm to them as I watch. But down from the mountain came a great eagle with a hooked beak, and broke all their necks and killed them; and they lay heaped together in the halls, while he was borne up into the bright sky. And I wept and wailed, though it was only a dream, and the fair-braided Achaean women gathered around me as I grieved pitifully because the eagle had killed my geese. But he came back and perched on a jutting roof-beam, and with a human voice he checked my weeping and said: Take heart, daughter of far-famed Icarius. This is no dream but a true waking vision, and it will be fulfilled. The geese are the suitors, and I, who was the eagle before, have now come back as your husband, and I will let loose a shameful death upon all the suitors. So he spoke, and honey-sweet sleep released me; and looking about I saw the geese in the halls, pecking their wheat beside the trough, just where they always did.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Lady, there is no way to wrench the dream aside and read it otherwise, since Odysseus himself has shown you how he will bring it to pass. Destruction stands plain for the suitors—for every one of them; not a man will escape death and the fates.”

Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Stranger, dreams are baffling things, and their speech is dark, and not everything in them is fulfilled for mortals. There are two gates for insubstantial dreams: one pair is made of horn, the other of ivory. The dreams that come through the sawn ivory deceive, carrying words that come to nothing; but those that come out through the polished horn accomplish the truth, whenever a mortal sees them. Yet I do not think my strange dream came from there—however welcome that would be to me and to my son. And I will tell you another thing—lay it up in your heart: the dawn now coming is the evil-named day that will cut me off from the house of Odysseus. For I am going to set a contest now: those axes he used to stand up in his halls, all twelve in a row, like the props under a ship's keel; then he would stand far off and send an arrow through them all. This contest I will now set before the suitors: whoever most easily strings the bow in his hands and shoots an arrow through all twelve axes, with him I will go, leaving this house of my marriage, this beautiful house so filled with the goods of life, which I think I shall remember even in my dreams.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Honored wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, do not put off this contest in your house a moment longer. Resourceful Odysseus will be here before those men, for all their fumbling at the polished bow, can string it and shoot an arrow through the iron.”

Circumspect Penelope answered him: “Stranger, if you were willing to sit beside me in the hall and delight me so, sleep would never be shed upon my eyes. But people cannot go without sleep forever; the immortals have set a proper portion of it for mortals on the grain-giving earth. So I will go up to my upper room and lie down on my bed—that bed of sighs, forever wet with my tears, ever since Odysseus went away to look on that accursed Ilion I will not name. There I will lie; and you lie down here in the house—spread something on the floor, or let them set out a bed for you.” So saying, she went up to her shining upper room, not alone, for her maids went up with her. And when she had climbed to her upper room with her serving-women, she wept there for Odysseus, her dear husband, until bright-eyed Athena cast sweet sleep upon her eyelids.

## Book XX

But godlike Odysseus made his bed in the forecourt. He spread down an undressed ox-hide, and on top of it many fleeces of the sheep the Achaeans kept slaughtering; and Eurynome threw a cloak over him once he had lain down. There Odysseus lay awake, plotting evil for the suitors in his heart. And the women came out of the hall—the ones who had been sleeping with the suitors all along—giving each other laughter and merriment. His heart was stirred in his breast, and he pondered much in mind and heart whether he should spring after them and deal death to every one, or let them lie with the arrogant suitors one last and final time; and the heart inside him growled.

As a bitch standing over her tender pups growls at a man she does not know and is eager to fight, so his heart growled within him in outrage at their evil doings. But he struck his chest and rebuked his heart: “Endure, my heart. You once endured something even more shameless, on the day when the Cyclops, unrestrained in his fury, ate my strong companions; and you bore it, until cunning led you out of the cave where you thought you would die.” So he spoke, chiding the dear heart in his breast; and his heart held obedient, enduring without pause—but he himself kept rolling this way and that.

As when a man over a great blazing fire turns a paunch stuffed with fat and blood this way and that, impatient for it to be roasted through, so Odysseus tossed from side to side, pondering how he could lay his hands on the shameless suitors, one man alone against many. Then Athena came near him, down from heaven, in the shape of a woman; she stood above his head and spoke to him: “Why awake again, most ill-fated of all men? This is your house; your wife is here in the house; and your child—such a son as anyone would pray to have.”

Answering her, resourceful Odysseus said: “Yes, goddess, all that you have said is right and true. But my heart keeps turning this over in my breast—how I am to lay my hands on the shameless suitors, being one man alone, while they are always inside in a crowd. And beyond that I ponder something greater still: even if I killed them, by the will of Zeus and by yours, where could I escape afterward? I ask you to think on that.”

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, answered him: “Stubborn man! A man will trust even a weaker companion, one who is mortal and knows no such counsels as mine; but I am a god, and I guard you to the end in all your labors. I will tell you plainly: even if fifty companies of mortal men stood around the two of us, burning to kill us in battle, even then you would drive off their cattle and their fat sheep. No—let sleep take you too. It is misery to lie watching all night long; and you will rise up out of your troubles soon.” So she spoke, and shed sleep over his eyelids, and she herself, shining among goddesses, went back to Olympus.

Just as sleep took hold of him, loosening the cares of his heart—sleep that unstrings the limbs—his devoted wife awoke, and wept, sitting up on her soft bed. When she had had her fill of weeping, the shining among women prayed first of all to Artemis: “Artemis, queenly goddess, daughter of Zeus, would that you would strike an arrow into my breast and take my life this very moment—or that a storm-wind would snatch me up and carry me away down the misty paths and fling me into the mouths of back-flowing Ocean.

“So it was when storm-winds carried off the daughters of Pandareus: the gods had destroyed their parents, and they were left orphans in the halls, and shining Aphrodite reared them on cheese and sweet honey and pleasant wine; Hera gave them beauty and good sense beyond all women, holy Artemis gave them stature, and Athena taught them to work glorious handiwork. But while shining Aphrodite was on her way to high Olympus to ask for the girls the fulfillment of flowering marriage—going to Zeus who delights in the thunderbolt, for he knows all things well, the fortune and misfortune of mortal men—in that very hour the snatching winds swept the girls away and gave them over to the hateful Furies to serve them. So may the gods who hold the halls of Olympus blot me out, or may fair-haired Artemis strike me down, so that I may go under the hateful earth with Odysseus before my eyes, and never gladden the heart of a lesser man. Grief is still bearable when a person weeps through the days, sore at heart, but sleep takes them at night—for sleep brings forgetfulness of everything, good and bad alike, once it closes over the eyelids. But to me some god sends evil even in dreams. This very night again there lay beside me one just like him, such as he was when he went away with the army; and my heart rejoiced, for I thought it was no dream but waking truth at last.”

So she spoke, and at once gold-throned Dawn came. Godlike Odysseus caught the sound of her weeping; and as he pondered, it seemed to his heart that she already knew him and was standing by his head. He gathered up the cloak and the fleeces he had slept in and laid them on a chair in the hall, carried the ox-hide outside and set it down, and lifting up his hands he prayed to Zeus: “Father Zeus, if you gods have willingly brought me over dry land and sea to my own country, after afflicting me so sorely, then let someone of the people waking in the house speak a word of omen for me, and let another sign from Zeus appear outside.”

So he spoke in prayer, and Zeus the counselor heard him, and at once he thundered from gleaming Olympus, from on high out of the clouds; and godlike Odysseus rejoiced. And the word of omen came from a grinding-woman inside the house, close by, where the mills of the shepherd of the people were set. Twelve women in all labored at them, making barley meal and wheat flour, the marrow of men. The others were asleep, having ground out their wheat; one alone had not yet finished, for she was the feeblest of them all. She brought her mill to a stop and spoke a word, a sign for her master: “Father Zeus, who rule over gods and men, how loudly you have thundered from the starry sky, and not a cloud anywhere! Surely you show this as a sign to someone. Fulfill now even for wretched me the word I speak: may the suitors on this day take their pleasant feast in the halls of Odysseus for the last and final time. They have unstrung my knees with heart-wearying toil as I grind their barley—may this be the last dinner they ever eat.” So she spoke, and godlike Odysseus rejoiced at the omen of her words and at the thunder of Zeus; for he told himself he would yet make the guilty pay.

The other serving-women were gathering through the fine house of Odysseus, kindling on the hearth the unwearying fire. And Telemachus rose from his bed, a man like a god, and dressed himself; he slung his sharp sword about his shoulder, bound fine sandals beneath his shining feet, and took up his strong spear, tipped with sharp bronze. He went and stood on the threshold, and said to Eurycleia: “Dear nurse, did you honor the stranger in our house with bed and food, or does he lie there uncared for, just as he is? For that is my mother's way, sensible though she is: on impulse she honors one man of mortal kind, the worse one, and sends the better away without honor.”

Circumspect Eurycleia answered him: “Do not blame her now, child—in this she is blameless. He sat and drank wine as long as he wished, and said he was hungry for no more food—for she did ask him. And when he began to think of rest and sleep, she told the maids to spread bedding for him; but he, like a man utterly wretched and luckless, would not sleep on a bed under blankets. He lay down on an undressed ox-hide and sheep fleeces in the forecourt; and it was we who threw a cloak over him.” So she spoke, and Telemachus strode out through the hall, spear in hand, and two swift dogs went with him. He made his way to the assembly-place, to join the well-greaved Achaeans.

Then the shining among women, Eurycleia, daughter of Ops, Peisenor's son, called out to the maids: “Come now, to work! Some of you sweep the house, busily, and sprinkle the floors, and throw the purple coverlets over the well-made chairs; others, wipe all the tables down with sponges, and clean the mixing-bowls and the well-wrought double-handled cups; and others, go to the spring for water, and be quick bringing it back. The suitors will not be long away from the hall—they will come very early, for today is a feast-day for everyone.” So she spoke, and they listened well and obeyed. Twenty went off to the spring of dark water, while the rest worked skillfully there about the house.

Then the serving-men of the Achaeans came in, and split the firewood well and skillfully, and the women came back from the spring. After them came the swineherd, driving three fatted boars, the best of all his herd. He left them to feed in the fine enclosure, and himself spoke to Odysseus with gentle words: “Stranger, do the Achaeans look on you with any more regard, or do they still dishonor you through the hall as before?” Answering him, resourceful Odysseus said: “Eumaeus, would that the gods might punish the outrage these men commit in their insolence, devising reckless deeds in another man's house, without one shred of shame.” So they spoke such things to one another; and Melanthius the goatherd came up close, driving the goats that were finest in all the flocks, a dinner for the suitors, and two herdsmen followed with him. He tethered the goats under the echoing portico, and himself spoke to Odysseus with taunting words: “Stranger, are you still going to plague us here about the house, begging from the men? Won't you get yourself out the door? I begin to think the two of us will not part for good until we have tasted each other's fists, since there is no decency in your begging. There are other feasts among the Achaeans, you know.” So he spoke, and resourceful Odysseus answered him not a word, but silently shook his head, brooding evil deep within.

And a third came to join them, Philoetius, a leader of men, bringing for the suitors a barren heifer and fat goats. Ferrymen had carried them across—the men who carry other travelers too, whoever comes to them. He tethered the beasts well under the echoing portico, and himself stepped close to the swineherd and asked: “Swineherd, who is this stranger newly come to our house? From what men does he claim to be born? Where are his family and his native fields? Poor man—in his build he truly looks like a royal king. But the gods plunge much-wandering men into misery, when they spin sorrow even for kings.” With that he came up and greeted Odysseus with his right hand, and speaking winged words he addressed him: “Welcome, father stranger! May good fortune be yours hereafter—though now you are held fast in many miseries.

“Father Zeus, no other god is deadlier than you: you have no pity on men, though you yourself give them birth, but plunge them into misery and bitter pain. I broke into a sweat the moment I noticed him, and my eyes filled with tears, remembering Odysseus—for I imagine he too is wandering among men in rags like these, if indeed he still lives somewhere and sees the light of the sun. But if he is dead already and in the house of Hades, then alas for blameless Odysseus, who set me over his cattle when I was still a boy, in the land of the Cephallenians. Now those cattle have grown past counting—no man could ask a breed of broad-browed cattle to yield a richer harvest. But now strangers order me to drive them in for their own eating; they care nothing for the son of the house, and they do not tremble before the watching gods; for they are eager now to divide up the possessions of a master so long gone. As for me, this thought keeps turning round and round in the heart in my breast: it is a very bad thing, while the son is alive, to go off to another people's country, cattle and all, to foreign men; but it is more wretched still to stay here, sitting over cattle handed to others, and suffer pain. Long ago I would have fled to some other of the proud kings, for things here are past all bearing; but still I think of that unlucky man, and how he might come from somewhere and scatter the suitors through the house.”

Answering him, resourceful Odysseus said: “Cowherd, since you seem no bad man and no fool, and I can see for myself that understanding has reached your mind, I will tell you something and swear a great oath upon it: now let Zeus be my witness, first among the gods, and this table of hospitality, and the hearth of blameless Odysseus to which I have come—while you are still here, Odysseus will come home; and with your own eyes, if you wish, you will see the killing of the suitors who lord it here.” The man who tended the cattle answered him: “Stranger, would that the son of Cronus might fulfill that word! Then you would learn what my strength is, and how my hands follow it.” And in the same way Eumaeus prayed to all the gods that wise Odysseus might return to his own home.

So they spoke such things to one another. Meanwhile the suitors were plotting death and doom for Telemachus; but a bird came to them on the left, a high-flying eagle, clutching a trembling dove. Then Amphinomus spoke up among them: “Friends, this plan of ours will not run smoothly—the killing of Telemachus. Let us think of the feast instead.” So spoke Amphinomus, and his word pleased them.

Going into the house of godlike Odysseus, they laid their cloaks down on the couches and chairs, and set about sacrificing great sheep and fat goats; they slaughtered fatted pigs and a heifer from the herd. They roasted the inner parts and served them round, and mixed wine in the bowls, and the swineherd handed out the cups. Philoetius, leader of men, passed them bread in fine baskets, and Melanthius poured their wine; and they reached out their hands to the good things spread before them. But Telemachus, with cunning in his mind, seated Odysseus inside the well-built hall beside the stone threshold, setting out for him a shabby stool and a little table. He put portions of the inner parts beside him, poured wine into a golden cup, and said to him: “Sit here now among the men and drink your wine. The taunts and the fists of all the suitors I will hold off you myself; for this house is no public hall—it belongs to Odysseus, and he won it for me. And you, suitors, keep your hearts in check, no insults and no blows, so that no strife or quarrel breaks out.”

So he spoke, and they all bit their lips and marveled at Telemachus, that he spoke so boldly. Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them: “Hard as it is, Achaeans, let us accept the word of Telemachus—and how he threatens us as he makes his speeches! Zeus the son of Cronus did not allow it; otherwise we would have silenced him in the halls before now, clear-voiced speaker though he is.” So spoke Antinous; but Telemachus paid no heed to his words. Meanwhile heralds were leading the sacred hecatomb of the gods through the town, and the long-haired Achaeans gathered beneath the shady grove of Apollo who shoots from afar. And when the men in the hall had roasted the outer flesh and drawn it off the spits, they divided the portions and feasted a glorious feast; and those who served set beside Odysseus a portion equal to what they took themselves; for so Telemachus ordered, the dear son of godlike Odysseus.

But Athena would not let the arrogant suitors hold back entirely from heart-stinging outrage, so that pain might sink still deeper into the heart of Odysseus, son of Laertes. There was among the suitors a man whose ways were lawless; Ctesippus was his name, and his home was in Same. Trusting in his prodigious wealth, he was courting the wife of Odysseus, so long away. Now he spoke among the arrogant suitors: “Hear me, proud suitors, while I say a word. The stranger has long had his portion, an equal one, as is proper; for it is neither fair nor just to cheat the guests of Telemachus, whoever comes to this house. But come, let me too give him a guest-gift, so that he in turn may have a prize to hand to the bath-woman, or to some other of the servants in the house of godlike Odysseus.” So saying, he snatched up an ox's hoof that lay in the basket and hurled it with his heavy hand. Odysseus avoided it with a slight tilt of his head, and in his heart he smiled a grim, sardonic smile; and the hoof struck the well-built wall.

Then Telemachus rebuked Ctesippus: “Ctesippus, it turned out luckier for you this way—you did not hit the stranger, for he dodged your throw himself. Otherwise I would have run you through the middle with my sharp spear, and instead of a wedding your father would have been busy here with a funeral. So let no man show his insolence in my house; for I now notice and understand everything, the good and the bad alike—before this, I was still a child. Even so, we endure to look on all this: the sheep slaughtered, the wine drunk, the bread eaten—for it is hard for one man to hold back many. But come, do me no more evil out of malice. And if you are bent on killing me myself with the bronze, I would choose even that: it would be far better to die than to look forever on these disgraceful doings—guests knocked about, and serving-women dragged shamefully through the beautiful house.”

So he spoke, and they all fell silent and still. At last Agelaus, son of Damastor, spoke among them: “Friends, when a thing is justly said, no one should take offense and answer it with hostile words. Do not strike the stranger, nor any of the servants in the house of godlike Odysseus. But to Telemachus and his mother I would say a gentle word, if it might find favor in the hearts of them both. As long as the hearts in your breasts still hoped that wise Odysseus would return to his own home, there was no blame in waiting and holding off the suitors in the house—that was the better course, if Odysseus had returned and come back to his home. But now it is plain that he will never return. Come then, sit down beside your mother and tell her this: let her marry whichever man is best and offers the most, so that you may keep and enjoy all your father's estate, eating and drinking, while she keeps another man's house.”

Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “No, Agelaus—by Zeus, and by the sufferings of my father, who far from Ithaca has perished or wanders lost—I put no delay in my mother's marriage. I urge her to marry whatever man she will, and I offer countless gifts besides. But I am ashamed to drive her from the hall against her will with a word of compulsion. May God never bring that to pass.”

So spoke Telemachus. And among the suitors Pallas Athena roused unquenchable laughter, and set their wits astray. Now they laughed with jaws that seemed not their own; the meat they ate was dabbled with blood; their eyes filled with tears, and their hearts felt the coming of wailing. Then godlike Theoclymenus spoke among them: “Poor wretches, what evil is this that has come over you? Your heads and your faces and your knees beneath are shrouded in night; wailing has blazed up; your cheeks are wet with tears; the walls and the fine crossbeams are spattered with blood. The porch is full of ghosts, the courtyard is full of them, hurrying down to Erebus beneath the gloom; the sun has perished out of heaven, and an evil mist has spread over everything.”

So he spoke, and they all laughed merrily at him. Eurymachus, son of Polybus, began to speak among them: “The stranger is out of his mind, this newcomer from abroad. Quick, young men, escort him out of the house and off to the assembly-place, since he finds it dark as night in here.” Godlike Theoclymenus answered him: “Eurymachus, I ask no escorts of you. I have eyes and ears and both my feet, and a mind in my breast that is soundly built and nothing shameful. With these I will go out the door, for I see evil coming upon you—evil that not one of you suitors can escape or avoid, you who in the house of godlike Odysseus commit outrage against men and devise reckless deeds.” So saying, he went out of the well-appointed house and came to Piraeus, who received him gladly.

But the suitors all looked at one another and set about provoking Telemachus, laughing at his guests. And one of the overbearing young men would say: “Telemachus, no man alive is unluckier in his guests than you. Look at this one you keep here, a grubby vagabond, craving bread and wine, useless for work and useless in a fight, a mere dead weight on the earth. And then this other one stood up to prophesy! If you would take my advice, it would be far better: let us throw these strangers into a many-benched ship and send them off to the Sicels, where they would fetch you a worthy price.” So spoke the suitors; but he paid no heed to their words. Silent, he kept his eyes on his father, waiting always for the moment when he would lay his hands on the shameless suitors.

And the daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope, had set her beautiful chair just opposite, and heard every man's word in the halls. For their dinner they had made ready with laughter was sweet and to their liking, since they had slaughtered so many beasts; but no supper could ever be more joyless than the one a goddess and a strong man were soon to set before them. For they had been the first to devise unseemly deeds.

## Book XXI

Now the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, put it into the mind of the daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope, to set the bow and the gray iron before the suitors in the halls of Odysseus — the contest, and the beginning of the slaughter. She climbed the high staircase of her house and took the well-curved key in her strong hand, a beautiful key of bronze, with a handle of ivory. She went with her serving-women to the farthest storeroom, where the treasures of her lord lay — bronze and gold and iron wrought with much labor. And there lay the backsprung bow and the quiver that held the arrows, and many groaning shafts were inside it: gifts a guest-friend had given him when they met in Lacedaemon — Iphitus, son of Eurytus, a man like the immortals.

The two had met each other in Messene, in the house of warlike Ortilochus. Odysseus had come to collect a debt the whole people owed him, for Messenian men had lifted three hundred sheep out of Ithaca in their many-benched ships, shepherds and all. On that errand Odysseus had come the long way while still a boy, for his father and the other elders had sent him. Iphitus, for his part, was searching for horses he had lost — twelve mares, with sturdy work-mules at foot. These were to prove his death and his doom in the end, when he came to the strong-hearted son of Zeus, the man Heracles, that master of mighty deeds, who killed him, guest though he was, in his own house — the pitiless man, with no regard for the watchful eyes of the gods or for the table he himself had set before him: he killed the man, and kept the strong-hoofed mares in his own halls. It was while asking after these that Iphitus met Odysseus, and gave him the bow, which great Eurytus once carried and left to his son when he died in his lofty house. And Odysseus gave him a sharp sword and a stout spear, the beginning of a heartfelt guest-friendship. But they never came to know each other at table, for before that could be, the son of Zeus killed Iphitus, son of Eurytus, the man like the immortals, who had given him the bow. Godlike Odysseus never took it with him when he went off to war in the black ships; it lay at home in his halls, a keepsake of a dear guest-friend, and he carried it only in his own land.

Now when she reached that storeroom, shining among women, and stepped up onto the oak threshold — which a carpenter had once planed skillfully and trued to the line, fitting the doorposts into it and hanging the polished doors — she quickly loosed the strap from its hook, slid in the key, and with a straight, sure thrust shot back the bolts of the doors. And they bellowed like a bull grazing in a meadow — so loud the beautiful doors bellowed at the stroke of the key, and they flew open before her. She stepped up onto the high platform where the chests stood in which fragrant clothing lay stored. Reaching up from there she took the bow from its peg, case and all — the shining case that wrapped it. And sitting down right there, laying it across her knees, she wept aloud, keening, as she drew out her lord's bow. When she had had her fill of tearful lamenting, she went to the hall, to the proud suitors, holding in her hand the backsprung bow and the quiver that held the arrows, and many groaning shafts were inside it. Beside her the serving-women carried a box in which lay much iron and bronze, her lord's gear for the contest.

When she came to the suitors, shining among women, she stood by the doorpost of the strongly built roof, holding her shining veil before her cheeks, and a devoted serving-woman stood on either side of her. Then at once she spoke to the suitors and said: “Hear me, you proud suitors, who have plagued this house with your eating and drinking, on and on without pause, all the long time its master has been gone; and you could not offer a single other excuse for it, no pretext at all, except your desire to marry me and take me as your wife. Come then, suitors — here is your prize set before you. I will set out the great bow of godlike Odysseus. Whoever most easily strings the bow in his hands and shoots an arrow through all twelve axes, with him I will go, leaving behind this house of my marriage, this beautiful house so full of the goods of life, which I think I shall remember even in my dreams.”

So she spoke, and told Eumaeus, the godlike swineherd, to set the bow and the gray iron before the suitors. Eumaeus burst into tears as he took them and laid them down; and elsewhere in the hall the cowherd wept when he saw his master's bow. But Antinous rebuked them both, and spoke out: “Stupid peasants, who cannot think past the day! Wretched pair, why are you streaming tears and stirring up the heart in the lady's breast — as if her heart did not lie in pain enough already, since she lost her beloved husband. Sit and eat in silence, or else take your weeping outside, and leave the bow lying where it is — a decisive contest for the suitors; for I do not think this polished bow will be strung easily. There is no man in this whole company such as Odysseus was. I saw him myself — I remember it well, though I was still a foolish child.”

So he spoke; but the heart in his breast was hoping that he himself would string the bow and shoot through the iron. In fact he was to be the first to taste an arrow from the hands of blameless Odysseus — the very man he was then dishonoring as he sat in his halls, stirring up all his companions against him.

Then the sacred strength of Telemachus spoke among them: “Well now — Zeus son of Cronus must have made me a fool! My dear mother, sensible as she is, says she will go off with another man and leave this house — and I laugh and amuse myself like an idiot. Come then, suitors, since here is your prize: a woman whose like there is not now in all the Achaean land, not in sacred Pylos, not in Argos or Mycenae, not in Ithaca itself or on the dark mainland. But you know that yourselves — why should I sing my own mother's praises? Come, no dragging it out with excuses, no more hanging back from stringing the bow — let us see it done. And I might try the bow myself. If I string it and shoot through the iron, then my honored mother would not be leaving this house with another man while I grieved, left behind — a man already able to take up his father's fine prizes.”

With that he sprang upright, threw the crimson cloak from his shoulders, and took the sharp sword from his back. First he set up the axes, digging one long trench for them all, trued to the line, and stamping the earth firm around them. Amazement seized all who watched, at how neatly he set them up, when he had never seen it done before. Then he went and stood on the threshold and began to try the bow.
