Three times he made it quiver, straining to bend it; three times he eased off his pull, though he still hoped in his heart to string the bow and shoot through the iron. And now, hauling it back a fourth time with all his force, he would have strung it — but Odysseus shook his head and stopped him, eager as he was. Then the sacred strength of Telemachus spoke among them again: “Ah well — it seems I shall be a coward and a weakling all my days; or perhaps I am only too young, and cannot yet trust my hands to defend me against a man who picks a quarrel first. Come then, you who are stronger than I am, try the bow, and let us finish the contest.” So saying he set the bow away from him on the ground, leaning it against the joined and polished planks of the door, propped the swift arrow against the beautiful door-hook, and sat back down on the chair from which he had risen.

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them: “Rise up in order, all you companions, from left to right, beginning from the place where the wine is poured.” So spoke Antinous, and his word pleased them. First to rise was Leodes, son of Oenops, who was their diviner over the sacrifices, and always sat in the farthest corner by the beautiful mixing-bowl; he alone hated their reckless doings, and was indignant at all the suitors. He was the first now to take up the bow and the swift arrow. He went and stood on the threshold and tried the bow, but he could not string it; long before that his soft, uncalloused hands gave out with hauling on it, and he said to the suitors: “Friends, I cannot string it — let someone else take it. This bow will strip many chief men of spirit and life; and truly it is far better to die than to live on and fail of the thing for whose sake we gather here forever, waiting day after day. Right now many a man hopes in his heart and longs to marry Penelope, the bedmate of Odysseus. But once he has tried the bow and seen for himself, let him go court some other Achaean woman of the beautiful robes and seek her with his bridal gifts; and let Penelope then marry the man who offers most, the one who comes as her destined husband.” So he spoke, and set the bow away from him, leaning it against the joined and polished planks of the door, propped the swift arrow against the beautiful door-hook, and sat back down on the chair from which he had risen.

But Antinous rebuked him, and spoke out: “Leodes, what a word has escaped the fence of your teeth — a dreadful, grievous word! I am angry just hearing it — that this bow will strip chief men of spirit and life, all because you cannot string it. Your honored mother did not bear you to be a drawer of bows and arrows, no; but others among the proud suitors will soon string it.” So he spoke, and gave his orders to Melanthius the goatherd: “Look alive, Melanthius — light a fire in the hall, and set a big stool beside it with a fleece over it, and bring out the great round of tallow that is stored inside, so we young men can warm the bow and grease it with fat, and then try it and finish the contest.” So he spoke, and Melanthius at once kindled the untiring fire, and brought up a stool and set it near with a fleece over it, and brought out the great round of tallow that was stored inside. Warming the bow with that, the young men kept trying; but they could not string it — they fell far short of the strength it needed. Antinous still held back, though, and godlike Eurymachus, the leaders of the suitors, far the best of them all in prowess.

Meanwhile the two of them had gone out of the house together, the cowherd and the swineherd of godlike Odysseus; and godlike Odysseus himself came out of the house after them. When they were outside the doors and the courtyard, he spoke to them with gentle words: “Cowherd — and you too, swineherd — shall I say a thing, or keep it to myself? My heart tells me to speak. What kind of men would you be about defending Odysseus, if he came from somewhere all of a sudden, and some god brought him here? Would you fight for the suitors, or for Odysseus? Say it just as your heart and spirit bid you.”

Then the man who herded the cattle answered him: “Father Zeus, would that you might bring that wish to pass — that the man himself might come, with some god to guide him! Then you would learn what strength is mine, and how my hands follow it.” And in the same way Eumaeus prayed to all the gods that wise Odysseus might come home to his own house.

When he had made sure of the true mind of these two, he answered them again, saying: “Home he is — here, I myself, before you. After suffering many evils I have come in the twentieth year to my native land. And I know that of all my servants, you two alone have longed for my coming; of the rest I have not heard one pray that I might come back home again. So to you two I will tell the truth of how it shall be. If a god brings the proud suitors down under my hands, I will find wives for you both, and give you possessions and houses built near my own; and from then on you shall be, in my eyes, companions and brothers of Telemachus. Come, I will show you an unmistakable sign as well, so that you may know me for certain and trust me in your hearts: the scar of the wound a boar dealt me long ago with his white tusk, when I went to Parnassus with the sons of Autolycus.”

So saying, he drew his rags aside from the great scar. And when the two of them had looked at it and taken in every detail, they threw their arms around warlike Odysseus and wept, and kissed his head and shoulders in loving welcome; and in the same way Odysseus kissed their heads and hands. And the light of the sun would have gone down on their weeping, if Odysseus himself had not checked them, saying: “Stop your crying and lamenting now, in case someone comes out of the hall and sees us, and tells them inside. Go in one after another, not all together — I first, and you after me. And let this be our signal: all the others, that whole company of proud suitors, will refuse to let the bow and quiver be given to me. But you, godlike Eumaeus, as you carry the bow through the hall, put it into my hands; and tell the women to bar the close-fitted doors of the hall, and if any of them hears groaning or the crash of men inside our walls, she is not to stir out of doors, but stay where she is, in silence, at her work. And you, godlike Philoetius — I put the courtyard gates in your charge: bolt them, and quickly lash the fastening on.” So saying, he went into the well-built house, and sat down on the stool from which he had risen; and then the two servants of godlike Odysseus went in as well.

Eurymachus by now was handling the bow, warming it this way and that in the glow of the fire; but even so he could not string it, and he groaned deep in his proud heart. Bitterly he spoke out: “Ah, misery — this galls me, for myself and for all of us! It is not the marriage I mourn so much, vexed though I am; there are plenty of other Achaean women, some in sea-girt Ithaca itself, some in the other cities. No — it is that we fall so far short of godlike Odysseus in strength that we cannot even string his bow. That is a disgrace for men still unborn to hear of.”

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, answered him: “Eurymachus, it will not come to that — and you know it yourself. Today, throughout the land, is the god's feast, a holy day. Who could be bending bows on such a day? Put it down and let it be. As for the axes — why not leave them all standing? I hardly think anyone is going to walk into the hall of Odysseus, son of Laertes, and carry them off. Come, let the cupbearer pour the first drops into the cups, so that we may make libation and lay the curved bow aside; and in the morning tell Melanthius the goatherd to bring goats, far the pick of all his herds, so we may lay the thigh-pieces on the altar for Apollo, the famous archer, and then try the bow and finish the contest.” So spoke Antinous, and his word pleased them.

Heralds poured water over their hands, and young men filled the mixing-bowls brim-full with drink and served it round to all, pouring the first drops into every cup. And when they had made libation and drunk as much as their hearts desired, resourceful Odysseus, his mind full of cunning, spoke among them: “Hear me, suitors of the far-famed queen, while I say what the heart in my breast commands. I appeal above all to Eurymachus and to godlike Antinous, since his word just now was rightly spoken: for today, let the bow rest, and turn the matter over to the gods; in the morning the god will give the victory to whomever he wishes. But come — give me the polished bow, and let me try my hands and my strength here among you, and see whether I still have the force that once lived in my supple limbs, or whether wandering and neglect have destroyed it by now.”

So he spoke, and they were all indignant beyond all measure, afraid that he might string the polished bow. And Antinous rebuked him, and spoke out: “You wretched stranger, you have not a scrap of sense in you. Is it not enough that you dine at your ease among us, your betters, undisturbed, with no share of the feast withheld from you, and that you listen to our words and our talk, when no other stranger or beggar gets to listen to our talk at all? It is the honey-sweet wine that is wounding you — wine, which harms any man who gulps it down open-mouthed and does not drink in measure. Wine addled even the Centaur, famous Eurytion, in the hall of great-hearted Peirithous, when he came to visit the Lapiths. When wine had addled his wits, in his madness he did terrible things in the house of Peirithous; grief seized the heroes, and they sprang up and dragged him out through the porch to the door, and sheared off his ears and his nose with the pitiless bronze; and he, his wits ruined, went off carrying the burden of his own folly in his reckless heart. From that day comes the feud between Centaurs and men — but it was for himself first that he found evil, heavy with wine. Just so I promise you great trouble, if you string that bow. You will meet with no kindness anywhere in our land; we will pack you off in a black ship to King Echetus, the mutilator of all men, and out of his hands you will not come alive. Sit quiet and drink, and do not pick quarrels with younger men.”

Then circumspect Penelope answered him: “Antinous, it is neither honorable nor just to cheat the guests of Telemachus, whoever it may be that comes to this house. Do you imagine that if this stranger strings the great bow of Odysseus, trusting in his hands and his strength, he will lead me home and make me his wife? He himself has no such hope in his breast. Let none of you sit at this feast eating his heart out over that — it would be altogether unfitting.”

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered her: “Daughter of Icarius, circumspect Penelope, we do not imagine that this man will take you — of course that would be unfitting. But we shrink from the talk of men and women, from what some baser Achaean may one day say: 'Far inferior men are courting the wife of a blameless man, and cannot even string his polished bow — and then some beggar, a wanderer who came from nowhere, strung the bow with ease and shot through the iron.' So they will say; and that would be our shame.”

Then circumspect Penelope answered him: “Eurymachus, men who dishonor and devour the house of a great man cannot in any case have a good name among the people — so why count this as the disgrace? This stranger is a big man, and well built, and he claims to be the son of a noble father. Come, give him the polished bow, and let us see. For I will tell you this, and it shall be done: if he strings it, and Apollo grants him that glory, I will dress him in a cloak and tunic, fine clothing, and give him a sharp javelin to keep off dogs and men, and a two-edged sword; and I will give him sandals for his feet, and send him wherever his heart and spirit bid him go.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered her: “Mother, no Achaean has a better right than I to give the bow, or to refuse it, to whomever I wish — not one of all who are lords in rocky Ithaca, nor in the islands over toward horse-pasturing Elis. None of them shall force me against my will, not even if I choose to give the stranger this bow outright, to carry away with him. Go back to your rooms and see to your own work, the loom and the distaff, and tell your serving-women to be about their tasks. The bow shall be men's concern — all men's, and mine above all; for the power in this house is mine.” Astonished, she went back to her rooms, and laid her son's thoughtful words away in her heart. Going up to her chamber with her serving-women, she wept there for Odysseus, her beloved husband, until bright-eyed Athena cast sweet sleep upon her eyelids.

Meanwhile the godlike swineherd had taken up the curved bow and was carrying it — and the suitors all raised an uproar through the hall, and one of the arrogant young men would say: “Where do you think you are taking that curved bow, you miserable swineherd, you vagrant? Soon enough, out among your pigs, alone and far from men, the swift dogs you bred yourself will eat you — if only Apollo and the other immortal gods are gracious to us.” So they spoke, and he set the bow down on the spot where he stood, frightened, because so many were shouting in the hall. But Telemachus from the other side called out to him with a threat: “Old friend, carry the bow onward! You will soon be sorry if you obey them all. Younger than you though I am, I may yet chase you into the fields, pelting you with stones — for in strength I am the better man. Would that I were as much stronger in hands and force than all the suitors in this house! Then I would soon send more than one of them off in misery, out of our house — for they are plotting evil.”

So he spoke, and the suitors all laughed sweetly at him, and let go of their bitter anger at Telemachus. The swineherd carried the bow on through the hall, came up to warlike Odysseus, and put it into his hands. Then he called the nurse Eurycleia aside and said to her: “Telemachus commands you, prudent Eurycleia: bar the close-fitted doors of the hall; and if any of the women hears groaning or the crash of men inside our walls, she is not to stir out of doors, but stay where she is, in silence, at her work.” So he spoke, and for her the word stayed wingless: she said nothing, and barred the doors of the well-built hall. And in silence Philoetius slipped out of the house and barred the gates of the well-fenced courtyard. Under the portico lay a curved ship's cable of papyrus fiber; with this he lashed the gates fast, and went back inside, and sat down on the stool from which he had risen, keeping his eyes on Odysseus.

And Odysseus was already handling the bow, turning it over and over, testing it this way and that, in case worms had eaten into the horn while its lord was away. And one man would say, with a glance at his neighbor: “Quite the connoisseur of bows, this fellow — a sly expert! No doubt he has bows like it lying at home himself, or else he means to make one, the way he turns it over in his hands, this way and that — a beggar schooled in mischief.” And another of the arrogant young men would say: “May he get exactly as much profit out of it, ever, as his chance of ever stringing that bow!”

So spoke the suitors. But resourceful Odysseus, the moment he had lifted the great bow and looked it over on every side — then, as easily as a man skilled in the lyre and in song stretches a string around a new peg, fastening the twisted sheep-gut at both ends, so without any effort Odysseus strung the great bow. And taking it in his right hand he tried the string, and under his touch it sang out sweetly, like a swallow's note. A great ache came over the suitors, and the color changed in every face; and Zeus thundered loud, showing forth his signs. Then much-enduring godlike Odysseus rejoiced, because the son of crooked-counseled Cronus had sent him an omen.

He took up a swift arrow that lay bare on the table beside him — the rest were stored inside the hollow quiver, those arrows the Achaeans were soon to taste. He set it on the bridge of the bow, drew back the string and the notched shaft together, sitting right where he was on his stool, and let the arrow fly with a straight, sure aim; and he did not miss a single axe, from the first helve-socket onward — clean through them all and out beyond went the arrow heavy with bronze. Then he said to Telemachus: “Telemachus, the stranger sitting in your halls does you no disgrace. I did not miss the mark, and I did not labor long over stringing the bow. My strength is still sound in me — not what the suitors, in their scorn, keep saying it is. But now it is time for supper to be made ready for the Achaeans, while there is still light; and afterward for other entertainment as well, song and the lyre — the crowning ornaments of a feast.”

He spoke, and signaled with his brows; and Telemachus, the beloved son of godlike Odysseus, slung on his sharp sword and closed his hand around his spear, and took his stand beside the chair, close at his father's side, armed in gleaming bronze.

## Book XXII

Then resourceful Odysseus stripped off his rags and leapt onto the great threshold, holding the bow and the quiver full of arrows, and he poured the swift shafts out at his feet and spoke among the suitors: “So this decisive contest is finished. Now for another mark, one that no man has ever hit—I will know whether I can strike it, and whether Apollo grants me glory.” He spoke, and aimed the bitter arrow at Antinous. At that moment Antinous was about to lift a beautiful cup, golden, two-handled, and was turning it in his hands, ready to drink the wine; slaughter was the last thing on his mind. Who would think that one man among many, at a crowded feast, however strong he might be, would bring down on him evil death and black fate? But Odysseus took aim and struck him with the arrow in the throat, and the point passed clean through his soft neck. He slumped to one side; the cup fell from his hand as he was hit, and at once a thick jet of human blood came up through his nostrils. Sharply he kicked the table away from him, spilling the food to the ground, and the bread and roasted meat were fouled in the blood.

The suitors raised an uproar through the house when they saw the man fall, and they sprang from their seats, driven in panic through the hall, peering everywhere along the well-built walls; and nowhere was there a shield or a stout spear to lay hold of. They berated Odysseus with angry words: “Stranger, you shoot at men to your own ruin. You will never enter another contest; now your sheer destruction is certain. You have killed the man who was far the best of the young men of Ithaca; for that the vultures will eat you here.” So each man guessed, for they truly believed he had killed the man without meaning to; the fools did not see that on them too, one and all, the cords of destruction had been fastened.

With a dark glance resourceful Odysseus said to them: “You dogs! You told yourselves I would never come home again from the land of the Trojans, and so you have been shearing my house bare, sleeping with my serving-women by force, and courting my wife while I still lived, fearing neither the gods who hold the wide heavens nor any vengeance from men to come after. Now on you, one and all, the cords of destruction are fastened.” So he spoke, and pale fear seized them all, and each man peered about for some way to escape sheer destruction.

Eurymachus alone answered him: “If you are truly Odysseus of Ithaca come home, then all you say of what the Achaeans have done is just—many reckless deeds in your halls, many in your fields. But the man to blame for everything already lies dead: Antinous. It was he who set these doings in motion, not so much from want or need of the marriage as with other designs, which the son of Cronus never brought to pass for him: to be king himself over the people of well-built Ithaca, and to ambush and kill your son. Now he has been killed, as he deserved. But spare your own people; and afterward we will make amends throughout the land for all that has been drunk and eaten in your halls—each of us bringing you recompense worth twenty oxen, paying you in bronze and gold until your heart is softened. Until then no one could blame you for your anger.”

With a dark glance resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Eurymachus, not if you handed over your whole inheritance—all you now possess, and more added from elsewhere—not even then would I stay my hands from killing until the suitors have paid for all their transgression. Now the choice lies open before you: fight, or run, if any of you can escape death and the fates. But I do not think one of you will escape sheer destruction.” So he spoke, and their knees went slack and their hearts sank where they stood.

But Eurymachus spoke among them a second time: “Friends, this man will not hold back his invincible hands. Now that he has the polished bow and the quiver, he will shoot from the smooth threshold until he has killed us all. Let us remember our fighting spirit. Draw your swords, hold the tables up against his deadly arrows, and let us all rush him together, and see if we can thrust him from the threshold and the doors and get through the town, and the alarm would be raised at once. Then this man would soon have shot his last.”

So saying, he drew his sharp sword of bronze, whetted on both edges, and leapt at him with a terrible cry; but in the same instant godlike Odysseus let fly an arrow and struck him in the chest beside the nipple, and drove the swift shaft into his liver. The sword dropped from his hand to the ground, and he doubled over the table and crashed down sprawling, spilling the food and the two-handled cup to the floor. In his agony he beat the ground with his forehead, and kicking with both feet he shook the chair; and mist poured down over his eyes.

Then Amphinomus went for glorious Odysseus, charging straight at him with his sharp sword drawn, hoping to force him back from the doors. But Telemachus was too quick for him, and struck him from behind with his bronze-tipped spear between the shoulders and drove it through his chest; he fell with a crash and struck the ground full with his forehead. Telemachus sprang away, leaving the long-shadowed spear where it stood, in Amphinomus; for he was afraid that if he tried to draw the long spear out, one of the Achaeans might rush him with a sword and stab him, or cut him down as he bent over the body.

He ran off, and quickly reached his dear father, and standing close he spoke winged words: “Father, now I will bring you a shield and two spears and a helmet all of bronze, fitted to your temples; and on my way I will arm myself, and give arms to the swineherd and the cowherd as well. Better to be armed.” Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Run and bring them, while I still have arrows to defend myself, or they may force me from the doors while I stand alone.” So he spoke, and Telemachus obeyed his dear father, and went to the storeroom where the famous arms were lying. From it he took out four shields, eight spears, and four bronze helmets with thick horsehair plumes, and carried them back, quickly reaching his dear father. He clothed his own body in the bronze first, and likewise the two servants put on the fine armor, and they took their stand around Odysseus, the cunning man of many devices.

As for Odysseus, as long as he had arrows to defend himself, he kept aiming and shooting the suitors down in his own house, one after another, and they fell thick and fast. But when the arrows failed the lord as he shot, he leaned the bow against the doorpost of the well-built hall, to stand against the gleaming entrance wall, and slung the four-layered shield about his shoulders. On his mighty head he set the well-made helmet with its horsehair crest—the plume nodded terribly above—and he took up two strong spears tipped with bronze.

Now there was a raised side-door in the well-built wall, and at the topmost level of the threshold of the strong hall was a way out into the passage, closed by well-fitted doors. Odysseus told the godlike swineherd to watch this door, standing close by it, for it was the only way out. Then Agelaus spoke among the suitors, declaring his word to all: “Friends, will no one climb up through the side-door and get word to the people, so the alarm is raised at once? Then this man would soon have shot his last.” But Melanthius the goatherd answered him: “It cannot be done, Agelaus, nursed by Zeus. The fine gates of the courtyard are terribly near, and the mouth of the passage is narrow; one man could hold off everyone, if he had any courage. But come, let me bring you armor from the storeroom to arm yourselves, for it is inside, I think, and nowhere else, that Odysseus and his glorious son have stowed the arms.”

So saying, Melanthius the goatherd climbed up through the vents of the hall to the storerooms of Odysseus. From there he took out twelve shields, as many spears, and as many bronze helmets with thick horsehair plumes, and went back quickly and gave them to the suitors. Then Odysseus's knees went slack and his heart sank, when he saw them putting on armor and brandishing long spears in their hands; the task before him now looked great. Quickly he spoke winged words to Telemachus: “Telemachus, surely one of the women in the halls is stirring up an evil fight against us—or it is Melanthius.” Thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “Father, the fault here is my own—no one else is to blame. I left the close-fitted door of the storeroom standing ajar, and their lookout was sharper than I. But go, godlike Eumaeus, shut the storeroom door, and find out whether it is one of the women doing this, or Dolius's son Melanthius, as I suspect.”

So they spoke to one another, and Melanthius the goatherd went again to the storeroom to fetch more fine armor. But the godlike swineherd saw him, and at once said to Odysseus, who was close by: “Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, resourceful Odysseus—there he goes again, that ruinous man, just as we guessed, off to the storeroom. Tell me truly: shall I kill him, if I prove the stronger, or bring him here to you, to pay for the many crimes he has plotted in your house?” Resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Telemachus and I will hold the proud suitors inside the hall, however hard they press. You two twist his feet and hands behind his back, throw him into the storeroom, and tie the doors fast behind you; then knot a braided rope to him and haul him up the tall pillar close to the roof-beams, so that he stays alive a long while and suffers cruel torment.”

So he spoke, and they heard him well and obeyed. They went to the storeroom, unnoticed by the man inside; he was hunting for armor in a far corner of the room, while the two of them stood waiting on either side of the doorposts. And when Melanthius the goatherd came over the threshold, carrying in one hand a fine helmet and in the other a broad old shield, spotted with mildew—the shield of the hero Laertes, which he had carried in his youth, but it had long since lain there, and the stitching of its straps had rotted—the two sprang on him and seized him, dragged him inside by the hair, and threw him to the floor, sick at heart. They bound his feet and hands with a bond that tortured the flesh, twisting them tight behind his back, as the son of Laertes, much-enduring godlike Odysseus, had ordered; then they knotted a braided rope to him and hauled him up the tall pillar close to the roof-beams. And Eumaeus the swineherd taunted him: “Now, Melanthius, you will keep watch all night through, lying on a soft bed, as you deserve; and early Dawn on her golden throne will not slip past you as she rises from the streams of Ocean, at the hour when you drive in your goats to the house for the suitors' feast.” So he was left there, stretched in that deadly bond; and the two put on their armor, closed the shining door, and went back to Odysseus, the cunning man of many devices. There they stood, breathing fury—the four of them on the threshold, and inside the house many brave men.

Then Athena, daughter of Zeus, came near them, likened to Mentor in form and voice. Odysseus rejoiced to see her and said: “Mentor, ward off ruin from us! Remember your dear companion, who used to do you good service; you and I are of one age.” So he spoke, though he guessed it was Athena, rouser of armies. But from the other side the suitors shouted in the hall, and first Agelaus, son of Damastor, threatened her: “Mentor, do not let Odysseus talk you over into fighting the suitors and defending him. For this is how I think our purpose will be accomplished: when we have killed these two, father and son, you will be killed beside them, for what you mean to do in these halls—you will pay for it with your head. And when with our bronze we have stripped you men of your power, all the possessions you have, indoors and out, we will pool together with those of Odysseus; we will not let your sons live in your halls, nor your daughters, nor your honored wife go about the town of Ithaca.”

So he spoke, and Athena grew angrier still at heart, and she scolded Odysseus with angry words: “Odysseus, you no longer have the steady strength and courage you had when for nine years, for the sake of white-armed Helen of noble birth, you fought the Trojans without pause, and killed many men in dread combat, and by your counsel the wide-wayed city of Priam was taken. How is it that now, when you have come to your own house and your own goods, you whine about facing the suitors and being brave? Come here, old friend, stand beside me and watch the work, and see what kind of man Mentor, son of Alcimus, is at repaying kindness in the midst of enemies.” So she spoke, but she did not yet grant him victory outright; she was still testing the strength and courage of Odysseus and his glorious son. She herself darted up to the roof-beam of the smoke-blackened hall and perched there, in the likeness of a swallow.

Now Agelaus, son of Damastor, urged on the suitors, with Eurynomus, Amphimedon, Demoptolemus, Peisander son of Polyctor, and warlike Polybus; for these were by far the best in prowess of the suitors who still lived and fought for their lives—the rest the bow and the showering arrows had already brought down. Agelaus spoke among them, declaring his word to all: “Friends, now this man will stay his invincible hands. Mentor has gone off after empty boasts, and they are left alone at the outer doors. So do not hurl your long spears all at once; come, you six throw first, and may Zeus grant that Odysseus is hit and we win the glory. The others are nothing to worry about, once he has fallen.”

So he spoke, and they all hurled their spears as he ordered, eagerly; but Athena made every cast useless. One man hit the doorpost of the well-built hall, another the close-fitted door; another's ash spear, heavy with bronze, struck in the wall. And when they had dodged the suitors' spears, much-enduring godlike Odysseus began: “Friends, now I give the word that we too throw into the crowd of the suitors, who on top of their earlier crimes are bent on cutting us down.” So he spoke, and they all hurled their sharp spears, aiming straight. Odysseus killed Demoptolemus; Telemachus, Euryades; the swineherd, Elatus; and the man who herded the cattle killed Peisander. All these together bit the vast floor, and the suitors drew back to the inner corner of the hall, while the others rushed forward and pulled their spears out of the corpses.

Again the suitors hurled their sharp spears, eagerly; but Athena made most of them useless. One man hit the doorpost of the well-built hall, another the close-fitted door; another's ash spear, heavy with bronze, struck in the wall. But Amphimedon caught Telemachus on the hand at the wrist, a glancing blow, and the bronze broke the surface of the skin; and Ctesippus grazed Eumaeus's shoulder over his shield with his long spear, which flew on past and fell to the ground. Then again the men around Odysseus, the cunning man of many devices, hurled their sharp spears into the crowd of the suitors. This time Odysseus, sacker of cities, hit Eurydamas; Telemachus, Amphimedon; the swineherd, Polybus; and afterward the man who herded the cattle struck Ctesippus in the chest, and boasted over him: “Son of Polytherses, you lover of insults, never again give way to your folly and talk big; leave the last word to the gods, for they are far stronger. This is your guest-gift, to match the ox-hoof you once gave godlike Odysseus when he went begging through the house.”

So spoke the herdsman of the spiral-horned cattle. Then Odysseus wounded the son of Damastor at close quarters with his long spear, and Telemachus wounded Leocritus, son of Euenor, driving his spear into the middle of his flank, and the bronze went clean through; he pitched forward on his face and struck the ground full with his forehead. And now Athena held up her man-destroying aegis, high overhead from the roof, and the suitors' wits were struck with panic. They stampeded through the hall like a herd of cattle when the darting gadfly attacks and scatters them, in the spring season, when the days grow long. And the others, like vultures with crooked talons and hooked beaks that come down from the mountains and swoop upon smaller birds—the birds skim the plain, cowering away under the clouds, but the vultures pounce and kill them, and there is no defense and no escape, and men rejoice at the hunt—so they charged the suitors through the house and struck them down on every side. A hideous groaning rose as heads were smashed, and the whole floor ran with blood.

Then Leodes rushed at Odysseus and caught him by the knees, and begged him with winged words: “By your knees, Odysseus—respect me and have mercy. I tell you I never said or did a reckless thing to any of the women in your halls; no, I kept trying to stop the other suitors whenever one behaved that way. But they would not listen to me and hold their hands from evil, and so by their own recklessness they have met a shameful fate. And I, who was only their priest of sacrifices and did nothing, shall lie among them—such is the thanks that good deeds earn hereafter.” With a dark glance resourceful Odysseus answered him: “If you claim to have been the priest of their sacrifices among these men, then you must often have prayed in my halls that the fulfillment of my sweet homecoming be kept far from me, and that my dear wife go with you and bear you children. For that you shall not escape a bitter death.” So saying, he took up in his strong hand a sword that lay there—Agelaus had let it fall to the ground when he was killed—and drove it through the middle of his neck; and his head, still speaking, was mingled with the dust.

But the singer, Phemius son of Terpius, still sought to escape black fate—the man who sang among the suitors under compulsion. He stood with the clear-toned lyre in his hands, close by the side-door, and his mind was divided two ways: whether to slip out of the hall and sit at the well-built altar of great Zeus of the Courtyard, where Laertes and Odysseus had burned many thigh-pieces of oxen, or to rush forward and clasp Odysseus by the knees in supplication. As he turned it over, this seemed the better course: to grasp the knees of Odysseus, son of Laertes. So he laid the hollow lyre on the ground, between the mixing-bowl and the silver-studded chair, rushed forward himself, and caught Odysseus by the knees, and begged him with winged words: “By your knees, Odysseus—respect me and have mercy. You will grieve for it afterward if you kill a singer, one who sings for gods and men alike. I am self-taught, and a god has planted in my heart songs of every kind; I am fit to sing before you as before a god. Do not be eager, then, to cut my throat. Telemachus, your own dear son, can tell you that it was not by my own will or wish that I kept coming to your house to sing for the suitors at their feasts; they were too many and too strong, and they brought me by force.”

So he spoke, and the sacred strength of Telemachus heard him, and quickly he called to his father, who was near: “Hold—do not wound this man with the bronze; he is innocent. And let us spare the herald Medon too, who always cared for me in our house when I was a child—unless Philoetius or the swineherd has killed him already, or he crossed your path as you stormed through the house.” So he spoke, and Medon heard him, a man of prudent mind; for he lay cowering under a chair, wrapped in the freshly flayed hide of an ox, trying to escape black fate. At once he sprang up from under the chair, quickly threw off the ox-hide, rushed forward, and caught Telemachus by the knees, and begged him with winged words: “Friend, here I am—hold back, and speak to your father, or in the pride of his strength he will destroy me with the sharp bronze, in his fury at the suitors, who wasted his goods in the halls and, the fools, showed you no honor.”

Resourceful Odysseus smiled at him and said: “Take heart; he has pulled you clear and saved you, so that you may know in your heart, and tell another too, how much better good deeds are than evil ones. But go out of the hall, both of you, and sit in the courtyard, away from the slaughter—you and the singer of many songs—while I finish in the house the work I must do.” So he spoke, and the two of them went out of the hall and sat down by the altar of great Zeus, peering about in every direction, expecting death at every moment. And Odysseus peered all through his house, to see whether any man was still hiding alive, trying to escape black fate. But he saw them, every one, fallen in the blood and the dust—so many of them, like fish that fishermen have hauled out of the gray sea onto the curved beach in a many-meshed net: they lie heaped on the sand, longing for the waves of the sea, and the shining sun takes the life out of them. So the suitors lay heaped upon one another.

Then resourceful Odysseus said to Telemachus: “Telemachus, go and call the nurse Eurycleia here, so that I may tell her what is in my mind.” So he spoke, and Telemachus obeyed his dear father; he rattled the door and called to the nurse Eurycleia: “Up and come here, old woman born long ago, you who watch over the serving-women in our halls. Come—my father calls you; he has something to say.” So he spoke, and her answer stayed unwinged. She opened the doors of the stately hall and came, and Telemachus led the way. She found Odysseus among the bodies of the slain, spattered with blood and gore like a lion that comes from feeding on a farmyard ox: all his chest and his cheeks on either side are bloody, and he is terrible to look upon. So Odysseus was spattered, his feet and his hands above. And when she saw the corpses and the unspeakable blood, she made ready to cry aloud in triumph, seeing what a great deed was done; but Odysseus checked her and held her back, eager as she was, and spoke winged words: “Rejoice in your heart, old woman, but keep it in—do not raise the cry. It is not holy to exult over slain men. These men the fate of the gods and their own hard deeds brought down; for they honored no one on this earth, low or high, whoever came among them, and so by their own recklessness they have met a shameful fate. But come, name me the women of the halls—which ones dishonor me, and which are guiltless.”

Then the dear nurse Eurycleia answered him: “Then I will tell you the truth, my child. You have fifty serving-women in your halls, women we have taught to work their tasks, to card wool and bear their servitude. Of these, twelve in all have gone the way of shamelessness, honoring neither me nor Penelope herself. Telemachus was only newly grown, and his mother would not let him give orders to the serving-women. But come, let me go up to the bright upper chamber and tell your wife—some god has laid sleep upon her.” Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Do not wake her yet. Tell the women who devised these shameful things to come here.”

So he spoke, and the old woman went off through the hall to carry word to the women and hurry them along. Then Odysseus called Telemachus and the cowherd and the swineherd to him, and spoke winged words: “Begin carrying out the dead, and set the women to the work; then have the beautiful chairs and the tables cleaned with water and porous sponges. And when you have set the whole house in order, take the serving-women out of the strong-built hall, to the space between the round-house and the good wall of the courtyard, and strike them with your long-edged swords until you have taken the life from them all, and they have forgotten the lovemaking they had with the suitors, coupling with them in secret.”

So he spoke, and the women came all in a huddle, wailing terribly, the big tears streaming down. First they carried out the bodies of the dead and laid them under the portico of the well-fenced courtyard, propping them against one another; Odysseus himself gave the orders and drove them on, and they carried the bodies out under compulsion. Then they cleaned the beautiful chairs and the tables with water and porous sponges, while Telemachus and the cowherd and the swineherd scraped the floor of the well-made house with hoes, and the serving-women carried the scrapings away and put them outside.

And when they had set the whole hall in order, they led the serving-women out of the strong-built hall, to the space between the round-house and the good wall of the courtyard, and penned them into the narrow place, from which there was no escape. Thoughtful Telemachus spoke first among them: “No clean death—let me never take the lives of these women by a clean death, women who poured insults on my head and on my mother, and slept beside the suitors.” So he spoke, and he made fast the cable of a dark-prowed ship to a great pillar and threw it around the round-house, stretching it high, so that no one's feet could reach the ground. As when long-winged thrushes or doves fly into a snare set in a thicket, seeking their roosting-place, and a hateful bed receives them, so the women held their heads in a row, and around every neck was a noose, so that they might die most piteously. They twitched with their feet a little while—not for long. Then they dragged Melanthius out through the porch and the courtyard; they cut off his nose and his ears with the pitiless bronze, tore out his genitals for the dogs to eat raw, and chopped off his hands and his feet in the fury of their hearts. Then they washed their hands and feet and went back into the house to Odysseus, and the work was done.

But Odysseus said to the dear nurse Eurycleia: “Bring sulfur, old woman, the cure for evils, and bring me fire, so that I may fumigate the hall; and tell Penelope to come here with her waiting-women, and order all the women of the house to come.” The dear nurse Eurycleia answered him: “Yes, my child, in all this you have spoken rightly. But come, let me bring you a cloak and a tunic to wear; do not stand in your halls like this, with those broad shoulders wrapped in rags—that would be cause for blame.” Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “First of all, let there be fire for me in the hall.” So he spoke, and the dear nurse Eurycleia did not disobey, but brought fire and sulfur; and Odysseus thoroughly fumigated the hall and the house and the courtyard. Then the old woman went back through the beautiful house of Odysseus to carry word to the women and hurry them along, and they came from their hall with torches in their hands. They poured around Odysseus and embraced him, kissing his head and shoulders in loving welcome and clasping his hands; and a sweet longing seized him to weep and sob, for in his heart he knew them all.

## Book XXIII

The old woman went up to the upper chamber, laughing aloud with joy, to tell her mistress that her dear husband was in the house. Her knees moved briskly, and her feet stumbled over each other in her haste. She stood at the head of the bed and spoke to her: “Wake up, Penelope, dear child, so you may see with your own eyes what you have longed for all your days. Odysseus has come—he has reached his home, late though his coming was—and he has killed the proud suitors who plagued his house, and ate up his possessions, and used violence against his son.”

Then circumspect Penelope answered her: “Dear nurse, the gods have driven you mad. They can turn even a very sensible person into a fool, and they have set many a simple soul on the path of good sense—and it is they who have addled you; before this your mind was sound. Why do you mock me, when my heart is full of grief, telling me this wild tale, and wake me from sleep—sweet sleep, that bound me fast and closed over my dear eyelids? I have not slept so soundly since Odysseus went away to look upon accursed Ilium, that should never be named. Come now, go back down to the hall. If any other of my women had come to tell me such things and woken me from sleep, I would soon have sent her packing back into the hall in disgrace; but your old age, at least, will spare you that.”

Then the dear nurse Eurycleia answered her: “I am not mocking you, dear child. It is really true: Odysseus has come and reached his home, just as I tell you—he is the stranger whom everyone treated with dishonor in the halls. Telemachus knew long ago that he was in the house, but with good sense he kept his father's plans hidden, until he could take vengeance on those overbearing men for their violence.”

So she spoke, and Penelope was overjoyed. She sprang from the bed and threw her arms around the old woman, and tears fell from her eyelids; and she spoke winged words to her: “Come then, dear nurse, tell me truly—if he really has reached home, as you say, how did he lay hands on the shameless suitors, one man alone, when they always stayed inside in a crowd together?”

Then the dear nurse Eurycleia answered her: “I did not see it, and no one told me; I only heard the groaning of men being killed. We sat in the innermost corner of our well-built quarters, terrified, with the close-fitted doors holding us shut in, until the moment your son Telemachus called me from the hall—his father had sent him to call me. Then I found Odysseus standing among the bodies of the slain, and they lay around him on the hard-trodden floor, heaped upon one another. It would have warmed your heart to see him. Now the dead are all gathered together at the courtyard gates, while he purifies the beautiful house with sulfur, having kindled a great fire; and he sent me to call you. Come with me, so that both your hearts may set foot at last on the road to happiness, after all the evils you have suffered. Now this long desire has been fulfilled at last: he has come to his own hearth alive, and found both you and his son in the halls; and the suitors who did him wrong—he has taken vengeance on every one of them in his own house.”

Then circumspect Penelope answered her: “Dear nurse, do not laugh in triumph over them yet. You know how welcome the sight of him in these halls would be to everyone, and to no one more than to me and to the son we two brought into the world. But this tale cannot be true as you tell it. No—it was one of the immortals who killed the lordly suitors, in anger at their galling insolence and their evil deeds. For they honored no one on this earth, good man or bad, whoever came among them; and so through their own recklessness they came to grief. But Odysseus, far away, has lost his homecoming to the land of Achaea—lost it, and been lost himself.”

Then the dear nurse Eurycleia answered her: “My child, what a word has escaped the barrier of your teeth—to say that your husband, who is here at his own hearth, would never come home! Your heart has always been unbelieving. But come, let me tell you another sign, and an unmistakable one: the scar that a boar dealt him long ago with its white tusk. I noticed it while I was washing him, and I wanted to tell you then and there, but he caught me by the mouth with his hands and would not let me speak, in the great cunning of his mind. Come with me. And I will stake my own life on it: if I am deceiving you, kill me by the most pitiful of deaths.”

Then circumspect Penelope answered her: “Dear nurse, it is hard for you to fathom the designs of the gods who live forever, however wise you are. But let us go to my son all the same, so that I may see the suitors dead, and the man who killed them.” With these words she went down from the upper chamber; and her heart wavered again and again—should she question her dear husband from a distance, or go to his side and kiss his head and take his hands? When she had come in and crossed the stone threshold, she sat down opposite Odysseus in the light of the fire, by the far wall; and he sat by a tall pillar, looking down, waiting to hear whether his brave wife would say anything to him when she saw him with her eyes. But she sat a long time in silence, and amazement had come over her heart. At one moment she would look full into his face; at the next she would fail to know him, for the wretched clothes he was wearing.

Telemachus rebuked her, and spoke to her directly: “Mother—no true mother, with that unfeeling heart—why do you keep away from my father like this? Why not sit beside him, and question him, and ask him everything? No other woman would steel her heart like this and stand apart from her husband, when after suffering many evils he had come home in the twentieth year to his native land. But your heart is always harder than stone.” Then circumspect Penelope answered him: “My child, the heart in my breast is stunned. I cannot speak a word to him, or ask a single question, or even look him in the face. But if he truly is Odysseus, come home at last, then we two shall surely know each other, and know beyond doubt; for there are signs between us, hidden from everyone else, that we two alone know.”

So she spoke, and much-enduring godlike Odysseus smiled, and at once spoke winged words to Telemachus: “Telemachus, let your mother test me here in the halls; she will soon see things more clearly. Right now I am filthy and dressed in wretched clothes, and for that she slights me and will not yet say that I am he. But let us consider how all this may turn out for the best. When a man kills even one man in a community—even a man who leaves few behind to avenge him—he goes into exile, abandoning his kin and his native land. And we have killed the very prop of the city, the finest by far of the young men of Ithaca. I urge you to think about that.”

Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “See to it yourself, dear father. Yours, they say, is the best counsel among men; no other mortal man could rival you. We will follow you eagerly, and I promise we will not fail in courage, as far as our strength holds.” Then resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Very well, I will tell you how it seems best to me. First bathe yourselves and put on tunics, and tell the serving women in the halls to choose their dresses. Then let the divine singer take his clear-toned lyre and lead us in a merry dance, so that anyone who hears it from outside—someone passing on the road, or one of our neighbors—will say there is a wedding going on. That way no broad rumor of the suitors' slaughter will spread down through the town before we can get away to our well-wooded farm. Once there, we shall see what advantage the Olympian may put into our hands.”

So he spoke, and they listened well and obeyed him. First they bathed and put on their tunics, and the women adorned themselves; and the divine singer took up his hollow lyre and stirred in them the longing for sweet song and the flawless dance. The great house rang all around with the feet of dancing men and fair-girdled women; and out in the street, anyone who heard it would say something like this: “So someone has married the much-courted queen after all. Heartless woman! She had not the endurance to keep her wedded husband's great house safe to the end, until he came.” So they would say; but they had no idea how things really stood.

Meanwhile, in his own house, the housekeeper Eurynome bathed great-hearted Odysseus and rubbed him with oil, and dressed him in a beautiful cloak and tunic; and Athena shed great beauty over his head, making him taller and broader to look upon, and made the curling locks flow down from his head like the hyacinth flower. As when a skilled craftsman overlays silver with gold—a man whom Hephaestus and Pallas Athena have taught every kind of art, and the work he finishes is full of grace—so she poured grace upon his head and shoulders. He came out of the bath looking in build like the immortals, and sat down again on the chair he had risen from, opposite his wife, and said to her:

“Strange woman! To you above all women the dwellers on Olympus have given an unyielding heart. No other woman would steel her heart like this and stand apart from her husband, when after suffering many evils he had come home in the twentieth year to his native land. Come then, nurse, make up a bed for me, so that I can lie down by myself; for the heart in this woman's breast is made of iron.” Then circumspect Penelope answered him: “Strange man! I am not being proud, or scornful, nor am I too much amazed; I know very well what you were like when you left Ithaca on your long-oared ship. Come, Eurycleia—make up the sturdy bedstead for him outside the well-built bridal chamber that he made himself. Move the sturdy bedstead out there, and pile it with bedding—fleeces and cloaks and bright coverlets.”

So she spoke, testing her husband. But Odysseus flared up, and spoke to his true-hearted wife: “Woman, that word cuts me to the heart. Who has moved my bed to another place? That would be hard even for the most skilled of men—unless a god himself should come and, if he wished, set it easily somewhere else; but of mortal men no one alive, not even a man in his full youth, could easily pry it loose. For a great secret is built into that finished bed, and it was I who made it, and no one else. Inside the courtyard grew a long-leaved olive tree, full-grown and flourishing, its trunk thick as a pillar. Around this I built my bridal chamber, working until I had finished it, with close-set stones, and I roofed it well above, and hung jointed doors on it, fitted close together. Then I cut away the foliage of the long-leaved olive, trimmed the trunk from the root up, and smoothed it round with the bronze, skillfully and well, and made it true to the line, shaping it into a bedpost; and I bored it all through with the auger. Starting from that post I worked at the bed and planed it smooth until I had finished, inlaying it with gold and silver and ivory; and across the frame I stretched a strap of oxhide, glowing with purple. There—I have laid the secret open to you. But I do not know, wife, whether my bed still stands firm in its place, or whether by now some man has cut through the olive trunk at the base and set the bed elsewhere.”

So he spoke, and where she stood her knees went slack, and her dear heart with them, as she recognized the sure signs that Odysseus had given her. Bursting into tears she ran straight to him, threw her arms around Odysseus's neck, and kissed his head, and said: “Do not be angry with me, Odysseus—you who in everything else have been the most understanding of men. It is the gods who gave us sorrow, the gods who begrudged us this: that we two should stay side by side, and enjoy our youth together, and come together to the threshold of old age. So do not be angry with me now, or hold it against me, that I did not welcome you like this the moment I first saw you. For the heart in my breast was always shivering with dread that some man would come and deceive me with his talk; there are so many who scheme for wicked gain. Not even Argive Helen, daughter of Zeus, would have lain in love with a stranger from another land, had she known that the warrior sons of the Achaeans would one day bring her home again to her own dear country. It was a god who stirred her to do that shameful thing; until that day she had never laid up in her heart the ruinous folly from which our sorrow, too, first came. But now—now that you have told me the unmistakable signs of our bed, which no other mortal has ever seen, no one but you and I and one single maidservant, Actoris, whom my father gave me when I first came here, and who kept the doors of our sturdy bridal chamber—now you convince my heart, unbending though it is.”

So she spoke, and stirred in him a still greater desire to weep; and he wept, holding the wife of his heart, so loyal and so true. As when land shows itself, welcome beyond words, to men swimming for their lives, men whose well-built ship Poseidon has smashed on the open sea, driving it under wind and heavy waves—only a few escape the gray sea and swim to shore, the salt crusted thick on their skin, and gladly they step out onto the land, delivered from ruin—so welcome to her was the sight of her husband as she gazed at him, and she could not bring herself to let her white arms go from his neck. And rosy-fingered Dawn would have risen upon their weeping, had not the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, thought of one thing more. She held the long night back at the world's edge, and kept golden-throned Dawn waiting at the stream of Ocean, and would not let her yoke her swift-footed horses that carry light to men, Lampus and Phaethon, the colts who draw the Dawn.

Then resourceful Odysseus said to his wife: “Wife, we have not yet come to the end of all our trials. There is still one labor ahead, beyond all measure, long and hard, and I must carry it through to the very end. So the soul of Tiresias prophesied to me on the day I went down into the house of Hades, seeking a homecoming for my companions and for myself. But come, wife, let us go to bed, so that we may take our pleasure at last in sweet sleep, lying side by side.” Then circumspect Penelope answered him: “Your bed will be ready for you the moment your heart desires it, since the gods have brought you home to your well-built house and your native land. But since you have thought of it—since a god has put it in your heart—come, tell me about this trial. I shall hear of it later in any case, I suppose, and it is no worse to learn of it right now.”

Resourceful Odysseus answered her: “Strange woman, why do you press me so urgently to tell it? Well, I will speak it out and hide nothing—though your heart will take no joy in it, and I take no joy in it myself. He told me to go out through many cities of men, carrying a well-balanced oar in my hands, until I come to a people who know nothing of the sea, men who eat their food unmixed with salt, who have never heard of crimson-cheeked ships, or of the well-balanced oars that are the wings of ships. And he gave me a sign, an unmistakable one, which I will not hide from you: when another traveler falls in with me on the road and says I am carrying a winnowing-fan on my shining shoulder, then and there I am to fix my oar in the earth and make handsome offerings to lord Poseidon—a ram, a bull, and a boar that mates with sows—and then turn back for home, and offer holy hecatombs to the immortal gods who hold the wide heavens, to every one of them in order. And death will come to me away from the sea, the gentlest death there could be, and take me when I am worn down by a sleek old age, with my people prosperous around me. All this, he told me, will be fulfilled.” Then circumspect Penelope answered him: “If the gods really do grant you a happier old age, then there is hope you will find escape from your troubles at last.”

So they talked of these things with each other; and meanwhile Eurynome and the nurse made up the bed with soft coverings by the light of blazing torches. When they had briskly spread the sturdy bed, the old woman went back to her own room to sleep, while Eurynome, the keeper of the chamber, walked ahead of them with a torch in her hands as they went to their bed. She led them to the chamber, and then withdrew; and gladly the two of them came again to the old rite of their own bed. Then Telemachus and the cowherd and the swineherd rested their feet from the dance, and made the women stop as well, and they all lay down to sleep through the shadowy halls.

But when the two of them had taken their fill of the delight of love, they took delight in talking, each telling their story to the other. She, noble among women, told all she had endured in the halls, watching that destroying crowd of suitors, who on her account slaughtered so many beasts, cattle and fat sheep, and drained so much wine from the jars; and Zeus-born Odysseus told all the sorrows he had dealt to men, and all he had toiled through in his own misery—he told it all, and she delighted in listening, and no sleep fell upon her eyelids until he had told everything to the end.

He began with how he first overcame the Cicones, and came next to the rich land of the Lotus-eaters; and all that the Cyclops did, and how he made him pay the price for the brave companions the monster had eaten without pity; and how he came to Aeolus, who welcomed him kindly and sent him on his way—but it was not yet his fate to reach his own dear country, and a storm-wind snatched him up again and carried him, groaning heavily, over the fish-filled sea; and how he came to Telepylus of the Laestrygonians, who destroyed his ships and all his well-greaved companions—only Odysseus escaped, in his black ship. He told of Circe's craft and all her cunning; and how he traveled in his many-benched ship to the dank house of Hades, to consult the soul of Theban Tiresias, and saw there all his companions, and the mother who had borne him and nursed him when he was small; and how he heard the voice of the never-silent Sirens; and how he came to the Wandering Rocks, and to dread Charybdis, and to Scylla, whom no men have ever yet escaped unscathed; and how his companions killed the cattle of Helios; and how Zeus who thunders on high struck his swift ship with a smoking thunderbolt, and his brave companions perished all together, while he alone escaped the evil fates; and how he came to the island of Ogygia and the nymph Calypso, who kept him there in her hollow caves, longing for him to be her husband, and cared for him, and kept promising to make him immortal and ageless all his days—but she never won over the heart in his breast; and how, after much suffering, he came to the Phaeacians, who honored him with all their hearts, like a god, and sent him home by ship to his own dear country, with gifts of bronze and gold in plenty, and clothing. That was the last word he spoke, as sweet sleep, the loosener of limbs, sprang upon him, loosening the cares of his heart.

Then the goddess, bright-eyed Athena, thought of one thing more. When she judged that Odysseus had had his heart's fill of his wife's bed and of sleep, at once she roused golden-throned, early-born Dawn from the stream of Ocean, to bring light to men. Odysseus rose from the soft bed and gave his wife her charge: “Wife, by now we have both had our fill of trials—you weeping here over my troubled homecoming, while Zeus and the other gods kept me bound in sorrows, far from my native land, for all my longing to return. Now that we have both come at last to the bed we so desired, you must take care of the possessions I still have within the halls. As for the flocks the overbearing suitors have wasted, many I will seize as plunder myself, and the Achaeans will give others, until they have filled all my pens. But now I am going out to the well-wooded farm, to see my noble father, who has grieved so long on my account. And on you, wife, sensible as you are, I lay this charge: with the rising of the sun, word will be out at once about the suitors I killed in the halls; so go up to your upper chamber with your serving women and stay there—look at no one, and ask no questions.”

He spoke, and put his beautiful armor about his shoulders, and roused Telemachus and the cowherd and the swineherd, and told them all to take up their weapons of war. They did not disobey him, but armed themselves in bronze, and opened the doors, and went out, with Odysseus leading. By now the light was already over the earth; but Athena hid them in night, and led them swiftly out of the city.

## Book XXIV

Hermes of Cyllene called forth the souls of the suitors, holding in his hands the beautiful golden wand with which he charms the eyes of men, of whomever he wishes, and just as easily wakes others from sleep. With this he roused them and led them on, and they followed, gibbering. As bats in the depths of some awesome cave flit about gibbering, when one of them has fallen from the cluster on the rock where they cling together, so these went their way gibbering together, and Hermes the Helper led them down the dank paths. They went past the streams of Ocean and the White Rock, past the gates of the Sun and the land of dreams, and quickly came to the field of asphodel, where the souls, the phantoms of the dead, have their home.

There they found the soul of Achilles, son of Peleus, and of Patroclus, and of noble Antilochus, and of Ajax, who was the finest in looks and build of all the Danaans after the peerless son of Peleus. So these were gathered around Achilles; and close by came the soul of Agamemnon, son of Atreus, grieving, with all the others gathered about him who had died with him in the house of Aegisthus and met their fate there.

The soul of Achilles spoke first to him: “Son of Atreus, we always said that you, above all heroes, were dear to Zeus who delights in thunder, all your days, since you ruled over so many brave men in the land of the Trojans, where we Achaeans suffered such hardship. And yet death's grim fate, which no man born can escape, was destined to come upon you early all the same. How I wish that, still holding the honor you commanded, you had met your death and fate in the land of the Trojans! Then the whole army of the Achaeans would have raised you a grave-mound, and you would have won great glory for your son as well. But as it is, you were doomed to be caught by the most pitiful death of all.”

Then the soul of the son of Atreus answered him: “Fortunate son of Peleus, godlike Achilles, who died at Troy, far from Argos! Around you others fell, the best of the sons of the Trojans and the Achaeans, fighting over you, while you lay great in the whirl of dust, mighty in your might, your horsemanship forgotten. We fought the whole day long; we would not have stopped fighting at all, if Zeus had not stopped us with a storm.

“But after we had carried you to the ships, away from the battle, we laid you on a bier, and cleansed your fair body with warm water and with oil; and the Danaans wept many hot tears around you, and cut their hair. Your mother came up from the sea with the immortal sea-nymphs, hearing the news, and a wondrous cry went up over the water, and trembling seized all the Achaeans; and they would have leapt up and gone to the hollow ships, had not a man who knew many things, both old and wise — Nestor, whose counsel had proved best before as well — held them back. He, meaning well, spoke and said to them: ‘Hold back, Argives, do not flee, you young men of the Achaeans; this is his mother coming up from the sea with the immortal sea-nymphs, to meet the body of her dead son.’ So he spoke, and the great-hearted Achaeans held back their fear. Around you the daughters of the old man of the sea stood, weeping piteously, and clothed you in immortal garments. And the nine Muses, singing in turn with beautiful voices, sang your dirge; there you would not have seen a single one of the Argives without tears, so piercingly did the clear-voiced Muse stir them. Seventeen days and nights alike we mourned you, both immortal gods and mortal men; on the eighteenth day we gave you to the fire, and killed around you many fat sheep and shambling cattle.

“You were burned in the garments of the gods, and with much oil, and with sweet honey; and many Achaean heroes moved in their armor around the burning pyre, both foot soldiers and horsemen, and a great clamor rose up. But when the flame of Hephaestus had finished with you, at dawn we gathered your white bones, Achilles, in unmixed wine and oil. Your mother gave a golden urn — she said it was a gift from Dionysus, the work of famous Hephaestus. In this your white bones lie, glorious Achilles, mixed with those of the dead Patroclus, son of Menoetius, but apart from those of Antilochus, whom you honored above all your other companions once Patroclus was dead. Around these bones we, the sacred army of Argive spearmen, heaped up a great and noble grave-mound, on a jutting headland by the broad Hellespont, so that it might be seen from far out at sea by men now living and those yet to be born.

“Your mother asked the gods for beautiful prizes, and set them out in the midst of the gathering for the best of the Achaeans. You have surely attended the funeral games of many other heroes before now, when at a king's death the young men gird themselves and make ready for the contests; but you would have marveled most of all at the sight of those prizes the goddess set out for you, silver-footed Thetis, for you were greatly loved by the gods. So even in death you have not lost your name, but your fame will always be good among all men, Achilles. But as for me, what joy is this to me, now that I have wound up the war? For on my homecoming Zeus devised a miserable death for me, at the hands of Aegisthus and my accursed wife.”

So they spoke such things to one another, and the messenger, the slayer of Argus, came near to them, leading down the souls of the suitors slain by Odysseus. The two of them, astonished, went straight up to them when they saw them. And the soul of Agamemnon, son of Atreus, recognized the dear son of Melaneus, glorious Amphimedon, for he had been his guest, living in a house in Ithaca.

The soul of the son of Atreus spoke first to him: “Amphimedon, what happened to you, that you have all come down to the dark earth, all of you chosen men and of an age? One could not have picked out better men in the whole city by seeking them out. Did Poseidon overwhelm you on your ships, raising harsh winds and long waves? Or did enemies harm you on land, as you were cutting out cattle and fine flocks of sheep, or fighting over a city and its women? Tell me, since I ask — I claim to be a guest-friend of yours. Do you not remember when I came down there, to your house, along with godlike Menelaus, to urge Odysseus to join us in going to Ilium aboard our well-benched ships? It took us a whole month to cross the whole wide sea, so hard were we pressed to persuade Odysseus, sacker of cities.”

Then the soul of Amphimedon answered him: “Most glorious son of Atreus, king of men, Agamemnon, I remember all this, foster son of Zeus, just as you say; and I will tell you fully and exactly the evil end of our death, how it came about.

“We were courting the wife of Odysseus, who had long been gone; and she neither refused the hateful marriage nor brought it to an end, but planned death and black doom for us in this other trick of hers: she set up a great loom in her halls and began weaving a web, fine and very wide, and said to us at once: ‘Young men, my suitors, since noble Odysseus is dead, wait, eager as you are for my marriage, until I finish this robe — so that my weaving not go to waste — a shroud for the hero Laertes, for the time when the cruel fate of death that lays men low will take him, lest any of the Achaean women in this land should reproach me, if he who won so much should lie without a shroud.’ So she spoke, and our proud hearts consented. Then by day she would weave at the great web, and by night she would unravel it, once she had torches set beside her. So for three years she deceived us with this trick and persuaded the Achaeans; but when the fourth year came and the seasons rolled on, as the months waned and many days had been completed, then one of her women, who knew the truth well, told us, and we caught her unraveling the splendid web. So she finished it then, against her will, forced to it.

“When she showed us the robe, having woven the great web and washed it, shining like the sun or the moon, then some hostile spirit brought Odysseus from somewhere to the edge of the estate, where the swineherd had his dwelling. There too came the dear son of godlike Odysseus, returning in his black ship from sandy Pylos; and the two of them, having plotted a wretched death for the suitors, came to the famous city — Odysseus following behind, Telemachus leading the way ahead of him. The swineherd brought him dressed in wretched clothes on his body, looking like a miserable beggar, and an old man too, leaning on a staff; and the ragged clothes he wore about his body were pitiful. None of us could tell who he was when he suddenly appeared, not even the older men among us, but we assailed him with harsh words and with missiles. And for a while he endured it patiently in his own halls, being struck and abused, with a heart that could bear it; but when the will of aegis-bearing Zeus roused him up, together with Telemachus he took up the beautiful armor and stored it away in an inner room, and bolted the doors; and then, with great cunning, he told his wife to set out the bow and the gray iron for the suitors, as a contest for us doomed men and the beginning of our slaughter. None of us could string the great bow's cord — we fell far short of the strength for it. But when the great bow came into the hands of Odysseus, then all of us shouted out not to give him the bow, no matter how much he might plead; only Telemachus urged him on and bid him take it. Then long-suffering, godlike Odysseus took it in his hand, and easily strung the bow, and shot an arrow clean through the iron; then he went and stood on the threshold, and poured out his swift arrows, glaring terribly about him, and struck down King Antinous. And then he let fly his grim shafts at the others too, aiming straight at them, and they fell one after another. It was plain that one of the gods was helping them, for at once they charged through the hall with all their fury and killed us one after another, and a hideous groaning arose from men's heads being struck, and the whole floor ran with blood.

“So we perished, Agamemnon, and even now our bodies lie uncared-for in the halls of Odysseus, for our own people in each of our homes do not yet know, those who would wash the dark clotted blood from our wounds, and lay us out, and mourn us — for that is the due of the dead.”

Then the soul of the son of Atreus answered him: “Fortunate son of Laertes, resourceful Odysseus, you have indeed won a wife of great virtue! How good was the heart of blameless Penelope, daughter of Icarius, how well she remembered Odysseus, the husband of her youth! And so the fame of her virtue will never die, but the immortal gods will make a lovely song for men on earth in honor of constant Penelope — unlike the daughter of Tyndareus, who devised wicked deeds, killing her wedded husband; a hateful song she will be among men, and she will bring a harsh reputation upon all women, even those who do right.”

So they spoke such things to one another, standing there in the house of Hades, in the depths of the earth. Meanwhile the others, having come down from the city, quickly reached the fine farm of Laertes, which he himself had won by hard toil, long ago. There was his house, with a shed running all around it, where the household slaves who worked for him ate and sat and slept, servants he was obliged to keep; and there was an old Sicilian woman too, who cared for the old man faithfully out in the countryside, away from the city.

There Odysseus spoke to the servants and to his son: “Go now into the well-built house, and quickly kill the best of the pigs for our meal; meanwhile I will test my father, to see whether he will recognize me and know me by sight, or whether he will fail to know me, gone so long as I have been.” So saying he gave the servants his weapons of war. They went off quickly to the house, while Odysseus went closer, to test the fruitful vineyard. He did not find Dolius as he went down into the great orchard, nor any of the servants or his sons — they had gone off to gather stones for a wall around the vineyard, and the old man was leading the way for them. He found his father alone in the well-kept vineyard, digging around a young plant. He wore a filthy, patched tunic, poor and shabby, and around his shins he had bound patched leather leggings, to guard against scratches, and gloves on his hands because of the brambles; and on his head he wore a cap of goatskin, nursing his grief.

When long-suffering, godlike Odysseus saw him worn down by old age, with such great grief in his heart, he stood under a tall pear tree and let fall a tear. Then he pondered in his mind and heart whether he should embrace his father and kiss him, and tell him everything — how he had come and reached his own country — or whether he should first question him and test him in every way. As he thought it over, it seemed better to him to test him first with mocking words.

With this in mind, godlike Odysseus went straight up to him. He was digging around a plant, his head bent down; and his glorious son stood by him and said: “Old man, there is no lack of skill in your care of this orchard — everything is well tended, not a single plant, not a fig tree, not a vine, not an olive, not a pear tree, not a vegetable bed in the garden goes without care. But I will say something else, and do not be angry with me for it in your heart: you yourself are not so well cared for — instead you bear a wretched old age, filthy and dressed in rags. It's surely not for laziness that your master fails to care for you; nor is there anything of a slave to be seen in your build or stature — you look like a man of royal blood. You look like the kind of man who, once he has bathed and eaten, should sleep in comfort — for that is the way of old men. But come, tell me this, and be honest with me: whose slave are you, whose orchard do you tend? And tell me this too, truly, so I may know for certain: is this really Ithaca that I have come to, as this man told me just now, whom I met on my way here — though he wasn't very sharp, since he didn't have the patience to tell me everything or listen to my question, when I asked him about my guest-friend, whether he is still alive, or whether he is already dead and in the house of Hades.

“For I will tell you this, and you listen and take note: once, in my own dear country, I hosted a man who had come to our house, and no other stranger from a foreign land who ever came to my home was dearer to me. He claimed he was of Ithacan birth, and said that Laertes, son of Arcesius, was his father. I brought him to my house and entertained him well, welcoming him warmly, since I had plenty in my house, and I gave him proper guest-gifts, as was fitting.
