I gave him seven talents of well-wrought gold, and I gave him a mixing bowl, all silver, decorated with flowers, and twelve single cloaks, and as many blankets, and as many fine robes, and as many tunics to go with them; and besides these, four women skilled in fine handiwork, and beautiful, whichever ones he wished to choose.”

Then his father answered him, shedding tears: “Stranger, you have indeed come to the land you are asking about, but insolent, reckless men now hold it. You have given all these countless gifts in vain — if you had found him still alive in the land of Ithaca, he would have sent you off well repaid with gifts and with good hospitality in return, as is only right for one who has shown kindness first. But come, tell me this, and be honest with me: how many years is it now since you entertained that guest of yours, my unlucky son — if he ever really existed at all? Poor wretch — far from his friends and his own country, either the fish ate him somewhere in the sea, or he became prey to wild beasts and birds on land; his mother did not weep over him and lay him out, nor did his father, we who bore him; nor did his richly-dowered wife, wise Penelope, wail over her husband on his bier, as would have been fitting, and close his eyes — for that is the due of the dead. And tell me this too truly, so that I may know: who are you, and where are you from? Where is your city, and your parents? Where is the swift ship at anchor that brought you here, and your godlike companions? Or did you come as a passenger on someone else's ship, and they set you ashore and went on their way?”

Then resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Well then, I will tell you everything quite exactly. I am from Alybas, where I have a famous house, and I am the son of King Apheidas, son of Polypemon; my own name is Eperitus. But some spirit drove me here from Sicania against my will, and my ship is anchored out in the country, away from the city. As for Odysseus, this is now the fifth year since he left there and departed from my country, poor man — though the birds were favorable for him as he went, on the right hand, and I sent him off gladly on account of them, and he too was glad to go; and our hearts still hoped to meet again as guest-friends and to exchange splendid gifts.”

So he spoke, and a black cloud of grief covered Laertes; with both hands he took up the dark dust and poured it over his gray head, groaning heavily. Odysseus's heart was stirred, and a sharp pang shot up through his nostrils as he looked at his father; he sprang forward, threw his arms around him, kissed him, and said: “It is I myself, father, the very man you're asking about — I have come back to my own country in the twentieth year. But hold back your weeping and tearful lamenting; for I will tell you plainly, though we must hurry all the same: I have killed the suitors in our house, taking vengeance for their heartbreaking insult and their wicked deeds.”

Then Laertes answered him and said: “If you really are my son Odysseus, come back here, then give me some clear sign now, so that I may believe it.”

Then resourceful Odysseus answered him: “First look with your own eyes at this scar, which a boar's white tusk dealt me on Parnassus, when I had gone there — you and my honored mother sent me, to my mother's father Autolycus, to receive the gifts he had promised me and agreed to give when he came here. And come, let me also name for you the trees in the well-kept vineyard that you once gave me, when I was a child following you through the garden and asking you for each one. We walked among them, and you named each one and told me what it was. You gave me thirteen pear trees and ten apple trees, and forty fig trees; and you promised to give me fifty rows of vines as well, each one ripening at a different time — for there are grapes of every kind on them — whenever the seasons of Zeus weighed down upon them from above.” So he spoke, and Laertes's knees gave way and his heart failed him, as he recognized the sure signs that Odysseus had shown him. He threw his arms around his beloved son; and long-suffering, godlike Odysseus caught him as he fainted, and held him close.

But when he had recovered his breath and his spirit had gathered back into his heart, he spoke again in answer, and said: “Father Zeus, you gods are indeed still there on high Olympus, if the suitors have truly paid for their reckless insolence! But now I am terribly afraid in my heart that soon all the men of Ithaca will come here against us, and send messages everywhere to the cities of the Cephallenians.” Then resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Take heart, do not let that trouble your mind. But let us go to the house, which lies close to the orchard; I have sent Telemachus and the cowherd and the swineherd on ahead there, to get a meal ready as quickly as possible.”

So saying, the two of them made their way to the fine house. When they arrived at the well-situated home, they found Telemachus and the cowherd and the swineherd carving up great quantities of meat and mixing sparkling wine. Meanwhile, in his own house, a Sicilian maidservant bathed great-hearted Laertes and anointed him with oil, and put a fine cloak around him; and Athena came close and filled out the limbs of the shepherd of his people, and made him taller and sturdier to look at than before. He stepped out of the bath, and his own son marveled to see him looking like one of the immortal gods; and he spoke to him and said: “Father, surely one of the gods who live forever has made you finer to see in form and stature.” Then wise Laertes answered him: “Would that, Father Zeus, and Athena, and Apollo, I were the man I was when I took Nericus, the well-built citadel on the mainland headland, ruling over the Cephallenians — had I been that man, standing beside you yesterday in our halls, with my armor on my shoulders, to fight off the suitors! Then I would have loosened the knees of many of them there in the hall, and you would have rejoiced in your heart.”

So they spoke such things to one another. But when the others had finished their work and prepared the meal, they sat down in order on couches and chairs; then, as they set about the meal, old Dolius arrived, and with him the old man's sons, tired from their labor, since their mother had gone out and called them — the old Sicilian woman who raised them, and who faithfully cared for the old man too, now that old age had overtaken him.

When they saw Odysseus and realized who he was, they stood in the hall, dumbfounded; but Odysseus spoke to them gently and said: “Old man, sit down to your meal, and put aside your amazement; for we have been waiting here in the hall a long while now, eager to begin, always expecting you.” So he spoke, and Dolius went straight up to him, both arms outstretched, and taking his hand, kissed it at the wrist, and spoke to him, saying: “Dear friend, since you have returned to us who longed for you so much, and no longer expected it, and the gods themselves have brought you back — hail, and welcome, and may the gods grant you happiness! But tell me this truly, so that I may know: does wise Penelope already know for certain that you have come home, or should we send a messenger?” Then resourceful Odysseus answered him: “Old man, she already knows; why should that be your concern?” So he spoke, and Dolius sat back down again on his polished chair. In just the same way, the sons of Dolius gathered around glorious Odysseus, greeting him with words and taking his hands, and then sat down in order beside Dolius, their father.

So they busied themselves with the meal there in the hall. Meanwhile Rumor went quickly through the whole city as a messenger, telling of the suitors' hateful death and fate. And hearing it, people came flocking from every side, with groaning and lamentation, in front of the house of Odysseus, and each family carried out its dead and buried them; and those from other cities they sent home, putting them aboard the swift ships of fishermen to be carried. Then they themselves went together to the assembly place, grieving in their hearts.

When they had gathered and were all assembled, Eupeithes rose among them and spoke, for unforgettable grief for his son lay in his heart — Antinous, the first man godlike Odysseus had killed. Weeping for him, he spoke and said: “Friends, this man has done a monstrous thing to the Achaeans. Some he led away with his ships, many brave men, and he lost the hollow ships, and lost the men as well; and now, come back, he has killed the very best of the Cephallenians. Come, then, before he can quickly reach Pylos, or even sacred Elis, where the Epeans rule, let us go after him — or else we will be forever disgraced. For it will be a shame even for men not yet born to hear of, if we do not take vengeance on the murderers of our sons and brothers. As for me, it would give me no pleasure in my heart to go on living — I would rather die at once and be with the dead. Let us go, before they can cross over and escape us.” So he spoke, weeping, and pity seized all the Achaeans.

Then Medon and the godlike singer came near them, out of the halls of Odysseus, since sleep had released them; and they stood in the midst of the crowd, and astonishment seized every man. Then Medon, who understood things well, spoke among them: “Listen to me now, men of Ithaca. Odysseus did not plan these deeds against the will of the immortal gods. I myself saw a deathless god standing close beside Odysseus, looking in every way like Mentor; and this immortal god appeared now in front of Odysseus, urging him on, now rushing among the suitors in fury, driving them in rout through the hall — and they fell one after another.” So he spoke, and pale fear seized them all.

Then the old hero Halitherses, son of Mastor, spoke among them as well, for he alone among them could see both forward and back; with good will he spoke and said: “Listen to me now too, men of Ithaca, and hear what I have to say. It is through your own cowardice, friends, that these things have happened — for you would not listen to me, nor to Mentor, shepherd of the people, and put a stop to your sons' folly, who did a monstrous thing in their wicked recklessness, wasting the property and dishonoring the wife of a great man, believing he would never come home again. So let it now be as it is; listen to me, as I tell you: let us not go, in case someone brings disaster down upon himself that he need not have found.”

So he spoke, and more than half of them sprang up with a great shout; but the rest remained there together, for this counsel did not please their hearts, and instead they followed Eupeithes; and at once they rushed to put on their armor. Then, once they had put the gleaming bronze about their bodies, they gathered together in front of the spacious city. Eupeithes led them in their folly; he thought he would avenge his son's murder, but he was not fated to return home again — instead he was to meet his doom there.

Meanwhile Athena spoke to Zeus, son of Cronus: “Father of us all, son of Cronus, highest of rulers, answer this question of mine: what is your mind hiding now, deep within? Will you stir up evil war and dreadful conflict further, or will you establish friendship between both sides?” Then Zeus, gatherer of the clouds, answered her: “My child, why do you ask me this and question me about it? Was it not you yourself who devised this plan, that Odysseus should come back and take vengeance on those men? Do as you wish; but I will tell you what is fitting: since godlike Odysseus has taken his vengeance on the suitors, let them swear a solemn oath, and let him be king always, and let us on our part bring about a forgetting of the killing of their sons and brothers; and let them love one another as before, and let there be wealth and peace in abundance.” So saying, he spurred on Athena, who was already eager, and she went darting down from the peaks of Olympus.

Now when they had satisfied their desire for the sweet food, long-suffering, godlike Odysseus was the first among them to speak: “Let someone go out and see whether they are already close by, coming this way.” So he spoke, and one of Dolius's sons went out, as he had ordered; he stood on the threshold and saw them all close at hand; and at once he spoke winged words to Odysseus: “Here they are, close by — let us arm ourselves quickly!” So he spoke, and they sprang up and put on their armor — four men around Odysseus, and the six sons of Dolius; and Laertes and Dolius too put on their armor, gray-haired as they were, soldiers out of necessity. When they had put the gleaming bronze about their bodies, they opened the doors and went out, with Odysseus leading the way.

Then Athena, daughter of Zeus, came close to them, looking like Mentor in form and voice. Seeing her, long-suffering, godlike Odysseus rejoiced, and at once he spoke to his own dear son Telemachus: “Telemachus, now that you have come to this yourself, you will soon learn this much — that where the best men are tested in battle, one should not bring shame upon one's fathers' line, who in strength and courage have always been distinguished over the whole earth.” Then thoughtful Telemachus answered him: “You will see, dear father, if you wish, that in this spirit at least I bring no shame upon your line, as you say.” So he spoke, and Laertes rejoiced, and said: “What a day is this for me, dear gods! I am overjoyed indeed — my son and my grandson are vying with each other in courage!”

Then bright-eyed Athena stood close beside him and said: “Son of Arcesius, dearest by far of all my companions, pray to the bright-eyed maiden and to father Zeus, then quickly poise and hurl your long-shadowed spear.” So spoke Pallas Athena, and breathed great strength into him. Then, praying to the daughter of great Zeus, he quickly poised and hurled his long-shadowed spear, and struck Eupeithes through his bronze-cheeked helmet. The bronze did not stop the spear but passed clean through, and he fell with a crash, and his armor clattered upon him. Then Odysseus and his glorious son fell upon the front ranks, striking with swords and double-edged spears, and now they would have destroyed them all and left none to return home —

had not Athena, daughter of Zeus who bears the aegis, cried out, and held back the whole army with her voice: “Hold back from this grievous war, men of Ithaca, so that you may part from one another as quickly as possible, without bloodshed.” So spoke Athena, and pale fear seized them; in their terror, their weapons flew from their hands and all fell to the ground as the goddess spoke, and they turned back toward the city, longing to save their lives.

Then long-suffering, godlike Odysseus gave a terrible shout, and gathering himself, swooped like a high-flying eagle. And at that moment the son of Cronus let fly a smoking thunderbolt, and it fell before the bright-eyed daughter of the mighty father. Then bright-eyed Athena spoke to Odysseus: “Zeus-born son of Laertes, resourceful Odysseus, hold back, put an end to this strife of even-handed war, lest the son of Cronus, wide-seeing Zeus, be angry with you.” So spoke Athena, and he obeyed, and his heart rejoiced. And then Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus who bears the aegis, made a solemn covenant between both sides for the future, still looking like Mentor in form and voice.
